# Dependent prepositions: noun patterns

> The prepositions that a noun drags along behind it — *a reason **for***, *a solution **to***, *an increase **in*** — chosen not by the writer but by the noun itself.

[← The grammar of prepositions](../README.md) &middot; [All categories](README.md)

Everywhere else in this dictionary a preposition is a word with a meaning, and you pick it because of what you want to say. Here it is a piece of another word's spelling. *Solution* takes *to*, *cause* takes *of*, *increase* takes *in*, and no fact about solutions, causes, or increases explains why — the pairing is arbitrary, historical, and simply has to be learned with the noun, the way a French learner learns that *table* is feminine. Nothing in this file is a choice, which is why the entries below read as a list of facts rather than a set of distinctions, and why the rest of the dictionary — [the core three](01-core-in-on-at.md), [the core relational six](02-core-relational.md), [place](03-place-position.md), [movement](04-movement-direction.md), [time](05-time.md), and the categories after them — treats prepositions as words in their own right, with meanings you can reason about.

Its companions are [dependent prepositions: verb patterns](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md) and [dependent prepositions: adjective patterns](15-dependent-adjectives.md). Take the three together: the same root often takes one preposition as a verb, a second as a noun, and a third as an adjective, and the shift between them is where fluent writers slip.

---

## The noun-versus-verb shift

**The most productive error in this file.** English routinely gives a noun a different preposition from the verb it comes from — and, worse, gives a preposition to the noun when the verb takes none at all. *Discuss* is a bare transitive verb (*we discussed the budget*, never ✗ *we discussed about the budget*), but the noun needs a preposition to attach anything (*a discussion **about** the budget*). The verb form is the one learners over-generalize from, so the ✗ forms below are the ones that survive into advanced writing.

| Verb (what it takes) | Noun (what it takes) | The error to kill |
|---|---|---|
| *discuss* something — **no preposition** | *a discussion **about / of*** something | ✗ *discuss about the budget* |
| *marry* someone — **no preposition** | *her marriage **to*** someone | ✗ *marry with him*, ✗ *married with her* |
| *lack* something — **no preposition** | *a lack **of*** something | ✗ *he lacks of experience* |
| *enter* a room — **no preposition** | *entry **into*** a room | ✗ *enter into the room* (literal sense) |
| *answer* a question — **no preposition** | *the answer **to*** a question | ✗ *the answer of the question* |
| *approach* the city — **no preposition** | *an approach **to*** the problem | ✗ *approach to the city* (as a verb) |
| *emphasize* the risk — **no preposition** | *the emphasis **on*** the risk | ✗ *emphasize on the risk* |
| *request* a copy — **no preposition** | *a request **for*** a copy | ✗ *request for a copy* (as a verb) |
| *attend* the meeting — **no preposition** | *attendance **at*** the meeting | ✗ *attend to the meeting* (different sense) |
| *reach* the summit — **no preposition** | *within reach **of*** the summit | ✗ *reach to the summit* |
| *resemble* his father — **no preposition** | *a resemblance **to*** his father | ✗ *resembles to his father* |
| *contact* the office — **no preposition** | *contact **with*** the office | ✗ *contact with the office* (as a verb) |
| *protest* the ruling — **no preposition** (American) | *a protest **against*** the ruling | *protest against the ruling* is British, not an error |

And the shift runs the other way just as often, where both forms take a preposition but not the same one:

| Verb | Noun | Note |
|---|---|---|
| *believe **in*** it | *a belief **in*** it | same — the easy case |
| *depend **on*** it | *dependence **on*** it | same |
| *reply **to*** it | *a reply **to*** it | same — *reply* keeps *to* in both, unlike *answer* |
| *compare* X **with** Y | *a comparison **with / to*** Y | verb prefers *with*, noun takes either |
| *substitute* X **for** Y | *a substitute **for*** Y | the verb reverses the roles — see [substitute for](#substitute-for) |
| *search **for*** a cure / *search* a house | *the search **for*** a cure | *search* is transitive in one sense and prepositional in the other |
| *benefit **from*** it | *the benefit **of*** it | *from* on the verb, *of* on the noun |

The complete list of zero-preposition verbs has [a section of its own at the end of this file](#zero-preposition-traps-verbs-that-take-no-preposition-at-all).

---

## *Reason for*, *reason why*, *reason that*

Four frames, three of them fully standard in American English and one of them a genuine error.

- **the reason for + noun phrase or *-ing*** — *the reason **for** the delay*, *the reason **for** postponing the vote*. The preposition frame. ✗ *the reason of the delay*.
- **the reason why + clause** — *the reason **why** she left*. Old, standard, and used continuously in edited American prose since Middle English. The claim that it is redundant is a nineteenth-century invention; ignore it. It is heaviest in emphatic and rhetorical writing (*that is the reason why*).
- **the reason that + clause**, or with nothing at all — *the reason **that** she left*, *the reason ø she left*. Both are lighter than *why* and slightly more formal in edited prose. The bare version is the commonest in speech.
- **the reason to + infinitive** — *a reason **to** leave early*. Not the same thing: this is a justification for a future act, not an explanation of a past one. Compare *the reason for leaving early* (why it happened) with *a reason to leave early* (why you should).
- ✗ **the reason is because** — the error. *Because* already means "for the reason that," so *the reason is because he was sick* says it twice. Write *the reason is **that** he was sick*, or drop the frame: *he was sick*. In speech nobody notices; in writing an editor will.

Full entry: [reason for](#reason-for).

---

## *A way to do it* against *a way of doing it*

Both are correct. They are not identical, and neither one is the safe default.

- **way to + infinitive** — a means of reaching a goal. *There has to be **a way to** fix this.* *That's not **the way to** talk to a customer.* It answers "how do I get the result I want?"
- **way of + *-ing*** — a manner, habit, or characteristic style. *He has **a way of** turning every conversation into an argument.* *It was her **way of** saying thank you.* It answers "what is this person's or thing's manner?"
- The overlap is real: *a way to make bread* and *a way of making bread* are both fine and mean nearly the same thing. The split shows up at the edges — you cannot say ✗ *a way to turning every conversation into an argument*, and *her way to say thank you* sounds wrong because it is a manner, not a method.
- ✗ **a way for doing it** and ✗ **a way of do it** are both errors. After *of* the verb takes *-ing*; after *to* it takes the bare form.

Full entry: [way of/to](#way-ofto).

---

## The *to* trap

Half the errors in this file come from one ambiguity: **the letters *t-o* spell two different words.** They are told apart by one test — put a plain noun in the slot.

- If a noun fits, *to* is a **preposition**, and a verb after it must be an ***-ing* form**: *an objection **to** the fee* → *an objection **to** being asked*, never ✗ *an objection to be asked*. Same for *an alternative to driving*, *an [obstacle to](#obstacle-to) finishing*, *the key to solving it*, *opposition to raising taxes*, *[resistance to](#resistance-to) being told*, *with a view to selling*.
- If a noun does not fit, *to* is the **infinitive marker**, and a bare verb follows: *a **willingness to** compromise* (✗ *a willingness to the compromise*, ✗ *a willingness to compromising*). Same for *permission to leave*, *a chance to speak*, *a decision to resign*, *the right to remain silent*, *a tendency to overreact*.
- A few nouns take both, with different meanings. *A chance **of** winning* is a probability; *a chance **to** win* is an opportunity. *An attempt **at** humor* is a try at a thing; *an attempt **to** be funny* is a try at an act. *Permission **for** the trip* is a thing permitted; *permission **to** travel* is an act permitted. And one noun takes both spellings of *to* with no change of meaning at all: *a [commitment to](#commitment-to) reducing emissions* and *a commitment **to** reduce them* are equally standard, which is exactly why the adjective *committed **to** reducing* — gerund only — catches people out.

---

## How to read the entries

Every entry names the pattern, not the bare noun, because the pattern is the unit you have to memorize. The **Complement** line is the one to read twice: it says what can follow the preposition and, more usefully, what cannot. The **Contrast** line is where the meaning-changing swaps live — *concern about* against *concern for*, *interest in* against *interest on*, *taste for* against *taste in*. Weak forms are given because the preposition in these patterns is almost never stressed: *a solution to it* comes out /ə səˈluʃən tə ɪt/ in any normal sentence, and stressing the *to* would sound like a correction.

A note on the American forms used throughout: *toward* and *afterward* without the *-s*; *different from* (with *different than* accepted before a clause and *different to* marked British); *talk with* alongside *talk to*; *on the weekend*, not *at the weekend*; *write me*, not only *write to me*; *protest something* transitively, where British English says *protest against something*.


---

The entries run alphabetically by headword. Where one noun takes two or three genuinely different prepositions, they share a single entry — *advantage of/over*, *concern about/for*, *taste for/in* — because the choice between them is the thing actually worth learning. The verbs that take no preposition at all are gathered in [a section of their own at the end](#zero-preposition-traps-verbs-that-take-no-preposition-at-all).


## A

### access to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈæksɛs tu/ &middot; *AK-ses too* &middot; weak /ˈæksɛs tə/
**Русский:** доступ к — право или возможность войти, дойти, воспользоваться
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (*access to funding*, *access to editing the master file*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *access to edit the file*, unless *to edit* is a purpose clause after a complete phrase) and **never a that-clause**
**Pattern:** *have / gain / get / deny / grant* **access to** [noun phrase] &middot; *access to* [someone] &middot; *give* [someone] *access to* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ***Access for*** names the beneficiary, not the thing reached: *access **to** the building* is what you can enter, *access **for** wheelchair users* is who gets in. The verb *access* takes a **direct object and no preposition** — *she accessed the file*, never ✗ *she accessed to the file*. Note also the adjective split: *accessible **to** people* (they can use it) against *accessible **by** road* (that is the route).

The right or ability to reach, enter, or use something.

1. Only three people have *access to* the payroll system.
2. The new ramp gives *access to* the second floor.
3. Rural counties still lack reliable *access to* broadband.
4. Her lawyer was denied *access to* the case file for two weeks.
5. Interns get *access to* the archive but not to the vault.
6. Nothing in the settlement guarantees *access to* editing the original recordings.

**See also:** [enter something](#enter-something), [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [permission for](#permission-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### adherence to

**Pronunciation:** /ədˈhɪrəns tu/ &middot; *ad-HEER-unss too* &middot; weak /ədˈhɪrəns tə/
**Русский:** приверженность чему-л., соблюдение чего-л. — верное следование правилу или режиму
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — policy, medicine, and compliance writing
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*adherence to logging every deviation*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *adherence to log every deviation*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *adherence that the rules be followed*). The *to* here is a preposition, so a verb after it must end in *-ing*.
**Pattern:** *adherence to* [a rule / a standard / a schedule / a regimen] &middot; *strict / rigid / close / slavish adherence to* [noun phrase] &middot; *in adherence to* [noun phrase] (formal) &middot; [someone's] *adherence to* [a party / a doctrine]
**Contrast:** ***Adherence*** **is sticking to a rule;** ***adhesion*** **is sticking to a surface.** *Adherence to the protocol* is following it; *the adhesion of the coating to the steel* is physical. In American medicine *adherence to* a drug regimen has largely displaced *compliance with* it, on the theory that patients cooperate rather than obey — [compliance with](#compliance-with) survives in the regulatory sense. The verb is *adhere **to*** (✗ *adhere with*), and the adjective is *adherent to*. ✗ *adherence of the rules* and ✗ *adherence with the policy* are both errors.

Faithful, continued observance of a rule, a standard, or a course of treatment.

1. Payment depends on strict *adherence to* the reporting schedule.
2. Only about half the patients showed good *adherence to* the six-month regimen.
3. The audit found no *adherence to* the written change-control policy at all.
4. Her *adherence to* the party line surprised people who had known her in law school.
5. Certification requires *adherence to* documenting every deviation, however small.
6. *Adherence to* the posted speed limit on that stretch is essentially voluntary.

**See also:** [compliance with](#compliance-with), [conformity with/to](#conformity-withto), [deference to](#deference-to), [comply with](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#comply-with), [in accordance with](09-comparison-opposition.md#in-accordance-with), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### advantage of/over

**Pronunciation:** /ədˈvæntɪdʒ ʌv/, /ədˈvæntɪdʒ ˈoʊvɚ/ &middot; *ad-VAN-tij uv, ad-VAN-tij OH-ver* &middot; weak /ədˈvænɪdʒ əv/ → /ədˈvænɪdʒ ə/; *over* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** преимущество: of — выгода от чего-л., over — превосходство над соперником
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase or gerund (*the advantage of arriving early*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the advantage to arrive early*). **over**: a noun phrase naming a rival or a competing option, never a gerund (✗ *an advantage over arriving early*).
**Pattern:** *the advantage of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *an advantage over* [competitor] &middot; *take advantage of* [noun phrase] &middot; *have the advantage of* [noun phrase] &middot; *to* [someone's] *advantage*
**Variants:** *the advantages to doing it this way* — an accepted informal American variant of *the advantage of*, but *of* is the edited form
**Contrast:** **The two prepositions are not interchangeable.** *The advantage **of** a hybrid* is the good thing a hybrid gives you; *an advantage **over** a hybrid* is the way something beats a hybrid. Mixing them produces nonsense: ✗ *the advantage over arriving early* has no rival to compare with. *Take advantage of* is fixed and has a second, negative sense — exploiting a person — that *take the advantage of* does not carry. ✗ *advantage on* and ✗ *advantage above* are errors.

A benefit something gives you, or a way in which one thing beats another.

1. The main *advantage of* leasing is that repairs aren't your problem.
2. Solar panels have a clear *advantage over* a diesel generator in the summer.
3. She had the *advantage of* having read the report the night before.
4. Don't let the contractor *take advantage of* your father.
5. The rule change works to the *advantage of* smaller schools.
6. Our only *advantage over* the incumbent is that we can move faster.

**See also:** [benefit of/to](#benefit-ofto), [disadvantage of](#disadvantage-of), [comparison with/to](#comparison-withto), [of](02-core-relational.md#of), [over](10-topic-reference-role.md#over)

### advice on/about

**Pronunciation:** /ədˈvaɪs ɑn/, /ədˈvaɪs əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *ad-VYSS on, ad-VYSS uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ədˈvaɪs ən/; *about* clips to /ˈbaʊt/ in fast speech
**Русский:** совет по поводу чего-л. — в русском исчисляемо, в английском нет (✗ an advice)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (*advice on choosing a plan*), or a *wh*-clause (*advice on what to do*, *advice on how to file*) — **never a that-clause** (✗ *advice that you should wait*; write *advice to wait*). *Advice to* + infinitive is possible and names the content of the advice: *his advice to sell was wrong*.
**Pattern:** *advice on / about* [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] &middot; *advice to* [person] &middot; *advice to* [verb] &middot; *give / take / follow / ignore* **advice** &middot; *a piece of advice*
**Variants:** *advice on* — the default for a domain or procedure &middot; *advice about* — slightly more conversational and more usual with a specific worry
**Contrast:** **Advice is uncountable in American English**: ✗ *an advice*, ✗ *some advices*, ✗ *many advices*. Count it with *a piece of advice*, *two pieces of advice*, or *some advice*. Do not confuse the noun *advice* /ədˈvaɪs/ with the verb *advise* /ədˈvaɪz/ — the *c* form is the noun. The verb takes a person as a direct object with no preposition (*she advised me*, ✗ *she advised to me*) and takes *on* for the topic (*she advised us on the merger*).

A recommendation about what someone should do.

1. I need *advice on* whether to refinance now or wait.
2. She gave me some good *advice about* handling difficult clients.
3. The website has *advice on* choosing a health plan.
4. His only *advice to* new teachers was to learn every name in the first week.
5. Take my *advice* and get it in writing.
6. That's the worst piece of *advice* I've heard all year.

**See also:** [information about/on](#information-abouton), [opinion on/of/about](#opinion-onofabout), [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### agreement with/on

**Pronunciation:** /əˈɡrimənt wɪθ/, /əˈɡrimənt ɑn/ &middot; *uh-GREE-munt with, uh-GREE-munt on* &middot; weak /əˈɡrimənt wəθ/, /əˈɡrimənt ən/
**Русский:** соглашение с кем-л. / о чём-л.; также согласие с кем-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **with**: a person, party, or institution. **on / about**: a topic — noun phrase, gerund (*agreement on postponing the vote*), or *wh*-clause (*no agreement on who pays*). *Agreement* also takes a bare **that-clause** with no preposition (*an agreement that he would leave*) and a **to-infinitive** (*an agreement to split the costs*).
**Pattern:** *an agreement with* [party] &middot; *an agreement on / about* [issue] &middot; *an agreement to* [verb] &middot; *an agreement that* [clause] &middot; *reach / sign / break* **an agreement** &middot; *in agreement with* [someone]
**Variants:** *agreement on* — an issue settled &middot; *agreement about* — looser and more conversational &middot; *agreement between* [two parties] — from the outside, naming both sides
**Contrast:** Three frames with three jobs: *an agreement **with** the union* names the other side, *an agreement **on** wages* names the subject, and *an agreement **between** the city and the union* names both sides from outside. The related verb splits the same way and is a classic error zone: *agree **with** someone* (share their view), *agree **to** a proposal* (accept it), *agree **on** a date* (settle it jointly), *agree **that**...* — and ✗ *agree something* is not American English, though British writers use *agree a deal*.

A settlement between parties, or the state of holding the same view.

1. The company signed a five-year *agreement with* the pilots' union.
2. We never reached *agreement on* who would pay the deductible.
3. There's broad *agreement about* the diagnosis and none at all about the treatment.
4. They came to an *agreement* that neither side would talk to reporters.
5. I'm in *agreement with* you on almost everything except the timeline.
6. The *agreement between* the two districts expires in June.

**See also:** [disagreement with/about](#disagreement-withabout), [opposition to](#opposition-to), [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin), [with](02-core-relational.md#with), [agree with, agree to, agree on](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### alternative to

**Pronunciation:** /ɔlˈtɜrnətɪv tu/ &middot; *awl-TUR-nuh-tiv too* &middot; weak /ɔlˈtɜrnətɪv tə/
**Русский:** альтернатива чему-л. — то, что можно выбрать вместо
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*an alternative to driving*) — **never a to-infinitive**. ✗ *an alternative to drive downtown* is the single commonest error with this word, because the *to* looks like an infinitive marker and is not. Test it: a noun fits the slot (*an alternative to the car*), so *to* is a preposition and the verb takes *-ing*.
**Pattern:** *an alternative to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *have no alternative but to* [verb] &middot; *the only alternative is to* [verb] &middot; *offer / propose / seek* **an alternative to** [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ***Alternative to*** names what you would be replacing; there is no ✗ *alternative for*, ✗ *alternative of*, or ✗ *alternative from*. Two other frames use a different *to*: in *no alternative **but to** wait* and *the only alternative **is to** wait*, the *to* really is an infinitive marker, which is why the bare verb is right there and wrong after *an alternative to*. Purists once insisted *alternative* could apply to exactly two options; American usage abandoned that long ago and *three alternatives* is unremarkable.

Something you can choose or use instead of something else.

1. Biking is a serious *alternative to* driving in a town this flat.
2. We're looking for an *alternative to* the current supplier.
3. Is there any *alternative to* filing an appeal?
4. Community college was the only affordable *alternative to* a four-year school.
5. She had no *alternative* but to resign.
6. The board rejected all three *alternatives to* the merger.

**See also:** [substitute for](#substitute-for), [objection to](#objection-to), [preference for](#preference-for), [solution to](#solution-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### amendment to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈmɛndmənt tu/ &middot; *uh-MEND-munt too* &middot; weak /əˈmɛndmənt tə/
**Русский:** поправка к — официальное изменение уже действующего документа
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — legislatures, contracts, bylaws, court filings
**Complement:** **either** a noun phrase naming the document amended (*an amendment to the lease*) **or a bare infinitive** naming what the amendment would do (*an amendment to strike the residency requirement*) — the two *to*'s are different words and both are standard. **Never a gerund** (✗ *an amendment to striking the requirement*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *an amendment that the fee be capped*; write *an amendment capping the fee*).
**Pattern:** *an amendment to* [a bill / a contract / the constitution] &middot; *an amendment to* [verb] &middot; *propose / offer / attach / adopt / defeat an amendment* &middot; *an amendment banning* [noun phrase] &middot; *the First / Fifth / Fourteenth Amendment* (usually cited bare)
**Contrast:** **The two *to*'s are the whole difficulty.** *An amendment **to** the lease* attaches to a document, and a noun follows; *an amendment **to** cap the fee* says what it does, and a bare verb follows — put a plain noun in the slot to tell them apart. ***Amendment of*** is the act of amending and is stiffer and rarer: *amendment **of** the bylaws requires a two-thirds vote*, but the sheet of paper is *an amendment **to** the bylaws*. ✗ *an amendment on the bill* is an error. A numbered constitutional amendment is normally cited bare (*the Fourth Amendment*), with *to the Constitution* added only in full formal citation.

A formal change proposed or made to a document already in force.

1. The committee attached an *amendment to* the highway bill at two in the morning.
2. We signed a one-page *amendment to* the lease so the dog could stay.
3. She offered an *amendment to* strike the residency requirement.
4. The *amendment to* the bylaws needed a two-thirds vote of the full membership.
5. The Twenty-Sixth *Amendment to* the Constitution lowered the voting age to eighteen.
6. Every *amendment to* the settlement has to be filed with the court within ten days.

**See also:** [appendix to](#appendix-to), [exhibit to/schedule to/annex to](#exhibit-toschedule-toannex-to), [waiver of](#waiver-of), [notice of/to](#notice-ofto), [change in/of](#change-inof), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### analogy to/with

**Pronunciation:** /əˈnælədʒi tu/, /əˈnælədʒi wɪθ/ &middot; *uh-NAL-uh-jee too, uh-NAL-uh-jee with* &middot; weak /əˈnælədʒi tə/, /əˈnælədʒi wəθ/
**Русский:** аналогия с — сходство, проводимое ради объяснения или довода
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; heaviest in law, philosophy, and science writing
**Complement:** **to / with**: a noun phrase or **gerund** (*an analogy to boiling a frog slowly*). **between**: two things joined by *and*. **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an analogy to boil a frog*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *an analogy that the market is a machine*).
**Pattern:** *an analogy to / with* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *an analogy between* X *and* Y &middot; *draw / make / press an analogy* &middot; *by analogy to / with* [noun phrase] &middot; *the analogy breaks down*
**Variants:** *analogy to* and *analogy with* are both standard American English — *to* is commoner in legal and mathematical writing, *with* in general prose. *Analogy between* is not a third alternative but a different frame: it needs two named terms.
**Contrast:** **The adjective takes only one preposition:** *analogous **to***, and ✗ *analogous with* is not the edited form even though it is heard. Against [similarity to/between](#similarity-tobetween): a similarity is a fact about two things, an analogy is an argument you build out of one — which is why an analogy can be *good*, *strained*, or *false*, and a similarity cannot. Watch the stress, which moves across the family: *analogy* /əˈnælədʒi/, *analogous* /əˈnæləɡəs/, but *analog* /ˈænəlɔɡ/ and *analogical* /ænəˈlɑdʒɪkəl/.

A likeness drawn between two things in order to explain or argue from one to the other.

1. He drew an *analogy to* the way a fever burns off an infection.
2. The *analogy with* the 1970s only goes so far, and it stops at the oil price.
3. There's a rough *analogy between* a watershed and a circuit board.
4. By *analogy to* the earlier case, the court found no duty of disclosure.
5. The *analogy to* a household budget is the one every politician reaches for, and it is wrong.
6. Her *analogy with* chess made the whole strategy suddenly obvious.

**See also:** [comparison with/to](#comparison-withto), [similarity to/between](#similarity-tobetween), [contrast to/with](#contrast-towith), [comparable to](09-comparison-opposition.md#comparable-to), [similar to](15-dependent-adjectives.md#similar-to)

### answer to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈænsɚ tu/ &middot; *AN-ser too* &middot; weak /ˈænsɚ tə/
**Русский:** ответ на (вопрос), решение (задачи) — рус. «на», не «к» и не «о»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** — usually a question, problem, or letter (*the answer to my email*), but a gerund is normal when the complement names a task rather than a question (*the answer to reducing traffic is not more lanes*, *the answer to getting kids to read*). That gerund is the proof that the *to* is a preposition, so it is **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *the answer to reduce traffic*. The noun also takes an appositive **that-clause** naming its content, with no preposition: *the answer, that nobody had checked the valve, took a week to surface*.
**Pattern:** *the answer to* [question / problem / letter] &middot; *an answer to* [someone] &middot; *in answer to* [noun phrase] &middot; *have / know / give* **the answer to** [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** **The classic noun-versus-verb shift.** The verb *answer* takes a **direct object and no preposition** — *answer the question*, *answer the phone*, *answer my letter*, never ✗ *answer to the question*. The noun requires *to*: *the answer **to** the question*, never ✗ *the answer of the question*. The one place *answer to* is a verb is the unrelated sense "report to, be accountable to": *she answers to the regional director*, *he'll answer for this*. Compare [reply to](#reply-to), where the *to* survives in both the noun and the verb — which is exactly why *answer* trips people.

A response to a question, or the solution to a problem.

1. Nobody could give me a straight *answer to* a simple question.
2. The *answer to* the housing shortage isn't going to be one policy.
3. I never got an *answer to* my email.
4. In *answer to* your question, yes, the lease renews automatically.
5. The *answer to* number twelve was on the back of the page.
6. She answers *to* the board, not to the CEO.

**See also:** [reply to](#reply-to), [response to](#response-to), [solution to](#solution-to), [answer something](#answer-something), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### apology for

**Pronunciation:** /əˈpɑlədʒi fɔr/ &middot; *uh-POL-uh-jee for* &middot; weak /əˈpɑlədʒi fɚ/
**Русский:** извинение за — выражение сожаления о содеянном
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*an apology for being late*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an apology to be late*). *Apology to* takes the person owed: *an apology to the family*. Both can appear at once: *an apology to the family for the delay*.
**Pattern:** *an apology for* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *an apology to* [person] &middot; *an apology to* [person] *for* [noun phrase] &middot; *owe / offer / demand / accept* **an apology** &middot; *make no apology for* [verb-ing]
**Contrast:** **Two prepositions, two roles:** *for* names the offense, *to* names the person wronged. Swapping them is ungrammatical, not just odd: ✗ *an apology for the family*. The verb follows the same split — *apologize **to** her **for** the delay* — and takes no direct object at all: ✗ *I apologized her*, ✗ *I apologized the delay*. Note the American spelling *apologize*, and that *apologia* is a different word meaning a formal defense, not a regret.

An expression of regret for something you did.

1. The airline sent a form *apology for* the four-hour delay.
2. You owe your sister an *apology for* what you said at dinner.
3. He offered an *apology to* the family but never admitted fault.
4. I make no *apology for* holding the vote in public.
5. Her *apology for* missing the funeral came three months too late.
6. The mayor's *apology to* the neighborhood was read aloud at the meeting.

**See also:** [excuse for](#excuse-for), [complaint about](#complaint-about), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for), [apologize to, apologize for](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#apologize-to-apologize-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### appendix to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈpɛndɪks tu/ &middot; *uh-PEN-diks too* &middot; weak /əˈpɛndɪks tə/
**Русский:** приложение к — раздел с дополнительным материалом в конце документа
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — academic writing, agency reports, and legal instruments
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the document the appendix hangs off — **never a gerund, never an infinitive** (✗ *an appendix to show the raw data*, unless *to show* is a purpose clause), and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *an appendix to* [a report / a book / an agreement] &middot; *Appendix A / Appendix 3* (cited bare, no preposition) &middot; *set out / attached / reproduced in the appendix* &middot; *see the appendix to* [document]
**Variants:** the plural is *appendices* /əˈpɛndəsiz/ for documents in scholarly and legal prose and *appendixes* for the organ; American general writing uses *appendixes* for both without anyone objecting
**Contrast:** In contract drafting the attached documents are *exhibits*, *schedules*, and *annexes*, not appendices — see [exhibit to/schedule to/annex to](#exhibit-toschedule-toannex-to). An ***addendum to*** adds material that did not exist before; an ***appendix to*** parks supporting material that did. ✗ *an appendix of the report* is wrong for the attachment sense, though *an appendix of tables* (what it contains) is fine. The organ takes no preposition at all: ✗ *an appendix to the colon*.

A section of supporting material attached at the end of a longer document.

1. The full data set is in the *appendix to* the final report.
2. She published the letters as an *appendix to* the second edition.
3. Appendix C *to* the grant application lists every subcontractor by name.
4. The methodology is buried in an *appendix to* a 1978 study nobody reads.
5. Nothing in the *appendix to* the contract changes the payment terms.
6. Reviewers rarely read past the first *appendix to* a submission this long.

**See also:** [exhibit to/schedule to/annex to](#exhibit-toschedule-toannex-to), [amendment to](#amendment-to), [citation to](#citation-to), [reference to](#reference-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### applicability to

**Pronunciation:** /əˌplɪkəˈbɪləti tu/ &middot; *uh-plik-uh-BIL-uh-tee too* &middot; weak /əˌplɪkəˈbɪləti tə/
**Русский:** применимость к — насколько правило распространяется на данный случай
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — regulatory analyses, court opinions, and standards documents; an American meets it as the heading "Applicability" at the front of a federal rule, a building code, or an insurance policy
**Complement:** a noun phrase or **gerund** (*its applicability to franchising the brand*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *applicability to cover franchises*) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *the applicability of* X *to* Y &middot; [possessive] *applicability to* [a case / a class of persons / a transaction] &middot; *question / dispute / concede / limit the applicability of* [rule] *to* [facts]
**Contrast:** **The frame has two halves and needs both.** Standard is *the applicability **of** the exemption **to** small carriers*; the *of* phrase may shrink to a possessive (*its applicability to small carriers*, *the statute's applicability to small carriers*) but it cannot simply vanish. The adjective is *applicable **to***, and ✗ *applicable for* is an error. Do not confuse it with ***application to***, which is an everyday word for a form you file: *her application **to** the program* is paperwork, *the rule's applicability **to** the program* is a question of law. ✗ *applicability for* and ✗ *applicability on* are errors.

Whether and how far a rule, standard, or principle reaches a particular case.

1. The brief spends four pages on the statute's *applicability to* out-of-state sellers.
2. Counsel conceded the rule's *applicability to* the 2019 transactions.
3. Section 1 sets out the code's *applicability to* existing buildings.
4. Nobody disputed the standard's *applicability to* leased equipment.
5. The agency limited the guidance's *applicability to* federally funded projects.
6. Its *applicability to* volunteer fire departments is still unsettled in this circuit.

**See also:** [relevance to](#relevance-to), [compliance with](#compliance-with), [exemption from](#exemption-from), [subject to](11-complex-multiword.md#subject-to), [pursuant to](11-complex-multiword.md#pursuant-to)

### appointment with

**Pronunciation:** /əˈpɔɪntmənt wɪθ/ &middot; *uh-POYNT-munt with* &middot; weak /əˈpɔɪntmənt wəθ/
**Русский:** приём у кого-л., назначенная встреча с; отдельно — назначение на должность
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **with**: a person, an office, or a business — **never a gerund** (✗ *an appointment with seeing the dentist*) and never a clause. **for**: the purpose or the day (*an appointment for a cleaning*, *an appointment for Tuesday*). **at**: the place or the clock time (*an appointment at the clinic*, *an appointment at four*). **to**: an office or a body, in the wholly different sense of the word (*her appointment to the bench*).
**Pattern:** *an appointment with* [person] &middot; *an appointment for* [purpose / day] &middot; *an appointment at* [place / time] &middot; *make / keep / miss / cancel / reschedule an appointment* &middot; [someone's] *appointment to* [a position] &middot; *by appointment only*
**Contrast:** ***With*** **names the other party,** ***for*** **the reason,** ***at*** **the place, and** ***to*** **the job.** ✗ *an appointment to the dentist* is an error in the meeting sense, though *his appointment **to** the FCC* is correct in the office sense — one spelling, two nouns. American English keeps *appointment* for professional slots and *reservation* for tables, rooms, and seats: you have an *appointment with* the dentist and a *reservation at* the restaurant, where British English would say *booking* for both.

A scheduled meeting with a professional or an official, or the act of placing someone in an office.

1. I've got an *appointment with* the orthodontist at eight-fifteen.
2. She made an *appointment with* the county assessor to contest the valuation.
3. Can you move my *appointment with* Dr. Reyes to Thursday afternoon?
4. His *appointment to* the appeals court took fourteen months and two hearings.
5. The clinic is *by appointment* only after four.
6. He missed a third *appointment with* the probation officer and a warrant issued.

**See also:** [conversation with](#conversation-with), [interview with](#interview-with), [encounter with](#encounter-with), [visit to](#visit-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### approach to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈproʊtʃ tu/ &middot; *uh-PROHCH too* &middot; weak /əˈproʊtʃ tə/
**Русский:** подход к — способ решения; также приближение к месту
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*an approach to teaching writing*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an approach to teach writing*). In the physical sense the complement is a place: *the approach to the bridge*. A *wh*-clause does not follow it (✗ *an approach to how we hire*; write *an approach to hiring*).
**Pattern:** *an approach to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *take an approach to* [noun phrase] &middot; *a* [adjective] *approach to* [noun phrase] &middot; *the approach to* [place]
**Contrast:** **The verb takes no preposition at all.** *The plane approached the runway*, *he approached me after class* — never ✗ *approached to the runway*. The noun requires *to*, and ✗ *an approach of*, ✗ *an approach on*, and ✗ *an approach for* are all wrong. Keep the *-ing* rule in view: because a noun fits the slot, the *to* is a preposition, so *an approach to solving it*, not ✗ *an approach to solve it*. The plural *approaches* is also the aviation term for the final descent path.

A way of dealing with something, or the final stretch leading to a place.

1. Her *approach to* classroom discipline is quieter than mine.
2. We need a completely different *approach to* funding rural clinics.
3. The company took a cautious *approach to* the acquisition.
4. Traffic backs up on the *approach to* the tunnel every weekday.
5. There's no single right *approach to* teaching a child to read.
6. His *approach to* negotiating is to say nothing for the first ten minutes.

**See also:** [attitude to/toward](#attitude-totoward), [solution to](#solution-to), [way of/to](#way-ofto), [approach something](#approach-something), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### argument with/about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɑrɡjəmənt wɪθ/, /ˈɑrɡjəmənt əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *AR-gyuh-munt with, AR-gyuh-munt uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ˈɑrɡjəmənt wəθ/, /ˈɑrɡjəmənt ˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** спор с кем-л. / о чём-л.; отдельно — довод, аргумент в пользу позиции
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **with**: a person. **about / over**: the subject — noun phrase, gerund (*an argument about paying the deposit*), or *wh*-clause (*an argument about who was driving*). **for / against**: a position, and here a **that-clause** is possible after the noun (*the argument that the tax is regressive*). ✗ *an argument to do something*.
**Pattern:** *an argument with* [person] &middot; *an argument about / over* [issue] &middot; *an argument for / against* [position] &middot; *an argument that* [clause] &middot; *have / start / win / lose* **an argument** &middot; *get into an argument with* [person]
**Variants:** *argument about* — neutral &middot; *argument over* — the same, with more heat and usually a scarce resource (*an argument over the last seat*)
**Contrast:** **Four prepositions, four jobs.** *With* is the opponent, *about/over* is the topic, *for/against* is the side taken — *an argument **for** raising the cap* is a reasoned case, while *an argument **about** raising the cap* is a quarrel. Because *argument* means both "quarrel" and "line of reasoning," the preposition is what tells the reader which one you mean. ✗ *an argument to the manager* and ✗ *an argument on the topic* are both wrong; use *with* and *about*. The verb keeps the same set: *argue with someone about something*, *argue for a position*, *argue that*.

A quarrel, or a reasoned case for a position.

1. I had a stupid *argument with* my brother about a parking space.
2. The *argument over* the property line has been going on since 1998.
3. Her *argument for* raising the minimum wage runs eighty pages.
4. There's a strong *argument against* rebuilding on the same site.
5. They got into an *argument about* who forgot to lock the shed.
6. The *argument that* the tax is regressive has never been answered.

**See also:** [disagreement with/about](#disagreement-withabout), [discussion about/of](#discussion-aboutof), [opposition to](#opposition-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with), [argue with, argue about, argue over](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#argue-with-argue-about-argue-over), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### association with/between

**Pronunciation:** /əˌsoʊsiˈeɪʃən wɪθ/, /əˌsoʊʃiˈeɪʃən bɪˈtwin/ &middot; *uh-soh-see-AY-shun with, uh-soh-shee-AY-shun bih-TWEEN* &middot; weak /əˌsoʊsiˈeɪʃən wəθ/, /əˌsoʊʃiˈeɪʃən bəˈtwin/ &middot; both /-siˈeɪ-/ and /-ʃiˈeɪ-/ are standard American
**Русский:** связь с / между — принадлежность к кругу либо статистическая связь величин
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; the *between* frame is technical
**Complement:** **with**: a person, group, or thing, or a **gerund** (*his association with running the fund*). **between**: two items joined by *and*. **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an association to reduce risk*) and **never a that-clause**.
**Pattern:** *an association with* [person / organization] &middot; *an association between* X *and* Y &middot; *in association with* [partner] &middot; *guilt by association* &middot; *break off / sever / deny an association with* [noun phrase] &middot; *a trade / homeowners' / alumni association* (no preposition)
**Contrast:** ***With*** **for affiliation,** ***between*** **for statistics.** *His association **with** the firm* is who he worked for; *an association **between** lead exposure and reading scores* is a pattern in a data set — and a researcher will insist that an *association between* claims far less than [a cause of](#cause-of). ✗ *an association to* is an error in both senses. Against [correlation with/between](#correlation-withbetween): a correlation is a measured coefficient, an association need only be a tendency, which is why epidemiologists reach for the looser word first.

A tie between a person and a group, or an observed pattern linking two variables.

1. He cut every *association with* the group the week after the indictment.
2. The study reports an *association between* night shifts and elevated blood pressure.
3. The exhibit was mounted in *association with* the state historical society.
4. Her only *association with* the case was one memo written in 2011.
5. Researchers found no *association between* the vaccine and the reported symptoms.
6. Guilt by *association* cost him the nomination before a single vote was counted.

**See also:** [connection with/to/between](#connection-withtobetween), [correlation with/between](#correlation-withbetween), [link between/to](#link-betweento), [relationship with/between](#relationship-withbetween), [in association with](11-complex-multiword.md#in-association-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### attack on

**Pronunciation:** /əˈtæk ɑn/ &middot; *uh-TAK on* &middot; weak /əˈtæk ən/
**Русский:** нападение на, атака на — также резкая публичная критика кого-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun — a target, whether physical (*an attack on the depot*) or reputational (*an attack on her record*). It does **not** take a gerund or a clause: ✗ *an attack on raising taxes*; write *an attack on the tax increase*. *Attack of* takes an illness or a fit: *an attack of nerves*.
**Pattern:** *an attack on* [target] &middot; *an attack against* [target] &middot; *an attack by* [attacker] &middot; *an attack of* [illness / emotion] &middot; *come / be under attack* &middot; *launch an attack on* [target]
**Variants:** *an attack upon* — formal and chiefly written &middot; *an attack against* — normal with abstract or institutional targets (*an attack against press freedom*), less usual with physical ones
**Contrast:** **Three different *of / on / by* jobs:** *on* names the target, *by* names the attacker, *of* names an ailment or a fit — *an attack **of** the flu*, *an attack **of** conscience*, *a heart attack*. The verb takes a **direct object and no preposition**: *they attacked the depot*, never ✗ *they attacked on the depot* or ✗ *attacked against the depot*. Compare [threat to](#threat-to), where the preposition names who is endangered rather than what is struck.

An act of violence, or a hostile public criticism.

1. The *attack on* the substation knocked out power to nine counties.
2. His column was a sustained *attack on* the mayor's housing record.
3. The clinic has been under *attack* from state legislators all spring.
4. She had an *attack of* vertigo halfway up the stairs.
5. Two of the *attacks on* the pipeline were never claimed.
6. The senator called the ruling an *attack on* the First Amendment.

**See also:** [threat to](#threat-to), [complaint about](#complaint-about), [opposition to](#opposition-to), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### attempt at

**Pronunciation:** /əˈtɛmpt æt/ &middot; *uh-TEMPT at* &middot; weak /əˈtɛmpt ət/
**Русский:** попытка чего-л. — рус. родительный, английское at предлогом не переводится
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*an attempt at reconciliation*, *an attempt at fixing the leak*) — **never a bare infinitive after *at*** (✗ *an attempt at fix the leak*). The same noun takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition at all**: *an attempt to fix the leak*. Both are correct; see the contrast.
**Pattern:** *an attempt at* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *an attempt to* [verb] &middot; *make an attempt at / to* &middot; *an attempt on* [someone's life / a record] &middot; *at the first attempt*
**Variants:** *an attempt at doing it* and *an attempt to do it* — both standard; *at* + *-ing* stresses the trying, *to* + infinitive stresses the goal
**Contrast:** ***At*** **and** ***to*** **divide the labor.** *An attempt **at** humor* is a try at producing a thing; *an attempt **to** be funny* is a try at performing an act, so the gerund frame is normal with result nouns (*an attempt at a soufflé*) and the infinitive frame with action verbs (*an attempt to reach him*). A third preposition changes the meaning entirely: *an attempt **on** his life* is an attempted murder, and *an attempt **on** the record* is a try at breaking it. ✗ *an attempt for* and ✗ *an attempt of* are errors. The verb *attempt* takes a direct object or an infinitive and no preposition: *she attempted the climb*, *she attempted to climb it*, never ✗ *attempted at the climb*.

A try at doing or producing something.

1. His *attempt at* an apology made everything worse.
2. This is our third *attempt at* getting the permit approved.
3. She made no *attempt to* hide her irritation.
4. The bread was a first *attempt at* sourdough and it showed.
5. There was an *attempt on* the governor's life in 1974.
6. He passed the exam at the second *attempt*.

**See also:** [willingness to](#willingness-to), [chance of](#chance-of), [success in/at](#success-inat), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### attention to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈtɛnʃən tu/ &middot; *uh-TEN-shun too* &middot; weak /əˈtɛnʃən tə/
**Русский:** внимание к — сосредоточенность и забота, направленные на что-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*attention to getting the details right*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *attention to get the details right*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *attention that the details were wrong*). A *wh*-clause is fine, chiefly in the *pay attention to* frame: *pay attention to what he says*, *little attention to how the work was funded*.
**Pattern:** *attention to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *pay attention to* [noun phrase / wh-clause] &middot; *draw / call / bring attention to* [noun phrase] &middot; *attention to detail* &middot; *the attention of* [person]
**Contrast:** **The preposition never changes, but the near-synonyms do.** *Attention **to*** is the only frame — ✗ *attention on*, ✗ *attention about*, ✗ *attention for* — yet the closely related *focus* takes *on* (*focus **on** the details*), which is where the mixing starts. The verb *attend* splits by sense: *attend the meeting* takes no preposition and means "be present at," while *attend **to** the patient* means "take care of." A speaker saying ✗ *I attended to the conference* has produced a different sentence from the one they meant.

Notice, care, or concentration directed at something.

1. The renovation shows real *attention to* detail.
2. Pay *attention to* the fine print on page nine.
3. The report drew *attention to* three unreported spills.
4. She gives more *attention to* her houseplants than to her email.
5. Very little *attention* has been paid *to* rural transit funding.
6. May I have your *attention* for one minute.

**See also:** [focus on](#focus-on), [emphasis on/stress on](#emphasis-onstress-on), [interest in](#interest-in), [respect for](#respect-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### attitude to/toward

**Pronunciation:** /ˈætɪtud tu/, /ˈætɪtud təˈwɔrd/ &middot; *AT-ih-tood too, AT-ih-tood tuh-WORD* &middot; weak /ˈætɪtud tə/, /ˈætɪtud tɔrd/
**Русский:** отношение к — устойчивый взгляд или чувство по поводу чего-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*her attitude toward working weekends*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *his attitude to work weekends*). No clause: ✗ *an attitude that we should wait* means something different (*attitude* + that-clause exists only in the rare "stance" sense).
**Pattern:** *an attitude to / toward* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *an attitude about* [noun phrase] &middot; *someone's attitude* &middot; *have / take an attitude toward* [noun phrase] &middot; *a change in attitude*
**Variants:** ***toward*** — the American default, with no *-s* &middot; ***towards*** — British &middot; ***to*** — standard everywhere but distinctly more British in feel &middot; ***about*** — colloquial American, common in speech (*his attitude about money*)
**Contrast:** ***Toward*** **is the American form**, and the *-s* on *towards* buys no formality — it just reads as British. All three of *toward*, *to*, and *about* are grammatical; ✗ *attitude against*, ✗ *attitude on*, and ✗ *attitude for* are not. Note that bare *attitude* with no complement has become an American noun in its own right meaning insolence (*don't give me that attitude*, *she's got attitude*), which is why *he has an attitude* and *he has an attitude toward authority* say almost opposite things about the speaker's judgment.

A settled way of thinking or feeling about something.

1. Her *attitude toward* deadlines has improved since the reorganization.
2. Public *attitudes to* nuclear power shifted sharply after 2011.
3. I don't like his *attitude about* being asked to show ID.
4. The whole department has a casual *attitude toward* recordkeeping.
5. There's been a real change in *attitude* among younger farmers.
6. You'll get further with a better *attitude toward* being coached.

**See also:** [approach to](#approach-to), [opinion on/of/about](#opinion-onofabout), [view on/of](#view-onof), [reaction to](#reaction-to), [toward](04-movement-direction.md#toward)

### authority on/over

**Pronunciation:** /əˈθɔrəti ɑn/, /əˈθɔrəti ˈoʊvɚ/ &middot; *uh-THOR-uh-tee on, uh-THOR-uh-tee OH-ver* &middot; weak /əˈθɔrəti ən/; *over* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** on — авторитет, специалист по чему-л.; over — власть над кем-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **on / in**: a field of knowledge — noun phrase only (✗ *an authority on how bridges fail*; write *an authority on bridge failures*). **over**: a person, territory, or budget. The same noun takes a **to-infinitive** with no preposition: *the authority to sign checks* — and there the *to* is an infinitive marker, so ✗ *the authority to signing checks*.
**Pattern:** *an authority on / in* [field] &middot; *authority over* [person / area / budget] &middot; *the authority to* [verb] &middot; *on the authority of* [source] &middot; *have / exercise / exceed one's authority*
**Variants:** *an authority on* — the usual for a subject &middot; *an authority in* — a field or discipline (*an authority in pediatric oncology*), slightly more institutional
**Contrast:** **Two nouns wearing one spelling.** *An authority **on** medieval coins* is an expert — countable, and you can be one of several. *Authority **over** the budget* is power — uncountable, and there is only so much of it. The prepositions keep them apart, so ✗ *an authority over medieval coins* claims a strange dominion and ✗ *authority on the budget* claims expertise where you meant control. A third frame, *the authority **to** approve it*, is a granted right, and it takes the infinitive, not a preposition.

An expert on a subject, or power exercised over people or resources.

1. She's the leading *authority on* nineteenth-century Shaker furniture.
2. The commission has no *authority over* privately owned wells.
3. He exceeded his *authority* when he signed the settlement.
4. The department has *authority to* issue permits but not to revoke them.
5. I have that on the *authority of* the county clerk herself.
6. Two *authorities in* the field reviewed the manuscript.

**See also:** [expert on/in](#expert-onin), [control over/of](#control-overof), [influence on/over](#influence-onover), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [over](10-topic-reference-role.md#over)

### awareness of

**Pronunciation:** /əˈwɛrnəs ʌv/ &middot; *uh-WAIR-nus uv* &middot; weak /əˈwɛrnəs əv/ → /əˈwɛrnəs ə/
**Русский:** осведомлённость о чём-л., осознание чего-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (*awareness of being watched*), or a *wh*-clause (*an awareness of what the job actually pays*) — **never a to-infinitive**. Unusually for a noun in this file, it also takes a bare **that-clause** with no preposition: *an awareness that the money would run out*.
**Pattern:** *awareness of* [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] &middot; *awareness that* [clause] &middot; *raise / increase awareness of* [noun phrase] &middot; *a growing awareness of* [noun phrase] &middot; *awareness among* [group]
**Variants:** *awareness about* — increasingly common in public-health and campaign writing (*awareness about the symptoms*), but *of* remains the edited form
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is the preposition; *about* is a marketing habit.** Both are heard, but *of* is what edited American prose uses, and ✗ *awareness on*, ✗ *awareness to*, and ✗ *awareness for* are simply wrong. Watch the two frames after the noun: *awareness **of** the risk* takes a noun, *awareness **that** there was a risk* takes a clause with no preposition at all. Dropping the *of* before a clause (✗ *awareness of that there was a risk*) is the error that follows from mixing them. The adjective *aware* takes the same two: *aware of the risk*, *aware that there was a risk*.

Knowledge or perception that something exists or is the case.

1. There's a growing *awareness of* how much water the plant actually uses.
2. The campaign raised *awareness of* early stroke symptoms.
3. She had a vague *awareness of* being followed down the block.
4. Public *awareness* of the recall was almost nonexistent.
5. An *awareness that* the fund would be exhausted by June changed the vote.
6. Safety *awareness among* the night crew has improved since the training.

**See also:** [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), [understanding of](#understanding-of), [insight into](#insight-into), [belief in](#belief-in), [aware of](15-dependent-adjectives.md#aware-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)


## B

### basis for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbeɪsɪs fɔr/ &middot; *BAY-siss for* &middot; weak /ˈbeɪsɪs fɚ/
**Русский:** основание для — то, на чём что-л. держится или может быть построено
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*a basis for believing him*) — the edited frame. **A that-clause is fine after the noun** (*no basis for the claim that the fund was solvent*). A **to-infinitive** after *basis* is common in legal and journalistic prose (*no basis to conclude that*) but is still the marked choice; *for* + *-ing* is the form no editor will touch.
**Pattern:** *a basis for* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *the basis of* [something] &middot; *on the basis of* [evidence] &middot; *have / provide / lay / lack a basis for* [noun phrase] &middot; *on a first-come basis*, *on a monthly basis* (adverbial, no complement)
**Variants:** *the basis of* and *the basis for* split by direction: *the basis **of** his argument* is what the argument rests on, *a basis **for** an argument* is material you could build one out of. Both are standard; they are not interchangeable in every sentence.
**Contrast:** **The plural is *bases* /ˈbeɪsiz/**, spelled like the plural of *base* /ˈbeɪsɪz/ and pronounced differently — a written trap in *on these two bases*. ✗ *a basis to the claim* is an error. Note the frozen adverbial *on a ... basis*, which takes no preposition of its own and which editors compress on sight: *on a weekly basis* is usually just *weekly*.

The evidence, principle, or foundation that something rests on or could be built on.

1. There's no *basis for* the claim that the well was tested in June.
2. The affidavit gave the judge a *basis for* issuing the warrant.
3. His whole case rests on the *basis of* one unsigned memo.
4. Attendance is not a *basis for* dismissal under this contract.
5. She had no *basis for* believing the transfer had cleared.
6. The board meets on a quarterly *basis*, which is to say four times a year.

**See also:** [grounds for](#grounds-for), [evidence of/for](#evidence-offor), [justification for](#justification-for), [rationale for](#rationale-for), [on the basis of](11-complex-multiword.md#on-the-basis-of), [based on](15-dependent-adjectives.md#based-on)

### bearing on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbɛrɪŋ ɑn/ &middot; *BAIR-ing on* &middot; weak /ˈbɛrɪŋ ən/
**Русский:** отношение к делу — have a bearing on = иметь значение для чего-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; common in courtrooms, editorials, and committee reports
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*no bearing on choosing a successor*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *no bearing to choose a successor*) and **never a that-clause**. The noun almost always appears inside *have a bearing on* or *have no bearing on*; a bare *the bearing on* is not idiomatic.
**Pattern:** *have a bearing on* [noun phrase] &middot; *have no bearing on* [noun phrase] &middot; *a direct / material / significant bearing on* [noun phrase] &middot; *bear on* [noun phrase] (the verb) &middot; *get / find one's bearings* (a different noun)
**Contrast:** **Two unrelated nouns share the spelling.** *A bearing **on*** something is relevance; *one's bearings* are orientation (*get your bearings*), and a *bearing* in a machine is a part — only the first takes *on*. ✗ *a bearing to the case* and ✗ *a bearing with the case* are errors. Against [relevance to](#relevance-to): the two say the same thing with different prepositions, and swapping the prepositions is the standard slip — ✗ *no relevance on the case*, ✗ *no bearing to the case*. The verb keeps *on*: *the memo bears **on** the timeline*.

Relevance — the quality of having something to do with the matter at hand.

1. His military record has no *bearing on* the zoning question.
2. What the neighbors saw has a direct *bearing on* the timeline.
3. The rate cut will have some *bearing on* next quarter's numbers.
4. Whether she liked him has no *bearing on* whether he was negligent.
5. The witness's memory has a real *bearing on* deciding which account to credit.
6. Anything with a *bearing on* the merger goes to outside counsel first.

**See also:** [relevance to](#relevance-to), [effect on](#effect-on), [impact on](#impact-on), [influence on/over](#influence-onover), [relevant to](15-dependent-adjectives.md#relevant-to), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### belief in

**Pronunciation:** /bɪˈlif ɪn/ &middot; *bih-LEEF in* &middot; weak /bəˈlif ən/
**Русский:** вера в — убеждённость, что нечто существует, работает или стоит того
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*a belief in taking the long view*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a belief in take the long view*, ✗ *a belief to take the long view*). Like *awareness*, it also takes a bare **that-clause** with no preposition: *a belief that the market will correct*.
**Pattern:** *a belief in* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a belief that* [clause] &middot; *in the belief that* [clause] &middot; *beliefs about* [topic] &middot; *contrary to popular belief*
**Variants:** *beliefs about* — the plural, for opinions on a topic (*beliefs about diet*), where singular *belief in* is about faith or confidence
**Contrast:** ***In*** **for faith,** ***that*** **for content, and no preposition before the clause.** *A belief **in** vaccines* is confidence in them; *a belief **that** vaccines work* is a specific proposition held — and ✗ *a belief in that vaccines work* mashes the two together. ✗ *belief on* and ✗ *belief to* are errors. The verb splits the same way and produces the file's most useful minimal pair: *I believe **you*** (I accept what you just said) against *I believe **in** you* (I have confidence in your ability), and ✗ *I believe in that you can do it* is wrong for the same reason.

Confidence that something exists, works, or is worth holding to.

1. Her *belief in* the program survived two rounds of budget cuts.
2. He lost his *belief in* the possibility of a negotiated settlement.
3. A *belief in* rewarding effort rather than results runs through the whole handbook.
4. The *belief that* housing prices only go up died in 2008.
5. They pressed on in the *belief that* the weather would clear.
6. Contrary to popular *belief*, the bridge was never named after him.

**See also:** [confidence in](#confidence-in), [faith in](#faith-in), [dependence on/reliance on](#dependence-onreliance-on), [doubt about](#doubt-about), [believe in](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#believe-in), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### beneficiary of/under

**Pronunciation:** /ˌbɛnəˈfɪʃieri ʌv/, /ˌbɛnəˈfɪʃieri ˈʌndɚ/ &middot; *ben-uh-FISH-ee-air-ee uv, ben-uh-FISH-ee-air-ee UN-der* &middot; weak /ˌbɛnəˈfɪʃieri əv/ → /ˌbɛnəˈfɪʃieri ə/; *under* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** юр. выгодоприобретатель, бенефициар — по завещанию, полису, договору
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** *of* is neutral; ***under*** **is legal drafting** — a will, a trust instrument, a life-insurance form, an ERISA plan document. An American meets *beneficiary under* on the day a parent's estate is probated, or on the form the HR office hands out in the first week of a job.
**Complement:** **of**: a noun phrase naming the instrument, the person, or the windfall (*the beneficiary of the trust*, *the beneficiary of a clerical error*). **under**: a noun phrase naming the legal instrument only — a will, a policy, a plan, a section (*a beneficiary under the policy*). **Never a gerund after *under***, and never an infinitive or a clause after either.
**Pattern:** *the beneficiary of* [an instrument / an act / a mistake] &middot; *a beneficiary under* [a will / a policy / a plan] &middot; *name / designate* [someone] *as beneficiary* &middot; *the primary / contingent beneficiary* &middot; *a third-party beneficiary of* [a contract]
**Variants:** *beneficiary under* the will and *beneficiary of* the will are both correct in a probate file; *under* is the drafter's form because it matches the other *under* phrases in the instrument (*rights under*, *obligations under*, *[hereunder](13-formal-latin-archaic.md#hereunder)*)
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **works everywhere;** ***under*** **only where there is a document.** You may be *the beneficiary **of** a lucky mistake*, but ✗ *a beneficiary **under** a lucky mistake* is nonsense — *under* needs an instrument to sit beneath. Do not confuse the noun with [benefit of/to](#benefit-ofto): *the benefit **of** the doubt* is an advantage, *the beneficiary **of** the estate* is a person. In American insurance the fixed phrases are *primary beneficiary* and *contingent beneficiary*, both without a preposition.

The person or entity legally entitled to receive something, or anyone who gains from an event.

1. She is the sole *beneficiary of* her aunt's estate.
2. Every *beneficiary under* the policy must be notified within thirty days.
3. The contractor was the unexpected *beneficiary of* a drafting error in section 9.
4. He named his daughter as the primary *beneficiary* and his brother as contingent.
5. Farmers in three counties were the real *beneficiaries of* the rate freeze.
6. A *beneficiary under* the trust cannot also serve as its sole trustee here.

**See also:** [benefit of/to](#benefit-ofto), [entitlement to](#entitlement-to), [party to](#party-to), [signatory to](#signatory-to), [title to](#title-to), [hereunder](13-formal-latin-archaic.md#hereunder)

### benefit of/to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbɛnəfɪt ʌv/, /ˈbɛnəfɪt tu/ &middot; *BEN-uh-fit uv, BEN-uh-fit too* &middot; weak /ˈbɛnəfɪt əv/ → /ˈbɛnəfɪt ə/; /ˈbɛnəfɪt tə/
**Русский:** of — польза от чего-л.; to — выгода для кого-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase or gerund (*the benefit of starting early*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the benefit of to start early*). **to**: a person or group who gains. A **to-infinitive** does attach directly to the noun in the informal *the benefits to doing it this way*, which is standard American speech but avoided in edited prose.
**Pattern:** *the benefit of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a benefit to* [person / group] &middot; *of benefit to* [person] &middot; *for the benefit of* [person] &middot; *give* [someone] *the benefit of the doubt* &middot; *a benefit for* [cause]
**Variants:** *the benefits to doing X* — accepted informal American variant of *the benefits of doing X*
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions, three meanings.** *The benefit **of** insulation* is the advantage insulation gives; *a benefit **to** homeowners* is who receives it; and *a benefit **for** the animal shelter* is a fundraising event, an entirely separate noun. The verb takes yet another preposition: *benefit **from*** — *homeowners benefit from the rebate*, never ✗ *benefit of the rebate*, and this verb-to-noun shift (*from* → *of*) is one of the commonest in the file.

An advantage gained, or the person or group that gains it.

1. The main *benefit of* the new schedule is that nobody drives home in the dark.
2. The tax credit is of real *benefit to* first-time buyers.
3. She had the *benefit of* eight years' experience in the same building.
4. I'll give him the *benefit of* the doubt one more time.
5. The concert was a *benefit for* the county food bank.
6. There's no obvious *benefit to* switching carriers midyear.

**See also:** [advantage of/over](#advantage-ofover), [disadvantage of](#disadvantage-of), [importance of/to](#importance-ofto), [price of/for](#price-offor), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### breach of

**Pronunciation:** /britʃ ʌv/ &middot; *breech uv* &middot; weak /britʃ əv/ → /britʃ ə/
**Русский:** нарушение чего-л. — договора, обязанности, стандарта; юр.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; the core term of American contract and fiduciary law, and an everyday word for a security failure
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the duty, agreement, or standard broken — **never a gerund** (✗ *a breach of failing to disclose*; write *a breach of the duty to disclose*), **never an infinitive**, and **never a that-clause**.
**Pattern:** *a breach of* [contract / duty / trust / the peace / etiquette] &middot; *in breach of* [an agreement] &middot; *a material / minor / anticipatory breach* &middot; *sue for breach of contract* &middot; *a data breach* (compound, no preposition)
**Contrast:** ***Breach*** **and** ***breech*** **are homophones and neither one is the other.** A *breach* is a break; a *breech* is the back end of a gun or a baby delivered feet first, and *breeches* are trousers. ✗ *a breech of contract* is the commonest spelling error in small-claims filings. Note that *breach of contract* takes no article inside the fixed phrase — *sued for breach of contract*, not ✗ *for a breach of the contract*, unless a particular breach is meant. The verb takes a bare object: *they breached the agreement*, ✗ *they breached of the agreement*. In American practice a *material* breach excuses the other side's performance; a minor one only supports damages.

The violation of a duty, an agreement, a standard, or a barrier.

1. Missing two deliveries is a *breach of* the supply agreement.
2. The suit alleges a *breach of* fiduciary duty by three former officers.
3. Reading her mail was a plain *breach of* trust.
4. The company disclosed the data *breach* eleven months after it happened.
5. Showing up in shorts was a *breach of* etiquette, not of the dress code.
6. He was arrested for *breach of* the peace outside the arena.

**See also:** [compliance with](#compliance-with), [default on/under](#default-onunder), [damages for](#damages-for), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [obligation to/under](#obligation-tounder), [remedy for/against](#remedy-foragainst), [in violation of](11-complex-multiword.md#in-violation-of)


## C

### case for/against

**Pronunciation:** /keɪs fɔr/, /keɪs əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *kayss for, kayss uh-GENST* &middot; weak /keɪs fɚ/, /keɪs əˈɡɛnst/
**Русский:** доводы за / против; отдельно — судебное дело и отдельный случай
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*the case for raising rates*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the case to raise rates*). A **that-clause** attaches to the verb frame instead: *she made the case **that** rates should rise*. The legal noun takes different prepositions: *a case against* a defendant, *a case for* the plaintiff, *the case of* Ruiz v. Kemper.
**Pattern:** *the case for / against* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *make / build / press / state the case for* [noun phrase] &middot; *a case against* [a person] (legal) &middot; *in case of* [emergency] &middot; *a case of* [flu / wine / mistaken identity]
**Contrast:** ***For*** **and** ***against*** **are the argument;** ***of*** **is the instance.** *The case **for** a bond issue* is the argument in favor; *a case **of** the flu* is one occurrence; *a case **against** Ruiz* is a prosecution. ✗ *the case of raising rates* means nothing. Note that *in case of* (a fixed compound preposition — see [in case of](11-complex-multiword.md#in-case-of)) and *in the case of* (= "as for") are different phrases doing different jobs. ✗ *a case to raise rates* is the infinitive error, and it is common in memos.

The argument in favor of or opposed to a course of action — and, separately, a legal action or a single instance.

1. Nobody has made a serious *case for* keeping the old boiler another winter.
2. The *case against* expanding the runway is mostly noise and mostly persuasive.
3. She built the *case for* consolidating the two departments over six months.
4. The state's *case against* him rested on a single unreliable witness.
5. There's a strong *case for* waiting until the audit closes.
6. It was a *case of* mistaken identity, and it cost him two nights in county.

**See also:** [argument with/about](#argument-withabout), [grounds for](#grounds-for), [justification for](#justification-for), [rationale for](#rationale-for), [in case of](11-complex-multiword.md#in-case-of), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### cause of

**Pronunciation:** /kɔz ʌv/ &middot; *kawz uv* &middot; weak /kɔz əv/ → /kɔz ə/
**Русский:** причина чего-л. — рус. родительный падеж, не «для»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or gerund (*the cause of the flooding*, *the cause of so many people leaving*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the cause of to flood*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *the cause that the pipe burst*; write *the cause of the burst pipe*).
**Pattern:** *the cause of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a cause for* [concern / alarm / celebration] &middot; *the cause of* [death / the fire / the delay] &middot; *a lost cause* &middot; *the leading cause of* [outcome]
**Contrast:** ***Cause of*** **against** ***cause for***, and the difference is total. *The cause **of*** names what produced a result — *the cause of the fire was faulty wiring*. *Cause **for*** appears only in a short fixed list of abstract nouns — *cause for concern*, *cause for alarm*, *cause for celebration*, *cause for complaint* — and means "grounds, justification." ✗ *the cause for the fire* is an error; *a cause of concern* is not wrong but says something different (what caused the concern, not what justifies it). Note also the unrelated third noun in *a good cause*, *a lost cause*, meaning a movement or aim.

The thing that makes something happen.

1. Investigators never determined the *cause of* the fire.
2. Heart disease is still the leading *cause of* death in the county.
3. The *cause of* the outage turned out to be a squirrel.
4. The lab results are no *cause for* alarm.
5. Falling enrollment is the *cause of* the budget gap, not the result of it.
6. There's *cause for* cautious optimism in the third-quarter numbers.

**See also:** [reason for](#reason-for), [result of](#result-of), [consequence of](#consequence-of), [effect on](#effect-on), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### chance of

**Pronunciation:** /tʃæns ʌv/ &middot; *chanss uv* &middot; weak /tʃæns əv/ → /tʃæns ə/
**Русский:** вероятность чего-л. (of + -ing); ср. chance to — возможность что-л. сделать
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase or **gerund** (*a chance of rain*, *a chance of winning*) — **never a to-infinitive after *of*** (✗ *a chance of to win*, ✗ *a chance of win*). The same noun takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition**: *a chance to speak*. Both are correct and they do not mean the same thing.
**Pattern:** *a chance of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a chance to* [verb] &middot; *the chances are that* [clause] &middot; *stand / have a chance of* [verb-ing] &middot; *by chance* &middot; *give* [someone] *a chance to* [verb]
**Contrast:** ***Chance of*** **is probability;** ***chance to*** **is opportunity.** *There's a chance **of** speaking to her* means it might happen; *there's a chance **to** speak to her* means the opening exists and you could take it. Weather forecasts and odds always use *of* (*a forty percent chance of rain*, ✗ *a chance to rain*), and offers always use *to* (*give me a chance to explain*, ✗ *a chance of explaining* in that sense). ✗ *chance for doing* is an error, though *a chance for the kids* (an opportunity for a person) is fine. Compare [possibility of](#possibility-of), which has only the *of* frame; *opportunity* behaves like *chance* but prefers the infinitive (*an opportunity to speak*) and takes *for* rather than *of* with a noun (*an opportunity for growth*).

The likelihood that something will happen, or an opportunity to do something.

1. There's a sixty percent *chance of* thunderstorms after four.
2. She has a real *chance of* making the Olympic team.
3. Give me a *chance to* explain before you decide.
4. What are the *chances of* the ferry running on a day like this?
5. We met entirely by *chance* at a gas station in Nebraska.
6. He never got the *chance to* say goodbye.

**See also:** [possibility of](#possibility-of), [risk of](#risk-of), [danger of/to](#danger-ofto), [hope for/of](#hope-forof), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### change in/of

**Pronunciation:** /tʃeɪndʒ ɪn/, /tʃeɪndʒ ʌv/ &middot; *chaynj in, chaynj uv* &middot; weak /tʃeɪndʒ ən/, /tʃeɪndʒ əv/ → /tʃeɪndʒ ə/
**Русский:** in — изменение в чём-л.; of — смена, замена одного другим
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **in**: a noun naming what is being altered (*a change in the weather*, *a change in how we bill*). **of**: a noun naming what is being swapped out (*a change of address*, *a change of clothes*). **to**: the modification made to a document or plan (*a change to the schedule*). Gerunds follow *in* freely (*a change in staffing*); **no to-infinitive** and **no that-clause**.
**Pattern:** *a change in* [thing that alters] &middot; *a change of* [thing replaced] &middot; *a change to* [document / plan] &middot; *a change from* X *to* Y &middot; *make a change to* [noun phrase] &middot; *for a change*
