**Variants:** *a change of* — swap &middot; *a change in* — internal alteration &middot; *a change to* — an edit &middot; *changes for* — never; use *changes to*
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions and three different events.** *A change **in** the schedule* means the schedule itself is now different; *a change **of** schedule* means one schedule has replaced another; *a change **to** the schedule* is a specific edit made to it. Errors cluster around *of*: ✗ *a change of the weather* (the weather alters, it is not swapped) and ✗ *a change in address* (an address is swapped, not internally altered). *Change from X to Y* names both endpoints. The verb takes a direct object with no preposition — *change the tire*, ✗ *change to the tire* — except in *change into* (transform) and *change from X to Y*.

An alteration, a replacement, or an edit.

1. There's been a *change in* the weather since Thursday.
2. You'd better pack a *change of* clothes.
3. I've made two small *changes to* the contract — see the redline.
4. The most striking *change in* the neighborhood is the number of children.
5. We're looking at a *change from* fee-for-service *to* a flat rate.
6. Let's eat outside for a *change*.

**See also:** [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin), [improvement in/on](#improvement-inon), [increase in/decrease in](#increase-indecrease-in), [effect on](#effect-on), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### citation to

**Pronunciation:** /saɪˈteɪʃən tu/ &middot; *sy-TAY-shun too* &middot; weak /saɪˈteɪʃən tə/
**Русский:** ссылка на источник; отдельно — уведомление о нарушении (штраф) или благодарность
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** *to* is legal and academic — briefs, law-review footnotes, style manuals; *for* is everyday American in two unrelated senses, a traffic ticket and a military commendation
**Complement:** **to**: a noun phrase naming a source — a case, a statute, a page, an authority. **for**: a noun phrase or **gerund** naming the offense or the deed (*a citation for speeding*, *a citation for running the light*, *a citation for valor*). **of**: what is quoted (*the citation of three district-court opinions*). **Never an infinitive or a clause** after any of them.
**Pattern:** *a citation to* [a case / a statute / page 14] &middot; *a citation for* [an offense / an act of bravery] &middot; *the citation of* [authorities] &middot; *issue / write / fight a citation* &middot; *a pin citation*
**Contrast:** ***To*** **points at a source,** ***for*** **at a reason.** *A citation **to** Miranda* sends the reader to a case; *a citation **for** speeding* is a ticket a Michigan trooper hands you; *a citation **for** valor* is a medal. ✗ *a citation of Miranda* is not wrong but means the act of citing rather than the reference itself. The verb splits the same way: *cite **to** the record* is legal usage, *cite the record* is general usage, and *cite someone **for** a violation* is the ticket. Do not confuse it with *sight* or *site*.

A pointer to a source in a text, or an official notice of an offense or a commendation.

1. The brief has forty pages of argument and one *citation to* the record.
2. He got a *citation for* an expired tag on the way to the hearing.
3. Every *citation to* an unpublished opinion has to be flagged.
4. Her *citation for* valor is framed in the hallway outside the chief's office.
5. The footnote gives a *citation to* the 1976 committee report.
6. Two paragraphs in, the memo drops a *citation to* a statute that was repealed.

**See also:** [reference to](#reference-to), [appendix to](#appendix-to), [penalty for](#penalty-for), [refer to](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#refer-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### claim against/for/to

**Pronunciation:** /kleɪm əˈɡɛnst/, /kleɪm fɔr/, /kleɪm tu/ &middot; *klaym uh-GENST, klaym for, klaym too* &middot; weak /kleɪm fɚ/, /kleɪm tə/; *against* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** юр. претензия к кому-л., требование о чём-л., притязание на что-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; the *against* and *to* frames are legal and insurance usage, met on a proof-of-claim form in probate or a homeowner's policy
**Complement:** **against**: the party or fund you are proceeding against. **for**: what you want — damages, benefits, a refund. **to**: the thing you assert a right in — a title, a throne, an inheritance. **on**: a demand on a resource (*a claim on her time*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause** (*the claim that the meter was broken*) but **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a claim to be paid*, in the entitlement sense — that reading is only the verb, *he claims to be paid*).
**Pattern:** *a claim against* [a person / an estate / an insurer] &middot; *a claim for* [damages / benefits] &middot; *a claim to* [property / a title] &middot; *a claim on* [resources / attention] &middot; *file / press / settle / deny a claim* &middot; *lay claim to* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** **Four prepositions and four different sentences.** *A claim **against** the estate* is who owes; *a claim **for** $40,000* is what is owed; *a claim **to** the mineral rights* is what you say is yours; *a claim **on** my afternoon* is a demand for time. Swap them and the meaning breaks: ✗ *a claim to the estate for the mineral rights* is word salad. Note the *to* trap: *a claim **to** the land* takes a noun, and *a claim to own the land* is not the entitlement noun at all. The verb *claim* takes a bare object (*she claimed the deduction*) and a that-clause (*he claims that he paid*), never ✗ *claim for the deduction* in American English.

An assertion of a right, a demand for payment, or a right asserted in something.

1. Three contractors filed a *claim against* the estate before the deadline.
2. Her *claim for* lost wages was denied twice before it was paid.
3. The family still has a *claim to* forty acres along the river.
4. Grandchildren have no *claim on* his time and he tells them so.
5. The insurer disputes the *claim for* water damage but not the one for wind.
6. He laid *claim to* the discovery in a letter nobody kept.

**See also:** [damages for](#damages-for), [entitlement to](#entitlement-to), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [right to/of/over](#right-toofover), [title to](#title-to), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### comment on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑmɛnt ɑn/ &middot; *KOM-ent on* &middot; weak /ˈkɑmɛnt ən/
**Русский:** комментарий к чему-л., замечание по поводу чего-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (*a comment on being passed over*), or a *wh*-clause (*no comment on what was said*) — **never a to-infinitive**. The noun takes a bare **that-clause** as its content: *his comment that the numbers were soft*.
**Pattern:** *a comment on / about* [noun phrase] &middot; *a comment to* [person / the press] &middot; *a comment that* [clause] &middot; *make a comment on* [noun phrase] &middot; *no comment* &middot; *without comment*
**Variants:** *comment on* — the default, and the only form in *no comment on* &middot; *comment about* — conversational, common in speech
**Contrast:** ***On*** **for the subject,** ***to*** **for the audience.** *A comment **on** the draft* is what the comment is about; *a comment **to** reporters* is who heard it. ✗ *a comment for the draft* and ✗ *a comment of the draft* are errors. The verb keeps *on* and cannot take a direct object: *she commented **on** the delay*, never ✗ *she commented the delay* — a frequent error because the parallel verb *criticize* does take a bare object (*criticized the delay*). *Commentary on* behaves identically to the noun.

A spoken or written remark about something.

1. The professor left three *comments on* my second paragraph.
2. Her *comment about* the parking situation got a bigger laugh than it deserved.
3. The agency declined to *comment on* the pending lawsuit.
4. His *comment that* the numbers were "directionally fine" did not reassure anyone.
5. The report was released without *comment*.
6. A single *comment to* the wrong reporter cost him the job.

**See also:** [opinion on/of/about](#opinion-onofabout), [complaint about](#complaint-about), [view on/of](#view-onof), [reference to](#reference-to), [comment on](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#comment-on), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### commitment to

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈmɪtmənt tu/ &middot; *kuh-MIT-munt too* &middot; weak /kəˈmɪtmənt tə/
**Русский:** обязательство, приверженность чему-л. — обещание или преданность делу
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*a commitment to the plan*, *a commitment to reducing emissions*) — a noun fits the slot, so that *to* is a **preposition** and the verb after it takes *-ing*. The same noun **also** licenses a **to-infinitive** in the undertaking sense (*a commitment to reduce emissions by half*, *a commitment to appear at the hearing*), where *to* is the infinitive marker; both frames are standard American English. A **that-clause** is possible in formal use (*a commitment that no jobs would be cut*). ✗ *a commitment of reducing* and ✗ *a commitment for reducing* are errors.
**Pattern:** *a commitment to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *make / honor / break / renew* **a commitment** &middot; *a commitment from* [party] &middot; *a prior commitment* &middot; *commitment to* [a person or a cause]
**Variants:** *a commitment toward* — occasional and slightly off; *to* is the edited form &middot; *a commitment on* — bureaucratic, as in *a commitment on funding levels*
**Contrast:** **The noun forgives what the adjective does not.** *A commitment **to** reducing emissions* and *a commitment **to** reduce emissions* are both good English, because *commitment* licenses a prepositional complement and an infinitive alike. The **adjective allows only the gerund**: *committed **to** raising standards*, ✗ *committed to raise standards* — see [committed to](15-dependent-adjectives.md#committed-to). A writer moving from the noun to the adjective therefore has to switch from "either" to "*-ing* only," and that is where the mistake gets made. ✗ *a commitment for* and ✗ *a commitment with* are errors: the party who gave the promise takes *from* (*a commitment **from** the vendor*), while *commitment to* plus a person names devotion rather than a promise (*her commitment to the students*).

A promise to do something, or a settled devotion to a person or a cause.

1. The city made a public *commitment to* cutting emissions in half by 2035.
2. Her *commitment to* the students never wavered, even in the worst years.
3. We need a firm *commitment* from the vendor before anyone signs.
4. I have a prior *commitment* on Thursday, so count me out.
5. Nothing in the current draft is a *commitment to* hiring locally.
6. He broke every *commitment* he had made to the union that spring.

**See also:** [contribution to](#contribution-to), [objection to](#objection-to), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [dependence on/reliance on](#dependence-onreliance-on), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### comparison with/to

**Pronunciation:** /kəmˈpɛrɪsən wɪθ/, /kəmˈpɛrɪsən tu/ &middot; *kum-PAIR-ih-sun with, kum-PAIR-ih-sun too* &middot; weak /kəmˈpɛrɪsən wəθ/, /kəmˈpɛrɪsən tə/
**Русский:** сравнение с чем-л., сопоставление — рус. «с», не «к»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or gerund (*no comparison with running a marathon*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a comparison to run a marathon*) and **never a clause**. *Comparison between* takes two items joined by *and*: *a comparison between the two designs*.
**Pattern:** *a comparison with / to* [noun phrase] &middot; *a comparison between* X *and* Y &middot; *a comparison of* X *with / to* Y &middot; *in comparison with / to* [noun phrase] &middot; *by comparison* &middot; *there's no comparison*
**Variants:** *comparison with* — the analytical default, and the usual form in academic prose &middot; *comparison to* — commoner in speech and in the figurative sense &middot; both are standard American English
**Contrast:** **The verb splits where the noun does not.** *Compare X **with** Y* means measure them against each other point by point; *compare X **to** Y* means liken them, assert a resemblance — *he compared the mayor to Nero* is an insult, *he compared the mayor with Nero* is a study. The noun tolerates either preposition for both jobs, which is why the distinction survives mainly in careful writing. ✗ *comparison than* is an error (*than* belongs with the adjective *different*, see [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin)), and *by comparison* takes **no object at all** — ✗ *by comparison with the others, by comparison* is a doubling.

The act of setting two things side by side, or the relation that results.

1. The report makes a careful *comparison with* three neighboring districts.
2. Any *comparison to* the 1970s energy crisis is going to be inexact.
3. There's simply no *comparison between* the two engines.
4. In *comparison with* last winter, this one has been mild.
5. The second draft is, by *comparison*, a model of clarity.
6. A side-by-side *comparison of* the two policies is on page four.

**See also:** [contrast to/with](#contrast-towith), [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin), [similarity to/between](#similarity-tobetween), [relationship with/between](#relationship-withbetween), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### complaint about

**Pronunciation:** /kəmˈpleɪnt əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *kum-PLAYNT uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /kəmˈpleɪnt ˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** жалоба на — рус. «на», не «о» (✗ жалоба о качестве)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **about**: noun phrase, gerund (*a complaint about being kept waiting*), or *wh*-clause (*complaints about how the tests are graded*) — **never a to-infinitive**. **against**: a person or institution, in the formal sense of a filed grievance. The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *a complaint that the heat was never turned on*.
**Pattern:** *a complaint about* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a complaint against* [person / company] &middot; *a complaint to* [authority] &middot; *a complaint that* [clause] &middot; *file / lodge / make a complaint* &middot; *a complaint of* [symptom]
**Variants:** *complain about* — the everyday verb &middot; *complain of* — used for symptoms and formal grievances (*he complained of chest pain*)
**Contrast:** **Four prepositions, four roles:** *about* is the subject, *against* is the party accused, *to* is the authority receiving it, and *of* is reserved for symptoms and legal pleading — *she complained **of** dizziness*. Choosing *against* for a subject produces ✗ *a complaint against the noise*, which reads as though the noise had been sued. The verb takes no direct object: ✗ *he complained the delay*, ✗ *I complained the manager*; it must be *complained about the delay*, *complained to the manager*.

An expression of dissatisfaction, formal or otherwise.

1. We've had four *complaints about* the noise from the loading dock.
2. She filed a formal *complaint against* her supervisor.
3. A *complaint to* the state board takes about ninety days to process.
4. His only *complaint about* the apartment was the water pressure.
5. The patient came in *complaining of* numbness in her left hand.
6. There were *complaints about* being asked to work through lunch.

**See also:** [objection to](#objection-to), [doubt about](#doubt-about), [problem with](#problem-with), [argument with/about](#argument-withabout), [complain to, complain about](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#complain-to-complain-about), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### compliance with

**Pronunciation:** /kəmˈplaɪəns wɪθ/ &middot; *kum-PLY-unss with* &middot; weak /kəmˈplaɪəns wəθ/
**Русский:** соблюдение чего-л., выполнение требований — рус. родительный, не «с»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — regulatory, corporate, and clinical writing; in American companies *Compliance* is also the name of a department
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming a rule, order, standard, or request — **never a gerund** (✗ *compliance with filing on time*; write *compliance with the filing deadline*), **never an infinitive**, and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *compliance with* [a rule / an order / a standard / a subpoena] &middot; *in compliance with* [noun phrase] &middot; *full / substantial / voluntary compliance* &middot; *bring* [something] *into compliance with* [noun phrase] &middot; *a compliance officer* (compound)
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Compliance to*** **is the error, and it is everywhere** — in vendor emails, in ISO paperwork, in job listings. The noun takes *with* and only *with*, matching the verb *comply **with*** and the adjective *compliant **with***. Against [adherence to](#adherence-to), which takes *to* and only *to*: the two nouns mean nearly the same thing and take opposite prepositions, which is exactly why each attracts the other's. American medicine has largely swapped *patient compliance with* a regimen for *adherence to* it. Note that *in compliance with* (the [complex preposition](11-complex-multiword.md#in-compliance-with)) is stiffer than the plain noun and belongs in filings, not in email.

The act or state of doing what a rule, order, or standard requires.

1. The plant was cited for failure of *compliance with* the emissions limit.
2. We are in *compliance with* every condition of the permit as of Friday.
3. *Compliance with* the subpoena took four paralegals and a weekend.
4. Voluntary *compliance with* the guideline has been about sixty percent.
5. Bringing the stairwell into *compliance with* the code cost more than the roof.
6. She runs *compliance* for a mid-sized bank in Columbus.

**See also:** [adherence to](#adherence-to), [conformity with/to](#conformity-withto), [breach of](#breach-of), [exemption from](#exemption-from), [comply with](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#comply-with), [in compliance with](11-complex-multiword.md#in-compliance-with)

### concern about/for

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈsɜrn əˈbaʊt/, /kənˈsɜrn fɔr/ &middot; *kun-SURN uh-BOWT, kun-SURN for* &middot; weak /kənˈsɜrn ˈbaʊt/, /kənˈsɜrn fɚ/
**Русский:** about — беспокойство из-за чего-л.; for — забота о ком-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **about / over**: what worries you — noun phrase, gerund (*concern about losing the contract*), or *wh*-clause. **for**: a person or a thing whose welfare is at stake. **with**: what someone is occupied with, an entirely different sense. The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *concern that the levee would not hold*. **Never a to-infinitive.**
**Pattern:** *concern about / over* [issue] &middot; *concern for* [person / welfare] &middot; *concern with* [subject matter] &middot; *concern that* [clause] &middot; *of concern to* [person] &middot; *express / raise / voice concern about* [issue]
**Variants:** *concern about* — neutral &middot; *concern over* — the same, slightly more journalistic &middot; *concerns regarding* — bureaucratic
**Contrast:** **The three-way split is the whole entry.** *Concern **about** the bridge* is worry that something is wrong with it; *concern **for** the residents* is care about their welfare; *concern **with** the bridge* is having the bridge as your subject or business — *the report's concern with rural roads*. Saying ✗ *concern for the delay* misfires, because delays do not have welfare. ✗ *concern on* is wrong. Note the third life of this word: the modern adjective *concerning* meaning "worrying" (*a concerning trend*) is separate from the preposition *concerning* meaning "about," for which see [concerning](10-topic-reference-role.md#concerning).

Worry about something, care for someone, or the subject someone is occupied with.

1. There's growing *concern about* the condition of the levee.
2. Her *concern for* the older residents kept the shelter open another week.
3. The book's central *concern with* labor history is clear from the first chapter.
4. The board raised *concerns over* the auditor's independence.
5. Water quality is of real *concern to* everyone downstream.
6. *Concern that* the vote had been miscounted led to a manual recount.

**See also:** [worry about](#worry-about), [doubt about](#doubt-about), [interest in](#interest-in), [sympathy for/with](#sympathy-forwith), [concerned about](15-dependent-adjectives.md#concerned-about), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### confidence in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑnfɪdəns ɪn/ &middot; *KON-fih-dunss in* &middot; weak /ˈkɑnfɪdəns ən/
**Русский:** уверенность в ком-л./чём-л., доверие к чьим-л. способностям
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (*confidence in getting it done on time*) — **never a to-infinitive after *in*** (✗ *confidence in to finish*). The noun separately takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition**, meaning self-assurance: *the confidence to ask for a raise*. It also takes a bare **that-clause**: *confidence that the funding will come through*.
**Pattern:** *confidence in* [person / thing] &middot; *the confidence to* [verb] &middot; *confidence that* [clause] &middot; *have / lose / restore confidence in* [noun phrase] &middot; *a vote of confidence in* [person] &middot; *in confidence*
**Variants:** *confidence about* — heard in speech about a future outcome (*confident about the exam*), but *in* is the noun's fixed preposition
**Contrast:** ***In*** **is the only preposition for the object of your trust**: ✗ *confidence on the team*, ✗ *confidence to the team*, ✗ *confidence of the team* — that last one means the team's own confidence, not yours in them. Distinguish the two frames: *confidence **in** speaking* is trust placed in the activity, while *the confidence **to** speak* is the nerve to do it. Note also *in confidence* (privately) and *a confidence* (a secret told), both unrelated senses. The adjective is *confident **in*** a person and *confident **about*** an outcome.

Trust that someone or something will perform.

1. I have complete *confidence in* the surgeon who did my mother's knee.
2. The board's *confidence in* the CEO has visibly eroded.
3. She lacked the *confidence to* speak up in the first three meetings.
4. Investors lost *confidence in* the currency within a week.
5. He told me in *confidence*, so I can't repeat it.
6. There's little *confidence that* the deadline will hold.

**See also:** [belief in](#belief-in), [faith in](#faith-in), [dependence on/reliance on](#dependence-onreliance-on), [doubt about](#doubt-about), [confident of](15-dependent-adjectives.md#confident-of), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### conformity with/to

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈfɔrməti wɪθ/, /kənˈfɔrməti tu/ &middot; *kun-FOR-muh-tee with, kun-FOR-muh-tee too* &middot; weak /kənˈfɔrməti wəθ/, /kənˈfɔrməti tə/
**Русский:** соответствие чему-л. — согласованность со стандартом или образцом
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — specifications, audits, court opinions, and theology; the social sense (*conformity* without a complement) is neutral
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the standard, rule, or model — **never a gerund** (✗ *conformity with reporting quarterly*) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *conformity with / to* [a standard / a rule / a type] &middot; *in conformity with* [noun phrase] &middot; *bring* [something] *into conformity with* [noun phrase] &middot; *a certificate of conformity* &middot; *conformity* (bare, the social pressure sense)
**Variants:** *conformity with* and *conformity to* are both standard American English and interchangeable in most sentences; *with* is commoner in legal and regulatory prose, *to* in engineering and in the sense "matching a model." Manufacturing also uses *conformance to*, a narrower technical variant.
**Contrast:** Against [compliance with](#compliance-with): compliance is obedience to something binding, conformity is resemblance to a pattern — which is why goods *conform to* a specification but a firm *complies with* an order. ✗ *conformity of the rules* is an error for *conformity with*. The verb keeps both (*conform **to** the spec*, *conform **with** the requirements*), and the adjective is *conformable to*, now rare. Note the bare social noun, which takes no preposition at all: *the pressure toward conformity* in a high school.

Agreement with a standard, rule, or model, or the act of matching one.

1. The inspector certified *conformity with* the 2021 electrical code.
2. Each casting is checked for *conformity to* the drawing before it ships.
3. The order was entered in *conformity with* the parties' stipulation.
4. Nothing in the design is in *conformity with* what the client approved.
5. *Conformity to* type is what breeders are paid to judge.
6. The whole essay is about *conformity* and none of it is about conforming.

**See also:** [compliance with](#compliance-with), [adherence to](#adherence-to), [consistent with](09-comparison-opposition.md#consistent-with), [in accordance with](09-comparison-opposition.md#in-accordance-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### connection with/to/between

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈnɛkʃən wɪθ/ &middot; *kuh-NEK-shun with* &middot; weak /kəˈnɛkʃən wəθ/, /kəˈnɛkʃən tə/
**Русский:** связь с чем-л. / между чем-л. — соединение или отношение
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **with / to**: a single noun phrase. **between**: two or more items joined by *and* (*a connection between the leak and the bad valve*). Gerunds are possible (*no connection with smuggling arms*); **never a to-infinitive** and **never a that-clause** (✗ *a connection that the two are related*).
**Pattern:** *a connection with / to* [noun phrase] &middot; *a connection between* X *and* Y &middot; *in connection with* [noun phrase] &middot; *make / see / deny a connection* &middot; *a connection to* [network / flight]
**Variants:** *connection with* — association, involvement &middot; *connection to* — a physical or network link, and increasingly the general form in American speech
**Contrast:** ***Between*** **needs two things and the others need one.** ✗ *a connection between the fire* is incomplete; ✗ *a connection with the fire and the wiring* should be *between*. *Connection **to*** is the everyday choice for a physical or digital link (*a connection to the water main*, *a connection to the airport*) and for a personal tie (*she has a connection to the family*), while *connection **with*** carries the sense of involvement, which is why *questioned in connection with the robbery* means suspected of it. **The fixed phrase is *in connection with*, not ✗ *in connection to*.**

A link, relationship, or association between things or people.

1. Police questioned two men in *connection with* the break-in.
2. There's no proven *connection between* the additive and the symptoms.
3. The house still has a *connection to* the old well.
4. She has a family *connection with* the shipyard going back four generations.
5. I missed my *connection* in Denver by nine minutes.
6. Investigators see a *connection between* the two fires.

**See also:** [link between/to](#link-betweento), [relationship with/between](#relationship-withbetween), [reference to](#reference-to), [similarity to/between](#similarity-tobetween), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### consensus on/about

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈsɛnsəs ɑn/, /kənˈsɛnsəs əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *kun-SEN-sus on, kun-SEN-sus uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /kənˈsɛnsəs ən/; *about* clips to /ˈbaʊt/ in fast speech
**Русский:** консенсус по чему-л., общее согласие в группе
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **on / about**: a noun phrase or **gerund** (*consensus on raising the levy*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a consensus to raise the levy*). **among / within**: the group that holds it. The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *the consensus **that** the levee will hold*.
**Pattern:** *a consensus on / about* [topic] &middot; *consensus among* [a group] &middot; *the consensus that* [clause] &middot; *reach / build / break a consensus* &middot; *by consensus* &middot; *the scientific consensus*
**Contrast:** **Two usage fights ride on this word.** *Consensus **of** opinion* is criticized as redundant, since a consensus is already an agreement of opinion, and *general consensus* draws the same complaint; both are old, both are common, and both are worth cutting in edited prose. ✗ *concensus* is a misspelling that survives spellcheck in nobody's favor. ✗ *a consensus to postpone* is the infinitive error — write *a consensus **on** postponing* or *an agreement **to** postpone*, since [agreement with/on](#agreement-withon) does take the infinitive and *consensus* does not.

General agreement within a group, or the position that group holds.

1. There's no *consensus on* whether to rebuild the pier or remove it.
2. The committee reached a *consensus about* the timeline in one afternoon.
3. *Consensus among* the county's engineers has shifted twice since March.
4. The scientific *consensus that* the warming is human-caused is not in serious dispute.
5. They never got to a *consensus on* funding the second position.
6. The board decided by *consensus* rather than by a recorded vote.

**See also:** [agreement with/on](#agreement-withon), [disagreement with/about](#disagreement-withabout), [opinion on/of/about](#opinion-onofabout), [view on/of](#view-onof), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### consequence of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑnsəkwɛns ʌv/ &middot; *KON-suh-kwenss uv* &middot; weak /ˈkɑnsəkwəns əv/ → /ˈkɑnsəkwəns ə/
**Русский:** следствие, последствие чего-л. — обычно нежелательное
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or gerund (*the consequence of ignoring the warning*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a that-clause**. In the separate sense "importance," it takes *to*: *a matter of no consequence to us*.
**Pattern:** *a consequence of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *as a consequence of* [noun phrase] &middot; *the consequences for* [person / group] &middot; *of no consequence to* [person] &middot; *face / suffer the consequences*
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **names the cause;** ***for*** **names who bears it.** *The consequences **of** the flood* are what the flood produced; *the consequences **for** homeowners* are what homeowners now face. Both can appear together: *the consequences of the flood for homeowners*. ✗ *a consequence to the flood* is an error, though *of no consequence to me* is correct in the unrelated "importance" sense. Compare [result of](#result-of), which is neutral, where *consequence* usually implies something unwelcome, and note the fixed connector *as a consequence of*, treated in full at [as a consequence of](06-cause-purpose.md#as-a-consequence-of).

Something that follows from an earlier action, usually an unwelcome one.

1. Higher premiums are a direct *consequence of* two bad fire seasons.
2. As a *consequence of* the recount, certification slipped by a week.
3. Nobody modeled the *consequences for* small landlords.
4. The *consequence of* ignoring the warning light was a $2,000 repair.
5. He's old enough to face the *consequences* of his own decisions.
6. The typo was, fortunately, of no *consequence*.

**See also:** [result of](#result-of), [effect on](#effect-on), [cause of](#cause-of), [impact on](#impact-on), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### consideration for

**Pronunciation:** /kənˌsɪdəˈreɪʃən fɔr/ &middot; *kun-sid-uh-RAY-shun for* &middot; weak /kənˌsɪdəˈreɪʃən fɚ/
**Русский:** внимание, уважение к кому-л.; юр. встречное предоставление по договору
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** senses 1 and 2 neutral; **sense 3 is legal** — the word that decides whether a promise is enforceable, met on the first page of almost every American contract and in the first month of law school
**Complement:** a noun phrase or **gerund** (*consideration for agreeing not to compete*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *consideration for be promoted*, ✗ *consideration to promote her*) and **never a clause**. In the courtesy sense the complement is a person or their interests; in the contract sense it is the promise being paid for.
**Pattern:** *consideration for* [a person / a position / a promise] &middot; *out of consideration for* [someone] &middot; *under consideration* (no complement) &middot; *take* [something] *into consideration* &middot; *in consideration of* [a promise] (the contract formula) &middot; *good and valuable consideration*
**Contrast:** **Three unrelated nouns, one spelling.** *Consideration **for** the neighbors* is courtesy; *consideration **for** the job* is being weighed as a candidate; *consideration **for** the promise* is the dollar or the peppercorn that makes a contract binding. Only the first two are interchangeable in tone; the third is a term of art, and in a contract *in consideration of* is a fixed formula — see [in consideration of](13-formal-latin-archaic.md#in-consideration-of). ✗ *consideration to the neighbors* is an error, and *considerate **of*** is the adjective (*considerate of her neighbors*, not ✗ *considerate for*). Note *under consideration*, which takes no complement at all.

A courtesy shown to others, the weighing of a candidate, or — in contract law — the price paid for a promise.

**1. Thoughtfulness toward other people.** Regard for someone else's comfort or feelings. The complement is a person or a group, and the phrase often appears as *out of consideration for*.

1. Out of *consideration for* the neighbors, they moved the band inside at ten.
2. He shows no *consideration for* anyone who has to drive behind him.

**2. Being weighed as a candidate.** The complement is a position, an award, or a slot, and the noun almost always follows *for*.

3. Her name is under *consideration for* the deputy's job.
4. Submitting after Friday means no *consideration for* this year's fellowship.

**3. In contract law, the thing of value exchanged for a promise.** Without it a promise is a gift and unenforceable. American drafters open with the formula *in consideration of the mutual covenants herein*, and the amount can be nominal — the classic recital is *ten dollars and other good and valuable consideration*.

5. The court found no *consideration for* the modification and refused to enforce it.
6. A promise to do what you already owe is not *consideration for* anything.

**See also:** [concern about/for](#concern-aboutfor), [respect for](#respect-for), [eligibility for](#eligibility-for), [in consideration of](13-formal-latin-archaic.md#in-consideration-of), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### contrast to/with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑntræst tu/, /ˈkɑntræst wɪθ/ &middot; *KON-trast too, KON-trast with* &middot; weak /ˈkɑntræst tə/, /ˈkɑntræst wəθ/
**Русский:** контраст с чем-л., противопоставление — рус. «с», не «к»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or gerund (*in contrast to spending a fortune on consultants*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *in contrast to spend*) and **never a clause** (✗ *in contrast to that he left*; write *in contrast to his departure*). *A contrast between* takes two items joined by *and*.
**Pattern:** *in contrast to / with* [noun phrase] &middot; *a contrast between* X *and* Y &middot; *by contrast* (no object) &middot; *a contrast to* [noun phrase] &middot; *stand in stark contrast to* [noun phrase]
**Variants:** *in contrast to* and *in contrast with* — both standard American English; *to* is commoner and slightly lighter
**Contrast:** **Watch the *-ing* rule in *in contrast to*.** The *to* is a preposition, so *in contrast to hiring outside counsel*, never ✗ *in contrast to hire outside counsel* — the same trap as [alternative to](#alternative-to) and [objection to](#objection-to). ✗ *in contrast of* is an error. ***By contrast*** **takes no object at all** and is a sentence adverb: *By contrast, the second quarter was flat* — ✗ *by contrast to the second quarter* is a blend of two constructions. Against [comparison with/to](#comparison-withto), which looks for likeness, *contrast* insists on difference. The verb stresses differently from the noun: CON-trast the noun, con-TRAST the verb.

A striking difference, or the act of setting differences side by side.

1. In *contrast to* last year's turnout, this election was quiet.
2. There's a sharp *contrast between* the two neighborhoods on either side of the highway.
3. Her calm stood in stark *contrast to* the panic in the hallway.
4. The southern route, by *contrast*, adds four hours and no scenery.
5. The essay draws a *contrast between* what the law says and what the clerks do.
6. In *contrast with* the federal rule, the state standard has no exemption.

**See also:** [comparison with/to](#comparison-withto), [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin), [similarity to/between](#similarity-tobetween), [opposition to](#opposition-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### contribution to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkɑntrɪˈbjuʃən tu/ &middot; *kon-trih-BYOO-shun too* &middot; weak /ˌkɑntrɪˈbjuʃən tə/
**Русский:** вклад в — рус. «в», не «к» (вклад в науку)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*a contribution to reducing emissions*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a contribution to reduce emissions*). This is one of the highest-frequency *-ing* traps in the file, because the sentence reads perfectly well as an infinitive and is wrong.
**Pattern:** *a contribution to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a contribution toward* [goal / cost] &middot; *a contribution of* [amount / item] &middot; *make a contribution to* [noun phrase] &middot; *contributions from* [donors]
**Variants:** *contribution toward* — a partial payment against a total (*a contribution toward the deductible*) &middot; *contribution to* — everything else
**Contrast:** **The *to* is a preposition, so the verb after it takes *-ing*:** *a contribution to **solving** the shortage*, never ✗ *a contribution to solve the shortage*. Test it with a noun — *a contribution to the solution* — and the trap is obvious. ✗ *a contribution for the fund* is wrong in the donation sense (use *to*), though *a contribution for the holiday party* names a purpose and is fine. The verb keeps the same preposition and takes no direct object: *she contributed **to** the fund*, ✗ *she contributed the fund*, though *contribute money to the fund* is correct.

Something given to help a cause, or a part played in a result.

1. Her *contribution to* the field was mostly methodological.
2. The company makes a monthly *contribution to* each employee's retirement account.
3. Poor drainage made a real *contribution to* the flooding.
4. We're asking for a small *contribution toward* the cost of the bus.
5. His biggest *contribution to* getting the bill passed was staying quiet.
6. *Contributions from* alumni were down eleven percent.

**See also:** [supply of](#supply-of), [commitment to](#commitment-to), [share in/of](#share-inof), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [attention to](#attention-to), [contribute to](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#contribute-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### control over/of

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈtroʊl ˈoʊvɚ/, /kənˈtroʊl ʌv/ &middot; *kun-TROHL OH-ver, kun-TROHL uv* &middot; weak *over* has **no weak form**; *of* → /əv/ → /ə/
**Русский:** контроль над, власть над чем-л.; также владение чем-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun. A gerund is possible but rare (*control over what gets published* is the natural form, using a *wh*-clause). **Never a to-infinitive** and **never a that-clause**. *Controls on* takes a noun naming what is being restricted: *controls on spending*.
**Pattern:** *control over / of* [noun phrase] &middot; *in control of* [noun phrase] &middot; *under the control of* [person] &middot; *out of control* &middot; *lose / take / regain control of* [noun phrase] &middot; *controls on* [activity]
**Variants:** *control over* — power to direct, especially over people and processes &middot; *control of* — possession of the thing itself, and the usual form in *take control of*, *lose control of*
**Contrast:** **The plural is a different word.** *Control **over** the budget* is authority; *controls **on** the budget* are the rules restricting it, and ✗ *control on the budget* is an error in the authority sense. *Of* and *over* overlap heavily — *control of the company* and *control over the company* are both fine — but only *of* works in the fixed phrases *in control of*, *out of control*, *lose control of the car*. ✗ *control to* and ✗ *control for* are wrong, though *control for* is a real technical verb in statistics (*controlled for income*).

Power to direct something, or possession of it.

1. The trustees have no *control over* how the money is spent day to day.
2. She lost *control of* the car on black ice near the county line.
3. The family retained *control of* the newspaper until 1997.
4. The fire was out of *control* within twenty minutes.
5. New *controls on* short-term rentals take effect in January.
6. He's not in *control of* his own calendar anymore.

**See also:** [authority on/over](#authority-onover), [influence on/over](#influence-onover), [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [over](10-topic-reference-role.md#over)

### conversation with

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkɑnvɚˈseɪʃən wɪθ/ &middot; *kon-ver-SAY-shun with* &middot; weak /ˌkɑnvɚˈseɪʃən wəθ/
**Русский:** разговор, беседа с кем-л. — тема вводится через about
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **with**: a person or group — **never a gerund** (✗ *a conversation with explaining it*) and never a clause. **about / on**: the topic, as a noun phrase or gerund (*a conversation about selling the house*). **Never a to-infinitive** after either (✗ *a conversation to sell the house*, unless *to sell* is a purpose clause).
**Pattern:** *a conversation with* [person] &middot; *a conversation about / on* [topic] &middot; *have / start / end / overhear a conversation* &middot; *in conversation with* [person] &middot; *make conversation* (small talk) &middot; *a conversation between* [two people]
**Contrast:** ***With*** **names the other party and never** ***to***: ✗ *a conversation to my landlord* is an error, even though the verb *talk* allows both *talk **to*** and *talk **with*** — see [talk to, talk with, talk about](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about). *A conversation **between** us* views the two speakers from outside; *a conversation **with** you* views it from my side. American usage note: since about 2010 *a conversation about* has become office-speak for a difficult topic (*we need to have a conversation about attendance*), which is a euphemism, not a new grammar.

An exchange of talk between people, and what it is about.

1. I had a long *conversation with* the county clerk about the easement.
2. Her *conversation with* the loan officer lasted four minutes.
3. He overheard a *conversation between* two of the contractors in the stairwell.
4. That's a *conversation* for after the audit, not before it.
5. We should have a *conversation about* who covers weekends.
6. She is in *conversation with* two publishers and will not say which.

**See also:** [appointment with](#appointment-with), [interview with](#interview-with), [discussion about/of](#discussion-aboutof), [argument with/about](#argument-withabout), [talk to, talk with, talk about](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### correlation with/between

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkɔrəˈleɪʃən wɪθ/, /ˌkɔrəˈleɪʃən bɪˈtwin/ &middot; *kor-uh-LAY-shun with, kor-uh-LAY-shun bih-TWEEN* &middot; weak /ˌkɔrəˈleɪʃən wəθ/, /ˌkɔrəˈleɪʃən bəˈtwin/
**Русский:** корреляция с / между — согласованное изменение двух величин
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; a technical term in statistics and a loose one in journalism
**Complement:** **between**: two variables joined by *and*. **with**: a single second variable. Both take noun phrases; a **gerund** is possible but stiff (*a correlation with starting school later*). **Never a to-infinitive** and **never a that-clause**.
**Pattern:** *a correlation between* X *and* Y &middot; *a correlation with* [variable] &middot; *a strong / weak / positive / negative / spurious correlation* &middot; *correlate with* [variable] (the verb) &middot; *the correlation coefficient*
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Correlation to*** **is the error** that gets past most editors; the noun takes *between* or *with*, and so does the verb — *X correlates **with** Y*, never ✗ *correlates to Y*. ***Between*** **needs two named terms**: ✗ *a correlation between income* is incomplete. And the sentence every statistics course exists to teach: **correlation is not causation** — *a correlation between ice cream sales and drownings* is real and explains nothing, which is why [cause of](#cause-of) claims more than [association with/between](#association-withbetween), and *association* claims more than a mere correlation coefficient.

A measured tendency for two variables to move together.

1. There's a strong *correlation between* household income and third-grade reading scores.
2. Sales show almost no *correlation with* the weather in this market.
3. The *correlation between* the two indexes broke down in 2020.
4. Any *correlation with* the treatment disappears once you control for age.
5. She warned the board that a *correlation between* the two lines proves nothing.
6. The reported *correlation between* screen time and sleep is weaker than the headline.

**See also:** [association with/between](#association-withbetween), [link between/to](#link-betweento), [relationship with/between](#relationship-withbetween), [ratio of ... to](#ratio-of--to), [cause of](#cause-of), [between](03-place-position.md#between)

### criterion for

**Pronunciation:** /kraɪˈtɪriən fɔr/ &middot; *kry-TEER-ee-un for* &middot; weak /kraɪˈtɪriən fɚ/; the plural is /kraɪˈtɪriə/ &middot; *kry-TEER-ee-uh*
**Русский:** критерий чего-л. — мерило отбора или оценки (мн. ч. criteria)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a noun phrase or **gerund** (*a criterion for awarding the contract*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the criteria to award the contract*) and **never a that-clause**. The frame *the criterion by which* + clause is the formal alternative.
**Pattern:** *a criterion for* [selection / eligibility / admission] &middot; *the criteria for* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *meet / satisfy / fail the criteria* &middot; *the criterion by which* [clause] &middot; *on what criteria* [clause]
**Contrast:** **The countability rule is the trap.** *Criterion* is singular and *criteria* is plural: ✗ *a criteria*, ✗ *this criteria is*, ✗ *criterias*. Write *one criterion*, *three criteria*, *these criteria are*. The error is now so common in job listings and grant guidelines that it is drifting toward acceptance, but it is still marked in edited prose. Against [grounds for](#grounds-for): a criterion is a test you apply in advance, grounds are reasons you already have. ✗ *criterion of eligibility* is not wrong but is stiffer than *for*.

A standard or test by which something is judged or selected.

1. Cost is only one *criterion for* awarding the contract.
2. The *criteria for* admission changed the year after she applied.
3. Two applicants met every *criterion for* the fellowship and one got it.
4. What *criteria* are you using to rank these?
5. Residency is not a *criterion for* jury service in this county.
6. The single *criterion for* replacement is whether the part has cracked.

**See also:** [grounds for](#grounds-for), [basis for](#basis-for), [eligibility for](#eligibility-for), [threshold for](#threshold-for), [rule against/for](#rule-againstfor), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### cure for

**Pronunciation:** /kjʊr fɔr/ &middot; *kyoor for* &middot; weak /kjʊr fɚ/
**Русский:** лекарство от, средство от — рус. «от», не «для»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a disease or a problem (*a cure for hiccups*). A gerund is unusual but possible (*a cure for waking up at three a.m.*). **Never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a cure for* [disease / problem] &middot; *find / seek a cure for* [noun phrase] &middot; *beyond cure* &middot; *the cure is worse than the disease*
**Contrast:** ***For*** **names the ailment;** ***of*** **belongs to the verb and names the patient's affliction.** *A cure **for** insomnia* is the remedy; *cure someone **of** insomnia* is what a doctor does — ✗ *cure someone for insomnia* and ✗ *a cure of insomnia* are both wrong. ✗ *cure to* and ✗ *cure against* are errors; the near-synonyms diverge, with *remedy **for***, *treatment **for***, *vaccine **against** or **for***, and *antidote **to** or **for***. Note the unrelated culinary and industrial verb (*cured ham*, *cured concrete*), which takes no preposition at all.

A remedy that ends a disease or a persistent problem.

1. There's still no *cure for* the common cold.
2. Everyone in the family has a different *cure for* hiccups.
3. The trial is testing a possible *cure for* sickle cell disease.
4. Sunlight is not a *cure for* a badly designed office.
5. The drug cured him *of* the infection but wrecked his hearing.
6. By then the rot was beyond *cure*.

**See also:** [solution to](#solution-to), [protection from/against](#protection-fromagainst), [damage to](#damage-to), [danger of/to](#danger-ofto), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### custody of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkʌstədi ʌv/ &middot; *KUSS-tuh-dee uv* &middot; weak /ˈkʌstədi əv/ → /ˈkʌstədi ə/
**Русский:** опека над кем-л., попечение; хранение чего-л.; юр. также содержание под стражей
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — family court, criminal procedure, records management; an ordinary American word because of divorce and arrest
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming a person, a thing, or a record — **never a gerund** (✗ *custody of keeping the files*), **never an infinitive**, and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *custody of* [a child / the records / the evidence] &middot; *in the custody of* [an authority] &middot; *take* [someone] *into custody* &middot; *have / share / lose custody* &middot; *joint / sole / physical / legal custody* &middot; *the chain of custody*
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is the only preposition the noun takes:** ✗ *custody over the children* and ✗ *custody on the evidence* are errors, even though *jurisdiction* and *control* do take *over* — see [jurisdiction over](#jurisdiction-over) and [control over/of](#control-overof). Two senses live side by side and neither uses a different preposition: *custody **of** a child* is a parental right, and *in custody* alone means under arrest. In American family law *legal custody* is decision-making and *physical custody* is where the child sleeps; in criminal cases *the chain of custody* is the paper trail on a piece of evidence, and breaking it can lose the case.

The right or duty of keeping and caring for someone or something.

1. She has legal *custody of* both children and he has alternate weekends.
2. The suspect has been in the *custody of* the marshals since Tuesday.
3. *Custody of* the original tapes passed to the university in 1998.
4. The defense attacked the chain of *custody of* the blood sample.
5. Two deputies took him into *custody* outside the courthouse.
6. The clerk has *custody of* every filing in the case.

**See also:** [possession of](#possession-of), [control over/of](#control-overof), [jurisdiction over](#jurisdiction-over), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [title to](#title-to), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)


## D

### damage to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdæmɪdʒ tu/ &middot; *DAM-ij too* &middot; weak /ˈdæmɪdʒ tə/
**Русский:** ущерб, вред, повреждение чему-л. — рус. дательный падеж
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun naming the thing harmed (*damage to the roof*, *damage to her reputation*). **Never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, **never a clause**. *Damage from* names the cause: *damage from the storm*.
**Pattern:** *damage to* [thing harmed] &middot; *damage from / by* [cause] &middot; *do / cause damage to* [noun phrase] &middot; *the damage was done* &middot; *damages* (plural) *for* [loss]
**Contrast:** ***To*** **for the victim,** ***from*** **for the cause** — *damage **to** the siding **from** hail*. ✗ *damage of the roof* is an error, and so is ✗ *damage on the roof*. **The plural is a legal term, not a plural**: *damages* means money awarded by a court, so *he sued for damages* is compensation and *he inspected the damage* is the harm itself — ✗ *the damages to my car were extensive* is a common and conspicuous slip. The verb takes a direct object and no preposition: *the hail damaged the siding*, ✗ *damaged to the siding*.

Physical or reputational harm done to something.

1. There's serious water *damage to* the subfloor under the sink.
2. *Damage to* the levee will take two seasons to repair.
3. The leak did more *damage to* the building's reputation than to the building.
4. Most of the *damage from* the storm was in the eastern half of the county.
5. By the time anyone noticed, the *damage* was done.
6. The jury awarded *damages* of $1.2 million.

**See also:** [threat to](#threat-to), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [effect on](#effect-on), [protection from/against](#protection-fromagainst), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### damages for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdæmɪdʒɪz fɔr/ &middot; *DAM-ij-iz for* &middot; weak /ˈdæmɪdʒɪz fɚ/
**Русский:** юр. убытки, возмещение за — присуждённая судом компенсация, всегда мн. ч.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal — complaints, jury instructions, settlement letters; an American meets it in a demand letter or on a small-claims form
**Complement:** **for**: the wrong or loss being compensated, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*damages for failing to warn*). **of**: an amount (*damages of $2.4 million*). **against**: the party ordered to pay. **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *damages to compensate her* as a complement of the noun) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *damages for* [breach / injury / lost wages] &middot; *damages of* [an amount] &middot; *damages against* [a defendant] &middot; *sue / recover / award damages* &middot; *compensatory / punitive / liquidated / nominal damages* &middot; *treble damages*
**Contrast:** ***Damage*** **is harm and takes** ***to***; ***damages*** **is money and takes** ***for***. *Damage **to** the roof* is the hole; *damages **for** the damage to the roof* is the check. ✗ *damages to the roof* is one of the commonest errors in insurance correspondence, and it converts a physical fact into a legal claim. The plural has no singular in this sense: ✗ *a damage of $500*. Note the stacking, exactly as with [tax on](#tax-on): *damages **of** $40,000 **for** lost profits **against** the general contractor*.

Money awarded by a court to compensate for a loss or wrong.

1. She sued for *damages for* lost wages and emotional distress.
2. The jury awarded *damages of* $1.2 million against the hospital.
3. *Damages for* breach of contract are limited to what the parties could foresee.
4. Punitive *damages* are rare in this state and capped when they happen.
5. The clause fixes liquidated *damages for* each day the work runs late.
6. He recovered nothing in *damages for* the delay because he could not prove it.

**See also:** [damage to](#damage-to), [claim against/for/to](#claim-againstforto), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [remedy for/against](#remedy-foragainst), [judgment against/for](#judgment-againstfor), [penalty for](#penalty-for)

### danger of/to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdeɪndʒɚ ʌv/, /ˈdeɪndʒɚ tu/ &middot; *DAYN-jer uv, DAYN-jer too* &middot; weak /ˈdeɪndʒɚ əv/ → /ˈdeɪndʒɚ ə/; /ˈdeɪndʒɚ tə/
**Русский:** of — опасность чего-л. (самого исхода); to — угроза для кого-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase or **gerund** naming the bad outcome (*the danger of infection*, *in danger of losing the house*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *in danger to lose the house*). **to**: a person or thing at risk. The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *the danger that the fund runs dry*.
**Pattern:** *the danger of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a danger to* [person / thing at risk] &middot; *in danger of* [verb-ing] &middot; *out of danger* &middot; *the danger that* [clause]
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is what might happen;** ***to*** **is who it happens to.** *The danger **of** fire* is the outcome feared; *a danger **to** firefighters* is who is exposed. Swapping them gives ✗ *the danger to fire*. ***In danger of*** **is a fixed frame that requires *-ing***: *in danger of collapsing*, ✗ *in danger to collapse* — the single commonest error with this word. ✗ *danger for* is wrong in American English, though it is a natural transfer from French and Spanish. Compare [risk of](#risk-of), which behaves identically and is commoner in technical prose.

Exposure to harm, or the harmful outcome itself.

1. There's a real *danger of* the ceiling coming down.
2. Loose gravel on that curve is a *danger to* cyclists.
3. Two of the buildings are in *danger of* collapsing.
4. She was out of *danger* by Tuesday morning.
5. The *danger of* signing before an inspection is that you own the problem.
6. Untreated, the infection poses a *danger to* the whole ward.

**See also:** [risk of](#risk-of), [threat to](#threat-to), [chance of](#chance-of), [protection from/against](#protection-fromagainst), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### default on/under

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈfɔlt ɑn/, /dɪˈfɔlt ˈʌndɚ/ &middot; *dih-FAWLT on, dih-FAWLT UN-der* &middot; weak /dɪˈfɔlt ən/; *under* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** юр. неисполнение обязательства, дефолт по кредиту — просрочка в срок платежа
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** **on** is financial and neutral — mortgages, student loans, sovereign debt; ***under*** **is legal drafting**, met in the "Events of Default" section of a loan agreement or a commercial lease
**Complement:** **on**: the obligation not met — a loan, a payment, a bond. **under**: the instrument the default arises beneath — an agreement, a lease, a section number. **Never a gerund** (✗ *default on paying the note*; write *default on the note*), **never an infinitive**, and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a default on* [a loan / a payment] &middot; *a default under* [an agreement / section 8] &middot; *in default of* [something owed] (formal) &middot; *by default* &middot; *cure a default* &middot; *an event of default* &middot; *a default judgment*
**Variants:** *in default of* survives in formal and legal prose meaning "for want of" (*in default of any other bidder*); it is a different frame from *default on* and is not interchangeable with it
**Contrast:** ***On*** **points at the debt,** ***under*** **at the paper.** You *default **on** the note* and are *in default **under** the credit agreement* — the same event described from the borrower's side and the drafter's side. ✗ *default of the loan* is an error in American usage. Keep the litigation sense separate: *a default judgment* is entered because a party failed to appear, and *win by default* means the other side did not show up. In computing the noun means a preset value and takes *to* on the verb (*it defaults to Eastern time*), a fourth sense with a fourth preposition.

A failure to meet an obligation when it comes due.

1. Two missed payments put him in *default on* the second mortgage.
2. Any transfer of the shares is an event of *default under* section 8.
3. The country's *default on* its dollar bonds was the first since 2001.
4. The tenant cured the *default* within the ten-day window.
5. She won the seat by *default* when no one else filed.
6. A *default under* the lease also triggers a *default on* the equipment loan.

**See also:** [breach of](#breach-of), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [obligation to/under](#obligation-tounder), [lien on/against](#lien-onagainst), [judgment against/for](#judgment-againstfor), [hereunder](13-formal-latin-archaic.md#hereunder)

### deference to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛfɚəns tu/ &middot; *DEF-ur-unss too* &middot; weak /ˈdɛfɚəns tə/
**Русский:** книжн. почтительное подчинение чьему-л. мнению или власти
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; a term of art in American administrative law, where courts owe agencies varying degrees of it, and an ordinary courtesy word in *out of deference to*
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the person, body, or judgment yielded to — **never a gerund** (✗ *deference to reading it his way*), **never an infinitive**, and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *deference to* [a person / a court / an agency / someone's judgment] &middot; *in / out of deference to* [noun phrase] &middot; *show / owe / accord deference to* [noun phrase] &middot; *undue / excessive deference* &middot; *with all due deference* (a hedge before disagreeing)
**Contrast:** **Three near neighbors, three different words.** *Deference* is yielding to someone's judgment; *deferment* and *deferral* are postponements (*a student-loan deferment*); *difference* is another word entirely, and the near-rhyme catches typists. ✗ *deference of* and ✗ *deference toward* are errors. The verb splits by sense and preposition: *defer **to** the chair* is yielding, *defer the payment* is postponing — one takes a preposition and one takes a bare object. In American law, *deference to* an agency is measured (*Chevron deference*, *Skidmore deference*) and *with all due deference* is the formula that introduces a refusal to defer.

Respectful submission to another's judgment, authority, or wishes.

1. Out of *deference to* his widow, the department held the announcement a week.
2. The panel showed unusual *deference to* the trial judge's findings.
3. Courts owe some *deference to* an agency's reading of its own rule.
4. Her *deference to* the older partners was strategic and did not last.
5. In *deference to* the family, no photographs were taken inside the church.
6. The report criticizes the board's *deference to* management on every close question.

**See also:** [adherence to](#adherence-to), [respect for](#respect-for), [attitude to/toward](#attitude-totoward), [authority on/over](#authority-onover), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### degree in

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈɡri ɪn/ &middot; *dih-GREE in* &middot; weak /dɪˈɡri ən/
**Русский:** диплом, учёная степень по специальности; отдельно — степень, градус
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **in**: the field of study, as a noun phrase — **never a gerund** (✗ *a degree in nursing patients*, though *a degree in nursing* is a field and fine) and never a clause. **from**: the institution. **of**: an amount or extent, in the wholly different sense (*a degree of certainty*), where a gerund is possible (*some degree of guessing*).
**Pattern:** *a degree in* [field] &middot; *a degree from* [institution] &middot; *a bachelor's / master's / law degree in* [field] &middot; *a degree of* [certainty / difficulty] &middot; *to a degree* &middot; *by degrees* &middot; *ninety degrees*
**Contrast:** ***In*** **for the subject,** ***from*** **for the school, and never the reverse:** ✗ *a degree of economics* and ✗ *a degree in Michigan* are both errors, though *a degree from Michigan in economics* stacks correctly. The measurement sense takes no preposition at all (*it's ninety degrees*), and *to a degree* means "somewhat" with no complement. American usage note: the possessive is *a bachelor's degree* and *a master's degree*, with the apostrophe, but *an associate degree* usually without one, and *a doctorate* replaces *a doctor's degree* entirely.

An academic qualification, named by the subject it was earned in — and, separately, an extent or a unit of measure.

1. She has a *degree in* environmental engineering from a state school.
2. He finished a master's *degree in* library science at night.
3. A *degree in* philosophy is not a bad way into law school.
4. There's always some *degree of* guessing in a first estimate.
5. The thermostat was set to seventy-two *degrees* and nobody believed it.
6. I agree with him to a *degree*, and the degree is small.

**See also:** [experience of/in/with](#experience-ofinwith), [expert on/in](#expert-onin), [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), [success in/at](#success-inat), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### delay in

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈleɪ ɪn/ &middot; *dih-LAY in* &middot; weak /dəˈleɪ ən/
**Русский:** задержка с чем-л., промедление — рус. чаще «с», а не «в»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **in**: a **gerund** or a noun of process (*a delay in processing*, *a delay in the delivery*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a delay to process the claims*). **of**: a length of time (*a delay of two hours*). **to**: what is being held up, chiefly in transport and construction writing (*delays to the northbound lanes*).
**Pattern:** *a delay in* [verb-ing / noun of process] &middot; *a delay of* [time span] &middot; *delays to* [service / project] &middot; *without delay* &middot; *there was a delay before* [clause]
**Variants:** *delays to* — normal in British transport English and in American engineering prose, less usual in general American writing, where *delays in* or *delays on* is commoner
**Contrast:** ***In*** **for the activity held up,** ***of*** **for how long.** *A delay **in** shipping* is the holdup itself; *a delay **of** three days* is its size; ✗ *a delay of shipping* mixes them. The gerund is obligatory after *in* — ✗ *a delay in to notify the family*, ✗ *a delay in notify the family*. The verb takes a direct object with no preposition (*the storm delayed the flight*, ✗ *delayed to the flight*) and a gerund when it is the subject's own action (*he delayed telling her*, ✗ *he delayed to tell her*).

A holdup, and the length of one.

1. A *delay in* processing the claims pushed everything into the next quarter.
2. There was a *delay of* almost four hours at the border.
3. Any *delay in* seeking treatment makes the outcome worse.
4. The bridge work is causing *delays to* the northbound lanes.
5. Report the loss without *delay*.
6. The *delay in* notifying the families became the center of the inquiry.

**See also:** [difficulty with/in](#difficulty-within), [problem with](#problem-with), [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [progress in/on](#progress-inon), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### demand for

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈmænd fɔr/ &middot; *dih-MAND for* &middot; weak /dəˈmænd fɚ/
**Русский:** спрос на что-л.; настойчивое требование чего-л. — рус. «на», не «для»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase naming what is wanted (*demand for housing*). A gerund is unusual after the economic sense; the "insistent request" sense takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition** (*a demand to see the warrant*) and a **that-clause with the subjunctive** (*a demand that he resign*, not ✗ *that he resigns*).
**Pattern:** *demand for* [goods / services] &middot; *a demand to* [verb] &middot; *a demand that* [clause with subjunctive] &middot; *in demand* &middot; *meet / satisfy the demand for* [noun phrase] &middot; *the demands of* [job / role]
**Contrast:** **Two nouns, two grammars.** The economic noun is uncountable and takes *for*: *demand **for** rentals is up*. The "insistent request" noun is countable and takes an infinitive or a subjunctive clause: *a demand **to** see the manager*, *a demand **that** the fee **be** waived*. A third pattern, *the demands **of** the job*, means the requirements it imposes — ✗ *the demands for the job* means something else entirely. ✗ *demand of housing* is an error. Note that the American subjunctive after *demand that* keeps the bare verb: *that he resign*, not *that he resigns*.

The wish or need for something, or an insistent request.

1. *Demand for* one-bedroom apartments has outrun supply for six years.
2. There's growing *demand for* bilingual paralegals.
3. The union's *demand for* a cost-of-living clause held up the whole contract.
4. She made a formal *demand to* inspect the books.
5. Welders are very much in *demand* right now.
6. The *demands of* the job finally got to him.

**See also:** [need for](#need-for), [supply of](#supply-of), [request for](#request-for), [order for](#order-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### departure from

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈpɑrtʃɚ frʌm/ &middot; *dih-PAR-cher frum* &middot; weak /dəˈpɑrtʃɚ frəm/
**Русский:** отъезд, отправление из; также отход от заведённой практики
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **from**: a place left, or a norm broken (*a departure from tradition*, *a departure from the airport*). A gerund is possible in the abstract sense (*a departure from doing it by hand*). **for**: the destination (*departure for Lisbon*). **Never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a departure from* [place / norm] &middot; *departure for* [destination] &middot; *departure time* &middot; *a radical / sharp departure from* [precedent] &middot; *on / upon departure*
**Contrast:** ***From*** **is where you left;** ***for*** **is where you are headed** — *departure **from** Newark **for** Lisbon*. ✗ *departure to Lisbon* is a widespread error, and *arrival* runs the other way (*arrival **in** Lisbon **from** Newark*, ✗ *arrival to Lisbon*). The abstract sense is the commoner one in writing: *a departure **from** precedent* means a break with it, and ✗ *a departure of precedent* is wrong. The verb takes *from* likewise (*depart from the script*) and is transitive only in travel announcements (*flights departing Gate 14*).

A leaving, or a break with an established practice.

1. The ruling is a sharp *departure from* thirty years of precedent.
2. *Departure from* the north terminal is now through Gate 44.
3. Her cover of the song is a real *departure from* the original.
4. Any *departure from* the written protocol has to be logged.
5. *Departure for* the base camp is at four in the morning.
6. The chairman's abrupt *departure from* the board surprised everybody.

**See also:** [escape from](#escape-from), [change in/of](#change-inof), [exception to](#exception-to), [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### dependence on/reliance on

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈpɛndəns ɑn/, /rɪˈlaɪəns ɑn/ &middot; *dih-PEN-dunss on, rih-LY-unss on* &middot; weak /dəˈpɛndəns ən/, /rəˈlaɪəns ən/
**Русский:** зависимость от чего-л., опора на что-л. — рус. «от», хотя англ. on
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*dependence on borrowing*, *reliance on being bailed out*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *reliance on be bailed out*) and **never a that-clause**.
**Pattern:** *dependence / reliance on* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *dependence of* X *on* Y &middot; *reduce / end dependence on* [noun phrase] &middot; *in reliance on* [noun phrase] &middot; *dependent on* [noun phrase]
**Variants:** *dependence upon* / *reliance upon* — identical in meaning, more formal and commoner in legal and academic writing &middot; *dependency on* — the same noun in bureaucratic and clinical registers (*chemical dependency*)
**Contrast:** ***On*** **is fixed and there is no alternative**: ✗ *dependence of foreign oil*, ✗ *dependence to foreign oil*, ✗ *reliance in the report*. The one place *of* is right is the two-place technical frame *the dependence **of** yield **on** temperature*, where *of* names the variable and *on* still names what it depends on. The related adjective and verb keep *on* as well — *dependent **on** the weather*, *it depends **on** the weather* — and ✗ *depend of*, ✗ *depend from* are the classic transfer errors from French and Italian. Bare *It depends.* with no complement is fine; ✗ *It depends of.* is not.

The state of needing something or someone in order to function.

1. The plan is meant to reduce our *dependence on* imported fertilizer.
2. Her whole argument rests on the town's *reliance on* a single employer.
3. *Dependence on* borrowing to cover payroll is what sank the company.
4. The bank acted in *reliance on* the appraisal it was given.
5. There's a documented *dependence of* crop yield *on* soil temperature.
6. Long-term *reliance on* the medication carries its own risks.

**See also:** [confidence in](#confidence-in), [faith in](#faith-in), [need for](#need-for), [control over/of](#control-overof), [dependent on](15-dependent-adjectives.md#dependent-on), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### desire for

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈzaɪr fɔr/ &middot; *dih-ZYRE for* &middot; weak /dəˈzaɪr fɚ/
**Русский:** желание чего-л., стремление к чему-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase naming what is wanted (*a desire for privacy*). The same noun takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition**: *a desire to be left alone*. **Never *of*** in modern American English (✗ *a desire of privacy*), and no gerund after *for* in edited prose (*a desire for privacy*, not *a desire for being private*).
**Pattern:** *a desire for* [noun phrase] &middot; *a desire to* [verb] &middot; *a desire that* [clause with subjunctive] &middot; *express / feel a desire for* [noun phrase] &middot; *out of a desire to* [verb]
**Contrast:** ***For*** **takes a thing;** ***to*** **takes an act.** *A desire **for** change* names the object wanted; *a desire **to** change* names the action wanted — and mixing them yields ✗ *a desire for change the schedule*. The word is markedly more formal than [wish for](#wish-for) or *want*, which is why *out of a desire to help* reads well in a report and stiffly in conversation. The verb is likewise formal and takes an object or an infinitive with no preposition: *she desired an explanation*, *she desired to know*, ✗ *she desired for an explanation*.

A wish or longing for something.

1. There's a widespread *desire for* some kind of independent review.
2. His *desire for* privacy is not the same as having something to hide.
3. The changes came out of a *desire to* simplify the paperwork.
4. She has no *desire to* relive any of it.
5. The survey found a strong *desire for* more frequent bus service.
6. A *desire for* approval runs through every page of the memoir.

**See also:** [wish for](#wish-for), [need for](#need-for), [hope for/of](#hope-forof), [preference for](#preference-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### deviation from

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdiviˈeɪʃən frʌm/ &middot; *dee-vee-AY-shun frum* &middot; weak /ˌdiviˈeɪʃən frəm/
**Русский:** отклонение от — отступление от нормы, плана или ожидаемого хода
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — engineering, statistics, auditing, and aviation
**Complement:** **from**: the norm, plan, standard, or course departed from, as a noun phrase — **never a gerund** (✗ *a deviation from following the plan*), **never an infinitive**, and **never a clause**. **in**: where the deviation shows up (*a deviation in the readings*). The statistical compound takes *of*: *the standard deviation of the sample*.
**Pattern:** *a deviation from* [the norm / the plan / the standard / course] &middot; *a deviation in* [a measurement] &middot; *the standard deviation of* [a data set] &middot; *any deviation from* [protocol] &middot; *two standard deviations above the mean*
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Deviation of the norm*** **is the error** — the noun takes *from*, exactly as the verb does (*deviate **from** the script*, never ✗ *deviate of*). Careful with the one compound that flips it: *the standard deviation **of** the sample* takes *of*, because there *deviation* is a measured quantity and not a departure. Against [departure from](#departure-from), which is the everyday synonym: a *deviation* implies a rule that was supposed to be followed, a *departure* need only imply a previous practice.

A departure from a standard, plan, or expected course.

1. Any *deviation from* the written protocol has to be logged and initialed.
2. The audit found three *deviations from* the approved change process.
3. A small *deviation from* course put them twelve miles off by morning.
4. There's a noticeable *deviation in* the readings after the sensor was moved.
5. The standard *deviation of* the sample is larger than the effect they claim.
6. His answer was a polite *deviation from* the truth.

**See also:** [departure from](#departure-from), [discrepancy between](#discrepancy-between), [disparity between/in](#disparity-betweenin), [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin), [conformity with/to](#conformity-withto), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### difference between/in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɪfrəns bɪˈtwin/, /ˈdɪfrəns ɪn/ &middot; *DIF-runss bih-TWEEN, DIF-runss in* &middot; weak /ˈdɪfrəns bəˈtwin/, /ˈdɪfrəns ən/
**Русский:** различие между чем-л. / разница в чём-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **between**: two or more items joined by *and*, or a plural (*the difference between the two engines*, *differences between states*). **in**: the respect in which they differ (*a difference in tone*). Gerunds follow both (*the difference between buying and leasing*). **Never a to-infinitive**; a *wh*-clause follows only the *between* frame indirectly (*the difference between what he said and what he did*).
**Pattern:** *the difference between* X *and* Y &middot; *a difference in* [respect] &middot; *make a difference to / in* [noun phrase] &middot; *tell the difference between* X *and* Y &middot; *a difference of* [amount / opinion]
**Variants:** *make a difference to* and *make a difference in* — both American; *to* is commoner with people (*it made a difference to her*), *in* with situations (*it made a difference in the outcome*)
**Contrast:** ***Between*** **needs two things.** *The difference between the two engines and the older model* is fine because both sides are named, but ✗ *the difference between the engine* is incomplete, and ✗ *the difference of the two engines* is wrong. Do not carry the adjective's preposition over to the noun: the adjective is *different **from*** (American standard), and ✗ *the difference from the two* is an error — though *a difference from last year* is correct when you are naming a single baseline.
