On *different than*: American English accepts it before a clause (*different than I expected*) and prefers *different from* before a noun; *different to* is British. *A difference **of** opinion* and *a difference **of** four points* are fixed quantity frames.

The way in which two things are not the same.

1. The *difference between* the two engines is mostly noise, not power.
2. There's a real *difference in* tone between her first book and this one.
3. Can you tell the *difference between* margarine and butter in a cookie?
4. One good sous chef makes all the *difference to* a small kitchen.
5. We had a *difference of* opinion about how to handle it.
6. The *difference between* buying and leasing comes down to how long you keep the car.

**See also:** [similarity to/between](#similarity-tobetween), [comparison with/to](#comparison-withto), [contrast to/with](#contrast-towith), [change in/of](#change-inof), [different from](15-dependent-adjectives.md#different-from), [between](03-place-position.md#between)

### difficulty with/in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɪfɪkəlti wɪθ/, /ˈdɪfɪkəlti ɪn/ &middot; *DIF-ih-kul-tee with, DIF-ih-kul-tee in* &middot; weak /ˈdɪfɪkəlti wəθ/, /ˈdɪfɪkəlti ən/
**Русский:** трудность с чем-л., затруднение в чём-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **with**: a noun phrase naming the troublesome thing (*difficulty with the software*). **in** + **gerund**, or a bare **gerund** with the preposition dropped (*difficulty in finding a job*, *difficulty finding a job*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *difficulty to find a job*). ✗ *difficulty with to find* is likewise impossible.
**Pattern:** *difficulty with* [noun phrase] &middot; *difficulty (in)* [verb-ing] &middot; *have difficulty* [verb-ing] &middot; *with difficulty* &middot; *the difficulty of* [verb-ing / noun phrase] &middot; *run into difficulties*
**Variants:** *difficulty in doing* and *difficulty doing* — both standard American English; dropping *in* is commoner in speech and increasingly in writing
**Contrast:** **The infinitive is the error, and it is everywhere:** ✗ *she has difficulty to concentrate*, ✗ *no difficulty to understand him*. Write *difficulty concentrating*, *no difficulty understanding him*. *With* takes a thing, *in* takes an activity, and *of* takes the activity when *difficulty* is the head of a definite description (*the difficulty of proving intent*). ✗ *difficulty for* is wrong. The same *-ing* rule governs the near-synonyms *trouble* and *a hard time*: *trouble sleeping*, *a hard time sleeping*, never ✗ *trouble to sleep*.

Trouble doing something, or the thing that causes it.

1. He's having *difficulty with* the new billing software.
2. She had no *difficulty* finding parking at that hour.
3. The *difficulty in* proving intent is what makes these cases rare.
4. Patients report *difficulty* swallowing in the first week.
5. The team ran into *difficulties* with the foundation almost immediately.
6. He got up the stairs, but with obvious *difficulty*.

**See also:** [trouble with](#trouble-with), [problem with](#problem-with), [delay in](#delay-in), [success in/at](#success-inat), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### disadvantage of

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsədˈvæntɪdʒ ʌv/ &middot; *dis-ad-VAN-tij uv* &middot; weak /ˌdɪsədˈvænɪdʒ əv/ → /ˌdɪsədˈvænɪdʒ ə/
**Русский:** недостаток, минус чего-л.; также невыгодное положение
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*the disadvantage of living downtown*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the disadvantage to live downtown*). In *at a disadvantage*, the complement is dropped entirely or introduced by *to*/*compared with*.
**Pattern:** *the disadvantage of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *at a disadvantage* &middot; *put* [someone] *at a disadvantage* &middot; *to* [someone's] *disadvantage* &middot; *the disadvantages for* [group]
**Variants:** *the disadvantages to doing it that way* — an accepted informal American variant, parallel to *the advantages to*
**Contrast:** **Mirror image of [advantage of/over](#advantage-ofover)**, but with one frame missing: there is no ✗ *disadvantage over*. Where *an advantage **over** a rival* is idiomatic, the negative version is *at a disadvantage **compared with** a rival* or simply *at a disadvantage*. *A disadvantage for the students* is not wrong but names who suffers rather than what the drawback is; keep *of* for the drawback itself. The adjective *disadvantaged* is a social term (*disadvantaged neighborhoods*) and takes no preposition.

A drawback, or a position of relative weakness.

1. The main *disadvantage of* the older model is that parts are hard to find.
2. There's one real *disadvantage of* living this close to the tracks.
3. Smaller districts are at a serious *disadvantage* when they bid for grants.
4. The rule change works to the *disadvantage of* first-time applicants.
5. The *disadvantage of* paying cash is that you have no record of it.
6. Arriving late put her at a *disadvantage* for the rest of the interview.

**See also:** [advantage of/over](#advantage-ofover), [benefit of/to](#benefit-ofto), [problem with](#problem-with), [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### disagreement with/about

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsəˈɡrimənt wɪθ/, /ˌdɪsəˈɡrimənt əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *dis-uh-GREE-munt with, dis-uh-GREE-munt uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ˌdɪsəˈɡrimənt wəθ/, /ˌdɪsəˈɡrimənt ˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** разногласие с кем-л. / по поводу чего-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **with**: a person or party. **about / over / on**: the issue — noun phrase, gerund (*disagreement about hiring outside counsel*), or *wh*-clause (*disagreement about who should sign*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**. **Never a to-infinitive.**
**Pattern:** *a disagreement with* [person] &middot; *a disagreement about / over / on* [issue] &middot; *a disagreement between* X *and* Y &middot; *in disagreement with* [person] &middot; *a sharp disagreement as to whether* [clause]
**Variants:** *disagreement over* — implies more heat &middot; *disagreement on* — an issue formally at stake &middot; *disagreement about* — the neutral default
**Contrast:** **Same three-way frame as [agreement with/on](#agreement-withon)**: *with* the person, *about/over/on* the issue, *between* both sides named from outside. ✗ *a disagreement to the proposal* is an error — for opposition to a specific proposal use [objection to](#objection-to). The verb takes the same set (*disagree **with** her **about** the timeline*) and, unlike its British cousin, ✗ *disagree something* is never American English. Note the mild-to-strong scale: *disagreement* is milder than [argument with/about](#argument-withabout), which in turn is milder than *dispute*.

A difference of opinion, and the people or issues involved.

1. I had a sharp *disagreement with* my accountant about the deduction.
2. The *disagreement over* the property line has been to court twice.
3. There's honest *disagreement about* what the data show.
4. A *disagreement between* the two departments delayed the report by a month.
5. She's in open *disagreement with* the rest of the committee.
6. The *disagreement about* who signs off on refunds is still unresolved.

**See also:** [agreement with/on](#agreement-withon), [argument with/about](#argument-withabout), [objection to](#objection-to), [opposition to](#opposition-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### discrepancy between

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈskrɛpənsi bɪˈtwin/ &middot; *dih-SKREP-un-see bih-TWEEN* &middot; weak /dɪˈskrɛpənsi bəˈtwin/
**Русский:** расхождение между — несовпадение данных, которые должны были сойтись
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — accounting, auditing, and reporting
**Complement:** **between**: two things joined by *and*, or a plural (*a discrepancy between the two counts*, *discrepancies between the invoices*). **in**: where the mismatch shows up (*a discrepancy in the ledger*). **Never a gerund, never an infinitive, never a clause.**
**Pattern:** *a discrepancy between* X *and* Y &middot; *a discrepancy in* [a record / an account] &middot; *account for / explain / reconcile a discrepancy* &middot; *a minor / material / unexplained discrepancy*
**Contrast:** ***Between*** **needs two terms and** ***in*** **needs one location:** ✗ *a discrepancy between the ledger* is incomplete, and ✗ *a discrepancy of the two counts* is an error. Against [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin): a difference is neutral, a discrepancy implies that the two figures were supposed to match and did not — which is why an auditor writes *discrepancy* and a shopper writes *difference*. Against [disparity between/in](#disparity-betweenin): a disparity is a gap that raises a question of fairness, not of arithmetic.

A mismatch between two accounts, figures, or records that ought to agree.

1. There's a $4,000 *discrepancy between* the invoice and the purchase order.
2. Nobody could explain the *discrepancy between* the two witnesses' timelines.
3. Auditors flagged three *discrepancies in* the payroll ledger.
4. The *discrepancy between* what he told the bank and what he told the IRS is the case.
5. A small *discrepancy between* the counts turned into a two-week recount.
6. She reconciled every *discrepancy* before the quarter closed.

**See also:** [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin), [disparity between/in](#disparity-betweenin), [deviation from](#deviation-from), [distinction between](#distinction-between), [between](03-place-position.md#between)

### discussion about/of

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈskʌʃən əˈbaʊt/, /dɪˈskʌʃən ʌv/ &middot; *dih-SKUSH-un uh-BOWT, dih-SKUSH-un uv* &middot; weak /dəˈskʌʃən ˈbaʊt/, /dəˈskʌʃən əv/ → /dəˈskʌʃən ə/
**Русский:** обсуждение чего-л. — рус. родительный (а глагол discuss идёт без предлога)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (*a discussion about closing the branch*), or *wh*-clause (*a discussion about what to do next*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a discussion to close the branch*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *a discussion that we should close it*).
**Pattern:** *a discussion about / of / on* [topic] &middot; *a discussion with* [person] &middot; *a discussion between* X *and* Y &middot; *under discussion* &middot; *have / open / close a discussion* &middot; *in discussion with* [party]
**Variants:** *discussion about* — conversational and the American default &middot; *discussion of* — more formal, and the usual choice when the noun heads a definite description (*a thorough discussion of the evidence*) &middot; *discussion on* — a scheduled or published treatment (*a panel discussion on water rights*)
**Contrast:** **The flagship noun-versus-verb shift, and the source of the single most persistent error in advanced English:** the verb *discuss* takes a **direct object and no preposition** — *we discussed the budget* — so ✗ *we discussed about the budget* is wrong, while the noun *cannot* attach anything without one — *a discussion **about** the budget*, ✗ *a discussion the budget*. Both halves have to be learned together, because dropping the preposition on the noun and adding it to the verb are two faces of the same confusion. ✗ *a discussion for the budget* is likewise an error; *with* names the other party, *between* names both from outside.

A conversation in which a subject is talked through.

1. We had a long *discussion about* whether to close the Elm Street branch.
2. Chapter six is a careful *discussion of* the evidence for the early date.
3. There was a panel *discussion on* water rights at the county fair.
4. I had a useful *discussion with* the inspector about the setback rules.
5. Three proposals are still under *discussion*.
6. The *discussion about* how to pay for it lasted longer than the vote.

**See also:** [argument with/about](#argument-withabout), [comment on](#comment-on), [agreement with/on](#agreement-withon), [discuss something](#discuss-something), [discuss](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#discuss), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### disparity between/in

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈspɛrəti bɪˈtwin/, /dɪˈspɛrəti ɪn/ &middot; *dih-SPAIR-uh-tee bih-TWEEN, dih-SPAIR-uh-tee in* &middot; weak /dɪˈspɛrəti bəˈtwin/, /dɪˈspɛrəti ən/
**Русский:** неравенство между, разрыв в — большой и обычно тревожный разрыв
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — policy writing, public health, civil-rights litigation, and journalism about them
**Complement:** **between**: two groups or figures joined by *and*. **in**: the dimension along which the gap runs (*a disparity in pay*, *disparities in outcomes*). **among**: three or more groups. **Never a gerund, never an infinitive, never a clause.**
**Pattern:** *a disparity between* X *and* Y &middot; *a disparity in* [pay / sentencing / access] &middot; *disparities among* [groups] &middot; *a wide / growing / persistent / racial disparity* &middot; *the disparity between* [rich and poor]
**Contrast:** ***Between*** **names the parties,** ***in*** **names the measure**, and the two stack: *a disparity **in** sentencing **between** federal and state courts*. ✗ *a disparity of pay* is an error, and ✗ *a disparity with* is another. Against [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin): every disparity is a difference, but the word carries a charge — it says the gap is large and probably unjust, which is why *pay disparity* and *pay difference* are not the same claim. Against [discrepancy between](#discrepancy-between): a discrepancy is a bookkeeping mismatch, a disparity is a social gap.

A wide and usually troubling gap between groups or measures.

1. The report documents a persistent *disparity between* rural and urban response times.
2. *Disparities in* maternal mortality have widened in three of the five counties.
3. There's a real *disparity in* pay between the two campuses for the same job.
4. Sentencing *disparities among* the district's judges drew the appeal.
5. The *disparity between* what was budgeted and what was spent is the whole story.
6. Nothing in the plan addresses the *disparity in* broadband access.

**See also:** [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin), [discrepancy between](#discrepancy-between), [distinction between](#distinction-between), [ratio of ... to](#ratio-of--to), [in proportion to](09-comparison-opposition.md#in-proportion-to), [between](03-place-position.md#between)

### distinction between

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈstɪŋkʃən bɪˈtwin/ &middot; *dih-STINK-shun bih-TWEEN* &middot; weak /dɪˈstɪŋkʃən bəˈtwin/
**Русский:** различие между — сознательно проводимое; отдельно — почётное отличие
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** **between**: two things joined by *and*. **of**: an honor, in the other sense (*the distinction of being the oldest bank in the state*), where a **gerund** is required — *of* + *-ing*, never ✗ *the distinction to be the oldest*. **Never a that-clause** after *between*.
**Pattern:** *a distinction between* X *and* Y &middot; *draw / make / blur a distinction between* X *and* Y &middot; *without distinction* &middot; *a distinction without a difference* &middot; *the distinction of* [verb-ing] &middot; *with distinction* (an honor)
**Contrast:** **A difference is a fact; a distinction is drawn.** *The difference **between** copper and aluminum wire* exists whether or not anyone notices; *the distinction **between** negligence and recklessness* is one the law draws on purpose — which is why you can accuse someone of making *a distinction without a difference* and cannot make the same complaint about a difference. ✗ *a distinction of X and Y* is an error in this sense. The second noun, the honor, takes *of* plus a gerund and has nothing to do with the first: *she graduated with distinction*.

A difference someone deliberately marks between two things — and, separately, an honor.

1. The opinion turns on a fine *distinction between* possession and control.
2. He draws no *distinction between* a friend and a client, which is a problem.
3. The *distinction between* weather and climate is the one every argument ignores.
4. That's a *distinction without* a difference and the judge said so.
5. She has the *distinction of* being the first woman elected from the district.
6. The old rules applied to everyone without *distinction*.

**See also:** [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin), [contrast to/with](#contrast-towith), [discrepancy between](#discrepancy-between), [comparison with/to](#comparison-withto), [as distinct from](09-comparison-opposition.md#as-distinct-from), [between](03-place-position.md#between)

### doubt about

**Pronunciation:** /daʊt əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *dowt uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /daʊt ˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** сомнение в чём-л., сомнения насчёт — рус. «в», не «о»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **about / as to**: noun phrase, gerund (*doubts about signing anything*), or *wh*-clause (*doubt about whether it will hold*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**, almost always under a negative (*no doubt that he meant it*). **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *doubt to sign*).
**Pattern:** *doubt about / as to* [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] &middot; *no doubt that* [clause] &middot; *in doubt* &middot; *beyond a reasonable doubt* &middot; *cast doubt on* [noun phrase] &middot; *have one's doubts about* [noun phrase]
**Variants:** *doubt about* — the everyday form &middot; *doubt as to* — formal and legal &middot; *doubt over* — journalistic (*doubts over the funding*) &middot; *doubt of* — archaic outside the fixed *no doubt of it*
**Contrast:** **Three frames that look alike and are not.** *Doubts **about** the plan* is uncertainty; *cast doubt **on** the plan* is the fixed collocation for undermining it — ✗ *cast doubt about* is wrong; and *no doubt **that** he meant it* takes a clause with no preposition — ✗ *no doubt about that he meant it*. The verb behaves differently again: *I doubt **it***, *I doubt **that** he'll come*, *I doubt **whether** it will hold* — all with no preposition, so ✗ *I doubt about his story* is an error where *I have doubts about his story* is correct.

Uncertainty about whether something is true or will happen.

1. I have serious *doubts about* the contractor's estimate.
2. There's some *doubt about* whether the roof will last another winter.
3. The new testimony casts *doubt on* the timeline.
4. There's no *doubt that* she knew before the meeting.
5. The outcome was in *doubt* until the last precinct reported.
6. Her *doubts about* signing anything without a lawyer were well founded.

**See also:** [confidence in](#confidence-in), [belief in](#belief-in), [concern about/for](#concern-aboutfor), [worry about](#worry-about), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### duty to/of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈduti tu/, /ˈduti ʌv/ &middot; *DOO-tee too, DOO-tee uv* &middot; weak /ˈduti tə/, /ˈduti əv/ → /ˈduti ə/ &middot; American /ˈduti/ or /ˈdjuti/; the flapped /d/ makes *duty* and *doody* homophones for most speakers
**Русский:** обязанность перед кем-л., долг; отдельно — пошлина на ввозимый товар
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** sense 1 neutral to formal, and a term of art in tort and corporate law; sense 2 is trade and customs usage
**Complement:** **to**: **either** a person or body owed the duty (*a duty to his clients*) **or a bare infinitive** naming the act required (*a duty to disclose*) — the two *to*'s are different words, and both are standard. **Never a gerund after *to*** (✗ *a duty to disclosing*). **of**: the kind of duty, as a noun (*a duty of care*, *a duty of loyalty*), or the person who bears it (*the duty of every officer*). **on**: what a customs duty falls on.
**Pattern:** *a duty to* [person] &middot; *a duty to* [verb] &middot; *a duty of* [care / loyalty / candor] &middot; *the duty of* [a person] *to* [verb] &middot; *on / off duty* &middot; *do one's duty* &middot; *a duty on* [imported goods] &middot; *duty-free*
**Contrast:** **Put a plain noun in the** ***to*** **slot to see which word it is.** *A duty **to** the shareholders* takes a noun; *a duty **to** disclose* takes a bare verb; ✗ *a duty to disclosing* is impossible either way. Against [obligation to/under](#obligation-tounder): the two behave alike, but obligations come from agreements and duties from roles — a director has *a duty of loyalty* and *an obligation under the credit agreement*. And keep the second noun clear of the first: *the duty **on** imported steel* is a tax and belongs with [tariff on/duty on/levy on](#tariff-onduty-onlevy-on), not with anything you owe your employer.

An obligation attached to a role or a relationship — and, separately, a tax on imported goods.

**1. What a person or office is bound to do.** The *to* frame names either the party owed or the act required; the *of* frame names the kind of duty. In American law the recurring pairs are *duty of care*, *duty of loyalty*, *duty to warn*, and *duty to mitigate*.

1. A landlord has a *duty to* warn tenants about a known hazard.
2. Directors owe a *duty of* loyalty to the corporation, not to the majority holder.
3. He was off *duty* and two blocks away when the call came in.
4. Jury *duty* pays fourteen dollars a day in this county.

**2. A government charge on imported goods.** This noun takes *on*, is countable, and sits beside *tariff* and *levy*. The compound *duty-free* is fixed.

5. The *duty on* imported olive oil went up in the spring.
6. She bought the perfume *duty-free* in Detroit on the way back.

**See also:** [obligation to/under](#obligation-tounder), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [onus on](#onus-on), [right to/of/over](#right-toofover), [tariff on/duty on/levy on](#tariff-onduty-onlevy-on)


## E

### easement over/across

**Pronunciation:** /ˈizmənt ˈoʊvɚ/, /ˈizmənt əˈkrɔs/ &middot; *EEZ-munt OH-ver, EEZ-munt uh-KRAWSS* &middot; weak /ˈizmənt əˈkrɔs/; *over* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** юр. сервитут — право ограниченного пользования чужим участком
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal — deeds, title reports, surveys, and zoning disputes. An American meets the word exactly once, at a real-estate closing, when the title company lists the utility easement running along the back of the lot.
**Complement:** **over / across / on**: the burdened land, as a noun phrase. **for**: the purpose (*an easement for ingress and egress*, *an easement for a sewer line*), where a **gerund** is possible (*an easement for maintaining the line*). **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an easement to cross the field*, as a complement of the noun) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *an easement over / across* [land] &middot; *an easement for* [a purpose] &middot; *grant / reserve / extinguish an easement* &middot; *an easement appurtenant / in gross* &middot; *a utility / drainage / access easement* &middot; *the dominant and servient estates*
**Variants:** *easement over*, *easement across*, and *easement on* are all used of the same right; *over* is the drafter's default, *across* is picked when the strip physically crosses the parcel, and *on* is commoner in surveys
**Contrast:** **The land is the object, not the beneficiary:** *an easement **over** the Harmons' pasture* burdens the Harmons and benefits somebody else, so ✗ *an easement over the power company* is nonsense. Note the near neighbors: a ***right of way*** is the commonest kind of easement, a ***license*** is revocable where an easement is not, and a ***covenant*** restricts what you may do rather than letting someone else in. ✗ *an easement of the pasture* is an error.

A legal right to use a strip of someone else's land for a limited purpose.

1. The deed reserves a ten-foot *easement over* the north edge of the lot.
2. There's a utility *easement across* the back of every parcel on this street.
3. They bought the cabin without noticing the logging *easement over* the access road.
4. The county holds a drainage *easement across* both fields.
5. An *easement for* ingress and egress runs with the land and binds the next owner.
6. Her neighbor sued to extinguish the *easement over* the driveway.

**See also:** [title to](#title-to), [right to/of/over](#right-toofover), [lien on/against](#lien-onagainst), [jurisdiction over](#jurisdiction-over), [access to](#access-to), [over](10-topic-reference-role.md#over)

### effect on

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈfɛkt ɑn/ &middot; *ih-FEKT on* &middot; weak /əˈfɛkt ən/
**Русский:** воздействие на, влияние на — рус. «на», не «в»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** naming what is affected (*an effect on morale*, *an effect on recruiting new staff*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an effect to lower morale*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *an effect that morale dropped*). A *wh*-clause follows *on* freely (*an effect on how the crew is scheduled*). The full frame names both sides: *the effect of X on Y*.
**Pattern:** *an effect on* [thing affected] &middot; *the effect of* X *on* Y &middot; *have / produce an effect on* [noun phrase] &middot; *in effect* &middot; *take effect* &middot; *side effects of* [drug]
**Variants:** *an effect upon* — formal, identical in meaning &middot; *an impact on* — the journalistic near-synonym; see [impact on](#impact-on)
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **names the cause,** ***on*** **names the target**: *the effect **of** the drug **on** blood pressure*. Reversing them is nonsense. ✗ *an effect to morale* and ✗ *an effect for morale* are errors. **The homophone problem is the real one here:** *effect* is the noun (*the effect on sales*) and *affect* is the verb (*it affected sales*), so ✗ *the affect on sales* and ✗ *it effected sales* are the two directions of the same mistake — with the rare exception of the verb *effect* meaning "bring about" (*effect a change*) and the psychological noun *affect* meaning observable emotion (*flat affect*). And note that *affect* the verb takes **no preposition**: ✗ *it affects on sales*.

The change produced in something by something else.

1. The tariff had almost no *effect on* retail prices.
2. Nobody studied the *effect of* the closure *on* the surrounding businesses.
3. Two cups of coffee have a noticeable *effect on* my sleep.
4. The rule takes *effect* on the first of the month.
5. Side *effects of* the medication include dizziness and dry mouth.
6. Her testimony had a visible *effect on* the jury.

**See also:** [impact on](#impact-on), [influence on/over](#influence-onover), [result of](#result-of), [consequence of](#consequence-of), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### eligibility for

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛlədʒəˈbɪləti fɔr/ &middot; *el-uh-juh-BIL-uh-tee for* &middot; weak /ˌɛlədʒəˈbɪləti fɚ/
**Русский:** право на что-л., соответствие условиям для получения или участия
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — benefits administration, college admissions, insurance, athletics
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase or **gerund** (*eligibility for enrolling mid-year*). **to**: a bare infinitive (*eligibility to vote*, *eligibility to receive benefits*). Both are standard, and the two *to*'s test cleanly: after *for* a noun fits, after *to* only a verb does. **Never a that-clause.**
**Pattern:** *eligibility for* [benefits / a program / a scholarship] &middot; *eligibility to* [verb] &middot; *determine / verify / establish / lose eligibility* &middot; *eligibility requirements* (compound) &middot; *eligible for* / *eligible to* (the adjective, same split)
**Contrast:** ***For*** **takes the thing,** ***to*** **takes the act:** *eligibility **for** parole* and *eligibility **to** be paroled* say the same thing with different grammar, and ✗ *eligibility for be paroled* and ✗ *eligibility to parole* are both errors. The adjective behaves identically — *eligible **for** the draft*, *eligible **to** enlist* — and ✗ *eligible on* is never right. In American college sports *eligibility* stands alone as a mass noun: *he has one year of eligibility left*.

Qualification to receive something or to take part.

1. Her *eligibility for* the subsidy ended when the second job started.
2. The clerk verifies *eligibility to* vote against the county roll.
3. A felony conviction does not affect *eligibility for* federal student aid the way it used to.
4. He has one year of *eligibility* left and a bad knee.
5. *Eligibility for* enrolling mid-year depends on why the family moved.
6. The form asks nine questions and settles *eligibility for* none of them.

**See also:** [entitlement to](#entitlement-to), [criterion for](#criterion-for), [threshold for](#threshold-for), [exemption from](#exemption-from), [qualify for](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#qualify-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### embargo on

**Pronunciation:** /ɛmˈbɑrɡoʊ ɑn/ &middot; *em-BAR-goh on* &middot; weak /ɛmˈbɑrɡoʊ ən/
**Русский:** эмбарго на — запрет торговли либо запрет публиковать раньше срока
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — trade policy, diplomacy, and journalism, where it also names an agreed hold on publishing a story
**Complement:** a noun phrase or **gerund** (*an embargo on selling spare parts*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an embargo to sell spare parts*) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *an embargo on* [goods / trade / news] &middot; *an embargo against* [a country] &middot; *impose / lift / defy / break an embargo* &middot; *under embargo* &middot; *an arms / oil / news embargo*
**Variants:** *an embargo on* names what is blocked and *an embargo against* names whom it is aimed at; *on* is far commoner in American writing, and both appear in the same paragraph without objection. The plural adds *-es*: *embargoes*.
**Contrast:** Against [sanction against/sanctions on](#sanction-againstsanctions-on): an embargo is a total ban on trade in something, sanctions are a package of measures that may include one. Against [moratorium on](#moratorium-on): a moratorium is a pause, an embargo is a prohibition with a political point. ✗ *an embargo of oil* is an error for the ban, though *an embargo of 1973* dates it. Note the newsroom sense, which surprises people: a press release *under embargo until Tuesday* may be read now and printed then.

An official ban on trade in something, or on publishing something before a set time.

1. The *embargo on* Cuban goods outlasted every president who inherited it.
2. Congress imposed an *embargo on* arms sales to the region in March.
3. The study is *under embargo* until nine Eastern on Thursday.
4. An *embargo on* exporting the ore would close two mines in a week.
5. Three outlets broke the *embargo* and published early.
6. The 1973 oil *embargo* is why the speed limit was fifty-five.

**See also:** [sanction against/sanctions on](#sanction-againstsanctions-on), [moratorium on](#moratorium-on), [prohibition on/against](#prohibition-onagainst), [tariff on/duty on/levy on](#tariff-onduty-onlevy-on), [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### emphasis on/stress on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛmfəsɪs ɑn/, /strɛs ɑn/ &middot; *EM-fuh-sis on, stress on* &middot; weak /ˈɛmfəsɪs ən/, /strɛs ən/
**Русский:** акцент на чём-л., упор на — особая важность, придаваемая чему-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*emphasis on getting the paperwork right*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *emphasis on get it right*, ✗ *emphasis to improve safety*) and **never a clause** (✗ *emphasis that safety comes first*; write *emphasis on the fact that...*).
**Pattern:** *emphasis / stress on* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *place / put / lay emphasis on* [noun phrase] &middot; *with (an) emphasis on* [noun phrase] &middot; *the emphasis of* [work / program]
**Variants:** *emphasis on* — the standard noun frame &middot; *stress on* — identical in meaning, plainer, and also the phonetic term (*the stress on the second syllable*)
**Contrast:** **Both nouns take *on*; neither related verb takes anything.** *Emphasize* and *stress* are bare transitive verbs — *she emphasized the deadline*, *he stressed the risk* — so ✗ *she emphasized on the deadline* and ✗ *he stressed on the risk* are errors, and they are among the most persistent in academic writing by non-native speakers. The noun cannot go without the preposition: ✗ *emphasis the deadline*. Note the unrelated *stressed about* (anxious) and *stressed out*, which have nothing to do with this pattern.

Special importance or attention given to something.

1. The program puts heavy *emphasis on* early literacy.
2. There's new *emphasis on* documenting every call.
3. Her training placed unusual *stress on* footwork.
4. The syllabus is history with an *emphasis on* primary sources.
5. The *emphasis on* test scores has crowded out everything else.
6. Note the *stress on* the second syllable: com-PA-ra-ble is a variant.

**See also:** [attention to](#attention-to), [focus on](#focus-on), [importance of/to](#importance-ofto), [emphasize something](#emphasize-something), [emphasize](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#emphasize), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### encounter with

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈkaʊntɚ wɪθ/ &middot; *in-KOWN-ter with* &middot; weak /ənˈkaʊntɚ wəθ/
**Русский:** встреча, столкновение с — обычно краткая, случайная или неприятная
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun naming a person, animal, or force met (*an encounter with a bear*). **No gerund**, **no infinitive**, **no clause** — ✗ *an encounter with being arrested*; write *an encounter with the police*.
**Pattern:** *an encounter with* [person / animal / force] &middot; *a chance / brief / violent encounter with* [noun phrase] &middot; *have an encounter with* [noun phrase] &middot; *a close encounter*
**Contrast:** ***With*** **is the only preposition** — ✗ *an encounter to*, ✗ *an encounter of*, ✗ *an encounter against*. The verb, by contrast, takes a **direct object and no preposition at all**: *we encountered heavy traffic*, never ✗ *we encountered with heavy traffic*. This is a textbook noun-versus-verb shift and worth pairing in memory with [meeting with](#interview-with) and [contact with](#contact-someone), both of which behave the same way. Note that the verb also covers abstract obstacles (*encountered resistance*) where the noun usually does not.

A meeting, usually brief, unplanned, or unwelcome.

1. Her first *encounter with* the county assessor did not go well.
2. We had a brief *encounter with* a black bear on the switchbacks.
3. The report documents six *encounters with* armed patrols.
4. A chance *encounter with* an old professor turned into a job.
5. His only *encounter with* the criminal justice system was a parking ticket in 1988.
6. The film opens with an *encounter with* a stranger on a train.

**See also:** [interview with](#interview-with), [relationship with/between](#relationship-withbetween), [contact someone](#contact-someone), [argument with/about](#argument-withabout), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### entitlement to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈtaɪtəlmənt tu/ &middot; *in-TY-tul-munt too* &middot; weak /ɪnˈtaɪtəlmənt tə/
**Русский:** право на что-л. — юридически обеспеченное притязание на получение
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — benefits law, employment contracts, pensions; in American politics *entitlements* is also the standing name for Social Security, Medicare, and Medicaid, and the bare noun is an insult
**Complement:** **to** + noun phrase (*an entitlement to severance*) or **to** + bare infinitive (*an entitlement to receive severance*) — both occur in legal writing, and the two *to*'s are again different words. **Never a gerund** (✗ *an entitlement to receiving severance* is not the edited form) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *an entitlement to* [a benefit / a hearing / notice] &middot; *an entitlement to* [verb] &middot; *establish / deny / forfeit an entitlement* &middot; *a statutory entitlement* &middot; *entitlement programs* (American policy usage) &middot; *a sense of entitlement*
**Contrast:** Against [eligibility for](#eligibility-for), which is the distinction that decides cases: *eligibility* means you may apply and might qualify; *an entitlement* means the thing is already yours and can be enforced. A benefit you are *eligible for* can be denied on the merits; a benefit you have *an entitlement to* can only be taken away with process. ✗ *entitlement of* and ✗ *entitlement for* are errors. The adjective is *entitled **to*** with the same two complements (*entitled to a refund*, *entitled to know*), and *entitled* alone, as a description of a person, is purely pejorative in American English.

An enforceable right to receive something.

1. Nothing in the handbook creates an *entitlement to* severance.
2. The statute gives every applicant an *entitlement to* a written decision.
3. Her *entitlement to* the survivor benefit vested after ten years.
4. He has an *entitlement to* be heard before the license is pulled.
5. Half the federal budget goes to *entitlement* programs and the argument never ends.
6. The kid's sense of *entitlement* did not come from nowhere.

**See also:** [eligibility for](#eligibility-for), [right to/of/over](#right-toofover), [claim against/for/to](#claim-againstforto), [beneficiary of/under](#beneficiary-ofunder), [obligation to/under](#obligation-tounder), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### escape from

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈskeɪp frʌm/ &middot; *ih-SKAYP frum* &middot; weak /əˈskeɪp frəm/
**Русский:** побег из, бегство от — из заточения, от опасности или от рутины
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **from**: a place, condition, or pursuer (*an escape from the county jail*, *an escape from routine*). A gerund is possible (*no escape from having to decide*). **to**: a destination or refuge. **Never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *an escape from* [place / condition] &middot; *an escape to* [refuge] &middot; *a narrow escape* &middot; *make one's escape* &middot; *there is no escape from* [noun phrase] &middot; *a fire escape*
**Contrast:** ***From*** **for what you got out of;** ***to*** **for where you went.** ✗ *an escape of the jail* is an error; *an escape **of** gas* is a different noun entirely, meaning a leak. The verb takes both a prepositional and a bare-object frame with a real difference in meaning: *escape **from** prison* is getting out of the building, while *escape prison* means avoiding a sentence, and *escape someone's notice*, *escape injury*, *escape punishment* are all bare and cannot take *from*. So ✗ *the detail escaped from my notice* is wrong: it *escaped my notice*.

A getting away from confinement, danger, or routine.

1. The *escape from* the county jail was captured on four cameras.
2. Gardening is her *escape from* a job she can't talk about.
3. There's no *escape from* the paperwork, only from doing it yourself.
4. They had a narrow *escape* on the icy stretch outside Laramie.
5. The book is an *escape to* a version of Maine that never quite existed.
6. An *escape of* natural gas forced the block to be evacuated.

**See also:** [departure from](#departure-from), [protection from/against](#protection-fromagainst), [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [danger of/to](#danger-ofto), [escape from](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#escape-from), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### evidence of/for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛvɪdəns ʌv/, /ˈɛvɪdəns fɔr/ &middot; *EV-ih-dunss uv, EV-ih-dunss for* &middot; weak /ˈɛvɪdəns əv/ → /ˈɛvɪdəns ə/; /ˈɛvɪdəns fɚ/
**Русский:** свидетельство чего-л., доказательства в пользу — в англ. неисчисляемо
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase or gerund (*evidence of tampering*, *evidence of having been moved*). **for**: a claim, theory, or proposition supported. The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *evidence that the seal was broken*. **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *evidence to prove* is a purpose clause, a different structure).
**Pattern:** *evidence of* [thing shown to exist] &middot; *evidence for* [claim / theory] &middot; *evidence that* [clause] &middot; *evidence against* [person / theory] &middot; *in evidence* &middot; *give / present / suppress evidence*
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is a trace;** ***for*** **is support for an argument.** *Evidence **of** tampering* means signs that tampering happened; *evidence **for** the early date* means material that argues for a conclusion; *evidence **against** him* is material that incriminates. **The countability rule is the trap:** *evidence* is uncountable in American English, so ✗ *an evidence*, ✗ *evidences*, ✗ *many evidences*. Say *a piece of evidence*, *two pieces of evidence*, *some evidence*, *much evidence*. Compare [proof of](#proof-of), which is also uncountable in this sense but countable in mathematics (*two proofs of the theorem*).

Facts or signs that support a conclusion.

1. There's clear *evidence of* water intrusion behind the drywall.
2. The *evidence for* an earlier eruption is mostly in the ash layers.
3. No *evidence that* the driver had been drinking was ever produced.
4. The strongest *evidence against* him was his own text messages.
5. We found no *evidence of* forced entry.
6. That's one piece of *evidence*, not a case.

**See also:** [proof of](#proof-of), [example of](#example-of), [reason for](#reason-for), [grounds for](#grounds-for), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### example of

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzæmpəl ʌv/ &middot; *ig-ZAM-pul uv* &middot; weak /əɡˈzæmpəl əv/ → /əɡˈzæmpəl ə/
**Русский:** пример чего-л. — рус. родительный, не «для»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or gerund (*an example of overreaching*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an example to overreach*) and **never a that-clause**. In *set an example*, the complement is a person introduced by *for* or *to*.
**Pattern:** *an example of* [category / behavior] &middot; *for example* &middot; *set an example for / to* [person] &middot; *make an example of* [person] &middot; *give / cite an example of* [noun phrase] &middot; *by way of example*
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **for the category,** ***for/to*** **for the audience, and the two must not be swapped.** *An example **of** good design* is an instance; *an example **for** the younger staff* is a model to imitate. ✗ *an example about* is an error, and ✗ *for example of* is a doubling. Note *make an example of someone*, which means to punish them publicly — the opposite of *set an example for someone*, though the words are nearly identical. The Latin abbreviation *e.g.* means *for example* and takes a comma after it in American style; *i.e.* means *that is* and is not interchangeable.

An instance that illustrates a general point.

1. This is a textbook *example of* how not to run a hearing.
2. Can you give me an *example of* what you mean?
3. The clock tower is a good *example of* Richardsonian Romanesque.
4. She set an *example for* every apprentice who came after her.
5. Some cities — Portland, *for example* — never adopted the rule.
6. It was an *example of* overreaching that the court declined to endorse.

**See also:** [evidence of/for](#evidence-offor), [proof of](#proof-of), [reference to](#reference-to), [exception to](#exception-to), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### exception to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsɛpʃən tu/ &middot; *ik-SEP-shun too* &middot; weak /əkˈsɛpʃən tə/
**Русский:** исключение из — рус. «из», не «к» (исключение из правила)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase naming a rule, generalization, or pattern (*an exception to the rule*). A gerund is possible in the *take exception to* frame (*took exception to being interrupted*) — and note that this proves the *to* is a preposition, so **never a bare infinitive** (✗ *took exception to be interrupted*).
**Pattern:** *an exception to* [rule] &middot; *with the exception of* [noun phrase] &middot; *make an exception for* [person] &middot; *take exception to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *without exception* &middot; *the exception that proves the rule*
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions doing three jobs.** *An exception **to** the rule* is a case the rule does not cover; *with the exception **of** Tuesday* means "except Tuesday" and is a fixed phrase, so ✗ *with the exception to Tuesday* is wrong; and *make an exception **for** Maria* means bending the rule in her favor, so ✗ *make an exception to Maria* misfires. The idiom *take exception **to*** means to object, not to exempt — *she took exception to his tone* is a complaint. ✗ *an exception of the rule* is an error.

A case that a rule or generalization does not cover.

1. Every rule in this handbook has at least one *exception to* it.
2. With the *exception of* two holdouts, the block voted the same way.
3. The zoning board made an *exception for* the corner lot.
4. She took *exception to* being told to smile more.
5. The policy applies to all employees without *exception*.
6. Weekends are the one *exception to* the parking ban.

**See also:** [rule against/for](#rule-againstfor), [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [objection to](#objection-to), [alternative to](#alternative-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### excuse for

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈskjus fɔr/ &middot; *ik-SKYOOSS for* &middot; weak /əkˈskjus fɚ/
**Русский:** оправдание за что-л.; также предлог для того, чтобы сделать что-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: noun phrase or **gerund** naming what is being excused (*an excuse for being late*) — **never a bare infinitive after *for*** (✗ *an excuse for be late*). The same noun takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition**, meaning a pretext: *an excuse to leave early*. Both are correct and mean different things.
**Pattern:** *an excuse for* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *an excuse to* [verb] &middot; *make excuses for* [person / verb-ing] &middot; *there's no excuse for* [verb-ing] &middot; *a poor excuse for a* [noun]
**Contrast:** ***For + -ing*** **looks backward;** ***to +*** **infinitive looks forward.** *An excuse **for** leaving early* justifies a departure that happened; *an excuse **to** leave early* is a pretext for one you want. ✗ *an excuse of being late* and ✗ *an excuse about being late* are errors. Note the sarcastic frame *a poor excuse for a lawyer*, where *for* means "in the role of." The noun is /ɪkˈskjus/ with an *s* sound; the verb *excuse* is /ɪkˈskjuz/ with a *z*, and it takes a person as its object with *for* on the offense: *excuse me for interrupting*, ✗ *excuse for me*.

A reason offered to justify a fault, or a pretext for doing something.

1. There's no *excuse for* leaving the gate open in bear country.
2. He gave the same *excuse for* missing three meetings in a row.
3. The rain was just an *excuse to* stay in and read.
4. Stop making *excuses for* him.
5. What's your *excuse for* not registering by the deadline?
6. That radio is a poor *excuse for* a public address system.

**See also:** [apology for](#apology-for), [reason for](#reason-for), [grounds for](#grounds-for), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### exemption from

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzɛmpʃən frʌm/ &middot; *ig-ZEMP-shun frum* &middot; weak /ɪɡˈzɛmpʃən frəm/
**Русский:** освобождение от — от обязанности, налога или требования
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — tax, regulatory, and school paperwork; an American meets it on a W-4, a property-tax form, or a vaccination waiver
**Complement:** **from**: the requirement escaped, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*an exemption from filing quarterly*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an exemption to file quarterly*) and **never a clause**. **for**: who or what gets the break (*an exemption for veterans*). **of**: whoever is exempted, in formal drafting (*the exemption of nonprofits from the tax*).
**Pattern:** *an exemption from* [a tax / a rule / a requirement] &middot; *an exemption for* [a group / a category] &middot; *claim / grant / seek / lose an exemption* &middot; *a personal / homestead / religious / medical exemption* &middot; *exempt from* [noun phrase] (the adjective)
**Contrast:** ***From*** **is what you escape,** ***for*** **is who escapes it.** *An exemption **from** the noise ordinance* is the relief; *an exemption **for** houses of worship* is the class that gets it; ✗ *an exemption of the noise ordinance* reverses the sense. ✗ *exemption to* is an error, and it is common because [exception to](#exception-to) does take *to* — but an exception is a case the rule does not cover and an exemption is a release granted to somebody, so the two nouns are not synonyms and do not share a preposition. The adjective is *exempt **from***, never ✗ *exempt of*.

Official release from a duty, tax, or requirement that otherwise applies.

1. The church claimed an *exemption from* the county's assembly permit.
2. Small carriers got a two-year *exemption from* the new logging rule.
3. She filed for a medical *exemption from* the requirement.
4. There's a homestead *exemption for* owner-occupied property in this state.
5. No *exemption from* filing quarterly is available once revenue crosses the line.
6. Losing the *exemption* cost the foundation about $80,000 a year.

**See also:** [exception to](#exception-to), [immunity from](#immunity-from), [waiver of](#waiver-of), [eligibility for](#eligibility-for), [compliance with](#compliance-with), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### exhibit to/schedule to/annex to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzɪbɪt tu/, /ˈskɛdʒul tu/, /ˈænɛks tu/ &middot; *ig-ZIB-it too, SKED-jool too, AN-eks too* &middot; weak /ɪɡˈzɪbɪt tə/, /ˈskɛdʒul tə/, /ˈænɛks tə/ &middot; the noun is *AN-eks*, the verb *uh-NEKS* /əˈnɛks/
**Русский:** юр. приложение к договору — документ, ставший его частью
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal — contract drafting, SEC filings, treaties. An American meets these on the signature-page side of a purchase agreement, a commercial lease, or an employment contract with a schedule of stock options.
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the instrument the document is attached to — **never a gerund, never an infinitive, never a clause**. In court, *exhibit* takes no preposition at all: *Exhibit 14*, *plaintiff's Exhibit A*.
**Pattern:** *Exhibit A to* [the Agreement] &middot; *Schedule 2 to* [the Purchase Agreement] &middot; *the Annex to* [the Treaty] &middot; *attached as* Exhibit B &middot; *set forth on Schedule 3.1* &middot; *incorporated by reference*
**Variants:** the three are drafting conventions, not synonyms with fixed meanings, and firms differ: an **exhibit** is usually a stand-alone document (a form of note, a bill of sale); a **schedule** is usually a list keyed to a representation (*the Disclosure Schedules*); an **annex** is commoner in treaties and in agreements drafted outside the United States. An **appendix** belongs to reports and books — see [appendix to](#appendix-to) — and an **addendum** adds new terms.
**Contrast:** **All three take *to* and none takes *of*:** ✗ *Exhibit A of the Agreement* is a drafting error a partner will circle, though it is everywhere in email. Note the case convention: capitalized and unhyphenated when they name a specific attachment (*as set forth in Schedule 4.2*), lowercase when generic (*the schedules to the agreement*). In litigation the word switches meaning entirely — *Exhibit 6* is a thing handed to a witness, and it hangs off nothing.

A document attached to and made part of an agreement.

1. The form of promissory note is attached as Exhibit B *to* the loan agreement.
2. Every open lawsuit is listed on the disclosure Schedule *to* the merger agreement.
3. The Annex *to* the treaty runs longer than the treaty.
4. Counsel added a schedule *to* the lease listing the fixtures that stay.
5. Nothing in the exhibits *to* the agreement changes the closing date.
6. She marked the letter as plaintiff's Exhibit 14 and handed it to the witness.

**See also:** [appendix to](#appendix-to), [amendment to](#amendment-to), [party to](#party-to), [signatory to](#signatory-to), [warranty as to](#warranty-as-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### experience of/in/with

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspɪriəns ʌv/ &middot; *ik-SPEER-ee-unss uv* &middot; weak /əkˈspɪriəns əv/ → /ə/; /ən/; /wəθ/
**Русский:** опыт в чём-л., опыт работы с чем-л.; также пережитое
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **in**: a field or activity — noun phrase or **gerund** (*experience in managing volunteers*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *experience to manage volunteers*). **with**: a tool, system, or population dealt with. **of**: something undergone. The noun also takes a bare **to-infinitive** in the frame *the experience to handle it*, meaning enough experience.
**Pattern:** *experience in* [field / verb-ing] &middot; *experience with* [tool / people] &middot; *the experience of* [thing undergone] &middot; *the experience to* [verb] &middot; *have / gain / lack experience* &middot; *from experience*
**Variants:** *experience in* — a field or discipline &middot; *experience with* — hands-on dealings, the American preference on résumés &middot; *experience of* — undergoing something, commoner in British English and in academic prose about lived experience
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions, three relationships to the thing.** *Experience **in** nursing* is a career; *experience **with** ventilators* is having handled them; *the experience **of** being a patient* is having lived through it. American job postings overwhelmingly want *experience **with*** a technology and *experience **in*** a field, so ✗ *experience of Excel* reads as British or as a translation. **Countability matters:** *experience* is uncountable when it means accumulated know-how (*she has ten years' experience*, ✗ *ten years' experiences*) and countable when it means an episode (*a strange experience*, *two bad experiences*).

Accumulated know-how, hands-on dealings, or something lived through.

1. She has eight years' *experience in* pediatric nursing.
2. Do you have any *experience with* payroll software?
3. The whole *experience of* moving cross-country aged me a decade.
4. He lacks the *experience to* run a crew that size.
5. I know from *experience* that the second week is the hard one.
6. Two bad *experiences with* the same contractor were enough.

**See also:** [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), [expert on/in](#expert-onin), [understanding of](#understanding-of), [success in/at](#success-inat), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### expert on/in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛkspɜrt ɑn/, /ˈɛkspɜrt ɪn/ &middot; *EK-spurt on, EK-spurt in* &middot; weak /ˈɛkspɜrt ən/, /ˈɛkspɜrt ən/
**Русский:** эксперт по чему-л., специалист в чём-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **on**: a subject known about — noun phrase only (✗ *an expert on how engines fail*; write *an expert on engine failures*). **in**: a field practiced. **at**: a skill, and here a **gerund** is normal (*an expert at getting funding*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an expert at get funding*).
**Pattern:** *an expert on* [subject] &middot; *an expert in* [field] &middot; *an expert at* [verb-ing / skill] &middot; *expert testimony on* [subject] &middot; *consult an expert on* [noun phrase]
**Variants:** *expert on* — knows about it &middot; *expert in* — works in it &middot; *expert at* — is good at doing it; all three are standard American English and the choice is real, not stylistic
**Contrast:** **Choose by what kind of thing follows.** *An expert **on** wolves* studies them; *an expert **in** wildlife biology* practices the discipline; *an expert **at** tracking wolves* is skilled at the activity. ✗ *an expert of wolves* is an error in all three senses. The parallel adjective *expert* takes the same *at* and *in* (*expert at splicing rope*, *expert in tax law*) but not *on*: ✗ *she is expert on wolves* wants the noun, *she is an expert on wolves*. Compare [authority on/over](#authority-onover), which shares the *on* frame but adds a power sense that *expert* does not have.

Someone with deep knowledge of a subject or skill.

1. She's the state's leading *expert on* groundwater contamination.
2. We brought in an *expert in* forensic accounting.
3. He's an *expert at* getting extensions out of the county.
4. The defense called two *experts on* eyewitness reliability.
5. You don't have to be an *expert in* nutrition to read a label.
6. My uncle is an *expert at* losing car keys.

**See also:** [authority on/over](#authority-onover), [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), [experience of/in/with](#experience-ofinwith), [grasp of](#grasp-of), [expert at](15-dependent-adjectives.md#expert-at), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### exposure to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspoʊʒɚ tu/ &middot; *ik-SPOH-zher too* &middot; weak /ɪkˈspoʊʒɚ tə/
**Русский:** подверженность чему-л., контакт с чем-л.; также разоблачение скрытого
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; technical in medicine, industrial hygiene, and finance
**Complement:** a noun phrase or **gerund** (*exposure to being outdoors all day*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *exposure to breathe the fumes*) and **never a clause**. The revealing sense takes *of* instead: *the exposure of the scheme*.
**Pattern:** *exposure to* [a substance / a risk / a language / the elements] &middot; *the exposure of* [a secret / a fraud] &middot; *limit / reduce / document exposure to* [noun phrase] &middot; *occupational exposure* &middot; *exposure to* [a currency / a sector] (finance) &middot; *die of exposure*
**Contrast:** ***To*** **for what reaches you,** ***of*** **for what gets revealed.** *Her exposure **to** asbestos* is contact; *the exposure **of** the cover-up* is disclosure — and ✗ *the exposure to the cover-up* would mean the cover-up was reaching her. ✗ *exposure with* and ✗ *exposure on* are errors. Note the American senses that take no complement: *die of exposure* is hypothermia, *an exposure* in photography is one frame, and *good exposure* for a business is publicity. In finance, *exposure to* the euro is how much you stand to lose if it moves, which is the risk sense, not the contact sense.

Contact with something that can affect you — a substance, a risk, an idea — or the act of revealing something hidden.

1. Years of *exposure to* solvents in the shop finally showed up on a scan.
2. Early *exposure to* a second language does more than later drilling does.
3. The fund cut its *exposure to* commercial real estate by half.
4. Two hikers died of *exposure* on the ridge that November.
5. The paper's *exposure of* the billing scheme took eighteen months.
6. OSHA limits *exposure to* silica dust to fifty micrograms per cubic meter.

**See also:** [risk of](#risk-of), [danger of/to](#danger-ofto), [protection from/against](#protection-fromagainst), [immunity from](#immunity-from), [vulnerable to](15-dependent-adjectives.md#vulnerable-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)


## F

### faith in

**Pronunciation:** /feɪθ ɪn/ &middot; *fayth in* &middot; weak /feɪθ ən/
**Русский:** вера в кого-л./что-л. — полное доверие, не требующее доказательств
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (*faith in doing things the slow way*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *faith to do things the slow way*). The noun does take a bare **that-clause**: *faith that it would work out*.
**Pattern:** *faith in* [person / thing] &middot; *faith that* [clause] &middot; *have / lose / keep faith in* [noun phrase] &middot; *in good faith* &middot; *an article of faith* &middot; *bad faith*
**Contrast:** ***In*** **is fixed** — ✗ *faith on*, ✗ *faith to*, ✗ *faith about*. The distinction from [confidence in](#confidence-in) is one of evidence: *confidence* implies grounds, *faith* implies going beyond them, which is why *blind confidence* is odd and *blind faith* is idiomatic. Note the fixed legal phrases *in good faith* and *in bad faith*, which take no complement at all, and *keep faith **with** someone* (honor a commitment to them), where the preposition changes with the sense.

Complete trust in someone or something, held without proof.

1. I've lost what *faith* I had *in* the appeals process.
2. She kept her *faith in* the students long after the district gave up.
3. His *faith in* the machine survived three breakdowns in one week.
4. They negotiated in good *faith* and got nothing for it.
5. The board's *faith that* enrollment would rebound was misplaced.
6. *Faith in* institutions is measurably lower than it was in 1975.

**See also:** [confidence in](#confidence-in), [belief in](#belief-in), [dependence on/reliance on](#dependence-onreliance-on), [hope for/of](#hope-forof), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### fear of

**Pronunciation:** /fɪr ʌv/ &middot; *feer uv* &middot; weak /fɪr əv/ → /fɪr ə/
**Русский:** страх перед чем-л., боязнь чего-л. — рус. родительный или «перед»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase or **gerund** (*a fear of flying*, *fear of losing the house*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a fear to fly*). **for**: a person or thing whose safety is at stake (*fear for her safety*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *a fear that the levee would fail*.
**Pattern:** *a fear of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *fear for* [someone / something at risk] &middot; *a fear that* [clause] &middot; *for fear of* [verb-ing] &middot; *for fear that* [clause] &middot; *out of fear of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is what frightens you;** ***for*** **is who you are frightened about.** *Fear **of** her father* means he frightens you; *fear **for** her father* means you are worried about him — a total reversal from one preposition. The fixed adverbial *for fear of* requires an *-ing* form (*he said nothing for fear of making it worse*, ✗ *for fear to make it worse*), while *for fear that* takes a clause. The verb allows the bare infinitive frame that the noun forbids — *I fear to think what it cost* is archaic but grammatical, whereas ✗ *a fear to think* is not.

Being afraid of something, or being afraid on someone's behalf.

1. Her *fear of* flying kept her off airplanes for twenty years.
2. There's a real *fear of* losing the farm if the drought holds.
3. The family expressed *fear for* his safety in the county lockup.
4. He never reported it, for *fear of* being blamed.
5. A *fear that* the levee would fail emptied the whole subdivision.
6. Out of *fear of* the paperwork, she let the claim lapse.

**See also:** [risk of](#risk-of), [danger of/to](#danger-ofto), [worry about](#worry-about), [concern about/for](#concern-aboutfor), [afraid of](15-dependent-adjectives.md#afraid-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### focus on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈfoʊkəs ɑn/ &middot; *FOH-kus on* &middot; weak /ˈfoʊkəs ən/
**Русский:** сосредоточенность на чём-л., фокус на; главный предмет внимания
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*a focus on reducing waste*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a focus to reduce waste*) and **never a clause** (✗ *a focus that we reduce waste*).
**Pattern:** *a focus on* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *the focus of* [study / attention] &middot; *place / put the focus on* [noun phrase] &middot; *in focus* &middot; *out of focus* &middot; *shift focus from* X *to* Y
**Variants:** *focus in on* — an informal American intensifier of the verb (*let's focus in on page three*); harmless in speech, redundant in writing
**Contrast:** ***On*** **for the target,** ***of*** **for whose focus it is**: *the focus **of** the study* is what the study concentrates on, and *a focus **on** cost* is the thing concentrated on — both can appear together, *the focus of the study on cost*. ✗ *a focus to*, ✗ *a focus about*, ✗ *a focus in* are errors. Unlike [emphasis on/stress on](#emphasis-onstress-on), the verb here **keeps** the preposition: *focus **on** the numbers*, ✗ *focus the numbers* — except in the optical and transitive sense (*focus the lens*, *focus your attention on it*).

Concentrated attention, or the thing it is directed at.

1. The district's new *focus on* attendance has already moved the numbers.
2. The *focus of* the audit is the 2023 fiscal year.
3. There's too much *focus on* the logo and not enough on the product.
4. The whole slide was out of *focus*.
5. Her *focus on* reducing packaging waste started as a side project.
6. The committee shifted its *focus from* prevention *to* treatment.

**See also:** [attention to](#attention-to), [emphasis on/stress on](#emphasis-onstress-on), [interest in](#interest-in), [concern about/for](#concern-aboutfor), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### fondness for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈfɑndnəs fɔr/ &middot; *FOND-nus for* &middot; weak /ˈfɑndnəs fɚ/
**Русский:** любовь, слабость к чему-л. — мягкая, чуть снисходительная симпатия
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, mildly literary
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*a fondness for taking the long way home*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a fondness to take the long way*) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a fondness for* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *develop / have a fondness for* [noun phrase] &middot; *look back with fondness on* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ***For*** **is fixed** — ✗ *fondness of*, ✗ *fondness to*, ✗ *fondness toward*. Do not carry over the adjective's preposition: the adjective is *fond **of*** (*fond of jazz*, ✗ *fond for jazz*), and the noun is *fondness **for***, so the pair is a small but reliable trap. Note the gentle irony the word usually carries in American prose: *a fondness for expensive shoes* is nearly always a mild accusation. Compare [taste for/in](#taste-forin), which is close in meaning but splits by preposition, and [preference for](#preference-for), which is neutral.

A liking for something, usually mild and slightly indulgent.

1. He has a well-known *fondness for* diner coffee.
2. Her *fondness for* nineteenth-century sermons is hard to explain.
3. The judge showed no particular *fondness for* long opening statements.
4. I've developed a *fondness for* taking the long way home.
5. A *fondness for* the underdog runs in that family.
6. His *fondness for* the sound of his own voice was the whole problem.

**See also:** [taste for/in](#taste-forin), [preference for](#preference-for), [love for/of](#love-forof), [interest in](#interest-in), [fond of](15-dependent-adjectives.md#fond-of), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)


## G

### grasp of

**Pronunciation:** /ɡræsp ʌv/ &middot; *grasp uv* &middot; weak /ɡræsp əv/ → /ɡræsp ə/
**Русский:** понимание чего-л., владение предметом — книжнее, чем handle on
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or a *wh*-clause (*a firm grasp of what the job involves*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a that-clause**. A gerund is possible but unusual (*a grasp of doing the math in your head* reads oddly; prefer *a grasp of the arithmetic*).
**Pattern:** *a grasp of* [subject / situation] &middot; *have / get / lose one's grasp of* [noun phrase] &middot; *beyond / within someone's grasp* &middot; *a firm / shaky / tenuous grasp of* [noun phrase]
**Variants:** *a grasp on* — heard in American speech (*a good grasp on the material*), but *of* is the edited form; *a grip on* is the idiomatic alternative with *on*
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **for understanding,** ***within/beyond*** **for the physical or figurative reach.** *A grasp **of** the material* is comprehension; *within his grasp* is attainability — ✗ *a grasp to the material* is an error. The near-synonym *grip* prefers *on* (*a grip on reality*, *get a grip on the situation*), which is where *grasp on* comes from; both are acceptable in speech, but *grasp of* is what edited prose uses. The verb takes a direct object with no preposition: *he grasped the point*, ✗ *he grasped at the point* (which means something else — a desperate attempt, as in *grasping at straws*).

An understanding of a subject or situation.

1. She has a firm *grasp of* the underlying statistics.
2. His *grasp of* what the job actually involves is shaky at best.
3. You can't teach this without a solid *grasp of* the chemistry.
4. A tenure-track job seemed, for one week, within her *grasp*.
5. The report shows almost no *grasp of* how the permitting process works.
6. The concept is not beyond the *grasp* of a bright ninth grader.

**See also:** [understanding of](#understanding-of), [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), [insight into](#insight-into), [awareness of](#awareness-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### grounds for

**Pronunciation:** /ɡraʊndz fɔr/ &middot; *growndz for* &middot; weak /ɡraʊndz fɚ/
**Русский:** основания для — достаточная юридически или логически причина; обычно мн. ч.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*grounds for firing him*) — and separately a **to-infinitive with no preposition** (*grounds to believe he lied*). Also a **that-clause** in the fixed frame *on the grounds that*. ✗ *grounds for to fire him* mixes the two.
**Pattern:** *grounds for* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *grounds to* [verb] &middot; *on the grounds that* [clause] &middot; *on the grounds of* [noun phrase] &middot; *have grounds for* [noun phrase] &middot; *no grounds whatsoever*
**Variants:** *grounds* (plural) — the normal form in this sense, even with a singular determiner (*a single ground for appeal* is possible but stiff) &middot; *ground for* — occurs in legal writing (*ground for reversal*)
**Contrast:** **Two fixed phrases that get blended:** *on the grounds **that** the notice was late* takes a clause, and *on the grounds **of** procedural error* takes a noun — ✗ *on the grounds of that the notice was late* is the blend. ✗ *grounds of appeal* is British legal usage; American practice says *grounds for appeal*. The unrelated singular *ground* meaning earth, and the plural *grounds* meaning the land around a building or the sediment in coffee, take no preposition at all.

A legally or logically sufficient reason for doing something.

1. Repeated absence is *grounds for* dismissal under the contract.
2. The tenant has *grounds for* withholding rent until the heat is fixed.
3. We have *grounds to* believe the meter was misread.
4. The appeal was denied on the *grounds that* it was filed nine days late.
5. There are no *grounds for* reopening the investigation.
6. The permit was refused on the *grounds of* inadequate parking.

**See also:** [reason for](#reason-for), [evidence of/for](#evidence-offor), [excuse for](#excuse-for), [cause of](#cause-of), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### guarantee of

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɡɛrənˈti ʌv/ &middot; *gair-un-TEE uv* &middot; weak /ˌɡɛrənˈti əv/ → /ˌɡɛrənˈti ə/
**Русский:** гарантия чего-л. — твёрдое обещание, что нечто произойдёт или выдержит
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase or gerund (*no guarantee of getting a refund*) — **never a to-infinitive after *of*** (✗ *a guarantee of to get a refund*). The noun separately takes a **that-clause** (*a guarantee that the work will hold*) and, in commercial use, *against* (*a guarantee against defects*).
**Pattern:** *a guarantee of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a guarantee that* [clause] &middot; *a guarantee against* [defect / loss] &middot; *a guarantee on* [product] &middot; *under guarantee* &middot; *no guarantee*
**Variants:** *guaranty* — the same word in the narrow financial sense of a surety obligation; in ordinary American writing use *guarantee* for everything
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **for the outcome promised,** ***against*** **for the risk covered,** ***on*** **for the product.** *A guarantee **of** delivery by Friday*, *a guarantee **against** rust*, *a two-year guarantee **on** the compressor* — swapping these produces ✗ *a guarantee for rust*, which reads as though rust were promised. ✗ *a guarantee to the buyer* is wrong in the promise sense, though a guarantee can of course be given *to* a buyer. The verb takes a direct object, an indirect object, or a that-clause and no preposition: *we guarantee the work*, *I guarantee you it will hold*, ✗ *we guarantee for the work*.

A firm promise that something will happen or hold up.

1. A high LSAT score is no *guarantee of* admission anywhere.
2. The dealer offers a three-year *guarantee on* the transmission.
3. There's a written *guarantee against* corrosion for the first decade.
4. We got a *guarantee that* the crew would be out by the fifteenth.
5. The compressor is still under *guarantee*.
6. Paying early is no *guarantee of* getting your deposit back.

**See also:** [proof of](#proof-of), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [protection from/against](#protection-fromagainst), [confidence in](#confidence-in), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)


## H

### habit of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈhæbɪt ʌv/ &middot; *HAB-it uv* &middot; weak /ˈhæbɪt əv/ → /ˈhæbɪt ə/
**Русский:** привычка делать что-л. — устойчивое повторяющееся поведение
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of** + **gerund**, almost always (*the habit of checking twice*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the habit to check twice*). A plain noun after *of* is rare and reads as a compound instead (*a coffee habit*, not ✗ *a habit of coffee*).
**Pattern:** *the habit of* [verb-ing] &middot; *in the habit of* [verb-ing] &middot; *get into / out of the habit of* [verb-ing] &middot; *make a habit of* [verb-ing] &middot; *break the habit* &middot; *out of habit* &middot; *a habit of mind*
**Contrast:** **The infinitive is the error and it is nearly universal among learners:** ✗ *he has the habit to interrupt*, ✗ *I'm in the habit to walk after dinner*. Every frame here requires *-ing* — *the habit of interrupting*, *in the habit of walking*. ✗ *a habit to* has no standing in American English. Compare the adjectives, which do take the infinitive and thereby reinforce the confusion: *he is accustomed **to** walking* (preposition, *-ing*) but *he tends **to** walk* (infinitive). Note *out of habit* (mechanically) against *out of the habit of* (no longer doing it) — one word apart, opposite meanings.

A settled, repeated behavior.

1. She has the *habit of* rereading every email before sending it.
2. I'm not in the *habit of* lending tools to people I just met.
3. He got into the *habit of* leaving the radio on all night.
4. Don't make a *habit of* parking there.
5. I checked the mailbox out of *habit*, though nothing comes on Sundays.
6. Breaking the *habit of* checking his phone at dinner took a year.

**See also:** [tendency to/toward](#tendency-totoward), [taste for/in](#taste-forin), [fondness for](#fondness-for), [difficulty with/in](#difficulty-within), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### handle on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈhændəl ɑn/ &middot; *HAN-dul on* &middot; weak /ˈhændəl ən/
**Русский:** разг. представление о проблеме — «ухватить суть» настолько, чтобы справиться
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** **informal American English** — spoken, and fine in email and reporting, out of place in a brief or a scientific paper
**Complement:** a noun phrase or **gerund** (*a handle on managing the backlog*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a handle to manage the backlog*) and **never a clause**. The noun is nearly always inside *get* or *have a handle on*; a bare *the handle on the problem* is not idiomatic in this sense.
**Pattern:** *get / have a handle on* [a problem / the numbers / a situation] &middot; *no handle on* [noun phrase] &middot; *a good / firm / rough handle on* [noun phrase] &middot; *a handle* (a nickname or a screen name) &middot; *the handle on* [a door] (the literal object)
**Contrast:** ***On*** **for understanding,** ***of*** **for the physical grip's owner:** *a handle **on** the budget* is comprehension, *the handle **of** the pitcher* is the part you hold, and *the handle **on** the door* is also literal — context, not the preposition, separates the last two. Against [grasp of](#grasp-of), which is the same idea one register up: *a grasp **of** the material* is what you write in a recommendation letter, *a handle **on** the material* is what you say out loud. Compare [understanding of](#understanding-of), the neutral form. In American slang *a handle* with no complement is a name (*what's your handle?*) or, in basketball, ball-handling ability.

An informal grip on a problem — enough understanding to manage it.

1. Nobody has a *handle on* how much the repairs will actually run.
2. Give me a week and I'll have a *handle on* the backlog.
3. She's got a good *handle on* the county's permitting mess.
4. We have no *handle on* why the returns spiked in March.
5. He's finally getting a *handle on* managing three sites at once.
6. His Twitter *handle* is the same one he used on CB radio in 1979.

**See also:** [grasp of](#grasp-of), [understanding of](#understanding-of), [insight into](#insight-into), [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), [control over/of](#control-overof), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### hope for/of

**Pronunciation:** /hoʊp fɔr/, /hoʊp ʌv/ &middot; *hohp for, hohp uv* &middot; weak /hoʊp fɚ/, /hoʊp əv/ → /hoʊp ə/
**Русский:** надежда на что-л. — рус. «на», не «для»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase naming what is wanted (*hope for peace*). **of** + **gerund**: the outcome hoped for (*hope of finding survivors*) — **never a to-infinitive after either preposition** (✗ *no hope to survive*, ✗ *hope of to survive*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *the hope that it would blow over*.
**Pattern:** *hope for* [noun phrase] &middot; *hope of* [verb-ing] &middot; *the hope that* [clause] &middot; *in the hope of* [verb-ing] &middot; *in the hope that* [clause] &middot; *give up hope of* [verb-ing] &middot; *hopes for* [the future]
**Contrast:** ***For*** **takes a thing;** ***of*** **takes an outcome, always in the *-ing* form.** ✗ *no hope to find survivors* is the classic error; write *no hope **of** finding survivors*. The verb behaves in the opposite direction and that is precisely why the noun trips people: *hope **to** find survivors* is perfectly good English with an infinitive, as are *hope for the best* and *hope that it holds*. So the same idea splits by word class — verb *hope to do*, noun *hope of doing*. ✗ *hope in* survives only in religious registers (*hope in the Lord*).

A wish for a good outcome, and the expectation that it might come.

1. There's still *hope for* a negotiated settlement.
2. Crews gave up *hope of* finding anyone alive on the fourth day.
3. She applied in the *hope of* getting in-state tuition.
4. The *hope that* the market would recover by spring did not survive February.
