5. My *hopes for* the season died in the first quarter.
6. He has no realistic *hope of* making the payments.

**See also:** [desire for](#desire-for), [wish for](#wish-for), [chance of](#chance-of), [chance of](#chance-of), [hope for](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#hope-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)


## I

### idea of/for

**Pronunciation:** /aɪˈdiə ʌv/, /aɪˈdiə fɔr/ &middot; *eye-DEE-uh uv, eye-DEE-uh for* &middot; weak /aɪˈdiə əv/ → /aɪˈdiə ə/; /aɪˈdiə fɚ/
**Русский:** of — представление о чём-л.; for — идея, замысел чего-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: a concept — noun phrase or **gerund** (*the idea of moving*) — **never a to-infinitive after *of*** (✗ *the idea of to move*). **for**: a proposal (*an idea for a documentary*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause** (*the idea that markets self-correct*) and a *wh*-clause after *no idea* (*no idea what he meant*).
**Pattern:** *the idea of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *an idea for* [project] &middot; *the idea that* [clause] &middot; *have no idea* [wh-clause] &middot; *get the idea* &middot; *ideas about* [topic] &middot; *the idea behind* [thing]
**Variants:** *ideas about* — opinions on a topic (*ideas about parenting*), distinct from both *of* and *for*
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is the concept,** ***for*** **is the proposal, and the two are not close.** *The idea **of** a second bridge* is the notion itself; *an idea **for** a second bridge* is somebody's plan. ✗ *the idea to move* is an error in careful writing, though it is common in speech; write *the idea of moving*. The clause frame drops the preposition entirely — *the idea **that** markets self-correct*, ✗ *the idea of that markets self-correct* — and *no idea* takes a *wh*-clause with nothing in between: *I have no idea what he meant*, where *no idea of what he meant* is heavier but not wrong.

A concept, a proposal, or an opinion.

1. The *idea of* selling the house had never occurred to either of them.
2. She had a good *idea for* a documentary about the last drive-in.
3. The *idea that* the deed was lost in the 1911 fire is family legend.
4. I have no *idea* what the second key opens.
5. His *ideas about* discipline are twenty years out of date.
6. The whole *idea behind* the redesign was to cut one page from the form.

**See also:** [plan for](#plan-for), [opinion on/of/about](#opinion-onofabout), [view on/of](#view-onof), [insight into](#insight-into), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### immunity from

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈmjunəti frʌm/ &middot; *ih-MYOO-nuh-tee frum* &middot; weak /ɪˈmjunəti frəm/
**Русский:** юр. иммунитет от ответственности; мед. невосприимчивость к болезни
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** **from** is legal and formal — prosecutors' letters, diplomatic notes, civil-rights suits; **to** is medical and everyday
**Complement:** **from**: the legal process or consequence escaped, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*immunity from being sued*) — never an infinitive. **to**: a disease or agent (*immunity to measles*). **against**: an agent, in immunology (*immunity against the variant*). **Never a clause** after any of them.
**Pattern:** *immunity from* [prosecution / suit / liability] &middot; *immunity to* [a disease] &middot; *immunity against* [a pathogen] &middot; *grant / claim / waive immunity* &middot; *diplomatic / sovereign / qualified / transactional immunity* &middot; *herd immunity* (no complement)
**Contrast:** ***From*** **for the law,** ***to*** **for the body**, and swapping them is the error: ✗ *immunity to prosecution* and ✗ *immunity from measles* both sound wrong to an American ear, though the second is heard. *Immunity **against*** is immunology's own variant and is normal in a virology paper, odd in a newspaper. Against [exemption from](#exemption-from): an exemption releases you from a requirement, immunity shields you from a consequence. American terms to keep straight: *qualified immunity* protects officials from suit, *sovereign immunity* protects governments, *transactional immunity* protects a witness from prosecution for whatever he testifies about.

Protection from legal consequences, or the body's resistance to a disease.

1. He testified only after the U.S. Attorney granted him *immunity from* prosecution.
2. Most adults have lifelong *immunity to* chickenpox after one infection.
3. Qualified *immunity* shielded both officers from the civil suit.
4. The statute gives volunteers *immunity from* liability for ordinary negligence.
5. *Immunity against* the newer variant faded within about five months.
6. Diplomatic *immunity* covers the parking tickets, which is why there are so many.

**See also:** [exemption from](#exemption-from), [protection from/against](#protection-fromagainst), [waiver of](#waiver-of), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [right to/of/over](#right-toofover), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### impact on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɪmpækt ɑn/ &middot; *IM-pakt on* &middot; weak /ˈɪmpækt ən/
**Русский:** сильное воздействие на, влияние на — рус. «на», не «для»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase naming what is affected (*an impact on enrollment*). Gerunds are possible but heavy; **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an impact to reduce costs*) and **never a clause**. The full frame names both sides: *the impact of X on Y*.
**Pattern:** *an impact on* [thing affected] &middot; *the impact of* X *on* Y &middot; *have / make an impact on* [noun phrase] &middot; *the impact of* [event] &middot; *high-impact* &middot; *on impact*
**Variants:** *an impact upon* — formal, identical in meaning &middot; *an effect on* — the plainer synonym; see [effect on](#effect-on)
**Contrast:** ***On*** **is the preposition and *to* is corporate noise.** ✗ *the impact to the schedule* has spread through American business writing since about 2000; it is not standard, and editors change it to *the impact on the schedule*. ✗ *an impact for* is likewise wrong. *Of* names the cause, *on* names the target, exactly as with [effect on](#effect-on). Note that *impact* as a verb in the figurative sense (*the tariff impacted sales*) is disliked by many American editors, who prefer *affected*; the literal transitive verb (*an impacted tooth*, *the bullet impacted the wall*) is unobjectionable.

A strong effect on something.

1. The closure had an immediate *impact on* enrollment at both schools.
2. Nobody has measured the *impact of* the ordinance *on* small landlords.
3. Her testimony made a real *impact on* the committee.
4. The new rules will have almost no *impact on* rural carriers.
5. The environmental *impact of* the pipeline is still under review.
6. The airbag deployed on *impact*.

**See also:** [effect on](#effect-on), [influence on/over](#influence-onover), [result of](#result-of), [consequence of](#consequence-of), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### implication for

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɪmpləˈkeɪʃən fɔr/ &middot; *im-pluh-KAY-shun for* &middot; weak /ˌɪmpləˈkeɪʃən fɚ/
**Русский:** последствие для кого-л.; скрытый намёк; причастность к чему-л. (in)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** **for**: who or what is affected, as a noun phrase — **never a gerund** (✗ *implications for raising rates*; write *implications for the rate decision*) and never an infinitive. **that**: a bare that-clause, in the "unstated suggestion" sense (*the implication that he knew*). **in**: involvement in wrongdoing (*his implication in the scheme*).
**Pattern:** *implications for* [a group / a policy / a field] &middot; *the implication that* [clause] &middot; *implication in* [wrongdoing] &middot; *by implication* &middot; *far-reaching / troubling / practical implications* &middot; *the implications of* [an event] *for* [a group]
**Contrast:** **Three frames, three meanings.** *Implications **for** small landlords* are consequences; *the implication **that** he knew* is a hint; *his implication **in** the fraud* is involvement. The full consequence frame stacks both prepositions: *the implications **of** the ruling **for** small landlords* — and dropping the *of* leaves the sentence hanging. Note the pair every editor watches: a writer or a fact ***implies***, a reader ***infers***, so *the implication* is in the text and *the inference* is in the reader's head. ✗ *implications to the industry* is an error.

A consequence for someone, an unstated suggestion, or involvement in something.

1. The ruling has real *implications for* every month-to-month tenant in the state.
2. Nobody has thought through the *implications for* the pension fund.
3. The *implication that* she approved the transfer is not supported by a single email.
4. His *implication in* the bid-rigging came out three years later.
5. By *implication*, the memo concedes the deadline was missed.
6. A four-cent change has *implications for* margins nobody wants to discuss.

**See also:** [consequence of](#consequence-of), [effect on](#effect-on), [impact on](#impact-on), [bearing on](#bearing-on), [relevance to](#relevance-to), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### importance of/to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪmˈpɔrtəns ʌv/, /ɪmˈpɔrtəns tu/ &middot; *im-POR-tunss uv, im-POR-tunss too* &middot; weak /əmˈpɔrtəns əv/ → /ə/; /əmˈpɔrtəns tə/
**Русский:** важность чего-л. для кого-л., значимость
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: what is important — noun phrase or gerund (*the importance of showing up*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the importance to show up*). **to**: the person or thing it matters to. Both frequently appear at once: *the importance of sleep to recovery*.
**Pattern:** *the importance of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *of importance to* [person] &middot; *attach importance to* [noun phrase] &middot; *of little / no importance* &middot; *stress the importance of* [verb-ing]
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **names what matters;** ***to*** **names whom it matters to.** ✗ *the importance to sleep* is the classic slip, produced by reaching for an infinitive where a gerund belongs. Note the separate frame *attach importance **to** something*, where *to* introduces the thing valued rather than the person — so *he attaches great importance to punctuality* is correct and does not contradict the rule. The adjective is *important **to** someone* and *important **for** something* (*important for growth*), and there ✗ *important of* is wrong.

The quality of mattering, and to whom.

1. The report stresses the *importance of* early screening.
2. She never grasped the *importance of* showing up on time to a union meeting.
3. Water rights are of enormous *importance to* every rancher in the basin.
4. The board attaches great *importance to* the auditor's independence.
5. The *importance of* the find was not appreciated until the 1960s.
6. That distinction is of no *importance* here.

**See also:** [emphasis on/stress on](#emphasis-onstress-on), [attention to](#attention-to), [benefit of/to](#benefit-ofto), [point in/of](#point-inof), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### improvement in/on

**Pronunciation:** /ɪmˈpruvmənt ɪn/, /ɪmˈpruvmənt ɑn/ &middot; *im-PROOV-munt in, im-PROOV-munt on* &middot; weak /əmˈpruvmənt ən/
**Русский:** in — улучшение в чём-л.; on — усовершенствование по сравнению с прежним
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **in**: what is getting better — noun phrase or gerund (*an improvement in reporting*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an improvement to report faster*). **on / over**: the earlier version being beaten. **to**: a physical alteration made to a property (*improvements to the building*).
**Pattern:** *an improvement in* [thing improving] &middot; *an improvement on / over* [earlier version] &middot; *improvements to* [property] &middot; *show / see an improvement in* [noun phrase] &middot; *room for improvement*
**Variants:** *an improvement on* — the standard comparative form &middot; *an improvement over* — equally American and slightly commoner in technical writing
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions and three claims.** *An improvement **in** air quality* says the air is better than it was; *an improvement **on** the 2019 model* says this one beats that one; *improvements **to** the building* are renovations. ✗ *an improvement of air quality* is not idiomatic in the change sense, though *the improvement of public health* is fine as a formal abstract noun. The verb takes a direct object with no preposition (*improve the design*) or stands alone (*she's improving*), and *improve on the design* is not an error but a different claim: it means "do better than it," not "make it better" — a real and easily missed difference.

A change for the better, or a version that beats an earlier one.

1. There's been a measurable *improvement in* air quality since the plant closed.
2. The second edition is a real *improvement on* the first.
3. Her handwriting shows some *improvement*, mostly in the numerals.
4. The lease requires the tenant to pay for any *improvements to* the storefront.
5. This engine is a clear *improvement over* the one it replaced.
6. There's still room for *improvement in* how we handle refunds.

**See also:** [increase in/decrease in](#increase-indecrease-in), [progress in/on](#progress-inon), [change in/of](#change-inof), [advantage of/over](#advantage-ofover), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### incidence of/prevalence of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɪnsədəns ʌv/, /ˈprɛvələns ʌv/ &middot; *IN-suh-dunss uv, PREV-uh-lunss uv* &middot; weak /ˈɪnsədəns əv/ → /ˈɪnsədəns ə/
**Русский:** заболеваемость (новые случаи) и распространённость (все случаи разом)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** technical — epidemiology, public health, criminology — and their reporting; both words are singular mass nouns
**Complement:** **of**: the condition or event counted, as a noun phrase — **never a gerund** (✗ *the incidence of smoking cigarettes* is odd; write *the incidence of smoking*), **never an infinitive**, and **never a clause**. **in / among**: the population (*prevalence among adolescents*, *incidence in the exposed group*).
**Pattern:** *the incidence of* [a disease / an event] &middot; *the prevalence of* [a condition] &middot; *incidence in / among* [a population] &middot; *a high / low / rising incidence* &middot; *per 100,000* &middot; *incidence rate*
**Contrast:** **The technical split is the point:** ***incidence*** **counts new cases over a period,** ***prevalence*** **counts existing cases at a moment.** A long-lasting disease has low incidence and high prevalence; a short one, the reverse — so ✗ *the incidence of diabetes in the county is nine percent* misuses the word, since a percentage of the living population is prevalence. And the spelling trap: ***incidence*** **is a rate,** ***incidents*** **are events.** ✗ *the incidents of theft rose* means individual thefts rose, which may be what you meant, but *the incidence of theft rose* is the statistic. Both words are uncountable in this sense: ✗ *two incidences*.

How often a condition occurs — either newly, over a period, or in total, at a moment.

1. The *incidence of* Lyme disease in the county has tripled since 2010.
2. *Prevalence of* untreated hypertension is highest in the two rural districts.
3. Researchers compared the *incidence of* infection in vaccinated and unvaccinated groups.
4. The *prevalence of* asthma among children near the interstate is the study's finding.
5. A rising *incidence of* fraud complaints prompted the audit.
6. Note that *incidence* is a rate and *incidents* are the things that happened.

**See also:** [probability of/likelihood of](#probability-oflikelihood-of), [ratio of ... to](#ratio-of--to), [rise in/fall in](#rise-infall-in), [correlation with/between](#correlation-withbetween), [exposure to](#exposure-to), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### increase in/decrease in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɪnkris ɪn/, /ˈdikris ɪn/ &middot; *IN-kreess in, DEE-kreess in* &middot; weak /ˈɪnkris ən/, /ˈdikris ən/
**Русский:** рост чего-л. / снижение чего-л. — рус. родительный, не «в»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **in**: the thing that grew or shrank (*an increase in premiums*). **of**: the size of the change (*an increase of nine percent*). **to**: the endpoint reached (*an increase to $18 an hour*). Gerunds follow *in* (*a decrease in reporting*); **never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *an increase / decrease in* [thing measured] &middot; *an increase of* [amount] &middot; *an increase to* [new level] &middot; *an increase from* X *to* Y &middot; *on the increase* &middot; *a* [percentage] *increase in* [noun phrase]
**Variants:** *a rise in* and *a fall in* behave identically; see [rise in/fall in](#rise-infall-in) &middot; *a decline in* is the formal synonym of *a decrease in* &middot; *a raise* in American English is a pay increase, where British English says *a rise*
**Contrast:** ***In*** **for what changed,** ***of*** **for how much, and this is the error people actually make.** ✗ *an increase of premiums* says the premiums themselves were increased in number; you want *an increase **in** premiums*. Conversely ✗ *an increase in nine percent* is wrong for the size. All four can stack: *an increase **in** premiums **of** nine percent, **from** $200 **to** $218*. ✗ *an increase on premiums* is an error, though *a tax on premiums* is fine — see [tax on](#tax-on). The verb takes no preposition when transitive (*they increased the fee*) and *by* for the amount (*increased by nine percent*).

A rise or fall in an amount, and the size of it.

1. There's been a sharp *increase in* emergency room visits since June.
2. The board approved an *increase of* four percent for all classified staff.
3. We saw a steady *decrease in* complaints after the new signage went up.
4. The fee goes up by an *increase to* $45 effective in March.
5. Enrollment showed an *increase from* 812 *to* 940 in two years.
6. Bicycle theft is on the *increase* downtown.

**See also:** [rise in/fall in](#rise-infall-in), [improvement in/on](#improvement-inon), [change in/of](#change-inof), [demand for](#demand-for), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### influence on/over

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɪnfluəns ɑn/, /ˈɪnfluəns ˈoʊvɚ/ &middot; *IN-floo-unss on, IN-floo-unss OH-ver* &middot; weak /ˈɪnfluəns ən/; *over* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** влияние на что-л., власть над кем-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun; a *wh*-clause follows *on* in the frame *influence on what gets published*. **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *influence to change the vote*) and **never a that-clause**.
**Pattern:** *an influence on* [outcome / person] &middot; *influence over* [person / process] &middot; *the influence of* X *on* Y &middot; *under the influence of* [noun phrase] &middot; *exert / have influence on* [noun phrase] &middot; *a bad influence on* [person]
**Variants:** *influence on* — the effect produced &middot; *influence over* — the power held, always with a person or process as complement &middot; *influence with* — standing that lets you get things done (*she has influence with the zoning board*)
**Contrast:** ***On*** **is an effect;** ***over*** **is power;** ***with*** **is access.** *His influence **on** the design* changed how it looks; *his influence **over** the committee* means he controls it; *his influence **with** the committee* means they listen to him. ✗ *influence to* and ✗ *influence for* are errors. *Under the influence* on its own is an American legal idiom for intoxication and takes no complement. The verb takes a direct object with no preposition: *the weather influenced the decision*, ✗ *influenced on the decision*.

Power to affect how something turns out, or the effect itself.

1. Her thesis adviser had an enormous *influence on* the way she writes.
2. The family retains real *influence over* the endowment's investments.
3. The *influence of* Dutch settlement *on* local place names is still visible.
4. He was arrested for driving under the *influence*.
5. That neighbor has been a bad *influence on* both of my kids.
6. She has some *influence with* the county commissioners.

**See also:** [effect on](#effect-on), [impact on](#impact-on), [control over/of](#control-overof), [authority on/over](#authority-onover), [over](10-topic-reference-role.md#over)

### information about/on

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɪnfɚˈmeɪʃən əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *in-fer-MAY-shun uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ˌɪnfɚˈmeɪʃən ˈbaʊt/, /ˌɪnfɚˈmeɪʃən ən/
**Русский:** информация о чём-л., сведения по — в англ. неисчисляемо (✗ informations)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (*information about applying for aid*), or *wh*-clause (*information on how to appeal*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *information to apply for aid*). It does take a bare **that-clause** in formal use: *information that the shipment had been diverted*.
**Pattern:** *information about / on* [topic] &middot; *information from* [source] &middot; *information for* [audience] &middot; *for your information* &middot; *a piece of information* &middot; *withhold / disclose information about* [noun phrase]
**Variants:** *information on* — a subject formally treated (*information on the vaccine*) &middot; *information about* — the neutral conversational form &middot; *information regarding* — bureaucratic
**Contrast:** **The countability rule is the whole trap.** *Information* is uncountable in American English: ✗ *an information*, ✗ *informations*, ✗ *many informations*, ✗ *these informations*. Use *a piece of information*, *some information*, *much information*, *a lot of information*. Speakers of French, Spanish, Italian, and German all have a countable equivalent, which is why this error is so durable. On prepositions, ✗ *information of the delay* is an error (though *notification of the delay* is correct), and *for* names the audience rather than the subject.

Facts provided or learned about something.

1. There's very little *information about* the site's earlier owners.
2. The website has *information on* how to appeal a denial.
3. I got that *information from* the county recorder's office.
4. This packet is *information for* new parents, not a legal document.
5. One useful piece of *information* came out of the whole meeting.
6. The agency withheld *information about* the second inspection.

**See also:** [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), [advice on/about](#advice-onabout), [report on/of](#report-onof), [evidence of/for](#evidence-offor), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### injunction against

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈdʒʌŋkʃən əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *in-JUNK-shun uh-GENST* &middot; weak *against* has **no weak form**; the noun keeps full stress on *-JUNK-*
**Русский:** юр. судебный запрет — предписание суда не делать чего-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal — motions, orders, and the news coverage of them; an American meets the word when a court blocks a law, a strike, or a merger
**Complement:** **against**: the person or act enjoined, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*an injunction against publishing the file*). **to**: a bare infinitive naming what the order compels (*an injunction to restore service*), which is the mandatory rather than the prohibitory kind. **Never a that-clause.** The archaic non-legal sense ("a solemn instruction") also takes *to* + infinitive.
**Pattern:** *an injunction against* [a person / an act] &middot; *an injunction to* [verb] &middot; *seek / obtain / dissolve / violate an injunction* &middot; *a preliminary / permanent injunction* &middot; *a temporary restraining order* &middot; *enjoin* [someone] *from* [verb-ing] (the verb)
**Contrast:** ***Against*** **stops something;** ***to*** **plus a verb starts something.** *An injunction **against** the demolition* forbids; *an injunction **to** reconnect the water* commands. ✗ *an injunction to demolish* would order the opposite of what the plaintiff wanted, so the slip is expensive. Note the verb, which takes a different preposition entirely: *the court **enjoined** the county **from** demolishing the building* — *enjoin from* + *-ing*, never ✗ *enjoin to stop*. Against [prohibition on/against](#prohibition-onagainst): a prohibition is a standing rule, an injunction is a court order aimed at named parties.

A court order forbidding or compelling a specific act.

1. The union sought an *injunction against* the lockout and got it in a day.
2. A federal judge issued an *injunction against* enforcing the ordinance.
3. The *injunction against* publishing the documents lasted nine days.
4. She violated the *injunction* twice and was held in contempt.
5. The order is an *injunction to* restore service, not merely to stop cutting it.
6. Plaintiffs asked for a preliminary *injunction against* the merger closing.

**See also:** [prohibition on/against](#prohibition-onagainst), [moratorium on](#moratorium-on), [judgment against/for](#judgment-againstfor), [remedy for/against](#remedy-foragainst), [jurisdiction over](#jurisdiction-over), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### inquiry into

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈkwaɪri ˈɪntu/, /ˈɪnkwəri ˈɪntu/ &middot; *in-KWY-ree IN-too, IN-kwuh-ree IN-too* &middot; weak /ˈɪntə/ &middot; both stress patterns are standard American; *IN-kwuh-ree* is commoner in academic speech
**Русский:** расследование чего-л.; также запрос, наведение справок о чём-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** **into**: the subject investigated, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*an inquiry into how the money moved* takes a *wh*-clause; *an inquiry into hiring practices* takes a gerund) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an inquiry to find the cause*, unless *to find* is a purpose clause). **about / regarding**: the simple-question sense (*an inquiry about the vacancy*). **from**: who is asking.
**Pattern:** *an inquiry into* [a subject] &middot; *an inquiry about* [a detail] &middot; *inquiries from* [customers / reporters] &middot; *open / launch / conduct / close an inquiry* &middot; *a line of inquiry* &middot; *a congressional / internal inquiry*
**Variants:** American English spells it *inquiry* in both senses; *enquiry* is British and, where the distinction is kept there, is reserved for the ordinary question. Do not import the split into American copy.
**Contrast:** ***Into*** **for an investigation,** ***about*** **for a question.** *An inquiry **into** the crash* is a formal proceeding; *an inquiry **about** the flight* is a phone call. ✗ *an inquiry on the crash* is an error. Against [investigation into](#investigation-into), which is nearly a synonym: an inquiry is the more neutral and more official-sounding word, which is why bodies that do not want to accuse anyone open an *inquiry* rather than an *investigation*. Note the American office phrase *for further inquiries, contact* — plural, and only in the question sense.

A formal investigation, or a request for information.

1. The board opened an *inquiry into* how the contract was awarded.
2. She had one *inquiry about* the apartment and it came from a bot.
3. A congressional *inquiry into* the outage is scheduled for June.
4. That line of *inquiry* went nowhere in the first two interviews.
5. The paper's *inquiry into* the county's overtime billing ran for a year.
6. All press *inquiries* go through the communications office.

**See also:** [investigation into](#investigation-into), [research on/into](#research-oninto), [report on/of](#report-onof), [information about/on](#information-abouton), [insight into](#insight-into), [into](01-core-in-on-at.md#into)

### insight into

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɪnsaɪt ˈɪntu/ &middot; *IN-syte IN-too* &middot; weak /ˈɪnsaɪt ˈɪntə/
**Русский:** понимание сути чего-л., проникновение в суть сложного
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (*insight into running a small hospital*), or a *wh*-clause (*insight into how the fund actually works*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *insight to run a hospital*) and **never a that-clause**.
**Pattern:** *insight into* [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] &middot; *gain / offer / provide insight into* [noun phrase] &middot; *a valuable insight into* [noun phrase] &middot; *with the insight of* [experience]
**Variants:** *insights into* (plural) — the commoner form in business and academic writing; the singular is more often abstract (*a person of great insight*)
**Contrast:** ***Into*** **is fixed** — ✗ *insight in*, ✗ *insight on*, ✗ *insight about*, ✗ *insight of*. The last of these is the trap for anyone who has learned [understanding of](#understanding-of), [grasp of](#grasp-of), and [awareness of](#awareness-of), all of which take *of*: *insight* is the odd one in that set. Note the second, uncountable sense meaning perceptiveness (*a woman of considerable insight*), which takes no complement at all, and the clinical sense in psychiatry (*the patient lacks insight*), likewise bare.

A clear or penetrating understanding of something complicated.

1. The diaries give real *insight into* how the shop was run.
2. Her time on the ward gave her *insight into* what the families actually need.
3. The audit offered no *insight into* where the money went.
4. That's a useful *insight into* the way the committee makes decisions.
5. Twenty years of teaching gave him *insight into* how children fake reading.
6. The book's *insights into* small-town politics are its best feature.

**See also:** [understanding of](#understanding-of), [grasp of](#grasp-of), [awareness of](#awareness-of), [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), [into](01-core-in-on-at.md#into)

### interest in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɪntrəst ɪn/ &middot; *IN-trust in* &middot; weak /ˈɪntrəst ən/
**Русский:** интерес к чему-л.; доля, участие в чём-л.; ср. interest on — проценты по кредиту
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*an interest in restoring old radios*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an interest to restore old radios*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *an interest that the radios be restored*). A *wh*-clause is fine (*an interest in what happens to the building*). The financial sense takes *on*: *interest on the loan*.
**Pattern:** *an interest in* [subject / verb-ing] &middot; *interest on* [a loan / a balance] &middot; *an interest in* [a company] (a stake) &middot; *in the interest(s) of* [noun phrase] &middot; *of interest to* [person] &middot; *take an interest in* [noun phrase] &middot; *a conflict of interest*
**Variants:** *in the interest of* and *in the interests of* — both standard American English; the singular is commoner in *in the interest of time* &middot; ***interest on*** is not a separate headword but the same noun in its financial frame: *interest **on** the loan*, *interest **on** unpaid balances*, *accrue / pay / earn interest **on*** [an amount] — and it is the one frame of the three where ✗ *interest of* is flatly wrong
**Contrast:** ***In*** **for curiosity or a stake;** ***on*** **for money.** *Her interest **in** the bank* is either her curiosity about it or her ownership share; *the interest **on** the loan* is what it costs to borrow — and ✗ *the interest of the loan* is an error. ✗ *an interest for photography* and ✗ *an interest about photography* are errors as well. Note that *of interest to* reverses the direction: *the file is of interest to investigators* means they are interested in it. The adjective pair is the notorious one — *interested **in*** something, but *interesting **to*** someone — and ✗ *I am interested to buy* should be *interested in buying* unless you mean curiosity about a result (*interested to hear how it went*).

Curiosity about something, a financial stake, or money paid for borrowing.

1. She's had an *interest in* nineteenth-century glass since college.
2. The *interest on* the second mortgage is what's killing them.
3. He holds a twelve percent *interest in* the brewery.
4. In the *interest of* time, let's take the rest by email.
5. The recordings are of considerable *interest to* linguists.
6. Nobody on the board took the slightest *interest in* the audit.

**See also:** [fondness for](#fondness-for), [focus on](#focus-on), [concern about/for](#concern-aboutfor), [share in/of](#share-inof), [interested in](15-dependent-adjectives.md#interested-in), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### interview with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɪntɚvju wɪθ/ &middot; *IN-ter-vyoo with* &middot; weak /ˈɪntɚvju wəθ/
**Русский:** интервью с кем-л.; также собеседование с кандидатом
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **with**: the other person, in either role (*an interview with the mayor*). **for**: the job or program sought. **about / on**: the subject. **Never a gerund after *with***, **never an infinitive**, **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *an interview with* [person] &middot; *an interview for* [job / program] &middot; *an interview about / on* [topic] &middot; *give / grant / conduct an interview* &middot; *in an interview with* [outlet] &middot; *a job interview*
**Contrast:** ***With*** **names the other party regardless of who is asking:** *an interview **with** the mayor* is the same event whether the mayor sat down with a reporter or with a hiring panel. ✗ *an interview to the mayor* is an error, as is ✗ *an interview of the mayor* in ordinary usage, though *an interview of forty candidates* is possible in formal survey writing. *For* names the position: *an interview **for** the assistant director job*, ✗ *an interview of the job*. The verb takes a **direct object and no preposition** — *she interviewed the mayor*, ✗ *she interviewed with the mayor* if she was the reporter — but American English does use *interview **with*** for the candidate's side: *I interviewed with three firms*.

A formal conversation, for a story or for a job.

1. In an *interview with* the local paper, she refused to rule it out.
2. I have an *interview for* the assistant curator position on Thursday.
3. The podcast is a two-hour *interview with* the man who built the bridge.
4. He gave one *interview about* the settlement and never spoke of it again.
5. The *interview with* the arresting officer was never recorded.
6. She interviewed *with* four firms and got three offers.

**See also:** [encounter with](#encounter-with), [discussion about/of](#discussion-aboutof), [reference to](#reference-to), [request for](#request-for), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### introduction to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɪntrəˈdʌkʃən tu/ &middot; *in-truh-DUK-shun too* &middot; weak /ˌɪntrəˈdʌkʃən tə/
**Русский:** знакомство с чем-л., введение в предмет; также внедрение нового
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **to**: a subject or a person — noun phrase, or a **gerund** in the subject sense (*an introduction to keeping bees*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an introduction to keep bees*). **of**: the thing or person being brought in (*the introduction of the new system*).
**Pattern:** *an introduction to* [subject / person] &middot; *the introduction of* [thing brought in] &middot; *a letter of introduction* &middot; *by way of introduction* &middot; *give* [someone] *an introduction to* [subject]
**Contrast:** ***To*** **for what you are being introduced to;** ***of*** **for what is being introduced.** *An introduction **to** statistics* is a first course; *the introduction **of** statistics into the curriculum* is the act of adding it. ✗ *an introduction of statistics* in the course sense is a common and confusing error. The *-ing* rule applies to the *to* frame because a noun fits the slot: *an introduction to **keeping** bees*, ✗ *an introduction to keep bees*. The verb takes both: *introduce someone **to** someone*, *introduce a bill*, and ✗ *introduce someone with someone* is an error.

A first exposure to a subject or a person, or the act of bringing something in.

1. The course is a gentle *introduction to* medieval Latin.
2. That summer was my *introduction to* commercial fishing.
3. She wrote him a letter of *introduction to* the archivist in Madrid.
4. The *introduction of* electronic filing cut the backlog in half.
5. This book is a decent *introduction to* keeping bees in a cold climate.
6. By way of *introduction*, I should say I have no legal training.

**See also:** [access to](#access-to), [reference to](#reference-to), [approach to](#approach-to), [experience of/in/with](#experience-ofinwith), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### investigation into

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˌvɛstəˈɡeɪʃən ˈɪntu/ &middot; *in-ves-tuh-GAY-shun IN-too* &middot; weak /ɪnˌvɛstəˈɡeɪʃən ˈɪntə/
**Русский:** расследование чего-л. — рус. родительный, не «в»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **into / of**: the subject or target, as a noun phrase, gerund, or *wh*-clause (*an investigation into how the fire started*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an investigation to determine the cause* as a complement, though that is fine as a purpose clause). **for**: the suspected offense, in *under investigation for*. **by**: the investigating body.
**Pattern:** *an investigation into* [an event / a practice] &middot; *an investigation of* [a person / a matter] &middot; *under investigation for* [an offense] &middot; *open / launch / drop / close an investigation* &middot; *a criminal / internal / independent investigation* &middot; *the subject of an investigation*
**Variants:** *investigation into* and *investigation of* are both standard American English: *into* emphasizes the probing of a question, *of* the examination of an object or a person. *An investigation **of** Senator Ruiz* points at a person; *an investigation **into** the contracts* points at a question — and journalists prefer *into* precisely because it accuses no one.
**Contrast:** ✗ ***An investigation on*** is an error, and it is the commonest one from Spanish and Portuguese speakers. Against [inquiry into](#inquiry-into): an investigation implies suspected wrongdoing, an inquiry need not. Against [research on/into](#research-oninto): research seeks knowledge, an investigation seeks a culprit or a cause — which is why *research into* the cause of a fire and an *investigation into* the same fire are done by different people.

An official effort to find out how something happened or who is responsible.

1. The NTSB opened an *investigation into* the derailment within hours.
2. An internal *investigation of* the sergeant's conduct is still open.
3. He has been *under investigation for* wire fraud since last spring.
4. The *investigation into* how the ballots were stored took four months.
5. Two reporters spent a year on an *investigation into* county overtime.
6. She was told the *investigation* had been closed without findings.

**See also:** [inquiry into](#inquiry-into), [research on/into](#research-oninto), [report on/of](#report-onof), [evidence of/for](#evidence-offor), [judgment against/for](#judgment-againstfor), [into](01-core-in-on-at.md#into)

### invitation to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɪnvɪˈteɪʃən tu/ &middot; *in-vih-TAY-shun too* &middot; weak /ˌɪnvɪˈteɪʃən tə/
**Русский:** приглашение на (мероприятие) или к кому-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **to** + noun phrase: the event (*an invitation to the wedding*). **to** + **bare infinitive**: the act invited (*an invitation to speak*). Both are standard, and the *to* is a preposition in the first and an infinitive marker in the second. **for**: a date or an occasion (*an invitation for Friday*). ✗ *an invitation to speaking*.
**Pattern:** *an invitation to* [event] &middot; *an invitation to* [verb] &middot; *an invitation from* [host] &middot; *an invitation for* [date] &middot; *accept / decline / extend an invitation* &middot; *at the invitation of* [person] &middot; *by invitation only*
**Contrast:** **This is the one noun in the file where *to* + noun and *to* + infinitive are both right, and the meanings differ.** *An invitation **to** the conference* is a summons to attend; *an invitation **to** speak at the conference* is a request to perform. Neither takes *-ing*: ✗ *an invitation to speaking*. ✗ *an invitation for the wedding* is an error where *an invitation for Friday* is fine, because *for* takes a time, not an event. The verb keeps both frames: *invite her **to** the wedding*, *invite her **to** speak*, and ✗ *invite her for speaking* is wrong.

A request that someone attend or take part.

1. The *invitation to* the wedding came six weeks late.
2. She turned down an *invitation to* testify before the subcommittee.
3. There's an *invitation from* the dean sitting in your inbox.
4. We got an *invitation for* the Saturday session instead.
5. He was there at the *invitation of* the tribal council.
6. The reception is by *invitation* only.

**See also:** [request for](#request-for), [demand for](#demand-for), [order for](#order-for), [permission for](#permission-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)


## J

### judgment against/for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdʒʌdʒmənt əˈɡɛnst/, /ˈdʒʌdʒmənt fɔr/ &middot; *JUJ-munt uh-GENST, JUJ-munt for* &middot; weak /ˈdʒʌdʒmənt fɚ/; *against* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** юр. решение суда против кого-л. / в чью-л. пользу; также суждение, рассудительность
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal in the *against/for* frames; neutral in the everyday sense of an opinion
**Complement:** **against / for**: the losing and winning party, as a noun phrase. **on**: the motion or claim decided (*judgment on the pleadings*). **of**: the court that entered it. **about / on**: what an opinion is about, in the everyday sense, where a **gerund** works (*a judgment about hiring him*). **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a judgment to hire him*) and, in the legal frames, **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a judgment against* [a defendant] &middot; *judgment for* [a plaintiff] &middot; *judgment on* [the pleadings / the merits] &middot; *enter / vacate / satisfy / appeal a judgment* &middot; *a default / summary / money judgment* &middot; *in my judgment* &middot; *against my better judgment*
**Variants:** the American spelling is ***judgment***, without the *e*, in both the legal and the everyday sense; *judgement* is British and, in an American brief, reads as a typo
**Contrast:** ***Against*** **is who pays,** ***for*** **is who collects:** *a judgment **against** the contractor* and *judgment **for** the homeowner* describe one piece of paper from two sides. ✗ *a judgment to the plaintiff* is an error. In the everyday sense the prepositions change completely — *a judgment **about** his character*, *in my judgment* — and *judgment* there is uncountable in some frames (*poor judgment*, ✗ *a poor judgment*). Note *judgment call*, an American fixed compound, and *against my better judgment*, which needs no complement at all.

A court's final decision in a case, or, in ordinary use, an opinion or the capacity to form one.

1. The bank has a *judgment against* him for $18,000 and it follows him.
2. The court entered *judgment for* the plaintiff on the second count only.
3. The motion asks for *judgment on* the pleadings before discovery starts.
4. She has good *judgment* about people and terrible judgment about cars.
5. Against my better *judgment*, I co-signed the lease.
6. A default *judgment against* the tenant issued when nobody appeared.

**See also:** [damages for](#damages-for), [claim against/for/to](#claim-againstforto), [injunction against](#injunction-against), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [default on/under](#default-onunder), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### jurisdiction over

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdʒʊrɪsˈdɪkʃən ˈoʊvɚ/ &middot; *joor-iss-DIK-shun OH-ver* &middot; *over* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** юрисдикция над, подсудность — правомочие суда или ведомства и его пределы
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal and governmental; also ordinary speech about which agency handles what
**Complement:** **over**: the person, place, subject, or dispute, as a noun phrase — **never a gerund** (✗ *jurisdiction over deciding the fee*), **never an infinitive**, and **never a clause**. **in**: a place or a field (*jurisdiction in admiralty*, *no jurisdiction in Ohio*). **to**: a bare infinitive, in the "power to act" frame (*jurisdiction to hear the appeal*).
**Pattern:** *jurisdiction over* [a person / a case / a territory] &middot; *jurisdiction to* [verb] &middot; *within / outside the jurisdiction of* [a body] &middot; *have / lack / decline / assert jurisdiction* &middot; *personal / subject-matter / original / appellate jurisdiction* &middot; *concurrent jurisdiction*
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Jurisdiction on*** **is an error** and a frequent one; the noun takes *over*, matching [authority on/over](#authority-onover) in its *over* sense and [control over/of](#control-overof). Note the two *to*'s again: *jurisdiction **to** hear the case* takes a bare verb, and there is no noun-phrase version of it — ✗ *jurisdiction to the case*. The word is also a countable noun meaning a place (*forty-two jurisdictions have adopted the rule*), and in that sense it takes no preposition at all.

A court's or an agency's legal power to act, and the reach of that power.

1. The federal court has no *jurisdiction over* a purely local zoning dispute.
2. Whether the sheriff has *jurisdiction over* the campus was the whole argument.
3. The agency asserted *jurisdiction over* wetlands connected to navigable water.
4. The panel lacks *jurisdiction to* hear an interlocutory appeal here.
5. Forty-two *jurisdictions* have adopted some version of the model act.
6. That building sits outside the *jurisdiction of* the city fire marshal.

**See also:** [authority on/over](#authority-onover), [control over/of](#control-overof), [custody of](#custody-of), [injunction against](#injunction-against), [applicability to](#applicability-to), [over](10-topic-reference-role.md#over)

### justification for

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdʒʌstəfəˈkeɪʃən fɔr/ &middot; *jus-tuh-fuh-KAY-shun for* &middot; weak /ˌdʒʌstəfəˈkeɪʃən fɚ/
**Русский:** оправдание чего-л., обоснование правильности или необходимости
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*no justification for raising the fee*) — the edited frame. A **to-infinitive** occurs (*no justification to raise the fee*) and is common in speech and business writing, but *for* + *-ing* is the form that never draws a mark. A bare **that-clause** attaches to the noun as well: *the justification that the roof was leaking*.
**Pattern:** *justification for* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *no justification for* [noun phrase] &middot; *offer / provide / demand a justification* &middot; *in justification of* [an act] (formal) &middot; *without justification*
**Contrast:** **The verb takes a gerund and refuses the infinitive outright:** *nothing justifies **raising** the fee*, ✗ *nothing justifies to raise the fee* — a harder rule than the noun's, and the reason writers who get the verb right still stumble on the noun. Against [reason for](#reason-for): a reason explains why something happened, a justification argues that it was right — which is why *the reason for the delay* is neutral and *the justification for the delay* implies somebody is being blamed. ✗ *justification of the fee increase* means the act of justifying it, not the argument.

An argument that something was right or necessary.

1. There's no *justification for* charging tenants for a repair the landlord caused.
2. The memo offers three *justifications for* the reorganization and believes none of them.
3. What was the *justification for* holding the vote without notice?
4. He fired her without *justification* and the arbitrator said so.
5. The only *justification for* keeping the second office is the lease.
6. In *justification of* the decision, the chair cited a rule that does not exist.

**See also:** [reason for](#reason-for), [grounds for](#grounds-for), [basis for](#basis-for), [rationale for](#rationale-for), [excuse for](#excuse-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)


## K

### key to

**Pronunciation:** /ki tu/ &middot; *kee too* &middot; weak /ki tə/
**Русский:** ключ к — то, что даёт разгадку или делает что-л. возможным
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*the key to solving it*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the key to solve it*). This is one of the most frequently botched *-ing* patterns in American business and self-help writing.
**Pattern:** *the key to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a key to* [lock / door] &middot; *hold the key to* [noun phrase] &middot; *the key to success* &middot; *key* [adjective] *to* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** **Because a noun fits the slot — *the key to the door*, *the key to success* — the *to* is a preposition, and a verb after it must take *-ing*:** *the key to **getting** funded*, ✗ *the key to get funded*. The adjective *key* uses the same preposition and is likewise fixed: *a factor key **to** the outcome*, ✗ *key for the outcome* in that sense, though *key for our purposes* is fine. ✗ *the key of success* is an error; *the key of C* is music, a different noun. Compare [solution to](#solution-to) and [answer to](#answer-to), which share the preposition and the trap.

The thing that makes something possible or explains it.

1. The *key to* getting a permit here is starting in January.
2. Patience is the *key to* not ruining a brisket.
3. The county clerk holds the *key to* the whole dispute.
4. The *key to* the back gate is under the third flowerpot.
5. Consistent watering is the *key to* keeping tomatoes from splitting.
6. Nobody has found the *key to* why the ledgers stop in 1913.

**See also:** [solution to](#solution-to), [answer to](#answer-to), [reason for](#reason-for), [access to](#access-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### knowledge of/about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈnɑlɪdʒ ʌv/ &middot; *NOL-ij uv* &middot; weak /ˈnɑlɪdʒ əv/ → /ˈnɑlɪdʒ ə/
**Русский:** знание чего-л., знания о чём-л. — в англ. неисчисляемо
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase, gerund (*knowledge of having been passed over*), or *wh*-clause (*no knowledge of what the account was for*) — **never a to-infinitive**. The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *knowledge that the tank was leaking*.
**Pattern:** *knowledge of* [subject] &middot; *knowledge about* [subject] &middot; *knowledge that* [clause] &middot; *a working knowledge of* [skill] &middot; *to the best of my knowledge* &middot; *without someone's knowledge* &middot; *common knowledge*
**Variants:** *knowledge of* — the edited default &middot; *knowledge about* — conversational, and normal when the subject is a person or an event rather than a skill
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is the form to write.** ✗ *knowledge on the subject* is an error; ✗ *knowledges* does not exist in American English, which treats the noun as uncountable — say *a body of knowledge*, *a working knowledge of Spanish*, ✗ *he has good knowledges*. **The one indefinite article that is allowed** is in the pattern *a working / thorough / detailed knowledge of X*, where the adjective licenses it. Distinguish from [awareness of](#awareness-of) (perception that something exists) and [understanding of](#understanding-of) (grasp of how it works); *knowledge* is the possession of facts.

Facts, information, and skills a person has.

1. She has a working *knowledge of* Portuguese and fluent Spanish.
2. His *knowledge of* the building's wiring saved us three days.
3. The lease was signed without my *knowledge*.
4. To the best of my *knowledge*, nobody has lived there since 2011.
5. *Knowledge about* the outbreak spread faster than the outbreak.
6. *Knowledge that* the tank was leaking would have changed the sale.

**See also:** [awareness of](#awareness-of), [understanding of](#understanding-of), [grasp of](#grasp-of), [information about/on](#information-abouton), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)


## L

### lack of

**Pronunciation:** /læk ʌv/ &middot; *lak uv* &middot; weak /læk əv/ → /læk ə/
**Русский:** отсутствие, нехватка чего-л. — рус. родительный (глагол lack идёт без of)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or gerund (*a lack of planning*, *a lack of anyone taking responsibility*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a lack of to plan*) and **never a that-clause**. The complement is normally uncountable or plural — *a lack of funding*, *a lack of options* — and a singular count noun needs a modifier to sit comfortably: *a lack of a clear plan* is fine where bare *a lack of a plan* is stiff and *no plan* is the plainer fix.
**Pattern:** *a lack of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *for lack of* [noun phrase] &middot; *through lack of* [noun phrase] &middot; *a lack of* [interest / evidence / sleep] &middot; *lacking in* [quality]
**Contrast:** **A star noun-versus-verb shift.** The verb *lack* is bare transitive — *the report lacks detail* — so ✗ *the report lacks of detail* and ✗ *he lacks of experience* are errors, and they are extremely common among speakers of Romance languages, where the equivalent verb is prepositional. The noun cannot go without *of*: ✗ *a lack detail*. A third form completes the set: the adjective is *lacking **in*** (*the argument is lacking in evidence*), so all three words in the family take a different construction — verb bare, noun *of*, adjective *in*. *Lack for* survives only in the fixed litotes *he lacked for nothing* (he had everything he needed); outside it, ✗ *the report lacks for detail* is an error.

The absence or shortage of something needed.

1. The whole project failed for *lack of* a single accountable owner.
2. There's a real *lack of* affordable rentals within walking distance of the plant.
3. Her *lack of* interest in the job showed in the first ten minutes.
4. The case was dismissed for *lack of* evidence.
5. A *lack of* sleep will do more damage than a bad diet.
6. The estate was large and the children *lacked for* nothing.

**See also:** [need for](#need-for), [supply of](#supply-of), [lack something](#lack-something), [demand for](#demand-for), [lack](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#lack), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### letter to/from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlɛtɚ tu/, /ˈlɛtɚ frʌm/ &middot; *LET-er too, LET-er frum* &middot; weak /ˈlɛtɚ tə/, /ˈlɛtɚ frəm/
**Русский:** письмо кому-л. / от кого-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **to**: the recipient. **from**: the sender. **of**: the letter's function, in fixed compounds (*a letter of resignation*, *a letter of recommendation*, *a letter of credit*). **about**: the subject. **Never a gerund, an infinitive, or a clause after *to*.**
**Pattern:** *a letter to* [recipient] &middot; *a letter from* [sender] &middot; *a letter of* [resignation / recommendation] &middot; *a letter about* [subject] &middot; *write a letter to* [person] &middot; *a letter to the editor*
**Contrast:** ***To*** **and** ***from*** **are unmissable; the trap is the verb.** American English allows *write **me*** with no preposition — *write me when you get in* — where British English requires *write **to** me*. Both *write to her* and *write her* are correct in the United States, but only *write to her* travels. ✗ *a letter for the editor* is an error where *a letter for you* (meaning addressed to you and now in my hand) is fine. The *of* compounds are fixed: ✗ *a letter for resignation*, ✗ *a recommendation letter of her* — say *a letter of recommendation for her*.

A written message, named by who sent it and who gets it.

1. She wrote a blistering *letter to* the editor about the sidewalk plan.
2. There was a *letter from* the county assessor waiting on the porch.
3. He needs three *letters of* recommendation by the fifteenth.
4. The *letter to* shareholders never mentioned the write-down.
5. I got a *letter about* the easement two years after the sale.
6. Her *letter of* resignation ran to a single sentence.

**See also:** [reply to](#reply-to), [response to](#response-to), [request for](#request-for), [report on/of](#report-onof), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### liability for/to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌlaɪəˈbɪləti fɔr/, /ˌlaɪəˈbɪləti tu/ &middot; *ly-uh-BIL-uh-tee for, ly-uh-BIL-uh-tee too* &middot; weak /ˌlaɪəˈbɪləti fɚ/, /ˌlaɪəˈbɪləti tə/
**Русский:** юр. ответственность за что-л. перед кем-л.; также обязательство, пассив баланса
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal, financial, and insurance; the "burden" sense is neutral and informal
**Complement:** **for**: the harm, debt, or act you can be made to answer for, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*liability for failing to inspect*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *liability to inspect*, which would mean something else entirely). **to**: the person or body you owe it to (*liability to third parties*). **under**: the instrument or statute it arises beneath. **Never a clause.**
**Pattern:** *liability for* [damages / negligence / the debt] &middot; *liability to* [a claimant / creditors] &middot; *liability under* [a statute / the contract] &middot; *limit / assume / disclaim / insure against liability* &middot; *joint and several liability* &middot; *a limited liability company* &middot; *liabilities* (the balance-sheet plural)
**Contrast:** ***For*** **the harm,** ***to*** **the person:** *liability **for** the collapse* names what happened, *liability **to** the tenants* names who can sue — and a single sentence often needs both, *its liability **to** the tenants **for** the collapse*. The adjective is the classic trap: *liable **for*** means responsible (*liable for the damage*), but *liable **to*** plus a verb means likely (*liable to fall*), and *liable to* plus a person means answerable to them. Note the accounting plural, which takes no preposition: *assets and liabilities*. In everyday American speech *a liability* with no complement means a burden — *the third baseman is a liability*.

Legal responsibility for something, or the person or body one is responsible to.

1. The waiver does not eliminate *liability for* gross negligence.
2. The company's *liability to* bondholders comes ahead of anything owed to shareholders.
3. *Liability for* failing to inspect the boiler fell on the management company.
4. He set up an LLC to keep personal *liability* away from the rental property.
5. The audit moved $4 million from assets to *liabilities*.
6. Their *liability under* the indemnity clause is capped at the purchase price.

**See also:** [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [damages for](#damages-for), [obligation to/under](#obligation-tounder), [duty to/of](#duty-toof), [immunity from](#immunity-from), [waiver of](#waiver-of)

### lien on/against

**Pronunciation:** /lin ɑn/, /lin əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *leen on, leen uh-GENST* &middot; weak /lin ən/ &middot; American /lin/ rhymes with *lean*; the older two-syllable /ˈliən/ survives in some courtrooms
**Русский:** юр. право удержания, залоговое обременение имущества в обеспечение долга
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal — real-estate closings, contractor disputes, tax collection. An American meets it when a title search turns up a mechanic's lien, or when the IRS files one.
**Complement:** **on / against**: the property or fund encumbered, as a noun phrase — **never a gerund**, **never an infinitive**, and **never a clause**. **for**: what is owed (*a lien for unpaid taxes*). **in favor of**: who holds it.
**Pattern:** *a lien on* [property] &middot; *a lien against* [property / an estate] &middot; *a lien for* [unpaid work / taxes] &middot; *file / perfect / release / foreclose a lien* &middot; *a mechanic's / tax / judgment lien* &middot; *free and clear of liens*
**Variants:** *lien on* and *lien against* are interchangeable in American practice; *on* is commoner in statutes and title reports, *against* in collection letters and in loose speech about a person (*a lien against him* really means against his property)
**Contrast:** **The object is the property, not the debtor:** strictly, ✗ *a lien on the contractor* is imprecise, because the lien attaches to the building. Against [claim against/for/to](#claim-againstforto): a claim is a demand for payment, a lien is a security interest that rides on a specific thing until the debt clears. A mortgage is one kind of consensual lien; a mechanic's lien is imposed by statute whether the owner agrees or not. ✗ *a lien of the property* is an error.

A legal claim attached to a piece of property as security for a debt.

1. The roofer filed a mechanic's *lien on* the house eleven days after the last invoice.
2. There's a tax *lien against* the property that predates their purchase.
3. The title came back with two *liens on* it and one of them was thirty years old.
4. A judgment *lien* attaches to any real estate he owns in the county.
5. The bank released its *lien on* the truck the week the note was paid.
6. Buyers want the parcel delivered free and clear of *liens*.

**See also:** [title to](#title-to), [claim against/for/to](#claim-againstforto), [easement over/across](#easement-overacross), [default on/under](#default-onunder), [judgment against/for](#judgment-againstfor), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### limit to/on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlɪmɪt tu/, /ˈlɪmɪt ɑn/ &middot; *LIM-it too, LIM-it on* &middot; weak /ˈlɪmɪt tə/, /ˈlɪmɪt ən/
**Русский:** предел чего-л., ограничение на что-л. — естественная или установленная граница
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **to**: what has a natural boundary (*a limit to my patience*). **on**: what is being restricted by rule (*a limit on spending*). Gerunds follow both (*no limit to what she'll try*, *a limit on borrowing*). **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a limit to spend*) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a limit to* [thing with a natural end] &middot; *a limit on* [activity restricted] &middot; *the limit of* [range / property] &middot; *within limits* &middot; *off limits* &middot; *set / impose / exceed a limit*
**Contrast:** ***To*** **is a natural ceiling;** ***on*** **is an imposed rule.** *There's a limit **to** how much I'll take* describes reality; *a limit **on** how much you may withdraw* describes policy. ✗ *a limit to withdrawals* in the policy sense is the usual slip. A third form, *the limit **of***, names the extent of a thing (*the limits of the property*, *the limits of my knowledge*). Note *off limits*, an American military idiom that takes no complement, and the plural in the fixed *within limits*. The verb takes a direct object and *to* for the ceiling: *limit contributions to $500*, ✗ *limit contributions on $500*.

A boundary, natural or imposed.

1. There's a *limit to* how many times I'll explain the same form.
2. The city put a *limit on* short-term rentals in the historic district.
3. The speed *limit* drops to twenty-five past the school.
4. That whole floor is off *limits* to visitors.
5. We're at the *limit of* what the old wiring can carry.
6. There seems to be no *limit to* what she'll volunteer for.

**See also:** [control over/of](#control-overof), [exception to](#exception-to), [rule against/for](#rule-againstfor), [room for](#room-for), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### link between/to

**Pronunciation:** /lɪŋk bɪˈtwin/, /lɪŋk tu/ &middot; *link bih-TWEEN, link too* &middot; weak /lɪŋk bəˈtwin/, /lɪŋk tə/
**Русский:** связь между чем-л. / с чем-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **between**: two or more items joined by *and* (*a link between diet and blood pressure*). **to / with**: a single item. Gerunds follow both. **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a link to reduce risk*) and **never a that-clause**.
**Pattern:** *a link between* X *and* Y &middot; *a link to / with* [noun phrase] &middot; *a link in the chain* &middot; *establish / find / deny a link between* X *and* Y &middot; *a link to* [a web page]
**Variants:** *link with* — an association or partnership, slightly more British in feel &middot; *link to* — the American default and the only form for hyperlinks
**Contrast:** ***Between*** **requires two things and the others require one:** ✗ *a link between smoking* is incomplete; ✗ *a link to smoking and cancer* should be *between*. In epidemiology and reporting, *a link between X and Y* is deliberately weaker than *a cause of* — see [cause of](#cause-of) — and confusing the two is the standard failure of science journalism. The verb takes both frames: *link X **to** Y*, *link X **with** Y*, and ✗ *link between X and Y* is not available to the verb.

A connection or association between two things.

1. Researchers found no *link between* the additive and the tumors.
2. There's a documented *link between* childhood lead exposure and later reading scores.
3. The company denies any *link to* the offshore accounts.
4. The archive page has a *link to* the original filing.
5. He was the last *link* to the generation that built the place.
6. The report suggests a *link with* two earlier cases in Ohio.

**See also:** [connection with/to/between](#connection-withtobetween), [relationship with/between](#relationship-withbetween), [cause of](#cause-of), [similarity to/between](#similarity-tobetween), [between](03-place-position.md#between)

### love for/of

**Pronunciation:** /lʌv fɔr/, /lʌv ʌv/ &middot; *luv for, luv uv* &middot; weak /lʌv fɚ/, /lʌv əv/ → /lʌv ə/
**Русский:** любовь к — к человеку или к своему делу; рус. «к», не «для»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a person, or a thing regarded with warmth (*her love for her students*). **of**: an activity, art, or abstraction (*a love of music*, *a love of arguing*) — gerunds go here, **never after *for*** in edited prose. **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a love to sing*). *In love with* takes a person.
**Pattern:** *love for* [person] &middot; *love of* [activity / art] &middot; *in love with* [person] &middot; *for the love of* [noun phrase] &middot; *a labor of love* &middot; *fall in love with* [person]
**Variants:** *love for* and *love of* overlap heavily with things (*a love for jazz* and *a love of jazz* are both fine); the split holds firmly only with people, where *for* is required
**Contrast:** ***For*** **points at a person,** ***of*** **at a pursuit** — *his love **for** his sister* is affection, while *his love **of** his sister* reads as connoisseurship, which is why the swap misfires. With activities, *of* is the safer choice, and it is close to obligatory before a gerund: *a love **of** cooking*, where *a love for cooking* is heard but marked. ✗ *love to* as a noun is an error, although the verb takes it freely (*I'd love to*). The adjective phrase is *in love **with***, never ✗ *in love to* or ✗ *in love of*, and *fall in love with* is fixed.

Deep affection for a person, or strong enthusiasm for a pursuit.

1. Her *love for* her students outlasted three principals.
2. A *love of* early jazz recordings runs through everything he wrote.
3. He married her for *love*, whatever the family said.
4. It was a labor of *love* and it took eleven years.
5. For the *love of* God, close the gate.
6. She fell in *love with* the house before she saw the roof.

**See also:** [fondness for](#fondness-for), [taste for/in](#taste-forin), [respect for](#respect-for), [desire for](#desire-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)


## M

### marriage to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmɛrɪdʒ tu/ &middot; *MAIR-ij too* &middot; weak /ˈmɛrɪdʒ tə/
**Русский:** брак с кем-л., женитьба на — рус. «с/на», англ. to (✗ marriage with)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **to**: the spouse (*her marriage to Tom*). **between**: both parties from outside (*a marriage between cousins*). **Never a gerund**, **never an infinitive**, **never a clause**. ✗ *marriage with* is not standard American English in the personal sense, though it survives in figurative use (*a marriage of convenience*, *a marriage with industry*).
**Pattern:** *a marriage to* [spouse] &middot; *a marriage between* X *and* Y &middot; *by marriage* &middot; *a marriage of convenience* &middot; *the marriage of* X *and* Y (figurative)
**Contrast:** **One of the sharpest verb-to-noun shifts in English, and the source of a famous error.** The verb *marry* is **bare transitive** — *she married Tom*, ✗ *she married with Tom*, ✗ *she married to Tom* — while the past-participial adjective takes *to*: *she is married **to** Tom*, ✗ *married with Tom*. The noun takes *to* as well: *her marriage **to** Tom*. So the sequence is: marry Ø, married to, marriage to. ✗ *married with* is the single most recognizable Spanish, Italian, and Portuguese transfer error in English, and *married with children* is correct only because *with* there means "having."

The union of two people, named by who married whom.

1. Her *marriage to* Tom lasted longer than either family expected.
2. This is his second *marriage*, and her first.
3. The *marriage between* the two families ended a forty-year feud over water.
4. He's my uncle by *marriage*, not by blood.
5. It was a *marriage of* convenience that turned out surprisingly well.
6. The book's subject is the *marriage of* engineering and design at Bell Labs.

**See also:** [relationship with/between](#relationship-withbetween), [marry someone](#marry-someone), [connection with/to/between](#connection-withtobetween), [love for/of](#love-forof), [married to](15-dependent-adjectives.md#married-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### matter with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmætɚ wɪθ/ &middot; *MAT-er with* &middot; weak /ˈmætɚ wəθ/
**Русский:** что не так с чем-л. — только в вопросах и отрицаниях: What's the matter with the car?
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, chiefly spoken
**Complement:** **with** + noun phrase, in the fixed interrogative frame *what's the matter with X?* — **never a gerund, an infinitive, or a clause**. The unrelated *a matter of* takes a noun or a gerund (*a matter of waiting*), and *a matter for* takes the party responsible (*a matter for the police*).
**Pattern:** *what's the matter with* [noun phrase] &middot; *there's something the matter with* [noun phrase] &middot; *a matter of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a matter for* [party] &middot; *as a matter of fact* &middot; *no matter what / who / how*
**Contrast:** ***With*** **belongs only to the "what's wrong" frame.** *What's the matter **with** the car?* asks what is broken; *a matter **of** money* means an issue of money; *a matter **for** the courts* means the courts' business — and ✗ *what's the matter of the car* mixes two idioms. The word takes no article in *no matter what*, and the fixed *as a matter of fact* has no complement. Note that in the "trouble" sense *matter* is almost always preceded by *the* and appears in questions and negatives, not in plain assertions: ✗ *there is a matter with the car* is not idiomatic; say *there's something wrong with the car*.

Whatever is wrong with something, and other fixed uses of the word.

1. What's the *matter with* the printer this time?
2. There's something the *matter with* the left rear tire.
3. Getting the permit is just a *matter of* waiting out the clerk.
4. Whether to prosecute is a *matter for* the district attorney.
5. As a *matter of* fact, I have the receipt right here.
6. We're going, no *matter what* the forecast says.

**See also:** [problem with](#problem-with), [trouble with](#trouble-with), [difficulty with/in](#difficulty-within), [point in/of](#point-inof), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### memory of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmɛməri ʌv/ &middot; *MEM-uh-ree uv* &middot; weak /ˈmɛməri əv/ → /ˈmɛməri ə/
**Русский:** память о, воспоминание о чём-л.; также память как способность
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: what is remembered — noun phrase or gerund (*a memory of standing in the driveway*) — **never a to-infinitive**. **for**: the faculty, what a person is good at remembering (*a memory for names*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause** in formal use: *a clear memory that the door was locked*.
**Pattern:** *a memory of* [thing remembered / verb-ing] &middot; *a memory for* [names / faces / dates] &middot; *in memory of* [person] &middot; *from memory* &middot; *within living memory* &middot; *jog someone's memory*
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is what you remember;** ***for*** **is what you are good at remembering.** *A memory **of** faces* is a recollection; *a memory **for** faces* is a talent — a total reversal from one preposition, and the pair is worth memorizing. ✗ *a memory about* is an error, and ✗ *in memory to* should be *in memory of*. Distinguish *from memory* (without notes) from *in memory* (as a memorial). The verb *remember* takes a direct object with no preposition (✗ *remember of it*, ✗ *remember about it*, though *remind someone of it* is correct).

A recollection, or the faculty that produces one.

1. My clearest *memory of* that summer is the smell of creosote.
2. She has an uncanny *memory for* names and none at all for faces.
3. The bench was placed there in *memory of* a teacher who drowned in 1962.
4. He recited all forty lines from *memory*.
5. The flood of '72 is still within living *memory* in this town.
6. A *memory of* standing in the driveway waiting for headlights is all he has left.

**See also:** [awareness of](#awareness-of), [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), [understanding of](#understanding-of), [idea of/for](#idea-offor), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### moratorium on

**Pronunciation:** /ˌmɔrəˈtɔriəm ɑn/ &middot; *mor-uh-TOR-ee-um on* &middot; weak /ˌmɔrəˈtɔriəm ən/
**Русский:** мораторий на — временный официальный запрет на какую-л. деятельность
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral — government, finance, and journalism
**Complement:** a noun phrase or **gerund** (*a moratorium on issuing new permits*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a moratorium to issue new permits*) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a moratorium on* [an activity] &middot; *impose / declare / extend / lift a moratorium* &middot; *a temporary / one-year / de facto moratorium* &middot; *under a moratorium*
**Variants:** the plural is *moratoriums* in American English and *moratoria* in stiffer or older prose; both are correct, and *moratoriums* is the safer choice outside a law review
**Contrast:** ✗ ***A moratorium of*** and ✗ *a moratorium against* are errors: the noun takes *on* and nothing else. Against [embargo on](#embargo-on): a moratorium is a pause, expected to end; an embargo is a ban with a political purpose. Against [prohibition on/against](#prohibition-onagainst): a prohibition is permanent until repealed. Against [injunction against](#injunction-against): an injunction comes from a court and names parties, a moratorium comes from a legislature or an agency and applies to everyone.

An official, temporary halt to an activity.

1. The county put a six-month *moratorium on* new short-term rental permits.
2. Congress extended the *moratorium on* evictions twice and then let it lapse.
3. There's been a de facto *moratorium on* hiring since the freeze in March.
4. The board lifted the *moratorium on* drilling in the watershed last fall.
5. A *moratorium on* issuing bonds would strand three projects mid-build.
6. Nothing in the ordinance says when the *moratorium* ends.

**See also:** [embargo on](#embargo-on), [prohibition on/against](#prohibition-onagainst), [injunction against](#injunction-against), [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [delay in](#delay-in), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)


## N

### need for

**Pronunciation:** /nid fɔr/ &middot; *need for* &middot; weak /nid fɚ/
**Русский:** потребность в чём-л., нужда в — рус. «в», не «для»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase naming what is needed (*a need for more housing*). The same noun takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition**: *no need to worry*. ✗ *a need of* survives only in the fixed *in need of*. ✗ *a need for to worry* mixes the two.
**Pattern:** *a need for* [noun phrase] &middot; *no need to* [verb] &middot; *in need of* [noun phrase] &middot; *meet / address a need for* [noun phrase] &middot; *if need be* &middot; *there's no need for* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ***For*** **takes a thing;** ***to*** **takes an act;** ***of*** **appears only after *in need*.** *A need **for** an inspection* names the thing wanted, *a need **to** inspect* names the act, and *the house is in need **of** an inspection* is the fixed adjectival phrase — ✗ *the house is in need for an inspection*. *There is need of* is stiff but not wrong; American writing prefers *there is a need for*. The verb takes an object or an infinitive and never a preposition: *we need an inspection*, *we need to inspect it*, ✗ *we need for an inspection* (except in the dialectal *need for you to sign*, common in the American South).

A requirement for something, and the act it calls for.

1. There's a documented *need for* another elementary school on the north side.
2. No *need to* apologize — the mail does that to everybody.
3. The whole porch is in *need of* rebuilding.
4. The grant is meant to meet a *need for* rural broadband.
5. There's no *need for* alarm; the readings are within range.
6. I'll drive, if *need* be.

**See also:** [demand for](#demand-for), [lack of](#lack-of), [desire for](#desire-for), [request for](#request-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### notice of/to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈnoʊtɪs ʌv/, /ˈnoʊtɪs tu/ &middot; *NOH-tiss uv, NOH-tiss too* &middot; weak /ˈnoʊtɪs əv/ → /ˈnoʊtɪs ə/, /ˈnoʊtɪs tə/
**Русский:** юр. уведомление о чём-л., направленное кому-л.; также предупреждение об увольнении
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal in the *of/to* frames — pleadings, leases, employment letters; neutral in *take notice of* and *give notice*
**Complement:** **of**: the subject of the notice, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*notice of intending to appeal* is stiff; *notice of intent to appeal* is the idiom). **to**: the recipient. **that**: a bare that-clause (*notice that the lease will not be renewed*). **to** + bare infinitive in fixed legal phrases: *notice to quit*, *notice to appear*. **Never *notice about*** in the legal sense.
**Pattern:** *notice of* [default / appeal / termination / a meeting] &middot; *notice to* [a party] &middot; *notice that* [clause] &middot; *give / serve / receive / waive notice* &middot; *on notice* &middot; *without notice* &middot; *thirty days' notice* &middot; *take notice of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **names the subject and** ***to*** **names the recipient**, and a single document carries both: *notice **to** the tenant **of** default*. ✗ *notice about the default* is an error in a legal document, though it is fine in conversation. Watch the possessive in periods of time: *thirty days' notice* takes an apostrophe, and ✗ *thirty days notice* is a common slip.
