Note *on notice*, which means warned, and the everyday verb *notice*, which takes a bare object and no preposition at all: *she noticed the leak*, ✗ *she noticed of the leak*.

A formal communication of a fact, given to someone entitled to know it.

1. The landlord served a *notice of* termination on the first of the month.
2. *Notice to* the guarantor has to be sent by certified mail.
3. She filed a *notice of* appeal one day inside the deadline.
4. The lease requires sixty days' *notice* before any rent increase.
5. The company put the vendor *on notice* that the account was under review.
6. The board gave *notice of* the special meeting by email, which the bylaws do not allow.

**See also:** [amendment to](#amendment-to), [default on/under](#default-onunder), [obligation to/under](#obligation-tounder), [waiver of](#waiver-of), [attention to](#attention-to), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)


## O

### objection to

**Pronunciation:** /əbˈdʒɛkʃən tu/ &middot; *ub-JEK-shun too* &middot; weak /əbˈdʒɛkʃən tə/
**Русский:** возражение против — рус. «против», не «к»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*an objection to being recorded*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an objection to be recorded*, ✗ *no objection to sign*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause** in formal and legal use: *an objection that the question assumes facts not in evidence*.
**Pattern:** *an objection to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *have no objection to* [verb-ing] &middot; *raise / overrule / sustain an objection* &middot; *an objection on the grounds that* [clause] &middot; *objections from* [party]
**Contrast:** **A prime *-ing* trap, and the test is easy:** a noun fits the slot (*an objection to the fee*), so the *to* is a preposition and any verb after it takes *-ing*. ✗ *I have no objection to pay* should be *to paying*; ✗ *her objection to be filmed* should be *to being filmed*. ✗ *an objection against* is an error, and *an objection about* is heard but marked. The verb keeps the same preposition and the same trap: *object **to** being asked*, ✗ *object to be asked*, and ✗ *object something* is not English.

A stated reason for disapproving of something.

1. Her only *objection to* the plan was the cost of the sidewalk.
2. I have no *objection to* working weekends if the schedule is posted in advance.
3. The neighbors raised an *objection to* rezoning the corner lot.
4. *Objections to* being recorded should be noted for the minutes.
5. The judge overruled the *objection* and let the answer stand.
6. There were three written *objections to* the easement.

**See also:** [opposition to](#opposition-to), [resistance to](#resistance-to), [complaint about](#complaint-about), [disagreement with/about](#disagreement-withabout), [exception to](#exception-to), [object to](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#object-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### obligation to/under

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɑbləˈɡeɪʃən tu/, /ˌɑbləˈɡeɪʃən ˈʌndɚ/ &middot; *ob-luh-GAY-shun too, ob-luh-GAY-shun UN-der* &middot; weak /ˌɑbləˈɡeɪʃən tə/; *under* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** обязательство перед кем-л. / по договору, обязанность
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; the *under* frame is legal drafting
**Complement:** **to**: **either** a person or body owed (*an obligation to his creditors*) **or a bare infinitive** naming the act required (*an obligation to disclose*). **Never a gerund after *to*** (✗ *an obligation to disclosing*). **under**: the agreement, statute, or section the duty arises beneath. **of**: whose obligation it is. **Never a that-clause.**
**Pattern:** *an obligation to* [person] &middot; *an obligation to* [verb] &middot; *obligations under* [a contract / the statute] &middot; *meet / discharge / breach an obligation* &middot; *be under an obligation to* [verb] &middot; *without obligation*
**Contrast:** **The two *to*'s, again, and the noun-slot test settles them:** *an obligation **to** the bank* takes a noun, *an obligation **to** repay* takes a bare verb. Against [duty to/of](#duty-toof): obligations are typically created by agreement and duties by role, which is why a contract lists *obligations* and a fiduciary owes *duties* — but the grammar is identical and the two words are swapped freely in ordinary writing. ✗ *an obligation for paying* is an error; *the obligation of paying* is possible but stilted. Note the American sales phrase *with no obligation to buy*, where the infinitive frame is doing the work.

Something a person is bound to do, and the instrument or relationship that binds them.

1. The company has no *obligation to* renew the contract and has said so.
2. Her *obligations under* the settlement end when the last payment clears.
3. He feels an *obligation to* the people who hired him in 1994.
4. There's a statutory *obligation to* report a spill within twenty-four hours.
5. You can cancel in thirty days with no *obligation to* buy anything.
6. Nothing in the letter creates an *obligation under* the master agreement.

**See also:** [duty to/of](#duty-toof), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [commitment to](#commitment-to), [entitlement to](#entitlement-to), [hereunder](13-formal-latin-archaic.md#hereunder)

### obstacle to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɑbstəkəl tu/ &middot; *OB-stuh-kul too* &middot; weak /ˈɑbstəkəl tə/
**Русский:** препятствие для чего-л., помеха чему-л. — рус. «для», не «к»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*an obstacle to finishing on time*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *an obstacle to finish on time*) and **never a that-clause**; a *wh*-clause is fine after the preposition (*an obstacle to what the district is trying to do*). A noun fits the slot (*an obstacle to peace*), which is what makes the *to* a preposition and forces the *-ing*.
**Pattern:** *an obstacle to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *the main / chief / biggest* **obstacle to** &middot; *pose / present / remove / clear* **an obstacle** &middot; *an obstacle in* [someone's] *path*
**Variants:** *a barrier to* &middot; *an impediment to* &middot; *a hurdle to* — all three take the same preposition and the same *-ing* rule
**Contrast:** ***To*** **is the only preposition here** — ✗ *an obstacle of finishing*, ✗ *an obstacle against the deal*, ✗ *an obstacle for finishing*. The near-synonyms behave identically, so the rule generalizes: *a barrier **to** entry*, *an impediment **to** recovery*, and ✗ *a barrier to enter the market* is the same mistake one word over. The one legitimate *for* frame takes a **person** and names who is blocked rather than what is blocked: *an obstacle **for** first-time buyers*. Compare [difficulty with/in](#difficulty-within), where American English drops the preposition before the gerund altogether (*difficulty finishing*), and [limit to/on](#limit-toon), which marks a ceiling rather than a blockage.

Something that stands in the way of progress.

1. The biggest *obstacle to* getting the permit is the parking study.
2. Financing turned out to be the only real *obstacle to* the sale.
3. Her age was treated as an *obstacle to* promotion, which was the whole problem.
4. Striking that one clause removed every *obstacle to* signing.
5. The court called the waiting period a substantial *obstacle to* access.
6. Language was never an *obstacle* between the two of them.

**See also:** [difficulty with/in](#difficulty-within), [problem with](#problem-with), [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [solution to](#solution-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### onus on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈoʊnəs ɑn/ &middot; *OH-nuss on* &middot; weak /ˈoʊnəs ən/
**Русский:** бремя доказывания на ком-л. — the onus is on you: доказывать придётся тебе
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; common in editorials, court opinions, and sports commentary, rare in casual speech
**Complement:** **on**: the person or body who bears the burden, as a noun phrase, usually followed by a **to-infinitive** saying what they must do (*the onus is on the state to prove it*). **of**: the burden itself (*the onus of proof*). **Never a gerund** (✗ *the onus on proving it*) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *the onus is on* [someone] &middot; *the onus is on* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *put / place / shift the onus on* [someone] &middot; *the onus of* [proof / responsibility] &middot; *the onus falls on* [someone]
**Contrast:** **The noun is nearly frozen in one frame.** *The onus **is on** you* is idiomatic; ✗ *you have the onus* and ✗ *the onus to you* are not. *Onus **of*** names the burden (*the onus of proof*) and *onus **on*** names the bearer, and mixing them gives ✗ *the onus on proof*. The word is a mass noun with no plural: ✗ *two onuses*. It is Latin for "burden," and it lives in English only in this frame, alongside the fuller legal phrase *burden of proof* — see [responsibility for](#responsibility-for) for the ordinary word.

The burden of doing or proving something, and who carries it.

1. The *onus is on* the state to prove every element beyond a reasonable doubt.
2. Putting the *onus on* tenants to document mold is how the ordinance fails.
3. After the fumble the *onus* was on the defense.
4. The *onus of* proof shifts once the plaintiff makes out a prima facie case.
5. The *onus is on* the seller to disclose a known defect in this state.
6. Nobody wants the *onus* and everybody has an opinion.

**See also:** [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [duty to/of](#duty-toof), [obligation to/under](#obligation-tounder), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [presumption of/against](#presumption-ofagainst), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### opinion on/of/about

**Pronunciation:** /əˈpɪnjən ɑn/ &middot; *uh-PIN-yun on* &middot; weak /əˈpɪnjən ən/, /əˈpɪnjən əv/ → /ə/, /əˈpɪnjən ˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** мнение о чём-л.; of — ещё и оценка чьих-л. качеств
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **on / about**: an issue — noun phrase, gerund (*an opinion on raising the levy*), or *wh*-clause (*an opinion on what to do*). **of**: a person or thing being evaluated (*a low opinion of him*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *the opinion that the levy is regressive*. **Never a to-infinitive.**
**Pattern:** *an opinion on / about* [issue] &middot; *an opinion of* [person / thing] &middot; *the opinion that* [clause] &middot; *in my opinion* &middot; *a matter of opinion* &middot; *a second opinion* &middot; *public opinion on* [issue]
**Variants:** *opinion on* — an issue formally at stake, and the form in *a legal opinion on* &middot; *opinion about* — the conversational default &middot; *opinion of* — an estimate of worth, not a position
**Contrast:** ***On*** **is a position;** ***of*** **is a verdict on someone's quality.** *Her opinion **on** the mayor's budget* is what she thinks should happen; *her opinion **of** the mayor* is what she thinks of him as a person — and they can be opposite. ✗ *my opinion for the plan* is an error. **The fixed hedge is *in my opinion***, never ✗ *according to my opinion*, ✗ *by my opinion*, or ✗ *to my opinion* — all three are frequent transfer errors. *According to* takes a source other than yourself (*according to the auditor*), which is why *according to me* sounds absurd.

A view held about an issue, or an estimate of someone's worth.

1. What's your *opinion on* the proposed levy?
2. He has an unaccountably high *opinion of* his own singing.
3. In my *opinion*, the second draft was the better one.
4. The county attorney issued a formal *opinion on* the setback rule.
5. Public *opinion about* the closure shifted after the first winter.
6. Whether it counts as art is a matter of *opinion*.

**See also:** [view on/of](#view-onof), [attitude to/toward](#attitude-totoward), [comment on](#comment-on), [advice on/about](#advice-onabout), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### opposition to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɑpəˈzɪʃən tu/ &middot; *op-uh-ZISH-un too* &middot; weak /ˌɑpəˈzɪʃən tə/
**Русский:** сопротивление чему-л., противодействие; несогласие с планом или человеком
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*opposition to raising taxes*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *opposition to raise taxes*) and **never a clause**. In the political sense the noun stands alone with the definite article: *the opposition*.
**Pattern:** *opposition to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *in opposition to* [noun phrase] &middot; *face / meet / drop opposition to* [noun phrase] &middot; *organized opposition to* [noun phrase] &middot; *the opposition* (a party)
**Contrast:** **The *-ing* rule is the entire difficulty:** ✗ *opposition to raise the levy* is the error, *opposition to **raising** the levy* the fix, and the noun test settles it (*opposition to the levy*). ✗ *opposition against* is a redundancy, though it appears in translated prose. *In opposition to* is a fixed connector meaning "contrary to" and behaves like a complex preposition — see [as opposed to](09-comparison-opposition.md#as-opposed-to) for its close relative. The verb takes a direct object with **no preposition**: *they opposed the levy*, ✗ *they opposed to the levy* — an error made especially often by people who know the noun well.

Resistance to a plan, policy, or person.

1. *Opposition to* the pipeline came from both ends of the county.
2. There was surprisingly little *opposition to* closing the pool.
3. She resigned in *opposition to* the new testing policy.
4. The bill faces organized *opposition to* raising the assessment cap.
5. *Opposition to* the war cost him his seat in 1968.
6. The measure passed with only token *opposition*.

**See also:** [objection to](#objection-to), [resistance to](#resistance-to), [argument with/about](#argument-withabout), [disagreement with/about](#disagreement-withabout), [contrast to/with](#contrast-towith), [opposed to](15-dependent-adjectives.md#opposed-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### order for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɔrdɚ fɔr/ &middot; *OR-der for* &middot; weak /ˈɔrdɚ fɚ/
**Русский:** заказ на что-л.; отдельно — приказ (order to) и порядок, последовательность
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: goods or services requested (*an order for fifty units*). **to** + **bare infinitive**: a command (*orders to evacuate*). **from**: the customer or the source. **of**: a quantity or a sequence (*an order of magnitude*, *the order of the names*). ✗ *an order for to evacuate*.
**Pattern:** *an order for* [goods] &middot; *an order to* [verb] &middot; *an order from* [customer / authority] &middot; *place / fill / cancel an order for* [noun phrase] &middot; *in order to* [verb] &middot; *out of order* &middot; *an order of* [food]
**Contrast:** **Commercial *for*, military *to*, and a pile of fixed phrases.** *An order **for** ammunition* is a purchase; *an order **to** withdraw* is a command; ✗ *an order for withdrawing* mixes them. *In order to* is a purpose connector, not this noun at all (see [in order to](06-cause-purpose.md#in-order-to)), and *out of order* means broken or procedurally improper. Note the American restaurant use: *an order **of** fries* is a portion, where British English says *a portion of chips*. The verb takes a person plus an infinitive (*ordered him to leave*, ✗ *ordered that he leaves* — the subjunctive is *ordered that he leave*) and a bare object for goods (*ordered fifty units*).

A request for goods, a command, or a sequence.

1. We placed an *order for* two hundred replacement filters in March.
2. The crew received *orders to* evacuate the platform by dawn.
3. That's the third *order from* the same address this week.
4. He put in an *order of* onion rings for the table.
5. The names are listed in *order of* seniority.
6. The second elevator has been out of *order* since Thanksgiving.

**See also:** [request for](#request-for), [demand for](#demand-for), [permission for](#permission-for), [rule against/for](#rule-againstfor), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)


## P

### party to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈpɑrti tu/ &middot; *PAR-tee too* &middot; weak /ˈpɑrti tə/
**Русский:** юр. сторона договора, участник разбирательства или сговора
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal — contracts, pleadings, treaties; also ordinary formal English in *be a party to* something disreputable
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming an agreement, a proceeding, or a scheme — **never a gerund** (✗ *a party to concealing it* is heard but ✗ in edited prose; write *a party to the concealment*), **never an infinitive**, and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a party to* [a contract / a suit / a treaty] &middot; *the parties to* [an agreement] &middot; *be / become / remain a party to* [noun phrase] &middot; *a third party* &middot; *an interested party* &middot; *a party of six* (the restaurant sense)
**Contrast:** ✗ ***A party of the contract*** **is the error**; the noun takes *to* and only *to*, and it appears in the opening line of nearly every American agreement (*the parties to this Agreement*). Keep the other nouns separate: *a party **of** six* is a table at a restaurant, *a party* is the event, and *the party* is a political organization — none of them takes *to*. Note the moral use, which is common in journalism: *he was a party to the cover-up* means he took part in it, and it does not require a signature. Against [signatory to](#signatory-to): every signatory is a party, but a party may be bound without signing.

Someone who takes part in an agreement, a lawsuit, or a scheme.

1. Both *parties to* the lease have to initial every change.
2. The United States is not a *party to* that convention.
3. She was never a *party to* the suit and was deposed anyway.
4. He refuses to be a *party to* anything that ends up in front of a grand jury.
5. The *parties to* the settlement agreed to keep the amount confidential.
6. A third *party to* the negotiation leaked the draft on Friday.

**See also:** [signatory to](#signatory-to), [beneficiary of/under](#beneficiary-ofunder), [claim against/for/to](#claim-againstforto), [exhibit to/schedule to/annex to](#exhibit-toschedule-toannex-to), [agreement with/on](#agreement-withon), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### penalty for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈpɛnəlti fɔr/ &middot; *PEN-ul-tee for* &middot; weak /ˈpɛnəlti fɚ/
**Русский:** наказание, штраф за что-л. — рус. «за», не «для»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; legal in *under penalty of*, and a fixed term in American sports
**Complement:** **for**: the offense, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*a penalty for filing late*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a penalty to file late*) and **never a clause**. **of**: the punishment itself (*a penalty of $500*, *the penalty of death*). **against / on**: the team or player, in sports.
**Pattern:** *a penalty for* [an offense / verb-ing] &middot; *a penalty of* [an amount] &middot; *under penalty of perjury* &middot; *impose / assess / waive / pay a penalty* &middot; *a penalty against* [a team] &middot; *the death penalty* &middot; *a prepayment penalty*
**Contrast:** ***For*** **the offense,** ***of*** **the punishment, and they stack:** *a penalty **of** $500 **for** filing late*. ✗ *a penalty for $500* reverses them and is a common slip in letters to the IRS. Against [damages for](#damages-for): a penalty punishes, damages compensate — a contract clause that sets a number too high to be compensation is void as a *penalty* and enforceable as *liquidated damages*, which is the whole fight. In American football the *penalty* is *against* a team and *on* a player, and *the penalty for* the foul is the yardage. Note *under penalty of perjury*, the fixed formula at the bottom of a federal form.

The punishment attached to an offense, and the offense that draws it.

1. The *penalty for* filing after April 15 compounds monthly.
2. There's a stiff *penalty for* early withdrawal from that certificate.
3. The court imposed a *penalty of* $2,500 and a year of probation.
4. I declare under *penalty of* perjury that the foregoing is true.
5. A holding *penalty against* the left tackle wiped out the touchdown.
6. The loan has no prepayment *penalty*, which is worth reading the note for.

**See also:** [damages for](#damages-for), [tax on](#tax-on), [sanction against/sanctions on](#sanction-againstsanctions-on), [prohibition on/against](#prohibition-onagainst), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### permission for

**Pronunciation:** /pɚˈmɪʃən fɔr/ &middot; *per-MISH-un for* &middot; weak /pɚˈmɪʃən fɚ/
**Русский:** разрешение на что-л. — рус. «на»; ср. permission to + инф. — разрешение сделать
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase naming the thing permitted (*permission for the field trip*). The same noun takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition**: *permission to leave early*. ✗ *permission of doing* and ✗ *permission for to leave* are errors. **from** names the person granting it.
**Pattern:** *permission for* [noun phrase] &middot; *permission to* [verb] &middot; *permission from* [authority] &middot; *with the permission of* [person] &middot; *ask for / grant / deny permission* &middot; *without permission*
**Contrast:** ***For*** **takes a thing;** ***to*** **takes an act;** ***of*** **appears only in *with the permission of***. *Permission **for** the recording* is consent for a thing; *permission **to** record* is consent for an act — and ✗ *permission of recording* is wrong in both senses. ✗ *permission of the teacher* is an error where *permission **from** the teacher* is correct, though *with the permission of the teacher* is the fixed formal frame. **The noun is uncountable**: ✗ *a permission*, ✗ *permissions* in the general sense, although the plural is standard in computing (*file permissions*).

Consent to do something.

1. We never got *permission for* the second dumpster.
2. She has *permission to* leave early on Thursdays.
3. You'll need written *permission from* the property owner.
4. The photograph is reproduced with the *permission of* the estate.
5. He took the truck without *permission*.
6. *Permission for* the field trip has to be back by Friday.

**See also:** [access to](#access-to), [invitation to](#invitation-to), [request for](#request-for), [authority on/over](#authority-onover), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### plan for

**Pronunciation:** /plæn fɔr/ &middot; *plan for* &middot; weak /plæn fɚ/
**Русский:** план чего-л., планы на что-л. — намеченный курс действий
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase naming the occasion or object (*a plan for the weekend*, *plans for the building*). The same noun takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition**: *a plan to sell the building*. **of** appears in the fixed *a plan of action*, *a plan of attack*. ✗ *a plan for to sell* and ✗ *a plan about* are errors.
**Pattern:** *a plan for* [occasion / object] &middot; *a plan to* [verb] &middot; *plans for* [the future] &middot; *a plan of action* &middot; *make plans for* [noun phrase] &middot; *according to plan* &middot; *a floor plan of* [building]
**Contrast:** ***For*** **takes a thing or a time;** ***to*** **takes an act.** *A plan **for** the renovation* names what the plan concerns; *a plan **to** renovate* names what will be done — and ✗ *a plan for renovate* mixes them. Note that *plans **for** the weekend* means arrangements, while *a plan **of** the weekend* would be a diagram. The verb takes both an infinitive and *on* + *-ing*, and the *on* frame is the one that trips people: *we plan **to** leave at six* and *we're planning **on** leaving at six* are both American, but ✗ *we plan on leave* and ✗ *we plan to leaving* are not.

An intended course of action, and what it concerns.

1. Do you have any *plans for* the long weekend?
2. The county has a *plan for* the old rail corridor that nobody likes.
3. There's a *plan to* sell the building and lease back two floors.
4. We need a *plan of* action before Tuesday's hearing.
5. Everything went according to *plan* until the fourth inning.
6. Her *plans for* retirement changed twice in one year.

**See also:** [idea of/for](#idea-offor), [reason for](#reason-for), [solution to](#solution-to), [approach to](#approach-to), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### pleasure in/of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈplɛʒɚ ɪn/, /ˈplɛʒɚ ʌv/ &middot; *PLEZH-er in, PLEZH-er uv* &middot; weak /ˈplɛʒɚ ən/, /ˈplɛʒɚ əv/ → /ˈplɛʒɚ ə/
**Русский:** удовольствие от чего-л., радость от — рус. «от», не «в»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** **in** + **gerund**: what someone enjoys doing (*takes pleasure in correcting people*) — **never a to-infinitive after *in*** (✗ *pleasure in to correct*). **of** + noun phrase: the thing enjoyed, in fixed formal frames (*the pleasure of your company*). The noun separately takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition** in the greeting formula *it's a pleasure to meet you*.
**Pattern:** *take pleasure in* [verb-ing] &middot; *the pleasure of* [noun phrase] &middot; *it is a pleasure to* [verb] &middot; *with pleasure* &middot; *at* [someone's] *pleasure* &middot; *for pleasure*
**Contrast:** **Three frames that cannot be swapped.** *Pleasure **in** doing it* is the enjoyment taken from an activity; *the pleasure **of** your company* is a formal noun frame from invitations; *a pleasure **to** meet you* is the greeting formula. ✗ *the pleasure to your company* and ✗ *I take pleasure of correcting you* are both errors. Note the register: *take pleasure in* usually carries a faint disapproval in American English (*he takes pleasure in saying no*), while *it's a pleasure to* is warm. The archaic *at your pleasure* (whenever you like) survives in legal and ceremonial writing.

Enjoyment, and the thing enjoyed.

1. He takes obvious *pleasure in* telling people the office is closed.
2. The *pleasure of* your company is requested at seven o'clock.
3. It's a *pleasure to* finally put a face to the emails.
4. She reads maps for *pleasure*, which explains a lot about our vacations.
5. There's real *pleasure in* watching somebody who is very good at a small job.
6. I'd help you with *pleasure*, but I'll be in Denver.

**See also:** [interest in](#interest-in), [fondness for](#fondness-for), [point in/of](#point-inof), [taste for/in](#taste-forin), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### point in/of

**Pronunciation:** /pɔɪnt ɪn/, /pɔɪnt ʌv/ &middot; *poynt in, poynt uv* &middot; weak /pɔɪnt ən/, /pɔɪnt əv/ → /pɔɪnt ə/
**Русский:** смысл делать что-л. (no point in waiting); также пункт, тезис в рассуждении
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **in / of** + **gerund**: the purpose or use of an activity (*no point in arguing*, *what's the point of arguing*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *no point to argue*). In American English the preposition can be **dropped** entirely: *no point arguing*. **about**: the topic of a remark (*a point about the deadline*).
**Pattern:** *there's no point in* [verb-ing] &middot; *what's the point of* [verb-ing] &middot; *no point* [verb-ing] &middot; *a point about* [topic] &middot; *the point of* [thing] &middot; *make / take / miss the point* &middot; *a point in someone's favor*
**Variants:** *no point in doing*, *no point of doing*, and bare *no point doing* — all three are current American English; *in* is the most usual in writing, and dropping the preposition is the most usual in speech
**Contrast:** ***In*** **or** ***of*** **plus** ***-ing***, **and never an infinitive:** ✗ *there's no point to complain*, ✗ *what's the point to wait*. Write *no point in complaining*, *what's the point of waiting*. The separate frame *the point **of** the exercise* takes a noun and is fine. Do not confuse this with the verb *point **to*** (indicate: *the evidence points to arson*) or *point **at*** (gesture toward), which are unrelated. *A point **about*** introduces the subject of a remark, and *a point **in** her favor* is a fixed idiom of credit.

The purpose or use of doing something, or an item in an argument.

1. There's no *point in* arguing with the parking division.
2. What's the *point of* a smoke detector nobody replaces the battery in?
3. No *point* waiting — the last bus left at nine.
4. She made a good *point about* the cost of the surveys.
5. The whole *point of* the exercise was to see who would speak up.
6. That he showed up at all is a *point in* his favor.

**See also:** [reason for](#reason-for), [importance of/to](#importance-ofto), [pleasure in/of](#pleasure-inof), [matter with](#matter-with), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### possession of

**Pronunciation:** /pəˈzɛʃən ʌv/ &middot; *puh-ZESH-un uv* &middot; weak /pəˈzɛʃən əv/ → /pəˈzɛʃən ə/
**Русский:** обладание чем-л., фактическое владение — независимо от права собственности
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; a term of art in criminal law and property law, and a fixed term in American sports
**Complement:** a noun phrase — **never a gerund** (✗ *possession of holding the deed*), **never an infinitive**, and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *possession of* [a thing] &middot; *in possession of* [a thing] &middot; *take / gain / lose possession of* [a thing] &middot; *in the possession of* [a person] &middot; *vacant possession* &middot; *possession of a controlled substance* &middot; *possession is nine-tenths of the law*
**Contrast:** ***In possession of*** **and** ***in the possession of*** **run in opposite directions:** *she was in possession **of** the file* says she had it; *the file was in the possession **of** her lawyer* says who had it — the article flips the perspective, and getting it backward produces a sentence that means the file owned the lawyer. ✗ *possession over* is an error, though [control over/of](#control-overof) does take *over*. Note the criminal-law senses that stack with no extra preposition: *constructive possession*, *possession with intent to distribute*. In basketball and football, *possession* takes *of* the ball and stands alone in *first possession*.

Physical control of a thing, whether or not you own it.

1. He was arrested for *possession of* a firearm by a convicted felon.
2. The buyer takes *possession of* the house on the fifteenth.
3. The originals are in the *possession of* the county recorder.
4. She was in *possession of* a key she was not supposed to have.
5. The team lost *possession* three times in the fourth quarter.
6. *Possession of* the deed is not the same thing as [title to](#title-to) the land.

**See also:** [custody of](#custody-of), [title to](#title-to), [control over/of](#control-overof), [claim against/for/to](#claim-againstforto), [access to](#access-to), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### possibility of

**Pronunciation:** /ˌpɑsəˈbɪləti ʌv/ &middot; *pos-uh-BIL-uh-tee uv* &middot; weak /ˌpɑsəˈbɪləti əv/ → /ˌpɑsəˈbɪləti ə/
**Русский:** возможность чего-л., вероятность того, что нечто случится
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase or **gerund** (*the possibility of rain*, *the possibility of losing the contract*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the possibility to rain*, ✗ *the possibility to lose the contract*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *the possibility that the seal failed*.
**Pattern:** *the possibility of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *the possibility that* [clause] &middot; *raise / rule out / consider the possibility of* [verb-ing] &middot; *there is a possibility of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ***Of + -ing*** **or** ***that*** **+ clause, never an infinitive.** ✗ *the possibility to work from home* is one of the most common errors in professional English written by Germans, Dutch speakers, and Scandinavians, whose languages allow it. Write *the possibility **of** working from home* — or, more naturally, *the option to work from home* or *the chance to work from home*, since [chance of](#chance-of) and *option* do take the infinitive. ✗ *possibility for* is likewise wrong. The adjective *possible* takes an infinitive freely (*it is possible to work from home*), which is exactly why the noun feels as though it should.

Something that might happen or might be done.

1. We can't rule out the *possibility of* a second recall.
2. There's a real *possibility of* frost before the end of the month.
3. She raised the *possibility that* the invoice had been paid twice.
4. The *possibility of* working four ten-hour days is being studied.
5. No one considered the *possibility of* the pump simply being unplugged.
6. The *possibility that* the seal had failed was dismissed too quickly.

**See also:** [chance of](#chance-of), [risk of](#risk-of), [danger of/to](#danger-ofto), [hope for/of](#hope-forof), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### precedent for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈprɛsədənt fɔr/ &middot; *PRESS-uh-dunt for* &middot; weak /ˈprɛsədənt fɚ/ &middot; the noun is *PRESS-*, not *pruh-SEED-*; the adjective *precedent* /prɪˈsidənt/ is a different word
**Русский:** прецедент для — более ранний случай, оправдывающий повторение
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; a term of art in American law, where *precedent* is what binds a lower court
**Complement:** **for**: what the precedent would support, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*a precedent for reopening the case*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a precedent to reopen the case*) and **never a clause**. **in**: the jurisdiction or body of law (*no precedent in this circuit*).
**Pattern:** *a precedent for* [an act / verb-ing] &middot; *precedent in* [a court / a field] &middot; *set / establish / follow / overrule / break with precedent* &middot; *without precedent* &middot; *binding / persuasive precedent* &middot; *legal precedent* (uncountable)
**Contrast:** **The countability rule is split by sense.** *A precedent* is countable when it means one earlier case or act (*that would set a bad precedent*); *precedent* is uncountable when it means the whole body of decided law (*there is no precedent for this in the circuit*). Watch the near-homographs: *precedent* the noun is *PRESS-uh-dunt*, and *president* is *PREZ-uh-dunt* — a spelling error that survives spellcheck. ✗ *a precedent of* is an error in this frame, and *the precedent of Marbury* means the case itself rather than what it supports.

An earlier case or act that justifies doing the same thing again.

1. There's no *precedent for* letting a board member vote by proxy.
2. Approving this variance sets a *precedent for* every corner lot on the street.
3. The ruling breaks with forty years of *precedent* in the Ninth Circuit.
4. She found one *precedent for* reopening a closed claim after five years.
5. The appointment was without *precedent* and drew two lawsuits.
6. Lower courts are bound by *precedent* whether or not they like it.

**See also:** [example of](#example-of), [basis for](#basis-for), [grounds for](#grounds-for), [judgment against/for](#judgment-againstfor), [citation to](#citation-to), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### preference for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈprɛfrəns fɔr/ &middot; *PREF-runss for* &middot; weak /ˈprɛfrəns fɚ/
**Русский:** предпочтение чего-л., склонность к одному в ущерб другому
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*a preference for working alone*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a preference to work alone*). The fixed comparative frame is *in preference to* + noun phrase.
**Pattern:** *a preference for* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *in preference to* [noun phrase] &middot; *have / show / express a preference for* [noun phrase] &middot; *give preference to* [group] &middot; *a stated preference*
**Contrast:** ***For*** **names what you like better;** ***to*** **appears only in *in preference to* and *give preference to***. ✗ *a preference to the older model* is an error where *a preference for the older model* and *chose the older model in preference to the new one* are both correct. ✗ *a preference of* is wrong. The related verb takes yet another construction and is the real trap: *prefer X **to** Y* (✗ *prefer X than Y*), *prefer **to** do it* (infinitive), and *prefer **doing** X **to doing** Y* — so the verb takes *to* where the noun takes *for*.

A liking for one thing over another.

1. She has a marked *preference for* older buildings with bad wiring.
2. The survey found a clear *preference for* evening appointments.
3. He chose the county road in *preference to* the interstate.
4. The scholarship gives *preference to* applicants from the district.
5. My *preference for* working alone is not a comment on the team.
6. State your *preference for* morning or afternoon on the form.

**See also:** [fondness for](#fondness-for), [taste for/in](#taste-forin), [desire for](#desire-for), [alternative to](#alternative-to), [prefer to](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#prefer-to), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### premium over/on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈprimiəm ˈoʊvɚ/, /ˈprimiəm ɑn/ &middot; *PREE-mee-um OH-ver, PREE-mee-um on* &middot; weak /ˈprimiəm ən/; *over* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** over — надбавка сверх базовой цены; on — страховой взнос либо особая ценность
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** finance and insurance; the *place a premium on* frame is general formal English
**Complement:** **over / above / to**: the baseline the price exceeds, as a noun phrase. **on**: the policy or the thing valued, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*a premium on getting there first*). **for**: what the extra buys. **Never an infinitive** (✗ *a premium to arrive first*) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a premium over* [a price / the market] &middot; *a premium on* [a policy] &middot; *place / put a premium on* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *pay a premium for* [something] &middot; *at a premium* &middot; *a premium of* [a percentage]
**Contrast:** ***Over*** **measures an excess,** ***on*** **names either a policy or a prized quality**, and the two never swap: ✗ *a premium on the market price* is an error for the takeover sense, and ✗ *the premium over my policy* is an error for the insurance sense. *At a premium* means scarce and expensive and takes no complement (*parking is at a premium*). Note that *premium* also works as an adjective with no preposition at all — *premium gas*, *a premium account* — which is where American advertising has taken the word.

An amount paid above a baseline, the payment on an insurance policy, or unusual value placed on something.

**1. The extra above a baseline price.** The complement is *over*, *above*, or *to*, and the baseline is a market price, a book value, or a previous offer. The percentage comes in with *of*: *a premium of 34 percent over Friday's close*.

1. The offer is a thirty percent *premium over* Friday's closing price.
2. Buyers are paying a *premium over* asking on anything with a garage.

**2. The periodic payment on an insurance policy.** The complement is *on*, and the noun is countable.

3. The *premium on* the flood policy doubled after the map was redrawn.
4. He let the *premium* lapse and found out in the worst possible week.

**3. High value placed on a quality.** Only in the frame *place / put a premium on*, which takes a noun phrase or a gerund.

5. The new manager puts a *premium on* showing up prepared.
6. A hospital that places a *premium on* speed will lose something else.

**See also:** [price of/for](#price-offor), [return on](#return-on), [interest in](#interest-in), [tax on](#tax-on), [importance of/to](#importance-ofto), [over](10-topic-reference-role.md#over)

### presumption of/against

**Pronunciation:** /prɪˈzʌmpʃən ʌv/, /prɪˈzʌmpʃən əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *prih-ZUMP-shun uv, prih-ZUMP-shun uh-GENST* &middot; weak /prɪˈzʌmpʃən əv/ → /prɪˈzʌmpʃən ə/; *against* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** юр. презумпция чего-л. / против чего-л. — исходное допущение до опровержения
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal and formal — jury instructions, statutory interpretation, editorial writing
**Complement:** **of**: what is assumed true until disproved, as a noun phrase (*a presumption of innocence*, *a presumption of validity*). **against**: what the law starts out disfavoring (*a presumption against retroactivity*). **in favor of**: the mirror image. The noun also takes a bare **that-clause** (*the presumption that the letter was received*). **Never a to-infinitive.**
**Pattern:** *a presumption of* [innocence / regularity / death] &middot; *a presumption against* [a result] &middot; *a presumption in favor of* [a party] &middot; *the presumption that* [clause] &middot; *rebut / overcome / raise a presumption* &middot; *on the presumption that* [clause] &middot; *a rebuttable presumption*
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is what you start by believing,** ***against*** **is what you start by doubting** — and the same statute can carry both (*a presumption **of** validity* for the agency's rule, *a presumption **against** applying it retroactively*). ✗ *a presumption to* is an error. Do not confuse the word with ***presumptuousness***: *the presumption of innocence* is a legal default, but *the presumption of the man* means his gall — a second, older sense that takes *of* as well and has ruined more than one sentence. Against [idea of/for](#idea-offor): an assumption is anything taken for granted; a presumption is one the law or the argument requires until rebutted.

A starting position that holds until someone proves otherwise.

1. The *presumption of* innocence is not a rule of evidence but the whole frame.
2. There's a strong *presumption against* reading a statute to apply retroactively.
3. Courts apply a *presumption of* regularity to what an agency says it did.
4. The *presumption that* a mailed notice was received can be rebutted by testimony.
5. A seven-year absence raises a *presumption of* death in most states.
6. The board acted on the *presumption that* the grant would be renewed.

**See also:** [onus on](#onus-on), [probability of/likelihood of](#probability-oflikelihood-of), [evidence of/for](#evidence-offor), [doubt about](#doubt-about), [judgment against/for](#judgment-againstfor), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### price of/for

**Pronunciation:** /praɪs ʌv/, /praɪs fɔr/ &middot; *pryce uv, pryce for* &middot; weak /praɪs əv/ → /praɪs ə/; /praɪs fɚ/
**Русский:** цена чего-л. (на рынке) / за что-л. (в конкретной сделке)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: what the item costs in general (*the price of gas*). **for**: an amount named in a particular transaction (*a fair price for the truck*). A gerund follows *of* in the figurative sense (*the price of being right*). **Never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *the price of* [commodity] &middot; *a price for* [item in a deal] &middot; *at a price of* [amount] &middot; *the price of* [verb-ing] (figurative) &middot; *put a price on* [noun phrase] &middot; *at any price* &middot; *price per* [unit]
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is the market;** ***for*** **is the deal.** *The price **of** the truck* is what trucks like that cost; *a good price **for** the truck* is what you and I agreed on. Neither is ungrammatical, but *the price for gas is up* reads oddly in a market report and *what's the price of your truck* reads oddly in a negotiation. *Put a price **on*** is fixed — ✗ *put a price to*. The figurative sense takes *of* + *-ing*: *the price of being right about this* is what it costs you socially.

What something costs, in a market or in a particular deal.

1. The *price of* diesel went up eleven cents in a week.
2. He got a decent *price for* the truck considering the mileage.
3. The *price of* being early to a bad idea is that everyone remembers.
4. The units sold at a *price of* $340,000 apiece.
5. You can't put a *price on* a Saturday morning with nothing scheduled.
6. The *price for* the same procedure varies by a factor of six across the state.

**See also:** [supply of](#supply-of), [demand for](#demand-for), [tax on](#tax-on), [importance of/to](#importance-ofto), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### probability of/likelihood of

**Pronunciation:** /ˌprɑbəˈbɪləti ʌv/, /ˈlaɪklihʊd ʌv/ &middot; *prob-uh-BIL-uh-tee uv, LYKE-lee-hood uv* &middot; weak /ˌprɑbəˈbɪləti əv/ → /ˌprɑbəˈbɪləti ə/
**Русский:** вероятность чего-л. — насколько правдоподобно, что нечто случится
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** *probability* is technical in statistics and neutral elsewhere; *likelihood* is neutral
**Complement:** **of**: a noun phrase or **gerund** (*the probability of rain*, *the likelihood of being audited*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the probability to rain*, ✗ *the likelihood to be audited*). Both also take a bare **that-clause**: *the probability that the well is dry*. **Never *probability for*.**
**Pattern:** *the probability of* [an event / verb-ing] &middot; *the likelihood of* [an event / verb-ing] &middot; *the probability that* [clause] &middot; *in all likelihood* &middot; *a high / low / vanishing probability* &middot; *increase / reduce the likelihood of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** **The adjectives take the infinitive and the nouns refuse it** — this is the single highest-yield fact in the entry. *It is **likely to** rain* is correct; ✗ *the likelihood to rain* is not. Convert with *of* + *-ing* or with *that*: *the likelihood **of** rain*, *the likelihood **that** it will rain*. Against [chance of](#chance-of), which splits by meaning: *a chance **of** winning* is a probability and *a chance **to** win* is an opportunity, so *chance* is the one noun in this family that does take an infinitive — with a different sense. ✗ *the probability for rain* and ✗ *likelihood on* are errors.

How probable something is.

1. The *probability of* a hundred-year flood in any given year is one percent.
2. The *likelihood of* being audited on a return like this is very small.
3. What's the *probability that* both machines fail in the same week?
4. Insulating the attic reduces the *likelihood of* ice dams.
5. In all *likelihood* the case settles before anyone sees a jury.
6. He put the *probability of* success at about one in three and was optimistic.

**See also:** [chance of](#chance-of), [possibility of](#possibility-of), [risk of](#risk-of), [incidence of/prevalence of](#incidence-ofprevalence-of), [presumption of/against](#presumption-ofagainst), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### problem with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈprɑbləm wɪθ/ &middot; *PROB-lum with* &middot; weak /ˈprɑbləm wəθ/
**Русский:** проблема с чем-л., неполадка, затруднение — рус. «с», не «в»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **with**: the thing or person that is causing trouble (*a problem with the wiring*). **of**: the nature of the problem, formal (*the problem of homelessness*). **in**: a domain (*a problem in logic*). Gerunds follow *with* (*a problem with getting parts*); **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a problem to get parts*) and **never a that-clause** in ordinary use.
**Pattern:** *a problem with* [thing / person] &middot; *the problem of* [social issue] &middot; *a problem in* [field] &middot; *have a problem with* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *no problem* &middot; *the problem is that* [clause] &middot; *solve / cause a problem*
**Contrast:** ***With*** **for a specific fault,** ***of*** **for a category.** *A problem **with** the wiring* is a fault to fix; *the problem **of** rural broadband* is a policy area. ✗ *a problem about* is an error, and ✗ *a problem for the wiring* means something else (whom it inconveniences). Note that *the problem is **that**...* takes a clause with no preposition — ✗ *the problem is because...* — the same fault treated under [reason for](#reason-for). *I have a problem with that* is American idiom for disagreement, not for a malfunction.

A fault, difficulty, or matter needing a solution.

1. There's a *problem with* the wiring in the back bedroom.
2. The *problem of* deferred maintenance is not going away on its own.
3. I have a *problem with* being told after the fact.
4. That's a classic *problem in* elementary probability.
5. The *problem is that* nobody kept the original survey.
6. Two coats and it's fine — no *problem*.

**See also:** [trouble with](#trouble-with), [difficulty with/in](#difficulty-within), [solution to](#solution-to), [matter with](#matter-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### progress in/on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈprɑɡrɛs ɪn/, /ˈprɑɡrɛs ɑn/ &middot; *PROG-ress in, PROG-ress on* &middot; weak /ˈprɑɡrɛs ən/
**Русский:** прогресс в чём-л., продвижение в работе над — в англ. неисчисляемо
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **in**: a field or general area, and gerunds (*progress in reducing waste*). **on**: a specific project or task (*progress on the report*). **toward**: a goal. **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *progress to reduce waste*) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *progress in* [field / verb-ing] &middot; *progress on* [project] &middot; *progress toward* [goal] &middot; *make progress* &middot; *in progress* &middot; *the progress of* [disease / negotiation]
**Variants:** *progress toward* — American, no *-s*; *progress towards* is British
**Contrast:** ***In*** **for a domain,** ***on*** **for a task,** ***toward*** **for a goal, and** ***of*** **for something unfolding.** *Progress **in** cancer research* is a field advancing; *progress **on** the grant application* is one job moving; *progress **toward** accreditation* is a target approached; *the progress **of** the disease* is its course. ✗ *progress for* is an error. **The noun is uncountable in this sense**: ✗ *a progress*, ✗ *progresses*, ✗ *many progresses* — say *some progress*, *a lot of progress*, *real progress*. Note that *progress* the verb (pro-GRESS) stresses differently from the noun (PROG-ress).

Forward movement toward a goal or through a task.

1. There's been real *progress in* reducing emergency room wait times.
2. What's the *progress on* the Fillmore Street repaving?
3. The district has made almost no *progress toward* its literacy goals.
4. Renovation is still in *progress* on the second floor.
5. The *progress of* the negotiations was reported daily and inaccurately.
6. Two years in, we've made some *progress* and no money.

**See also:** [improvement in/on](#improvement-inon), [increase in/decrease in](#increase-indecrease-in), [success in/at](#success-inat), [delay in](#delay-in), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### prohibition on/against

**Pronunciation:** /ˌproʊəˈbɪʃən ɑn/, /ˌproʊəˈbɪʃən əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *proh-uh-BISH-un on, proh-uh-BISH-un uh-GENST* &middot; weak /ˌproʊəˈbɪʃən ən/ &middot; note the silent *h*: *proh-uh-*, not *pro-hib-*
**Русский:** запрет на что-л. — правило, запрещающее какое-л. действие
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — statutes, ordinances, policy writing
**Complement:** **on / against**: the act forbidden, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*a prohibition on selling alcohol before noon*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a prohibition to sell alcohol*) and **never a clause**. **of** is possible and formal (*the prohibition of torture*).
**Pattern:** *a prohibition on / against* [an act / verb-ing] &middot; *the prohibition of* [a practice] (formal) &middot; *impose / lift / enforce a prohibition* &middot; *Prohibition* (capitalized: the American era, 1920–1933) &middot; *a blanket prohibition*
**Variants:** *prohibition on* and *prohibition against* are both standard American English and freely interchangeable; *on* is slightly commoner in ordinances, *against* in constitutional writing (*the prohibition against cruel and unusual punishment*)
**Contrast:** ✗ ***A prohibition to*** **is the error**, and it is the direct translation from several European languages. Against [moratorium on](#moratorium-on): a moratorium pauses, a prohibition forbids indefinitely. Against [injunction against](#injunction-against): an injunction is a court order against named parties, a prohibition is a rule against everyone. Capitalized and bare, ***Prohibition*** is the American constitutional experiment of 1920–1933, and it takes no complement at all.

A rule forbidding something.

1. The ordinance imposes a *prohibition on* overnight parking from November to April.
2. The constitutional *prohibition against* ex post facto laws settled the question.
3. There's a blanket *prohibition on* accepting gifts from vendors.
4. The lease has a *prohibition against* subletting that nobody enforces.
5. A *prohibition on* selling flavored products took effect in three cities at once.
6. *Prohibition* closed the brewery in 1920 and the family never reopened it.

**See also:** [moratorium on](#moratorium-on), [embargo on](#embargo-on), [injunction against](#injunction-against), [rule against/for](#rule-againstfor), [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### proof of

**Pronunciation:** /pruf ʌv/ &middot; *proof uv* &middot; weak /pruf əv/ → /pruf ə/
**Русский:** доказательство чего-л., подтверждение — то, что устраняет сомнения
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase or gerund (*proof of having paid*) — **never a to-infinitive**. The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *proof that the check cleared*. ✗ *proof for* is not standard in the evidentiary sense; use [evidence of/for](#evidence-offor).
**Pattern:** *proof of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *proof that* [clause] &middot; *proof of purchase / address / insurance* &middot; *the burden of proof* &middot; *beyond proof* &middot; *a proof of* [theorem]
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is the only preposition here.** ✗ *proof for the theory* should be *evidence for the theory*, because *proof* in American usage claims certainty where *evidence* claims support — a difference worth keeping. **The countability rule is split by domain:** in ordinary use *proof* is uncountable, so ✗ *two proofs that he paid* and ✗ *he offered several proofs* are errors — the fixed compounds *a proof of purchase* and *a proof of address* are the one place an article turns up. In mathematics and printing the noun is fully countable (*two proofs of the theorem*, *the galley proofs*). Note the fixed *proof of purchase*, *proof of address*, and *proof of insurance*, which take no article.

Something that establishes a fact beyond doubt.

1. Bring *proof of* residence and a photo ID.
2. There's no *proof of* forced entry, only a broken latch.
3. The receipt is your *proof of* purchase.
4. He wanted *proof that* the check had actually cleared.
5. The burden of *proof* is on the party seeking the injunction.
6. The paper gives two independent *proofs of* the same result.

**See also:** [evidence of/for](#evidence-offor), [guarantee of](#guarantee-of), [example of](#example-of), [grounds for](#grounds-for), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### protection from/against

**Pronunciation:** /prəˈtɛkʃən frʌm/, /prəˈtɛkʃən əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *pruh-TEK-shun frum, pruh-TEK-shun uh-GENST* &middot; weak /prəˈtɛkʃən frəm/, /prəˈtɛkʃən əˈɡɛnst/
**Русский:** защита от чего-л. — рус. «от», не «против»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or gerund (*protection against being sued*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *protection to be sued*) and **never a clause**. *Protection for* names who is protected; *protection of* names what is being kept safe.
**Pattern:** *protection from / against* [threat] &middot; *protection for* [person protected] &middot; *the protection of* [thing kept safe] &middot; *under the protection of* [authority] &middot; *offer / provide protection against* [threat]
**Variants:** *protection from* and *protection against* — both standard American English; *from* is commoner with immediate physical threats (*protection from the rain*), *against* with risks and adversaries (*protection against fraud*)
**Contrast:** **Four prepositions, and only two of them are interchangeable.** *Protection **from/against** the wind* names the threat; *protection **for** the seedlings* names who benefits; *the protection **of** the wetlands* names what is being conserved — and ✗ *protection of the wind* would mean the wind is what you are safeguarding. ✗ *protection to* is an error. The verb takes both prepositions of threat and a bare object: *protect the seedlings **from** frost*, ✗ *protect from the seedlings*.

A defense against a threat, and who or what it covers.

1. The awning offers some *protection from* the afternoon sun.
2. The policy provides *protection against* flood damage up to $250,000.
3. There's almost no legal *protection for* tenants in month-to-month leases.
4. The statute concerns the *protection of* archaeological sites.
5. Witnesses were placed under the *protection of* the marshals.
6. Two coats of sealer give real *protection against* freeze-thaw cracking.

**See also:** [danger of/to](#danger-ofto), [threat to](#threat-to), [risk of](#risk-of), [cure for](#cure-for), [protect from, protect against](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#protect-from-protect-against), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)


## R

### ratio of ... to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈreɪʃoʊ ʌv/, /ˈreɪʃoʊ tu/ &middot; *RAY-shoh uv, RAY-shoh too* &middot; weak /ˈreɪʃoʊ əv/ → /ˈreɪʃoʊ ə/, /ˈreɪʃoʊ tə/ &middot; American *RAY-shoh*; the older *RAY-shee-oh* /ˈreɪʃioʊ/ is heard in mathematics classrooms
**Русский:** соотношение чего-л. к чему-л., пропорция двух величин
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; technical in finance, statistics, and engineering
**Complement:** two noun phrases, the first after *of* and the second after *to* — **never a gerund**, **never an infinitive**, and **never a clause**. The numbers themselves may fill the *of ... to* frame directly (*a ratio of three to one*).
**Pattern:** *the ratio of* X *to* Y &middot; *a ratio of* [number] *to* [number] &middot; *in a ratio of* 2:1 &middot; *a high / low ratio* &middot; *the student-teacher ratio* (compound, no preposition) &middot; *the debt-to-equity ratio*
**Contrast:** **Both halves are obligatory in the full frame**: ✗ *the ratio of doctors and patients* is an error — *and* cannot replace *to* — and ✗ *the ratio between doctors to patients* mixes two frames. *A ratio **between*** X *and* Y does exist and is looser; the edited form for a computed figure is *of ... to*. Note that the compound forms hyphenate and drop the prepositions entirely (*the debt-to-equity ratio*, *a 3-to-1 ratio*, *the student-teacher ratio*), which is why writers forget the prepositions when they need them. Against [in proportion to](09-comparison-opposition.md#in-proportion-to): a proportion is a part of a whole, a ratio compares two quantities that need not belong to the same whole.

A comparison of two quantities, expressed as one number against another.

1. The *ratio of* applicants *to* seats was almost nine to one.
2. Mix it in a *ratio of* three parts sand *to* one part cement.
3. The county's *ratio of* nurses *to* residents is the lowest in the state.
4. Their debt-to-equity *ratio* got worse every quarter for two years.
5. The *ratio of* signal *to* noise in the recording is hopeless.
6. A student-teacher *ratio* of thirty-two to one is not a class, it is a crowd.

**See also:** [in proportion to](09-comparison-opposition.md#in-proportion-to), [correlation with/between](#correlation-withbetween), [incidence of/prevalence of](#incidence-ofprevalence-of), [share in/of](#share-inof), [disparity between/in](#disparity-betweenin), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### rationale for

**Pronunciation:** /ˌræʃəˈnæl fɔr/ &middot; *rash-uh-NAL for* &middot; weak /ˌræʃəˈnæl fɚ/ &middot; four syllables, final stress; not *RASH-un-ul*, which is the adjective *rational*
**Русский:** обоснование чего-л., логика решения или практики
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — memos, opinions, grant applications
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*the rationale for closing the branch*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the rationale to close the branch*) and **never a clause**; a that-clause needs a verb between (*the rationale was that the branch lost money*).
**Pattern:** *the rationale for* [a decision / verb-ing] &middot; *the rationale behind* [a decision] &middot; *offer / explain / question the rationale* &middot; *the stated rationale* &middot; *the underlying rationale*
**Variants:** *rationale for* and *rationale behind* are both standard American English; *behind* suggests a reason that was not stated openly, *for* the reason given. *Rationale of* occurs in older and stiffer prose and is now uncommon.
**Contrast:** ***Rationale*** **is a noun and** ***rational*** **is an adjective**, and they are not pronounced alike: *rash-uh-NAL* against *RASH-un-ul*. ✗ *the rational for the decision* is a spelling error that spellcheck will not catch. Against [reason for](#reason-for): a reason can be a bare fact, a rationale is a worked-out justification — which is why an agency publishes *the rationale for* a rule and a person gives *a reason for* being late. Against [justification for](#justification-for): a justification argues that the act was right, a rationale only explains the thinking.

The reasoning offered for a decision or a practice.

1. Nobody has explained the *rationale for* moving the deadline twice.
2. The *rationale behind* the merger was cost savings that never showed up.
3. The memo gives a two-sentence *rationale for* closing the branch.
4. What's the *rationale for* charging residents and not commuters?
5. The stated *rationale* and the actual reason were not the same thing.
6. Her *rationale for* keeping the old system is that the new one has never been tested.

**See also:** [reason for](#reason-for), [justification for](#justification-for), [basis for](#basis-for), [grounds for](#grounds-for), [excuse for](#excuse-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### reaction to

**Pronunciation:** /riˈækʃən tu/ &middot; *ree-AK-shun too* &middot; weak /riˈækʃən tə/
**Русский:** реакция на — рус. «на», не «к»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*her reaction to being passed over*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a reaction to be passed over*) and **never a clause**. *Reaction against* names a movement in opposition to something.
**Pattern:** *a reaction to* [event / substance] &middot; *a reaction against* [trend / movement] &middot; *an allergic reaction to* [substance] &middot; *in reaction to* [event] &middot; *provoke / gauge a reaction*
**Contrast:** ***To*** **for a response,** ***against*** **for a backlash.** *A reaction **to** modernism* is what people thought of it; *a reaction **against** modernism* is a movement rejecting it. ✗ *a reaction on the news* and ✗ *a reaction about the news* are errors, produced by borrowing from [comment on](#comment-on) and [opinion on/of/about](#opinion-onofabout). The *-ing* rule holds: a noun fits the slot (*a reaction to the news*), so ✗ *a reaction to be told* should be *to being told*. The verb keeps the preposition — *react **to** the news*, ✗ *react the news* — and the chemistry sense adds *with* (*sodium reacts with water*).

A response to something that has happened.

1. Her *reaction to* the news was to reread the letter twice.
2. The doctor thinks it's an allergic *reaction to* the new detergent.
3. The whole style was a *reaction against* the ornament of the previous decade.
4. Public *reaction to* the closure was louder than anyone at the district expected.
5. In *reaction to* the audit, three managers took early retirement.
6. His *reaction to* being corrected in public was predictably awful.

**See also:** [response to](#response-to), [reply to](#reply-to), [attitude to/toward](#attitude-totoward), [opposition to](#opposition-to), [react to](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#react-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### reason for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrizən fɔr/ &middot; *REE-zun for* &middot; weak /ˈrizən fɚ/
**Русский:** причина чего-л., основание для — reason why и reason that тоже норма
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: noun phrase or **gerund** (*the reason for the delay*, *the reason for leaving early*) — **never a to-infinitive after *for*** (✗ *the reason for to leave*). The same noun takes a bare **to-infinitive** meaning a justification (*a reason to leave*), a bare ***why*-clause** (*the reason why she left*), a bare ***that*-clause** (*the reason that she left*), and a **zero clause** (*the reason she left*).
**Pattern:** *the reason for* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a reason to* [verb] &middot; *the reason why* [clause] &middot; *the reason that* [clause] &middot; *the reason* ø [clause] &middot; *the reason is that* [clause] &middot; *for some reason* &middot; *by reason of* [noun phrase] &middot; *within reason*
**Variants:** *the reason why*, *the reason that*, and the bare *the reason* + clause are all standard American English; *why* is the most emphatic, the bare form the most conversational
**Contrast:** **The four good frames and the one bad one.** *The reason **for** the delay* takes a noun; *the reason **why / that / ø** she left* takes a clause; *a reason **to** leave* takes an infinitive and means a justification for a future act rather than an explanation of a past one. **The error is *the reason is because***: *because* already means "for the reason that," so ✗ *the reason is because he was sick* says it twice — write *the reason is **that** he was sick*, or just *he was sick*. Two further notes: ✗ *the reason of the delay* is not American English, and the old objection to *the reason why* as redundant is a nineteenth-century invention with no basis in the record; it has been in continuous good use since Middle English.

The explanation for something, or a justification for doing it.

1. Nobody has given us a *reason for* the delay.
2. The *reason for* leaving early was that the last ferry is at six.
3. That's the *reason why* she never puts anything in writing.
4. The *reason that* the deed was never filed is buried in a 1948 probate file.
5. There's every *reason to* believe the numbers will be revised.
6. The *reason* the road floods is that the culvert was sized for 1962.
7. The *reason is that* the county never accepted the dedication.

**See also:** [cause of](#cause-of), [grounds for](#grounds-for), [excuse for](#excuse-for), [point in/of](#point-inof), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### recipe for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛsəpi fɔr/ &middot; *RESS-uh-pee for* &middot; weak /ˈrɛsəpi fɚ/ &middot; three syllables, stress on the first; not */rɪˈsaɪp/*
**Русский:** рецепт чего-л. — кулинарный и переносный («верный способ получить»)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; the figurative frame is idiomatic and very common in American speech and journalism
**Complement:** a noun phrase or **gerund** (*a recipe for getting yourself fired*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a recipe to get yourself fired*) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a recipe for* [a dish] &middot; *a recipe for* [disaster / trouble / success] &middot; *follow / double / ruin a recipe* &middot; *a recipe calls for* [an ingredient] &middot; *my grandmother's recipe*
**Contrast:** ✗ ***A recipe of*** **is an error** in both the literal and the figurative sense. The figurative frame is nearly fixed and overwhelmingly negative: *a recipe for disaster*, *for trouble*, *for failure* — *a recipe for success* exists but sounds like a motivational poster. Note *the recipe calls for*, an idiom where the recipe itself does the asking, and note that *receipt* was once the same word and now means only a proof of purchase: ✗ *a receipt for banana bread* is a nineteenth-century usage that will read as a mistake.

A set of instructions for making a dish — or, figuratively, a reliable way of producing a result.

1. She still uses her grandmother's *recipe for* cornbread and refuses to write it down.
2. Cutting the inspection budget is a *recipe for* disaster.
3. Do you have a good *recipe for* pickled okra?
4. Three managers with the same authority is a *recipe for* nobody deciding anything.
5. The *recipe for* the sauce *calls for* twice as much butter as anyone admits.
6. Borrowing against a house to pay a credit card is a *recipe for* losing both.

**See also:** [cure for](#cure-for), [solution to](#solution-to), [way of/to](#way-ofto), [key to](#key-to), [plan for](#plan-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### recourse to/against

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrikɔrs tu/, /rɪˈkɔrs əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *REE-korss too, rih-KORSS uh-GENST* &middot; weak /ˈrikɔrs tə/ &middot; American stress varies: *REE-korss* and *rih-KORSS* are both current
**Русский:** юр. обращение к средству защиты; право регресса к должнику
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; legal and financial in the *against* and *without* frames
**Complement:** **to**: the remedy, authority, or means turned to, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*recourse to litigating every claim*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *recourse to sue*). **against**: the person or asset a creditor can proceed against. **Never a clause.**
**Pattern:** *recourse to* [the courts / force / an authority] &middot; *recourse against* [a borrower / a guarantor] &middot; *have / seek / be left with no recourse* &middot; *without recourse* &middot; *a non-recourse loan* &middot; *as a last recourse*
**Contrast:** ***To*** **is what you resort to,** ***against*** **is whom you can pursue.** *Recourse **to** arbitration* is a path; *recourse **against** the guarantor* is a target — and the American mortgage term *non-recourse* means the lender may take the house and nothing else. ✗ *recourse for* is an error. The bare *no recourse* is the everyday form (*the tenant has no recourse*), and *without recourse* written above a signature on a negotiable instrument disclaims liability. The verb is *resort **to***, not ✗ *recourse to* — *recourse* is a noun only in modern American usage.

The option of turning to some remedy or authority, and whom a creditor may pursue.

1. Tenants in that building have almost no *recourse* when the heat fails.
2. The clause forces *recourse to* arbitration before anyone files suit.
3. The lender has no *recourse against* her personally, only against the property.
4. He was left with no *recourse* but to sell at a loss.
5. Any *recourse to* the courts under this section waives the informal process.
6. She endorsed the note *without recourse* and slept better for it.

**See also:** [remedy for/against](#remedy-foragainst), [claim against/for/to](#claim-againstforto), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [lien on/against](#lien-onagainst), [resort to](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#resort-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### reference to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛfrəns tu/ &middot; *REF-runss too* &middot; weak /ˈrɛfrəns tə/
**Русский:** упоминание чего-л., ссылка на; также рекомендация или рекомендатель
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*a reference to having served overseas*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a reference to serve overseas*) and **never a that-clause**. In the employment sense, *a reference for* takes the person recommended.
**Pattern:** *a reference to* [thing mentioned] &middot; *a reference for* [job applicant] &middot; *with reference to* [noun phrase] &middot; *in reference to* [noun phrase] &middot; *make reference to* [noun phrase] &middot; *a frame of reference* &middot; *for future reference*
**Variants:** *with reference to* and *in reference to* — both formal correspondence openers; American editors cut both to *about* or *regarding* wherever possible. See [with reference to](10-topic-reference-role.md#with-reference-to)
**Contrast:** ***To*** **for a mention,** ***for*** **for a recommendation.** *A reference **to** the 1996 agreement* is a mention of it; *a reference **for** the applicant* is a letter vouching for her — ✗ *a reference of the applicant* is an error. The *-ing* rule holds because a noun fits the slot: *a reference to **being** overseas*, ✗ *a reference to be overseas*. Note that the phrasal opener *with reference to* is a preposition in its own right, treated in the [topic and reference](10-topic-reference-role.md) file, and that *without reference to* means "disregarding."

A mention of something, or a person who vouches for you.

1. The contract makes no *reference to* the easement at all.
2. There's a passing *reference to* the fire in the county history.
3. She listed two former supervisors as *references*.
4. I'm writing with *reference to* your letter of March 4.
5. The map was drawn without *reference to* the actual property lines.
6. Keep the confirmation number for future *reference*.

**See also:** [comment on](#comment-on), [report on/of](#report-onof), [connection with/to/between](#connection-withtobetween), [example of](#example-of), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### relationship with/between

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈleɪʃənʃɪp wɪθ/ &middot; *rih-LAY-shun-ship with* &middot; weak /rəˈleɪʃənʃəp wəθ/, /rəˈleɪʃənʃəp bəˈtwin/
**Русский:** отношения с кем-л.; связь, зависимость между величинами
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **with**: the other party (*a relationship with my father*). **between**: both parties or variables named from outside (*the relationship between diet and blood pressure*). **to**: a formal or structural connection (*her relationship to the deceased*). **Never a gerund after *with***, **never an infinitive**, **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a relationship with* [person / institution] &middot; *the relationship between* X *and* Y &middot; [someone's] *relationship to* [person / thing] &middot; *have / build / end a relationship with* [person] &middot; *in a relationship*
**Contrast:** ***With*** **is personal,** ***between*** **is external,** ***to*** **is structural.** *Her relationship **with** the victim* describes how they got along; *her relationship **to** the victim* asks how she is related — sister, employer, neighbor — and a police report that confuses the two produces a very different document. *Between* requires two named items: ✗ *the relationship between diet* is incomplete. ✗ *a relationship of* is an error except in formal compounds (*a relationship of trust*). In American speech *in a relationship* with no complement means romantically involved.

A connection between people, or between variables.

1. She has a complicated *relationship with* her father's second family.
2. The study found no *relationship between* the additive and the symptoms.
3. What is your *relationship to* the deceased?
4. The firm has had a *relationship with* that supplier since 1978.
5. The *relationship between* rainfall and yield is not linear.
6. They've been in a *relationship* for about two years.

**See also:** [connection with/to/between](#connection-withtobetween), [link between/to](#link-betweento), [marriage to](#marriage-to), [similarity to/between](#similarity-tobetween), [between](03-place-position.md#between)

### relevance to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛləvəns tu/ &middot; *REL-uh-vunss too* &middot; weak /ˈrɛləvəns tə/
**Русский:** отношение к делу, значимость для рассматриваемого вопроса
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; a working term in courtrooms, where an objection is simply "Relevance."
**Complement:** a noun phrase or **gerund** (*its relevance to choosing a site*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *relevance to choose a site*) and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *relevance to* [a question / a case / a decision] &middot; *the relevance of* X *to* Y &middot; *have / lack / question the relevance of* [noun phrase] &middot; *of no relevance* &middot; *contemporary relevance*
**Variants:** *relevance* and *relevancy* are both current in American English; *relevance* is the ordinary form and *relevancy* survives in evidence law (*the relevancy of the exhibit*)
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Relevance on*** **and** ✗ ***relevance for*** **are errors** — the noun takes *to*, matching the adjective [relevant to](15-dependent-adjectives.md#relevant-to). Against [bearing on](#bearing-on), which means the same thing with a different preposition: *no relevance **to** the case* and *no bearing **on** the case* are equivalent, and swapping the prepositions produces two errors at once. The full frame needs both halves when the source is named: *the relevance **of** his tax returns **to** the zoning question*.

The quality of having a bearing on the matter under discussion.

1. I don't see the *relevance to* anything we're deciding tonight.
2. Counsel objected to the *relevance of* the question *to* the charged conduct.
3. The 1974 study has real *relevance to* how the pipe was specified.
4. His military service is of no *relevance to* the permit application.
5. The committee questioned the survey's *relevance to* rural districts.
6. Half the exhibit was excluded for lack of *relevance*.

**See also:** [bearing on](#bearing-on), [applicability to](#applicability-to), [importance of/to](#importance-ofto), [connection with/to/between](#connection-withtobetween), [relevant to](15-dependent-adjectives.md#relevant-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### remedy for/against

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛmədi fɔr/, /ˈrɛmədi əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *REM-uh-dee for, REM-uh-dee uh-GENST* &middot; weak /ˈrɛmədi fɚ/; *against* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** средство от чего-л.; юр. способ правовой защиты
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral in the everyday sense; legal in the *against* frame and in *remedies at law*
**Complement:** **for**: the problem or ailment cured, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*a remedy for overcharging customers*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a remedy to overcharge*). **against**: the party a legal remedy runs against. **Never a clause.**
**Pattern:** *a remedy for* [a problem / an illness] &middot; *a remedy against* [a defendant] &middot; *seek / have / exhaust a remedy* &middot; *remedies at law and in equity* &middot; *a home remedy* &middot; *the exclusive remedy*
**Contrast:** ***For*** **the trouble,** ***against*** **the person.** *A remedy **for** the leak* fixes the leak; *a remedy **against** the seller* is what you can make him do. ✗ *a remedy to the problem* is an error, and it is the commonest one here, borrowed from [solution to](#solution-to) — which does take *to*. Note the American contract phrase *sole and exclusive remedy*, which limits everything a party can ask for, and *exhaust your remedies*, which means using every available process before going to court. In everyday speech, *a home remedy* takes *for* an ailment.

Something that cures a problem, and, in law, the relief a court can give.

1. There's no quick *remedy for* a foundation that has already shifted.
