2. The statute gives homeowners a *remedy against* the original builder.
3. Repair or replacement is the buyer's sole and exclusive *remedy* under this warranty.
4. Her grandmother's *remedy for* a cough involved more whiskey than honey.
5. Plaintiffs must exhaust administrative *remedies* before filing.
6. Firing the vendor is not a *remedy for* a process that was broken to begin with.

**See also:** [cure for](#cure-for), [solution to](#solution-to), [recourse to/against](#recourse-toagainst), [damages for](#damages-for), [injunction against](#injunction-against), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### reply to

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈplaɪ tu/ &middot; *rih-PLY too* &middot; weak /rəˈplaɪ tə/
**Русский:** ответ на письмо или реплику — рус. «на», не «к»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun naming the message answered (*a reply to my email*). **Never a gerund**, **never an infinitive**, **never a that-clause** in ordinary use. *In reply to* is the fixed formal opener.
**Pattern:** *a reply to* [message / person] &middot; *in reply to* [noun phrase] &middot; *a reply from* [sender] &middot; *await / receive a reply* &middot; *reply all*
**Contrast:** **The preposition survives the shift from verb to noun, which is what makes [answer to](#answer-to) treacherous.** *Reply* keeps *to* in both classes: *she replied **to** my email* and *a reply **to** my email* — so ✗ *she replied my email* is an error. *Answer* drops it in the verb and keeps it in the noun: *she answered my email*, *the answer to my email*. Learn the two as a pair. ✗ *a reply of my email* and ✗ *in reply of* are errors, and *reply* takes no direct object at all: ✗ *he replied me*.

An answer to a message or a statement.

1. I never got a *reply to* the second letter.
2. In *reply to* your inquiry, the fee schedule is attached.
3. His *reply to* the accusation was four words long.
4. There's been no *reply from* the county in six weeks.
5. She hit *reply* all by mistake and copied the whole board.
6. A prompt *reply to* the notice will keep the account open.

**See also:** [answer to](#answer-to), [response to](#response-to), [letter to/from](#letter-tofrom), [request for](#request-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### report on/of

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈpɔrt ɑn/, /rɪˈpɔrt ʌv/ &middot; *rih-PORT on, rih-PORT uv* &middot; weak /rəˈpɔrt ən/, /rəˈpɔrt əv/ → /rəˈpɔrt ə/
**Русский:** доклад, отчёт о чём-л.; также сообщение о происшествии
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **on**: a formal study of a subject — noun phrase or gerund (*a report on closing the plant*). **of**: an account that something occurred (*reports of gunfire*). **to**: the recipient or the supervisor. The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *reports that the levee had failed*. **Never a to-infinitive.**
**Pattern:** *a report on* [subject] &middot; *reports of* [occurrence] &middot; *a report to* [authority] &middot; *a report from* [source] &middot; *reports that* [clause] &middot; *file / issue / suppress a report on* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ***On*** **is a study;** ***of*** **is a rumor or an account.** *A report **on** gunfire* is a document analyzing it; *reports **of** gunfire* are people saying they heard it — a newsroom distinction with real consequences. *A report about the incident* is acceptable but vaguer than either. The verb has three frames worth separating: *report **on** the hearing* (write it up), *report the theft* (notify authorities, no preposition), and *report **to** the regional director* (be supervised by) — so ✗ *report about the theft* and ✗ *report to the theft* are both wrong.

A formal account of a subject, or word that something happened.

1. The consultant's *report on* deferred maintenance runs to 300 pages.
2. There were *reports of* looting on the second night.
3. She files a monthly *report to* the state board.
4. *Reports that* the levee had failed turned out to be premature.
5. The *report from* the field office contradicted the press release.
6. He was asked to write a short *report on* whether the software is worth renewing.

**See also:** [information about/on](#information-abouton), [research on/into](#research-oninto), [comment on](#comment-on), [evidence of/for](#evidence-offor), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### reputation for

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛpjəˈteɪʃən fɔr/ &middot; *rep-yuh-TAY-shun for* &middot; weak /ˌrɛpjəˈteɪʃən fɚ/
**Русский:** репутация, слава человека, известного каким-л. качеством
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a quality or an activity, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*a reputation for showing up unprepared*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a reputation to show up late*) and **never a clause**. **as**: a role or identity (*a reputation as a fixer*). **with / among**: whose opinion it is.
**Pattern:** *a reputation for* [a quality / verb-ing] &middot; *a reputation as* [a role] &middot; *a reputation with / among* [a group] &middot; *build / earn / ruin / live down a reputation* &middot; *by reputation* &middot; *a reputation to protect*
**Contrast:** ***For*** **takes a quality,** ***as*** **takes a role:** *a reputation **for** candor* and *a reputation **as** a straight shooter* say nearly the same thing with different grammar, and ✗ *a reputation as candor* is an error. *A reputation **of** being difficult* is heard everywhere and is still the unedited form — write *a reputation **for** being difficult*. Note the one place *to* does appear, and it is not a complement: *a reputation to protect* is a reduced relative clause (*a reputation that she has to protect*), not the noun's preposition.

What people generally believe about someone, named by the quality involved.

1. The shop has a *reputation for* honest estimates and a two-month wait.
2. She earned a *reputation for* saying the thing everyone else was avoiding.
3. His *reputation as* a closer did not survive the third quarter.
4. The firm has a bad *reputation with* subcontractors and a good one with banks.
5. A *reputation for* paying late follows a company from job to job.
6. He knew the judge only by *reputation* and it did not prepare him.

**See also:** [fondness for](#fondness-for), [talent for/knack for](#talent-forknack-for), [respect for](#respect-for), [opinion on/of/about](#opinion-onofabout), [known for](15-dependent-adjectives.md#known-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### request for

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkwɛst fɔr/ &middot; *rih-KWEST for* &middot; weak /rəˈkwɛst fɚ/
**Русский:** просьба о чём-л., запрос на — глагол request идёт без предлога
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase naming what is asked for (*a request for records*). The same noun takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition** (*a request to speak*) and a **that-clause with the subjunctive** (*a request that the fee be waived*). ✗ *a request for to speak* mixes them; ✗ *a request of records* is an error.
**Pattern:** *a request for* [thing asked for] &middot; *a request to* [verb] &middot; *a request that* [clause with subjunctive] &middot; *a request from* [asker] &middot; *at the request of* [person] &middot; *on request* &middot; *deny / grant / submit a request*
**Contrast:** **A textbook noun-versus-verb shift.** The verb *request* is **bare transitive** — *she requested the records*, ✗ *she requested for the records* — while the noun requires *for*. The error is reinforced by *ask*, which does take *for* (*ask for the records*), so the pair *ask for* / *request Ø* has to be memorized together. Note the American subjunctive after the noun: *a request that the fee **be** waived*, not ✗ *that the fee is waived*. *On request* and *at the request of* are fixed and take no article.

A formal asking for something.

1. The agency denied our *request for* the inspection records.
2. She submitted a written *request to* address the council.
3. The audit was conducted at the *request of* the state treasurer.
4. There's a standing *request for* volunteers on Saturday mornings.
5. A *request that* the penalty be waived is pending.
6. Copies of the plat are available on *request*.

**See also:** [demand for](#demand-for), [order for](#order-for), [invitation to](#invitation-to), [permission for](#permission-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### research on/into

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrisɜrtʃ ɑn/, /ˈrisɜrtʃ ˈɪntu/ &middot; *REE-surch on, REE-surch IN-too* &middot; weak /ˈrisɜrtʃ ən/, /ˈrisɜrtʃ ˈɪntə/
**Русский:** исследование чего-л. — в англ. неисчисляемо (✗ researches)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, gerund (*research into reducing infection rates*), or a *wh*-clause (*research on how children learn to read*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *research to reduce infection rates*, unless *to* introduces a purpose clause) and **never a that-clause**.
**Pattern:** *research on / into* [subject] &middot; *research in* [field] &middot; *do / conduct / publish research on* [subject] &middot; *a body of research on* [subject] &middot; *research shows that* [clause]
**Variants:** *research on* — the American default &middot; *research into* — implies an inquiry with a question to answer, slightly more investigative &middot; *research in* — a discipline (*research in organic chemistry*)
**Contrast:** **The countability rule is the trap and it costs credibility.** *Research* is **uncountable** in American English: ✗ *a research*, ✗ *researches*, ✗ *many researches*, ✗ *I did a research on it*. Say *a piece of research*, *a study*, *some research*, *a body of research*. The verb is likewise a problem: ✗ *make a research* — the verb is *do research* or the transitive *research the subject*, with no preposition. ✗ *research about* is an error, though it is spreading in student writing.

Systematic investigation of a subject.

1. There's surprisingly little *research on* long-term outcomes for this procedure.
2. Her *research into* nineteenth-century land grants took eleven years.
3. He does *research in* soil microbiology at the extension station.
4. The *research on* early reading intervention is fairly settled.
5. The lab published two pieces of *research* on the same sample.
6. *Research into* why the runs failed is still ongoing.

**See also:** [report on/of](#report-onof), [evidence of/for](#evidence-offor), [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), [insight into](#insight-into), [into](01-core-in-on-at.md#into)

### resistance to

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈzɪstəns tu/ &middot; *rih-ZISS-tunss too* &middot; weak /rəˈzɪstəns tə/
**Русский:** сопротивление чему-л., неприятие; также устойчивость к воздействию
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*resistance to being told what to do*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *resistance to change the schedule*) and **never a that-clause**; a *wh*-clause is possible (*resistance to what the board proposed*). A noun fits the slot (*resistance to change*), so the *to* is a preposition.
**Pattern:** *resistance to* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *meet / encounter / face / overcome* **resistance** &middot; *stiff / fierce / passive* **resistance** &middot; *resistance from* [party] &middot; *the path of least resistance*
**Variants:** *resistance against* — normal only with an occupying force (*resistance against the occupation*) &middot; *resistant to* — the adjective, same preposition and same *-ing* rule
**Contrast:** ***To*** **names what is being resisted;** ***from*** **names who is doing the resisting** — *resistance **to** the merger* against *resistance **from** the field offices*, and the two often appear together. ✗ *resistance of the merger* is an error, though *the resistance **of** the copper wire* is the unrelated electrical noun. The *-ing* trap is live here because *change* is the commonest complement and a verb is always lurking behind it: ✗ *resistance to change the schedule* has to be *resistance to changing the schedule*. The verb is bare — *they resisted the merger*, ✗ *they resisted to the merger* — one more verb-bare / noun-prepositional pair of the kind collected at the [end of this file](#zero-preposition-traps-verbs-that-take-no-preposition-at-all).

Refusal to accept something, or the act of opposing it.

1. There was fierce *resistance to* raising the assessment.
2. The plan met more *resistance* than anyone in the mayor's office expected.
3. *Resistance to* antibiotics is now the central problem on the ward.
4. She takes the path of least *resistance* every single time.
5. Most of the *resistance* came from the two oldest departments.
6. His *resistance to* being photographed made the profile nearly impossible.

**See also:** [opposition to](#opposition-to), [objection to](#objection-to), [reaction to](#reaction-to), [protection from/against](#protection-fromagainst), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### respect for

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈspɛkt fɔr/ &middot; *rih-SPEKT for* &middot; weak /rəˈspɛkt fɚ/
**Русский:** уважение к кому-л. — рус. «к»; ср. with respect to — «что касается»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a person, institution, or principle — noun phrase or gerund (*respect for doing the unglamorous work*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *respect to do the work*). The fixed phrases *with respect to* and *in respect of* are prepositions in their own right and mean "regarding," not "esteem."
**Pattern:** *respect for* [person / rule] &middot; *out of respect for* [person] &middot; *with respect to* [topic] &middot; *in this respect* &middot; *pay one's respects to* [person] &middot; *have / earn / lose respect for* [person]
**Variants:** *with respect to* — American &middot; *in respect of* — British and Commonwealth; see [in respect of](10-topic-reference-role.md#in-respect-of)
**Contrast:** **Two unrelated words share the spelling.** *Respect **for** the judge* is esteem; *with respect **to** the judge's ruling* means "concerning" it, and the two must not be blended — ✗ *respect to the judge* in the esteem sense is an error. *In this respect* means "in this particular way" and takes no complement. The plural *respects* appears only in *pay one's respects to* (attend a funeral or call formally). ✗ *respect of a person* is not American English. The verb takes a bare object: *she respects the process*, ✗ *she respects for the process*.

Esteem for a person or principle, and the unrelated "regarding" phrases.

1. He has enormous *respect for* anyone who does that job for twenty years.
2. *Respect for* the process is what keeps the hearings short.
3. Out of *respect for* the family, the names were withheld.
4. With *respect to* the second question, the county has no position.
5. The two proposals differ in one important *respect*.
6. Half the town came to pay their *respects*.

**See also:** [sympathy for/with](#sympathy-forwith), [opinion on/of/about](#opinion-onofabout), [attention to](#attention-to), [confidence in](#confidence-in), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### response to

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈspɑns tu/ &middot; *rih-SPONSS too* &middot; weak /rəˈspɑns tə/
**Русский:** ответ на, отклик на — рус. «на», не «к»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*a response to being asked twice*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a response to be asked*) and **never a that-clause**. *In response to* is the fixed connector.
**Pattern:** *a response to* [message / event] &middot; *in response to* [noun phrase] &middot; *a response from* [party] &middot; *elicit / draw a response* &middot; *the response of* [group] &middot; *an emergency response to* [event]
**Contrast:** ***To*** **for what is being responded to,** ***from*** **for who responds.** ✗ *a response of the letter* is an error, though *the response **of** the neighborhood* correctly names whose response it is. Distinguish from [reply to](#reply-to): a *reply* is specifically an answer to a message, while a *response* can be any reaction, which is why *emergency response* and *immune response* work and ✗ *emergency reply* does not. The verb keeps the preposition: *respond **to** the letter*, ✗ *respond the letter*.

An answer or reaction to something.

1. The department's *response to* the complaint took four months.
2. In *response to* the outcry, the board reopened public comment.
3. We got no *response from* the insurer until we hired a lawyer.
4. Her *response to* being asked twice was to leave the room.
5. The community *response to* the fundraiser was overwhelming.
6. Emergency *response* times in the north county average eleven minutes.

**See also:** [reply to](#reply-to), [reaction to](#reaction-to), [answer to](#answer-to), [request for](#request-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### responsibility for

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˌspɑnsəˈbɪləti fɔr/ &middot; *rih-spon-suh-BIL-uh-tee for* &middot; weak /rəˌspɑnsəˈbɪləti fɚ/
**Русский:** ответственность за что-л. — рус. «за», не «для»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase or **gerund** naming what one is answerable for (*responsibility for closing up*) — **never a to-infinitive after *for*** (✗ *responsibility for close up*). The noun separately takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition** (*a responsibility to report it*) and *to* + a person (*a responsibility to the shareholders*).
**Pattern:** *responsibility for* [thing / verb-ing] &middot; *a responsibility to* [person] &middot; *a responsibility to* [verb] &middot; *take / accept / deny responsibility for* [noun phrase] &middot; *claim responsibility for* [attack] &middot; *on one's own responsibility*
**Contrast:** ***For*** **is what you answer for;** ***to*** **is who you answer to** — *responsibility **for** the accounts* against *responsibility **to** the board*. Both can appear at once: *a responsibility to the board for the accounts*. The third frame, *a responsibility **to** report it*, is an infinitive, not the same *to*: the test is that a noun cannot replace it. ✗ *responsibility of the accounts* is an error, though *the responsibility **of** the treasurer* correctly names whose duty it is. Note *claim responsibility for*, the fixed news idiom.

The duty to answer for something, and to whom.

1. She has *responsibility for* everything east of the river.
2. Nobody wanted to take *responsibility for* the missing key.
3. The board has a fiduciary *responsibility to* the members.
4. You have a *responsibility to* report it within twenty-four hours.
5. No group has claimed *responsibility for* the vandalism.
6. *Responsibility for* closing up rotates weekly.

**See also:** [authority on/over](#authority-onover), [control over/of](#control-overof), [apology for](#apology-for), [need for](#need-for), [responsible for](15-dependent-adjectives.md#responsible-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### result of

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈzʌlt ʌv/ &middot; *rih-ZULT uv* &middot; weak /rəˈzʌlt əv/ → /rəˈzʌlt ə/
**Русский:** результат, следствие чего-л. — рус. родительный
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase or gerund (*the result of cutting the maintenance budget*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a that-clause**. **from** appears with the verb, not the noun: *the damage resulted from the leak*.
**Pattern:** *the result of* [cause] &middot; *as a result of* [noun phrase] &middot; *as a result* (no object) &middot; *the results of* [test / election] &middot; *with the result that* [clause] &middot; *without result*
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is the only preposition for the noun**: ✗ *the result from the leak*, ✗ *the result to the leak*. The verb is where *from* and *in* live, and they point in opposite directions: *X results **from** Y* means Y caused X, while *X results **in** Y* means X caused Y — reversing them inverts the sentence. *As a result of* is a fixed causal connector treated at [as a result of](06-cause-purpose.md#as-a-result-of); bare *as a result* takes no object and works as a sentence adverb. Compare [consequence of](#consequence-of), which implies something unwelcome where *result* is neutral.

What something produced.

1. The backlog is a direct *result of* two unfilled positions.
2. As a *result of* the storm, the county offices closed for three days.
3. The *results of* the soil test came back on Friday.
4. Higher rents are the predictable *result of* building nothing for a decade.
5. The line was down for six hours; as a *result*, the whole shift was lost.
6. Two years of appeals, and without *result*.

**See also:** [consequence of](#consequence-of), [effect on](#effect-on), [cause of](#cause-of), [impact on](#impact-on), [result in, result from](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#result-in-result-from), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### return on

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈtɜrn ɑn/ &middot; *rih-TURN on* &middot; weak /rɪˈtɜrn ən/
**Русский:** доходность чего-л., отдача от вложений — return on investment
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** financial and neutral; *return on investment* is ordinary American business speech
**Complement:** **on**: what was invested, as a noun phrase — **never a gerund** (✗ *a return on buying the building*; write *a return on the building*), **never an infinitive**, and **never a clause**. **of**: the rate or the amount (*a return of nine percent*), or the giving back of a thing (*the return of the deposit*). **to**: a place or a state, in the ordinary movement sense.
**Pattern:** *a return on* [investment / capital / equity / assets] &middot; *a return of* [a percentage] &middot; *return on investment (ROI)* &middot; *the return of* [a thing / a person] &middot; *a return to* [a place / normal] &middot; *in return for* [something] &middot; *a tax return* (compound)
**Contrast:** ***On*** **names what the money was in,** ***of*** **names how much came back**, and the two stack exactly as in [tax on](#tax-on): *a return **of** nine percent **on** capital*. ✗ *a return of the investment* means the principal came back, not that it earned anything — a distinction traders make out loud ("I'm less worried about the return **on** my money than the return **of** my money"). Keep the homographs apart: *a tax return* is a form, *the return of the deposit* is a refund, *a return to* the office is a movement, and only *return **on*** is the yield.

The gain produced by an investment, named by what was invested in.

1. The *return on* the endowment was four percent after fees.
2. Nobody could show a *return on* the eleven million the city spent downtown.
3. They want a *return of* at least eight percent *on* invested capital.
4. The *return on* equity looks good until you notice the leverage.
5. He filed the tax *return* in October and the refund came in March.
6. In *return for* the easement, the utility repaved the whole driveway.

**See also:** [premium over/on](#premium-overon), [interest in](#interest-in), [benefit of/to](#benefit-ofto), [tax on](#tax-on), [share in/of](#share-inof), [in return for](11-complex-multiword.md#in-return-for)

### right to/of/over

**Pronunciation:** /raɪt tu/, /raɪt ʌv/, /raɪt ˈoʊvɚ/ &middot; *ryte too, ryte uv, ryte OH-ver* &middot; weak /raɪt tə/, /raɪt əv/ → /raɪt ə/; *over* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** право на что-л., право сделать что-л.; over — право на чужое имущество
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; the central noun of American constitutional and property law
**Complement:** **to**: **either** a noun phrase (*the right to counsel*, *a right to privacy*) **or a bare infinitive** (*the right to remain silent*) — both are standard and the *to* is a different word in each. **Never a gerund after *to*** (✗ *the right to remaining silent*). **of**: whose right it is (*the right of the people*), or a fixed compound (*the right of way*). **over**: property or persons controlled (*rights over the mineral estate*). **in**: an interest in an intangible (*rights in the copyright*).
**Pattern:** *the right to* [noun phrase] &middot; *the right to* [verb] &middot; *the right of* [a person] *to* [verb] &middot; *rights over* [property] &middot; *rights in* [a work] &middot; *have / assert / waive / reserve a right* &middot; *within one's rights* &middot; *by rights* &middot; *the right of way*
**Contrast:** **The noun-slot test, one more time.** *The right **to** an attorney* takes a noun; *the right **to** confront the witnesses* takes a bare verb; ✗ *the right to confronting* is impossible either way. Against [entitlement to](#entitlement-to): a right is held against the world, an entitlement is a specific benefit owed under a program. ✗ *the right of vote* and ✗ *the right for privacy* are errors. Note the fixed compounds that drop the preposition entirely — *civil rights*, *water rights*, *mineral rights*, *voting rights*, *the right of way* — and the adverbial *by rights*, which means "properly speaking" and takes no complement.

A legally or morally protected claim to do something or to have something.

1. You have the *right to* remain silent, and almost nobody exercises it.
2. The lease gives the tenant a *right of* first refusal on the adjoining unit.
3. The ranch was sold without the mineral *rights over* the eastern section.
4. She reserved all *rights in* the photographs, including reprints.
5. He was within his *rights to* refuse entry without a warrant.
6. The *right of* way belongs to whoever is already in the intersection.

**See also:** [entitlement to](#entitlement-to), [claim against/for/to](#claim-againstforto), [duty to/of](#duty-toof), [title to](#title-to), [easement over/across](#easement-overacross), [access to](#access-to)

### rise in/fall in

**Pronunciation:** /raɪz ɪn/, /fɔl ɪn/ &middot; *ryze in, fawl in* &middot; weak /raɪz ən/, /fɔl ən/
**Русский:** рост чего-л. / падение чего-л. — рус. родительный, не «в»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **in**: the thing that rose or fell (*a rise in unemployment*). **of**: the size of the change (*a rise of two points*). **to / from**: the endpoints. Gerunds follow *in*; **never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a rise / fall in* [thing measured] &middot; *a rise of* [amount] &middot; *a rise from* X *to* Y &middot; *on the rise* &middot; *the rise and fall of* [empire / person] &middot; *give rise to* [noun phrase]
**Variants:** *a rise in* and *an increase in* are interchangeable in American English; see [increase in/decrease in](#increase-indecrease-in) &middot; **but** a pay increase is *a raise* in American English and *a rise* in British English
**Contrast:** ***In*** **for what moved,** ***of*** **for how far** — the same split as [increase in/decrease in](#increase-indecrease-in), and the same error: ✗ *a rise of unemployment* should be *a rise **in** unemployment*. **The American-British split on wages is worth knowing:** *she got a raise* is American, *she got a rise* is British and reads as a different word entirely to American ears. Note the fixed *give rise to* (cause), which is not this pattern at all, and *the rise and fall of*, which takes *of* because it is a narrative title frame.

An upward or downward movement in a measured quantity.

1. There's been a steady *rise in* emergency room visits since June.
2. A *fall in* enrollment of nine percent closed two schools.
3. The index showed a *rise of* four points in a single session.
4. Bicycle theft is on the *rise* downtown.
5. The book traces the *rise and fall of* the local canning industry.
6. The ambiguity in the deed gave *rise to* forty years of litigation.

**See also:** [increase in/decrease in](#increase-indecrease-in), [improvement in/on](#improvement-inon), [change in/of](#change-inof), [progress in/on](#progress-inon), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### risk of

**Pronunciation:** /rɪsk ʌv/ &middot; *risk uv* &middot; weak /rɪsk əv/ → /rɪsk ə/
**Русский:** риск чего-л., опасность того, что нечто плохое случится
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase or **gerund** naming the bad outcome (*the risk of infection*, *the risk of losing everything*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the risk to lose everything*). **to**: the person or thing endangered (*a risk to public health*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *the risk that the seal fails*.
**Pattern:** *the risk of* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a risk to* [person / thing endangered] &middot; *at risk of* [verb-ing] &middot; *run the risk of* [verb-ing] &middot; *at the risk of* [verb-ing] &middot; *take a risk* &middot; *at your own risk*
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is what might go wrong;** ***to*** **is who is exposed** — *the risk **of** contamination* against *a risk **to** the water supply*. ✗ *a risk to contamination* is the error. **Every one of the fixed frames requires *-ing***: *at risk of losing the house*, *run the risk of being sued*, *at the risk of stating the obvious* — ✗ *at risk to lose*, ✗ *run the risk to be sued*. *Risk for* belongs to American medical writing (*a risk factor for stroke*, *at risk for readmission*) and reads as clinical jargon anywhere else, so ✗ *the risk for a strike* should be *the risk of a strike*. The verb takes a gerund too: *he risked losing his license*, ✗ *he risked to lose his license*.

The chance that something bad happens, and who it happens to.

1. Every additional day in the hospital raises the *risk of* infection.
2. The unsecured tank is a *risk to* everyone downstream.
3. Two hundred households are at *risk of* losing coverage in January.
4. You run the *risk of* voiding the warranty if you open it.
5. At the *risk of* stating the obvious, somebody has to sign it.
6. Swim at your own *risk*; there is no lifeguard after Labor Day.

**See also:** [danger of/to](#danger-ofto), [chance of](#chance-of), [possibility of](#possibility-of), [threat to](#threat-to), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### role in

**Pronunciation:** /roʊl ɪn/ &middot; *rohl in* &middot; weak /roʊl ən/
**Русский:** роль в чём-л. — участие в событии, организации или сюжете
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **in**: the event, process, or organization, as a noun phrase or **gerund** (*a role in raising the money*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a role to raise the money*, unless *to raise* is a purpose clause). **as**: the identity or part played (*her role as treasurer*). **for**: who or what should have a part (*a role for the county*). **on**: a team or a cast, in American usage.
**Pattern:** *a role in* [an event / verb-ing] &middot; *a role as* [a part / a position] &middot; *a role for* [a person / an institution] &middot; *a role on* [a team / a show] &middot; *play / have / take on a role* &middot; *a leading / supporting / minor role*
**Contrast:** ***In*** **for what you took part in,** ***as*** **for who you were, and both can appear at once**: *her role **as** treasurer **in** the reorganization*. ✗ *a role of treasurer* is an error, and ✗ *a role to play in the merger* is fine only because *to play* is a reduced relative — the noun's own preposition is still *in*. American usage note: a part in a cast or on a team takes *on* (*his role **on** the show*, *her role **on** the defense*), which surprises learners who have been taught that *in* is the only option. ✗ *a role at the decision* is an error.

The part someone or something plays in an event, an organization, or a story.

1. She had no *role in* the decision and said so in writing.
2. His *role as* interim director lasted nineteen months.
3. Salt plays a bigger *role in* baking bread than most people think.
4. There's a *role for* the state here, and nobody wants to fund it.
5. He had a small *role on* a show nobody remembers.
6. Her *role in* raising the money was larger than the plaque suggests.

**See also:** [share in/of](#share-inof), [contribution to](#contribution-to), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [influence on/over](#influence-onover), [participate in](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#participate-in), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### room for

**Pronunciation:** /rum fɔr/ &middot; *room for* &middot; weak /rum fɚ/
**Русский:** место для чего-л.; перен. простор для — room for improvement: есть куда расти
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase naming what fits (*room for one more*, *room for improvement*). The same noun takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition**: *room to breathe*, *room to maneuver*. ✗ *room for to breathe* mixes them; ✗ *room of* is an error.
**Pattern:** *room for* [noun phrase] &middot; *room to* [verb] &middot; *make room for* [noun phrase] &middot; *no room for* [noun phrase] &middot; *room for improvement* &middot; *leave room for* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ***For*** **takes a thing;** ***to*** **takes an act.** *Room **for** doubt* is space for a thing; *room **to** doubt* is freedom to act — both are English, and *room for doubting* is not. ✗ *room for improve* is the error to watch. In this pattern *room* is **uncountable** and takes no article (*there's room for one more*, ✗ *there's a room for one more*, which would mean a spare bedroom). *Make room for* and *room for improvement* are fixed.

Available space, literal or figurative.

1. There's *room for* one more at the end of the table.
2. The report shows *room for* improvement in almost every category.
3. The lease leaves no *room for* interpretation.
4. Move the chair and give him *room to* work.
5. We made *room for* the piano by getting rid of the sofa.
6. There's not much *room for* error on a one-lane bridge.

**See also:** [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [chance of](#chance-of), [need for](#need-for), [supply of](#supply-of), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### rule against/for

**Pronunciation:** /rul əˈɡɛnst/, /rul fɔr/ &middot; *rool uh-GENST, rool for* &middot; weak /rul əˈɡɛnst/, /rul fɚ/
**Русский:** правило, запрещающее / предписывающее что-л.; норма, принцип
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **against**: a noun phrase or **gerund** naming the prohibited act (*a rule against smoking*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a rule against smoke*). **on**: the topic a rule governs (*the rules on overtime*). **for**: whom or what the rules apply to (*the rules for the tournament*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *a rule that all bids be sealed*.
**Pattern:** *a rule against* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *the rules on* [topic] &middot; *the rules for* [event / group] &middot; *a rule that* [clause] &middot; *as a rule* &middot; *a rule of thumb* &middot; *break / bend / enforce a rule*
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions, three angles.** *A rule **against** parking overnight* prohibits; *the rules **on** parking* govern the subject; *the rules **for** residents* apply to a group. ✗ *a rule to park overnight* is an error, and ✗ *a rule of parking* is wrong outside fixed compounds. *As a rule* means "usually" and takes no complement, while *a rule of thumb* is a rough guide. The related verb splits by sense: *rule **on** the motion* (decide it), *rule **against** the plaintiff* (decide adversely), *rule **out** the possibility* (eliminate it).

A prohibition, requirement, or governing principle.

1. There's a *rule against* parking on the street after two in the morning.
2. The *rules on* overtime changed in January and nobody told the crews.
3. The *rules for* the tournament fit on one index card.
4. The board adopted a *rule that* all bids be sealed.
5. As a *rule*, I don't sign anything on the same day I read it.
6. A good *rule of thumb* is one cup of water to two of rice.

**See also:** [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [exception to](#exception-to), [order for](#order-for), [permission for](#permission-for), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)


## S

### sanction against/sanctions on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsæŋkʃən əˈɡɛnst/, /ˈsæŋkʃənz ɑn/ &middot; *SANK-shun uh-GENST, SANK-shunz on* &middot; weak /ˈsæŋkʃənz ən/; *against* has **no weak form**
**Русский:** санкции против кого-л.; в ед. ч. — наоборот, официальное разрешение
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — foreign-policy reporting, Treasury press releases, and litigation. An American meets the plural in a headline about Iran or Russia and the singular in a judge's order sanctioning a lawyer who missed a deadline.
**Complement:** **on / against**: a noun phrase naming a country, a person, a company, or a sector (*sanctions on Russian banks*, *sanctions against the junta*). **for**: the conduct being punished (*sanctions for discovery abuse*). **of**: in the approval sense, the body that gives permission (*the sanction of Congress*). **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *sanctions to export oil*) and **never a that-clause**. A gerund after *on* is possible in the ban sense (*sanctions on doing business with the regime*) but reads as compressed journalese; [prohibition on/against](#prohibition-onagainst) is the natural frame for an act.
**Pattern:** *impose / lift / tighten / ease sanctions on* [country / entity] &middot; *sanctions against* [regime / individual] &middot; *economic / financial / secondary sanctions* &middot; *a sanction for* [misconduct] &middot; *under sanctions* &middot; *with the sanction of* [authority] &middot; *give sanction to* [an act]
**Variants:** *sanctions on* and *sanctions against* are interchangeable for a target; *on* is commoner in American trade and Treasury writing, *against* in political and historical prose &middot; the singular *a sanction* in the penalty sense is normal only in law (*a sanction for late filing*); elsewhere the plural is fixed
**Contrast:** **One spelling, two opposite meanings.** Plural *sanctions **on** a country* are punishments; singular *the sanction **of** the crown* is permission — and the verb inherits both, so *the court sanctioned the attorney* punishes her while *the board sanctioned the merger* approves it. Context is the only guide, which is why careful American writers reach for *penalize* or *approve* when the stakes are high. Against [embargo on](#embargo-on): an embargo is one total ban on trade, sanctions are a package that may contain one. Against [prohibition on/against](#prohibition-onagainst): a prohibition forbids an act, sanctions punish an actor. Against [penalty for](#penalty-for): a penalty is a single consequence, sanctions are a policy. ✗ *sanctions of Iran* and ✗ *sanctions to Iran* are both errors.

Coercive penalties imposed on a state, a company, or a person — and, in the singular, official permission.

**1. Penalties meant to force a change in behavior.** Nearly always plural. *On* and *against* both name the target; *for* names the offense, which is the standard frame in American litigation, where a judge imposes *sanctions for* misconduct.

1. The Treasury imposed *sanctions on* three shipping companies the same afternoon.
2. *Sanctions against* the generals were lifted as soon as the vote was certified.
3. Europe has been slower than Washington to tighten *sanctions on* the energy sector.
4. The judge threatened *sanctions for* any further delay in producing the emails.

**2. Official approval or authorization.** Singular, uncountable, formal and faintly old-fashioned; it survives mainly in *with the sanction of* and *without the sanction of*.

5. The raid went ahead without the *sanction of* Congress.
6. No agreement of that size carries weight without the *sanction of* the states that signed it.

**See also:** [embargo on](#embargo-on), [prohibition on/against](#prohibition-onagainst), [penalty for](#penalty-for), [injunction against](#injunction-against), [tariff on/duty on/levy on](#tariff-onduty-onlevy-on), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### search for

**Pronunciation:** /sɜrtʃ fɔr/ &middot; *surch for* &middot; weak /sɜrtʃ fɚ/
**Русский:** поиск, поиски чего-л.; отдельно — обыск помещения
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase or gerund naming what is sought (*the search for a new director*). **Never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the search to find a director*) and **never a that-clause**; a *wh*-clause is fine (*the search for what caused the outage*). The fixed phrase is *in search of* + noun phrase, with no article before *search*.
**Pattern:** *the search for* [thing sought] &middot; *in search of* [noun phrase] &middot; *a search of* [place] &middot; *call off / widen a search* &middot; *a search warrant for* [premises]
**Contrast:** ***For*** **is what you are looking for;** ***of*** **is the place being looked through.** *A search **for** the missing hiker* is an effort to find him; *a search **of** the cabin* is going through it — police reports depend on the difference, and ✗ *a search for the cabin* would mean the cabin itself is lost. **The verb carries the same split:** *search **for** the keys* (try to find them) against *search the drawer* (go through it, no preposition) — so ✗ *search for the suspect* when you mean frisking him is a real error. Note *in search of*, never ✗ *in search for*.

An effort to find something, or an examination of a place.

1. The *search for* a new superintendent has been going on since March.
2. A *search of* the storage unit turned up the missing ledgers.
3. They went out in *search of* firewood and came back with a dog.
4. The *search for* the missing hiker was called off at dusk on the fourth day.
5. Police executed a *search* warrant at two addresses.
6. Her *search for* an affordable rental within the district lasted eight months.

**See also:** [demand for](#demand-for), [need for](#need-for), [research on/into](#research-oninto), [request for](#request-for), [search for](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#search-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### share in/of

**Pronunciation:** /ʃɛr ɪn/, /ʃɛr ʌv/ &middot; *shair in, shair uv* &middot; weak /ʃɛr ən/, /ʃɛr əv/ → /ʃɛr ə/
**Русский:** доля чего-л.; участие, пай в чём-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: a portion of a divisible whole (*a share of the profits*). **in**: a stake in an enterprise or a part in an activity, and here a **gerund** is possible (*a share in running the place*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a share in run the place*).
**Pattern:** *a share of* [divisible whole] &middot; *a share in* [company / activity] &middot; *shares in* [a company] &middot; *do one's share of* [work] &middot; *a fair share of* [noun phrase] &middot; *the lion's share of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is a slice;** ***in*** **is a stake.** *A share **of** the company's profits* is money; *a share **in** the company* is ownership — and in American usage stock is *shares **in** Ford* or, more commonly, *shares **of** Ford stock*. ✗ *a share to the profits* is an error. Note the idiom *had my share of* + noun, which means "more than enough" rather than a fair portion (*I've had my share of bad landlords*), and *the lion's share*, which despite the fable now means most of it.

A portion of something, or a stake in something.

1. Each partner gets a *share of* the net after expenses.
2. She holds a small *share in* the brewery her cousin started.
3. He's had more than his *share of* bad luck with contractors.
4. The state's *share of* the settlement was under two million.
5. I want a *share in* deciding how the money is spent.
6. The lion's *share of* the grant went to administrative costs.

**See also:** [interest in](#interest-in), [supply of](#supply-of), [contribution to](#contribution-to), [responsibility for](#responsibility-for), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### shot at/crack at

**Pronunciation:** /ʃɑt æt/, /kræk æt/ &middot; *shot at, krak at* &middot; weak /ʃɑt ət/, /kræk ət/; the *k* of *crack* is fully pronounced, unlike the silent one in *knack*
**Русский:** разг. попытка, шанс попробовать — «дай-ка я попробую»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** **informal American English** — speech, email, and sports writing. In a brief, a grant application, or a scientific paper, use [attempt at](#attempt-at) or *an opportunity to* instead.
**Complement:** a noun phrase (*a shot at the title*) or a **gerund** (*a crack at fixing it*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a shot to make the team*, ✗ *a crack to fix it*) and **never a clause**. The string *a shot to win the game* is grammatical but is a different noun altogether: that is the basketball shot itself, not the chance of winning.
**Pattern:** *have / get / take a shot at* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *give someone a shot at* [noun phrase] &middot; *take a crack at* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a first / second / last crack at* [noun phrase] &middot; *a fair shot at* [noun phrase] &middot; *a long shot* (no complement)
**Variants:** *take a stab at* and *have a go at* belong to the same set and behave identically, taking a noun phrase or a gerund and refusing the infinitive &middot; British *have a bash at* is not American
**Contrast:** **Both mean a try, and neither takes an infinitive** — which is the whole trap, because the neutral synonyms split the other way: *a chance **to** speak* and *an opportunity **to** speak* take the bare verb, while *a shot **at** speaking* and *a crack **at** speaking* demand the *-ing* form. Between the two, a *shot at* is a chance at something worth winning and a *crack at* is a turn at a job, often one somebody else has already botched — *give me a crack at the title* sounds off, and *a shot at the wiring* sounds like gunfire. Against [attempt at](#attempt-at), the same shape one register up, and [chance of](#chance-of), which is a probability rather than an opportunity.

An informal chance or turn at something.

1. The kid finally got a *shot at* the starting job in August.
2. Give me a *crack at* the wiring before you call an electrician.
3. She wants a *shot at* running the Denver office.
4. Everybody in the family has taken a *crack at* fixing that gate.
5. He never got a fair *shot at* the promotion and he knows it.
6. It's a long *shot*, but a second *crack at* the exam costs nothing.

**See also:** [attempt at](#attempt-at), [chance of](#chance-of), [handle on](#handle-on), [success in/at](#success-inat), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### signatory to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɪɡnəˌtɔri tu/ &middot; *SIG-nuh-tor-ee too* &middot; weak /ˈsɪɡnəˌtɔri tə/
**Русский:** подписант чего-л. — государство, компания или лицо, подписавшее документ
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and legal — treaties, contracts, and diplomatic reporting. An American meets it in a story about the Paris Agreement, on the signature page of a company's credit agreement, and on the bank card that lists who may sign checks.
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming **a document** — a treaty, an agreement, a convention, a charter (*a signatory to the convention*) — **never a person** (✗ *a signatory to the president*), **never a gerund** (✗ *a signatory to reducing emissions*), and **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a signatory to reduce emissions*). The *to* is a preposition, and what follows it has to be the thing that was signed.
**Pattern:** *a signatory to* [a treaty / an agreement] &middot; *the signatories to this Agreement* &middot; *an original / founding signatory* &middot; *become / remain a signatory to* [noun phrase] &middot; *a signatory of* [a letter] &middot; *a signatory on* [an account]
**Variants:** *a signatory of* — standard for a single letter or memo, where *of* is in fact the commoner choice; *to* is the form for treaties and multiparty agreements &middot; in American banking the fixed phrase is *a signatory **on** the account*, not *to* it
**Contrast:** ***To*** **for the instrument you joined,** ***of*** **for the paper you put your name at the bottom of.** *A signatory **to** the Geneva Conventions* is a state that acceded to them; *the signatory **of** the memo* is one person. Against [party to](#party-to): every signatory is a party, but a party can be bound without ever signing — a successor, a guarantor, or a company bound by its own conduct. Note that signing and ratifying are not the same act, which is why a country can be a signatory to a treaty for decades without being bound by it. Keep the word clear of *signature* (the mark) and *signer* (the everyday term); ✗ *a signature to the treaty* is an error.

A state, company, or person that has signed a formal instrument.

1. The United States is a *signatory to* the convention but has never ratified it.
2. All *signatories to* the agreement must be given thirty days' notice.
3. Only two of the founding *signatories* are still in the alliance.
4. Her mother is the only other *signatory on* the checking account.
5. Canada became a *signatory to* the treaty in 1994 and a party to it two years later.
6. The letter went out over the names of forty *signatories*.

**See also:** [party to](#party-to), [beneficiary of/under](#beneficiary-ofunder), [successor to](#successor-to), [exhibit to/schedule to/annex to](#exhibit-toschedule-toannex-to), [warranty as to](#warranty-as-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### similarity to/between

**Pronunciation:** /ˌsɪməˈlɛrəti tu/ &middot; *sim-uh-LAIR-uh-tee too* &middot; weak /ˌsɪməˈlɛrəti tə/, /ˌsɪməˈlɛrəti bəˈtwin/
**Русский:** сходство с чем-л. / между чем-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **to**: a single item resembled (*a similarity to the original*). **between**: two or more items joined by *and* (*similarities between the two accounts*). **in**: the respect in which they are alike (*a similarity in tone*). **Never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a similarity to* [item] &middot; *similarities between* X *and* Y &middot; *a similarity in* [respect] &middot; *bear a similarity to* [noun phrase] &middot; *any similarity to* [persons living or dead]
**Variants:** *similarity with* — occurs and is not wrong, but *to* is the American default for the one-item frame
**Contrast:** ***To*** **for the model,** ***between*** **for the pair, and never ✗ *similarity from***. The adjective is *similar **to*** (*similar to the original*, ✗ *similar with*, ✗ *similar as*), so for once the noun and the adjective agree — which makes this the mirror image of [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin), where the noun takes *between* and the adjective takes *from*. ✗ *a similarity of the original* is an error where *a similarity to the original* is meant; the *of* frame works only when it names the pair whose likeness is at issue (*the similarity of the two accounts*), and even there *between* is the plainer choice. Note the standard film disclaimer: *any similarity to actual persons is coincidental*.

Resemblance between things, and the respect in which they resemble.

1. The second signature bears an obvious *similarity to* the first.
2. There are unsettling *similarities between* the two collapses.
3. The *similarity in* tone between the anonymous letters was the giveaway.
4. Any *similarity to* actual events is, the publisher insists, coincidental.
5. The *similarities between* the dialects run deeper than the vocabulary.
6. Its *similarity to* a species found only in Chile puzzled everyone.

**See also:** [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin), [comparison with/to](#comparison-withto), [contrast to/with](#contrast-towith), [link between/to](#link-betweento), [similar to](15-dependent-adjectives.md#similar-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### solution to

**Pronunciation:** /səˈluʃən tu/ &middot; *suh-LOO-shun too* &middot; weak /səˈluʃən tə/
**Русский:** решение задачи или проблемы — рус. родительный, не «к»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **to**: a problem — noun phrase or gerund (*a solution to getting the water out*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a solution to get the water out*). **for**: an intended market or user, in product writing (*a solution for small businesses*). **of**: chemistry and mathematics only (*a solution of sodium chloride*, *the solution of the equation*).
**Pattern:** *a solution to* [problem] &middot; *a solution for* [user / market] &middot; *a solution of* [equation / compound] &middot; *find / propose a solution to* [noun phrase] &middot; *part of the solution*
**Contrast:** ***To*** **for a problem,** ***of*** **only in the laboratory.** ✗ *a solution of the parking problem* is a common error produced by translation from French and German; the English is *a solution **to** the parking problem*. *A solution **of** salt* is a liquid mixture and *the solution **of** the equation* is mathematics — neither has anything to do with problem-solving. *A solution for the parking problem* is heard in business writing and reads as vendor jargon; keep *for* for the audience (*a solution for hospitals*) and *to* for the problem. The *-ing* rule applies: *a solution to **getting** the water out*, ✗ *to get the water out*.

An answer to a problem.

1. There's no cheap *solution to* the drainage problem on that lot.
2. The county proposed three *solutions to* the parking shortage and adopted none.
3. The vendor sells a scheduling *solution for* small clinics.
4. A saturated *solution of* sodium chloride will not dissolve any more salt.
5. The only *solution to* getting the water out is a bigger pump.
6. If you're not part of the *solution*, you know the rest.

**See also:** [answer to](#answer-to), [key to](#key-to), [obstacle to](#obstacle-to), [problem with](#problem-with), [cure for](#cure-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### substitute for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsʌbstɪtut fɔr/ &middot; *SUB-stih-toot for* &middot; weak /ˈsʌbstɪtut fɚ/
**Русский:** замена чему-л., заменитель — то, что используют вместо
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase naming what is being replaced (*a substitute for butter*). Gerunds occur (*no substitute for showing up*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *no substitute to show up*). ✗ *a substitute of* and ✗ *a substitute to* are errors.
**Pattern:** *a substitute for* [thing replaced] &middot; *substitute* X *for* Y (verb) &middot; *substitute for* [person] (verb, fill in) &middot; *there is no substitute for* [noun phrase] &middot; *a substitute teacher*
**Variants:** *substitute X with Y* — a newer American verb frame, now common and accepted in speech, still resisted by careful editors, who prefer *replace X with Y* or *substitute Y for X*
**Contrast:** **The verb reverses the roles and that is the whole difficulty.** *Substitute margarine **for** butter* means margarine goes in and butter comes out — the **new** thing follows the verb and the **old** thing follows *for*. Because that feels backward, people write ✗ *substitute butter for margarine* when they mean the opposite, and the sentence says the reverse of what they intend. The noun is safer: *a substitute **for** butter* is always the replacement. When in doubt, use *replace X with Y*, which nobody misreads. Note *substitute **for** the regular teacher* (fill in for), where the verb is intransitive.

Something used in place of something else.

1. Applesauce is a decent *substitute for* oil in that recipe.
2. There's no *substitute for* showing up and asking in person.
3. She worked as a *substitute* in three districts that year.
4. The recipe says to *substitute* buttermilk *for* the cream.
5. Video calls are a poor *substitute for* being in the room.
6. He *substituted for* the regular organist all through Lent.

**See also:** [alternative to](#alternative-to), [preference for](#preference-for), [comparison with/to](#comparison-withto), [supply of](#supply-of), [substitute for](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#substitute-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### success in/at

**Pronunciation:** /səkˈsɛs ɪn/, /səkˈsɛs æt/ &middot; *suk-SESS in, suk-SESS at* &middot; weak /səkˈsɛs ən/, /səkˈsɛs ət/
**Русский:** успех в чём-л. — достижение цели и область, где оно случилось
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **in / at** + **gerund** or a noun of activity (*success in reducing costs*, *success at persuading the board*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *success to reduce costs*). **with**: a person or a project (*success with the new supplier*). *Success of* is a different relation: it names whose success it is, not what the success consists in.
**Pattern:** *success in* [field / verb-ing] &middot; *success at* [activity / verb-ing] &middot; *success with* [person / project] &middot; *the success of* [venture] &middot; *have success in* [verb-ing] &middot; *without success* &middot; *the key to success*
**Variants:** *success in* — a domain or an effort &middot; *success at* — a specific activity or knack; both are standard American English and often interchangeable
**Contrast:** **The gerund is obligatory** — ✗ *she had success to negotiate a lower rate* — and the fix is *success **in** negotiating*. ✗ *success for* is an error. Distinguish *the success **of** the campaign* (how the campaign fared) from *success **in** the campaign* (what someone achieved during it). The adjective takes *in*: *successful **in** getting the permit*, and ✗ *successful to get the permit* is the same mistake one word-class over. Note *without success*, a fixed adverbial with no article.

The achieving of an aim, and the area in which it happens.

1. The clinic has had real *success in* reducing missed appointments.
2. He's had more *success at* raising money than at spending it well.
3. Our *success with* the new supplier lasted exactly one quarter.
4. The *success of* the festival surprised the people who ran it.
5. They tried three times to reach him, without *success*.
6. *Success in* getting the grant depends almost entirely on the narrative section.

**See also:** [progress in/on](#progress-inon), [experience of/in/with](#experience-ofinwith), [expert on/in](#expert-onin), [attempt at](#attempt-at), [successful in](15-dependent-adjectives.md#successful-in), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### successor to

**Pronunciation:** /səkˈsɛsɚ tu/ &middot; *suk-SESS-er too* &middot; weak /səkˈsɛsɚ tə/
**Русский:** преемник кого-л./чего-л. — следующий в должности, роду или ряду
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — business reporting, obituaries, and legal drafting. An American meets the phrase *successors and assigns* in the boilerplate at the end of almost every contract.
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming a person, an office, a title, or a thing being replaced (*a successor to the chairman*, *a successor to the Apollo program*) — **never a gerund** (✗ *a successor to running the program*) and **never a to-infinitive**. Note that *a successor to run the program* is grammatical but is not this pattern: there *to run* is a purpose infinitive hanging off a complete noun phrase, meaning someone brought in **in order to** run it.
**Pattern:** *a successor to* [person / office / product] &middot; *name / choose / appoint* [someone] *as successor to* [person] &middot; *the successor to the throne* &middot; *a successor in interest* &middot; *successors and assigns* (contract boilerplate) &middot; *the successor entity*
**Variants:** *a successor of* — not an error, but markedly less idiomatic in American English than *to* &middot; *successor in interest*, *successor in title*, and *successors and assigns* are fixed legal phrases that take no *to* at all
**Contrast:** **The person, the office, and the product all take *to*:** *a successor **to** Justice O'Connor*, *a successor **to** the chairmanship*, *a successor **to** the Model T*. Its opposite number is *predecessor*, which takes *to* as well but also *in* for the job itself — *her predecessor **in** the job*, *her predecessor **to** the seat*. Watch the noun-verb split: the verb *succeed **to*** takes an office or an estate (*he succeeded to the title in 1901*) while *succeed **in*** takes an effort (*she succeeded in raising the money*) — see [succeed in](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#succeed-in). ✗ *the successor for the chairman* and ✗ *a successor of running the program* are errors.

The person or thing that comes next in a position, a line, or a series.

1. The board still has not named a *successor to* the retiring chief executive.
2. She was his hand-picked *successor to* the seat and lost it in eighteen months.
3. The F-35 was sold to Congress as the *successor to* three separate airframes.
4. Under the Constitution the vice president is the *successor to* the presidency.
5. This Agreement binds the parties and their *successors* and assigns.
6. Every *successor to* the trusteeship inherits the same reporting obligation.

**See also:** [signatory to](#signatory-to), [party to](#party-to), [alternative to](#alternative-to), [substitute for](#substitute-for), [entitlement to](#entitlement-to), [succeed in](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#succeed-in)

### supply of

**Pronunciation:** /səˈplaɪ ʌv/ &middot; *suh-PLY uv* &middot; weak /səˈplaɪ əv/ → /səˈplaɪ ə/
**Русский:** запас чего-л., снабжение чем-л.; эконом. предложение
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: a noun phrase naming the commodity (*a supply of clean water*). **to**: the recipient (*supply to the northern towns*). **Never a gerund**, **never an infinitive**, **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a supply of* [commodity] &middot; *the supply to* [recipient] &middot; *supplies for* [purpose / trip] &middot; *in short supply* &middot; *supply and demand* &middot; *cut off the supply of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **is what the supply consists of;** ***to*** **is where it goes;** ***for*** **belongs to the plural** ***supplies***, meaning provisions. *A supply **of** water*, *the supply **to** the hospital*, *supplies **for** the trip* — and ✗ *a supply for water* is an error. **The plural is a different noun**: *supplies* means the things themselves (*office supplies*, *art supplies*), so *the supplies of water were cut off* reads oddly where *the supply of water was cut off* is right. *In short supply* is a fixed phrase with no article.

The amount of something available, and who gets it.

1. The town has a two-day *supply of* drinking water and no backup well.
2. The *supply to* the northern half of the county runs through one pump station.
3. We loaded *supplies for* a four-day trip and used most of it in two.
4. Qualified welders are in short *supply* everywhere in the state.
5. The whole argument comes down to *supply* and demand.
6. A steady *supply of* volunteers keeps the pantry open six days a week.

**See also:** [demand for](#demand-for), [need for](#need-for), [lack of](#lack-of), [price of/for](#price-offor), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### sympathy for/with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɪmpəθi fɔr/, /ˈsɪmpəθi wɪθ/ &middot; *SIM-puh-thee for, SIM-puh-thee with* &middot; weak /ˈsɪmpəθi fɚ/, /ˈsɪmpəθi wəθ/
**Русский:** for — сочувствие, жалость к кому-л.; with — согласие с позицией
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a person or group pitied (*sympathy for the families*). **with**: a view, cause, or position agreed with (*sympathy with the strikers' demands*). Gerunds are marginal; **never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause**.
**Pattern:** *sympathy for* [person] &middot; *sympathy with* [view / cause] &middot; *in sympathy with* [noun phrase] &middot; *have / feel / express sympathy for* [person] &middot; *my sympathies to* [the bereaved] &middot; *a sympathy card*
**Contrast:** ***For*** **is pity;** ***with*** **is agreement.** *Sympathy **for** the strikers* means you feel sorry for them; *sympathy **with** the strikers' demands* means you think they are right — and you can have one without the other, which is why the preposition matters. ✗ *sympathy to* is an error except in the fixed condolence formula *my sympathies to the family*. Note that *sympathetic **to*** and *sympathetic **toward*** both exist for the agreement sense (*sympathetic to the proposal*), so the adjective does not follow the noun's split cleanly. *In sympathy with* can also mean mechanically in step (*the second gauge moves in sympathy with the first*).

Pity for someone, or agreement with a position.

1. I have enormous *sympathy for* anyone trying to buy a first house right now.
2. The editorial board expressed *sympathy with* the union's core demand.
3. There was little public *sympathy for* the bank after the third fee increase.
4. She acted in *sympathy with* a strike she was not part of.
5. Please pass on my *sympathies to* the whole family.
6. His *sympathy for* the tenants did not extend to lowering the rent.

**See also:** [concern about/for](#concern-aboutfor), [respect for](#respect-for), [opposition to](#opposition-to), [agreement with/on](#agreement-withon), [sympathetic to](15-dependent-adjectives.md#sympathetic-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)


## T

### talent for/knack for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtælənt fɔr/, /næk fɔr/ &middot; *TAL-unt for, nak for* &middot; weak /ˈtælənt fɚ/, /næk fɚ/; the *k* of *knack* is silent, so the word is /næk/, a homophone of *nack*
**Русский:** талант к чему-л.; сноровка, умение ловко делать что-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** *talent for* neutral; *knack for* neutral to informal. Both are at home in speech, in a recommendation letter, and in a performance review.
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*a talent for getting people to talk*, *a knack for finding parking*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a talent to draw*, ✗ *a knack to find parking*) and **never a that-clause**. The *for* is a preposition, so a verb after it has to end in *-ing*.
**Pattern:** *a talent for* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *a knack for* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *have / show / display a talent for* [noun phrase] &middot; *a real / rare / genuine talent for* [noun phrase] &middot; *the knack of* [verb-ing] &middot; *get the knack of* [noun phrase] &middot; *there's a knack to it* &middot; *talent show*, *talent scout* (fixed compounds, no preposition)
**Variants:** *the knack of* + gerund — equally standard, and commoner in older writing &middot; *there's a knack to it* — a fixed phrase in which *knack* takes *to* and a pronoun; ✗ *there's a talent to it* is not possible &middot; *a talent to* + bare verb survives only as a literary archaism (Noël Coward's *A Talent to Amuse*) and is an error in ordinary modern American prose
**Contrast:** ***Talent*** **is capacity;** ***knack*** **is a trick you picked up.** A *talent for* the piano suggests something innate and large; a *knack for* fixing printers suggests something small and learned, so calling a concert pianist's ability a *knack* is faint praise. *Knack* alone takes *of* and *to* as well; *talent* takes only *for*. Against [fondness for](#fondness-for), which is liking rather than ability, and [success in/at](#success-inat), which is the result rather than the capacity. The adjective side takes *at*, never *for*: *good **at** languages* and *skilled **at** negotiation* — see [good at](15-dependent-adjectives.md#good-at) — while ✗ *good for languages* means something else entirely.

A natural ability, or a small acquired skill for doing something well.

1. He has a *talent for* remembering names that has carried his whole career.
2. She's got a *knack for* getting the county clerk to answer the phone.
3. Nobody in that family has any *talent for* keeping a secret.
4. There's a *knack to* opening the back gate without waking the dog.
5. The program looks for a *talent for* math long before it looks at grades.
6. It took me a month to get the *knack of* the espresso machine.

**See also:** [fondness for](#fondness-for), [reputation for](#reputation-for), [success in/at](#success-inat), [grasp of](#grasp-of), [good at](15-dependent-adjectives.md#good-at), [skilled at](15-dependent-adjectives.md#skilled-at)

### tariff on/duty on/levy on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtɛrɪf ɑn/, /ˈduti ɑn/, /ˈlɛvi ɑn/ &middot; *TAIR-if on, DOO-tee on, LEV-ee on* &middot; weak /ˈtɛrɪf ən/, /ˈduti ən/, /ˈlɛvi ən/
**Русский:** тариф, пошлина, сбор на что-л. — государственный сбор с товара
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — trade reporting, customs paperwork, and statute. An American meets *duty* at the customs desk on the way home and *tariff* in every election-year argument about trade.
**Complement:** **on**: a noun phrase naming the goods taxed, or the country they come from (*a tariff on imported steel*, *tariffs on China*). **of**: the rate (*a duty of twelve percent*). **against**: a country, in the retaliatory sense (*tariffs against Canada*), commoner in speech than in trade writing. **Never a gerund** — the complement names goods, not an activity, so ✗ *a tariff on importing steel* is not idiomatic — and **never an infinitive or a clause**.
**Pattern:** *a tariff on* [goods] &middot; *impose / levy / slap a tariff on* [noun phrase] &middot; *a duty of* [rate] *on* [goods] &middot; *a levy on* [goods / property / activity] &middot; *retaliatory / punitive / protective tariffs* &middot; *duty-free* &middot; *tariff rate*, *tariff schedule* (fixed compounds)
**Variants:** *a levy on* also covers local government charges that have nothing to do with imports — *a school levy*, *a levy on new construction* — where *tariff* and customs *duty* cannot be used &middot; the verb *tariff* exists but is rare; American idiom is *impose* or *slap tariffs on*
**Contrast:** **Three near-synonyms, one preposition, three different homes.** *Tariff* is the trade-policy word and the one used in politics; *duty* is the customs word an American actually pays at a border; *levy* is the broadest and lives mostly in local government and as a verb. All three take *on* for what is charged and *of* for the rate, and the two stack: *a duty **of** six percent **on** olive oil*. Against [tax on](#tax-on), the general term — every tariff is a tax, not every tax is a tariff. Keep this *duty* apart from the obligation sense at [duty to/of](#duty-toof): *a duty **on** olive oil* is money, *a duty **to** your clients* is not. ✗ *a tariff for imported steel* and ✗ *a tariff to imports* are errors.

A government charge on goods, named by what it falls on.

1. The administration imposed a twenty-five percent *tariff on* imported washing machines.
2. There's still a *duty on* wine over a liter, even in your own suitcase.
3. Voters rejected the *levy on* new construction for the second time in three years.
4. Canada answered with retaliatory *tariffs on* bourbon and motorcycles.
5. The *duty of* six percent applies whether the parts arrive assembled or loose.
6. Growers say the *tariff on* fertilizer costs them more than the one on equipment saves them.

**See also:** [tax on](#tax-on), [duty to/of](#duty-toof), [embargo on](#embargo-on), [sanction against/sanctions on](#sanction-againstsanctions-on), [price of/for](#price-offor), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### tax on

**Pronunciation:** /tæks ɑn/ &middot; *taks on* &middot; weak /tæks ən/
**Русский:** налог на что-л. — рус. «на», не «с»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **on**: a noun phrase naming what is taxed (*a tax on imported steel*). **of**: the rate or amount (*a tax of nine percent*). **for**: the purpose the revenue serves (*a tax for the new library*). **Never a gerund, an infinitive, or a clause.**
**Pattern:** *a tax on* [goods / activity] &middot; *a tax of* [rate / amount] &middot; *a tax for* [purpose] &middot; *pay tax on* [income] &middot; *before / after tax* &middot; *a tax break for* [group]
**Contrast:** ***On*** **for what is taxed,** ***of*** **for how much, and the two stack**: *a tax **of** nine percent **on** short-term rentals*. ✗ *a tax of short-term rentals* is an error. ✗ *a tax to imports* is likewise wrong. The verb takes a bare object for the thing or person taxed (*they tax capital gains*, *the county taxes nonresidents*) and no preposition at all: ✗ *they tax on capital gains*. Note the American terms *tax break*, *tax bracket*, and *tax return*, all fixed compounds that take *for*, *of*, and no preposition respectively.

A compulsory charge, named by what it falls on.

1. The council is considering a *tax on* short-term rentals.
2. There's a *tax of* nine percent on prepared food in this county.
3. Voters approved a small *tax for* the new library and rejected the one for streets.
4. You'll pay *tax on* the interest even if you never withdraw it.
5. The figures in column three are before *tax*.
6. The *tax on* diesel has not been raised since 1993.

**See also:** [price of/for](#price-offor), [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [demand for](#demand-for), [rule against/for](#rule-againstfor), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### taste for/in

**Pronunciation:** /teɪst fɔr/, /teɪst ɪn/ &middot; *tayst for, tayst in* &middot; weak /teɪst fɚ/, /teɪst ən/
**Русский:** for — вкус к чему-л., пристрастие; in — вкус в чём-л. (в одежде, в музыке)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a liking — noun phrase or gerund (*a taste for taking risks*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a taste to take risks*). **in**: the domain in which someone exercises judgment (*taste in music*). **of**: a literal or figurative sample (*a taste of what's coming*).
**Pattern:** *a taste for* [thing liked] &middot; *taste in* [domain] &middot; *a taste of* [sample / experience] &middot; *acquire a taste for* [noun phrase] &middot; *in good / poor taste* &middot; *to taste* (in recipes)
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions, three completely different claims.** *A taste **for** opera* means you like it; *taste **in** opera* means your judgment about which opera is good; *a taste **of** opera* means a small sample of it. ✗ *a taste of adventure* when you mean a liking is the usual slip. *In good taste* and *in poor taste* judge propriety, not preference, and take no complement. Note the fixed *an acquired taste* and the recipe instruction *salt to taste*, both bare.

A liking, a faculty of judgment, or a sample.

1. He developed a *taste for* very strong coffee in the Navy.
2. Her *taste in* music stopped developing somewhere around 1994.
3. The first week gave them a *taste of* what winter here is actually like.
4. The joke was in poor *taste* and the room let him know.
5. Olives are an acquired *taste* in most families.
6. Add salt and pepper to *taste*.

**See also:** [fondness for](#fondness-for), [preference for](#preference-for), [love for/of](#love-forof), [interest in](#interest-in), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### tendency to/toward

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtɛndənsi tu/, /ˈtɛndənsi təˈwɔrd/ &middot; *TEN-dun-see too, TEN-dun-see tuh-WORD* &middot; weak /ˈtɛndənsi tə/, /ˈtɛndənsi tɔrd/
**Русский:** склонность к чему-л., тенденция к — устойчивая наклонность
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **to** + **bare infinitive**: the habitual act (*a tendency to overreact*) — here *to* is the infinitive marker, not a preposition. **toward** + noun phrase or **gerund**: a direction or inclination (*a tendency toward caution*, *a tendency toward exaggerating*). ✗ *a tendency to overreacting* and ✗ *a tendency toward overreact* are both errors.
**Pattern:** *a tendency to* [verb] &middot; *a tendency toward* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *have a tendency to* [verb] &middot; *a growing tendency to* [verb] &middot; *tendencies* (plural, of a person)
**Variants:** ***toward*** — American, no *-s* &middot; ***towards*** — British &middot; *a tendency for* X *to* [verb] — the technical frame naming the thing that tends (*a tendency for the gasket to fail*)
**Contrast:** **This is the entry where the *to* trap runs in the opposite direction from the rest of the file.** After *tendency*, *to* is an **infinitive marker**, so the bare verb is correct — *a tendency **to** interrupt*, ✗ *a tendency to interrupting* — while after *toward* it is a preposition and *-ing* is correct. A learner who has just memorized *objection to being asked* will overcorrect into ✗ *a tendency to being late*. Test it as always: a noun cannot follow *tendency to* (✗ *a tendency to lateness*, where *a tendency toward lateness* is right), which proves *to* is not a preposition here.

An inclination to behave or develop in a particular way.

1. He has a *tendency to* interrupt when he's nervous.
2. There's a general *tendency toward* consolidation in the industry.
3. The soil here has a *tendency to* hold water through April.
4. Her *tendency toward* understatement makes the memoir harder to read than it should be.
5. There's a documented *tendency for* the older valves *to* stick.
6. The whole department has a *tendency to* wait for permission.

**See also:** [habit of](#habit-of), [attitude to/toward](#attitude-totoward), [willingness to](#willingness-to), [risk of](#risk-of), [toward](04-movement-direction.md#toward)

### threat to

**Pronunciation:** /θrɛt tu/ &middot; *thret too* &middot; weak /θrɛt tə/
**Русский:** угроза кому-л./чему-л. — рус. дательный, не «к»
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **to**: the person or thing endangered (*a threat to national security*). **of**: what is threatened (*a threat of violence*). **against**: the target of a stated menace (*a threat against a judge*). **from**: the source. The noun also takes a **to-infinitive** naming the threatened act (*a threat to resign*) and a **that-clause** (*a threat that the plant would close*).
**Pattern:** *a threat to* [thing endangered] &middot; *a threat of* [bad outcome] &middot; *a threat against* [target] &middot; *a threat from* [source] &middot; *a threat to* [verb] &middot; *under threat* &middot; *pose a threat to* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ***To*** **is who is endangered,** ***of*** **is what might happen, and the two produce different sentences from the same words**: *a threat **to** the water supply* is the supply being at risk, while *a threat **of** contamination* is the contamination being feared. ✗ *a threat for the water supply* is an error. Note that *a threat **to** resign* is an infinitive, not the preposition — the test is that no noun fits (✗ *a threat to resignation*). The verb takes a bare object and *with*: *they threatened the mayor*, *threatened him with a lawsuit*, ✗ *threatened to the mayor*.

Something that endangers, or a stated intention to do harm.

1. The unlined pit is a direct *threat to* the shallow aquifer.
2. There was a vague *threat of* legal action in the second paragraph.
3. The court received two credible *threats against* the presiding judge.
4. Her *threat to* resign ended the argument in about four seconds.
5. The whole business district is under *threat* from the next big flood.
6. Invasive mussels pose a real *threat to* the hatchery.

**See also:** [danger of/to](#danger-ofto), [risk of](#risk-of), [protection from/against](#protection-fromagainst), [attack on](#attack-on), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### threshold for
