**Pronunciation:** /ˈθrɛʃhoʊld fɔr/ &middot; *THRESH-hohld for* &middot; weak /ˈθrɛʃhoʊld fɚ/; /ˈθrɛʃoʊld/ without the second *h* is equally standard in American speech
**Русский:** порог чего-л. — уровень, с которого что-л. начинает действовать или засчитываться
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal — medicine, statistics, statute, and business policy. An American meets it as the income *threshold for* a tax credit, the *threshold for* reporting a cash payment, and, in a doctor's office, a *threshold for* pain.
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase naming what the level governs (*the threshold for eligibility*) or a **gerund** (*the threshold for reporting a spill*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *the threshold to report a spill*, ✗ *a threshold to qualify*) and **never a that-clause**. **of**: the quantity itself (*a threshold of five thousand dollars*, *the threshold of pain*). **at / on**: the level treated as a place (*on the threshold of a decision*).
**Pattern:** *the threshold for* [action / status / benefit] &middot; *a threshold of* [amount] &middot; *raise / lower / set / meet / cross / exceed the threshold* &middot; *above / below the threshold* &middot; *on the threshold of* [a change] &middot; *a pain threshold*, *a threshold question* (fixed compounds)
**Variants:** *a threshold of* + amount and *a threshold for* + purpose combine freely in one phrase — *a threshold **of** ten thousand dollars **for** reporting cash payments* &middot; British writing sometimes has *the threshold at which*, which is standard in American technical prose too
**Contrast:** ***For*** **names what the line governs;** ***of*** **names the line itself.** *The threshold **for** filing* is who has to file; *a threshold **of** ten thousand dollars* is the number. ✗ *the threshold to filing* is an error. Against [limit to/on](#limit-toon), which runs the other way: a limit is a ceiling you may not pass, a threshold is a floor you have to reach. Against [criterion for](#criterion-for), which is a test rather than a level, and [eligibility for](#eligibility-for), which is the status a threshold decides. Keep the literal doorsill in view — *on the threshold of* takes *of* and means about to begin, never a number.

The level at which something starts to count, apply, or take effect.

1. Congress raised the income *threshold for* the credit to eighty thousand dollars.
2. Her *threshold for* nonsense in a budget meeting is famously low.
3. The statute sets a *threshold of* ten thousand dollars for reporting cash payments.
4. Nothing below the *threshold for* reporting ever reaches the regional office.
5. Two of the four counties fell just under the *threshold* and got nothing.
6. He has an unusually high *threshold* for pain and a very low one for noise.

**See also:** [limit to/on](#limit-toon), [criterion for](#criterion-for), [eligibility for](#eligibility-for), [grounds for](#grounds-for), [exemption from](#exemption-from), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### title to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtaɪtəl tu/ &middot; *TY-tul too* &middot; weak /ˈtaɪtəl tə/; the flapped *t* makes the first syllable sound like *tie-dul*
**Русский:** юр. право собственности на что-л.; также правоустанавливающий документ
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal — deeds, closings, title insurance, and vehicle registration. An American meets it twice: when the state mails the title to a car, and at a closing table, where a title company certifies clear title to a house.
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming **property** — land, a vehicle, goods (*title to the property*) — **never a gerund** (✗ *title to owning the land*), **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *title to own the land*), and **never a person**. In this sense the noun is uncountable and normally takes no article: *she has title to the land*, not ✗ *she has a title to the land*. The unrelated noun *title* meaning a name or a rank takes *of*: *the title of the book*, *the title of vice chair*.
**Pattern:** *title to* [real property / a vehicle / goods] &middot; *hold / take / convey / pass title to* [noun phrase] &middot; *clear / marketable / good title to* [noun phrase] &middot; *a cloud on title* &middot; *title insurance*, *title search*, *title company* (fixed compounds) &middot; *the title of* [a work / an office] (the other noun entirely)
**Variants:** *title in* [property] appears in older deeds and in some state statutes and means the same thing; *to* is the modern American form &middot; in the sale of goods the verb phrase *title passes* is intransitive and takes no preposition at all
**Contrast:** ***Title to*** **is ownership;** ***the title of*** **is a name.** *Title **to** the house* is the legal right to it; *the title **of** the song* is what it is called; ✗ *the title of the house* means nothing. Against [possession of](#possession-of): possession is holding the thing, title is owning it, and a tenant has the first without the second. Against [claim against/for/to](#claim-againstforto): a claim to land is an assertion, title is the settled answer. Against [right to/of/over](#right-toofover): title is the strongest of the rights in property, which is why drafters write *title to and all rights in the parcel*. And keep [lien on/against](#lien-onagainst) separate — a lien sits on title without taking it.

Legal ownership of property, and the document that proves it.

1. The seller could not produce clear *title to* the back forty and the deal died.
2. *Title to* the vehicle passes when the lien is released, not when the last payment clears.
3. A quitclaim deed conveys whatever *title to* the parcel the grantor happens to have.
4. The bank will not close until the *title* search comes back clean.
5. Squatters can acquire *title to* land in this state after twenty years of open use.
6. She kept the car's *title* in a folder in the freezer.

**See also:** [possession of](#possession-of), [claim against/for/to](#claim-againstforto), [right to/of/over](#right-toofover), [easement over/across](#easement-overacross), [lien on/against](#lien-onagainst), [custody of](#custody-of)

### trouble with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtrʌbəl wɪθ/ &middot; *TRUB-ul with* &middot; weak /ˈtrʌbəl wəθ/
**Русский:** беда, проблема с кем-л./чем-л. — the trouble with him: беда в том, что он...
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **with**: a person or thing causing difficulty (*trouble with the transmission*). Also a bare **gerund** with no preposition at all in the *have trouble* frame (*trouble finding parking*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *trouble to find parking*). *The trouble is that* takes a clause.
**Pattern:** *trouble with* [person / thing] &middot; *have trouble* [verb-ing] &middot; *the trouble with* [noun phrase] *is that* [clause] &middot; *in trouble* &middot; *go to the trouble of* [verb-ing] &middot; *ask for trouble*
**Contrast:** **The gerund is obligatory and the infinitive is the error:** ✗ *I had trouble to understand him*, ✗ *she has trouble to sleep*. Write *trouble understanding him*, *trouble sleeping*. American English usually drops any preposition before the gerund, where *difficulty* optionally keeps *in* — see [difficulty with/in](#difficulty-within). Note *go to the trouble **of** doing something* (fixed, *of* required), *in trouble* (in a bad position, no complement), and *the trouble **with** him is that...*, the standard American frame for a character criticism. **The noun is uncountable here**: ✗ *a trouble with the transmission*, ✗ *troubles to sleep*.

Difficulty, and the thing or person causing it.

1. We've had nothing but *trouble with* that transmission since the rebuild.
2. She has *trouble* sleeping in any room with a streetlight.
3. The *trouble with* the new form is that nobody knows who signs it.
4. He's in *trouble* with the licensing board again.
5. Nobody went to the *trouble of* checking whether the permit was still valid.
6. I had *trouble* finding the entrance; it's behind the loading dock.

**See also:** [difficulty with/in](#difficulty-within), [problem with](#problem-with), [matter with](#matter-with), [delay in](#delay-in), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### trust in

**Pronunciation:** /trʌst ɪn/ &middot; *trust in* &middot; weak /trʌst ən/
**Русский:** доверие к кому-л. — рус. «к», не «в»; отдельно фин. траст, доверительный фонд
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; the *in* frame is slightly elevated in speech, where the noun often appears bare (*there's no trust left in that office*)
**Complement:** **in**: a person, an institution, or a system (*trust in the courts*), or a **gerund** (*trust in getting a fair hearing*, uncommon) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *trust in to get a fair hearing*) and **never a clause after the preposition** (✗ *trust in that the courts will act*). The noun does take a bare *that*-clause with no preposition at all (*a naive trust that things would work out*). **for**: in the financial sense, the beneficiary (*a trust for her grandchildren*).
**Pattern:** *trust in* [person / institution] &middot; *put / place one's trust in* [noun phrase] &middot; *have trust in* [noun phrase] &middot; *a breach of trust* &middot; *a position of trust* &middot; *take* [something] *on trust* &middot; *hold* [property] *in trust* &middot; *in trust for* [beneficiary]
**Variants:** *trust that* + clause, with no preposition — the standard way to attach a proposition &middot; the verb *trust to* survives in the fixed phrase *trust to luck*, and nowhere else in ordinary American usage
**Contrast:** **The noun needs *in*; the verb refuses it.** *I trust her* takes a bare object, and *I trust in her* sounds biblical, but the noun cannot do without the preposition: *my trust **in** her*. Against [confidence in](#confidence-in) and [faith in](#faith-in), which take the same preposition and differ in kind — confidence is calculated, trust is relational, faith is held without evidence — and against [belief in](#belief-in), which is about existence or value rather than reliability. ✗ *trust on* and ✗ *trust to* are errors in this sense. The financial noun is a different word wearing the same spelling: a *trust **for** the children* is a legal arrangement, and property held *in trust* takes no complement — see [in trust for](11-complex-multiword.md#in-trust-for).

Reliance on someone's honesty or reliability — and, in finance, the arrangement that holds property for someone else.

**1. Belief that a person or institution will not let you down.** The complement after *in* is the party relied on, never the thing hoped for.

1. Her *trust in* the county board did not survive the second audit.
2. He put far too much *trust in* a contractor he had never met.
3. Public *trust in* the agency has been falling for a decade.
4. Nothing rebuilds *trust in* an institution faster than firing the person responsible.

**2. A legal arrangement holding property for a beneficiary.** Countable, and it takes *for* naming the beneficiary; the property itself is held *in trust*.

5. The money sits in a *trust for* his grandchildren until they turn twenty-five.
6. The land is held *in trust* by the tribe and cannot be sold.

**See also:** [confidence in](#confidence-in), [faith in](#faith-in), [belief in](#belief-in), [dependence on/reliance on](#dependence-onreliance-on), [beneficiary of/under](#beneficiary-ofunder), [in trust for](11-complex-multiword.md#in-trust-for)



## U

### understanding of

**Pronunciation:** /ˌʌndɚˈstændɪŋ ʌv/ &middot; *un-der-STAN-ding uv* &middot; weak /ˌʌndɚˈstændɪŋ əv/ → /ˌʌndɚˈstændɪŋ ə/
**Русский:** понимание чего-л.; отдельно — неформальная договорённость
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of**: noun phrase, gerund (*an understanding of how to file*), or a *wh*-clause (*an understanding of what the job requires*) — **never a to-infinitive**. **with**: the other party to an informal agreement. The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *an understanding that the rent would not rise*.
**Pattern:** *an understanding of* [subject] &middot; *an understanding with* [person] &middot; *on the understanding that* [clause] &middot; *reach an understanding* &middot; *a working understanding of* [subject] &middot; *beyond my understanding*
**Contrast:** **Two different nouns.** *An understanding **of** the rules* is comprehension; *an understanding **with** the landlord* is an informal agreement, and the two senses share nothing but a spelling. ✗ *an understanding about the rules* is an error in the comprehension sense, though *an understanding about the parking* works in the agreement sense. *On the understanding that* is a fixed conditional frame — ✗ *on the understanding of that*. Distinguish from [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), which is possession of facts, and [grasp of](#grasp-of), which is nearly synonymous.

Comprehension of something, or an informal agreement.

1. She has a solid *understanding of* how the assessment appeal works.
2. We reached an *understanding with* the neighbors about the shared driveway.
3. He signed on the *understanding that* the rent would not rise for two years.
4. The report shows almost no *understanding of* how the permit process runs.
5. A working *understanding of* basic statistics would help here.
6. Why they filled in the well is beyond my *understanding*.

**See also:** [knowledge of/about](#knowledge-ofabout), [grasp of](#grasp-of), [insight into](#insight-into), [agreement with/on](#agreement-withon), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)


## V

### view on/of

**Pronunciation:** /vju ɑn/, /vju ʌv/ &middot; *vyoo on, vyoo uv* &middot; weak /vju ən/, /vju əv/ → /vju ə/
**Русский:** мнение о чём-л.; вид на что-л.; намерение (with a view to)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **on / about**: an opinion about an issue — noun phrase, gerund (*views on raising the levy*), or *wh*-clause. **of**: what can be seen from a place (*a view of the harbor*), or an assessment (*a dim view of his chances*). **to**: only in *with a view to* + **gerund** (*with a view to selling*), never an infinitive. **Never a that-clause after a preposition**, though *the view that* takes one bare.
**Pattern:** *views on / about* [issue] &middot; *a view of* [scene] &middot; *the view that* [clause] &middot; *in my view* &middot; *in view of* [noun phrase] &middot; *with a view to* [verb-ing] &middot; *take a dim view of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** **Four frames and one classic trap.** *Views **on** the levy* are opinions; *a view **of** the harbor* is what you see; *in view **of** the weather* means "because of"; and *with a view **to** selling* means "intending to." **That last *to* is a preposition, so the gerund is required**: ✗ *with a view to sell* is the error, and it is common in legal drafting. *My view about* is acceptable but *on* is the edited form, and ✗ *according to my view* should be *in my view*. Note *take a dim view of*, a fixed idiom of disapproval.

An opinion, a scene, or an intention.

1. Her *views on* zoning have hardened since she bought the lot.
2. The room has a *view of* the harbor and, unfortunately, of the parking structure.
3. In my *view*, the second bid was never serious.
4. In *view of* the forecast, the ceremony was moved indoors.
5. The property was bought with a *view to* subdividing it.
6. The board takes a dim *view of* people who miss three meetings in a row.

**See also:** [opinion on/of/about](#opinion-onofabout), [attitude to/toward](#attitude-totoward), [comment on](#comment-on), [idea of/for](#idea-offor), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### visit to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈvɪzɪt tu/ &middot; *VIZ-it too* &middot; weak /ˈvɪzɪt tə/
**Русский:** визит к кому-л., посещение чего-л., поездка куда-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **to**: the place or person visited (*a visit to the dentist*). **from**: the visitor. **with**: in American English, spending time with someone (*a visit with my grandmother*). **Never a gerund, an infinitive, or a clause.**
**Pattern:** *a visit to* [place / person] &middot; *a visit from* [visitor] &middot; *a visit with* [person] (American) &middot; *pay a visit to* [place] &middot; *on a visit* &middot; *a home visit*
**Variants:** *a visit with* — distinctly American; British English uses *a visit to* for people as well, and *visit with* can strike British readers as odd
**Contrast:** ***To*** **is where you went;** ***from*** **is who came to you;** ***with*** **is the time spent together.** *A visit **to** my grandmother* emphasizes going there; *a visit **with** my grandmother* emphasizes the conversation, and only American English has the second. The verb shows the same American split: *visit her* (go see her) against *visit **with** her* (sit and talk), and ✗ *visit to her* as a verb is an error — *visit* is bare transitive: *we visited the museum*, ✗ *we visited to the museum*.

A short stay somewhere, or time spent with someone.

1. Her annual *visit to* the dentist is the only appointment she never misses.
2. We had a *visit from* the building inspector on Tuesday.
3. I had a long *visit with* my aunt on the porch.
4. The senator paid a *visit to* the flooded neighborhoods on Friday.
5. They're here on a *visit* from Duluth.
6. A home *visit* is scheduled within ten days of discharge.

**See also:** [access to](#access-to), [introduction to](#introduction-to), [interview with](#interview-with), [encounter with](#encounter-with), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)


## W

### waiver of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈweɪvɚ ʌv/ &middot; *WAY-ver uv* &middot; weak /ˈweɪvɚ əv/ → /ˈweɪvɚ ə/
**Русский:** юр. отказ от права или требования; также документ, фиксирующий такой отказ
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal and official — contracts, court filings, insurance forms, and school and sports paperwork. An American signs one before a field trip, a 5K, or a trampoline park, and reads about one when a ballplayer clears waivers.
**Complement:** **of**: a noun phrase naming the right, claim, fee, or requirement given up (*a waiver of liability*), or a **gerund** (*a waiver of having to appear in person*, rare and clumsy) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a waiver to appear in person*) and **never a that-clause**. **from**: the requirement escaped, in administrative usage (*a waiver from the residency rule*). **for**: the person or program excused (*a waiver for rural districts*).
**Pattern:** *a waiver of* [a right / a claim / a fee / a requirement] &middot; *sign / grant / seek / obtain / deny a waiver* &middot; *a waiver from* [a rule] &middot; *a waiver for* [a person / a program] &middot; *no waiver of any provision shall be deemed a waiver of any other* (contract boilerplate) &middot; *on waivers*, *the waiver wire* (baseball and football)
**Variants:** *a waiver on* — heard in American administrative speech (*a waiver on the deadline*) but not standard in drafting, where *of* or *from* is written &middot; the fixed sports phrases *place a player on waivers* and *claim a player off waivers* use the bare plural with no complement
**Contrast:** ***Of*** **for the right surrendered,** ***from*** **for the rule escaped:** *a waiver **of** the fee* is the fee forgiven, *a waiver **from** the vaccination requirement* is the rule lifted for one person. ✗ *a waiver to liability* is an error. Against [exemption from](#exemption-from): an exemption is written into a rule in advance, a waiver gives up something you already held. Against [immunity from](#immunity-from), which is a standing status rather than a single act. And mind the homophone — you *waive* a right and you *waver* in a decision, and no spellchecker will catch ✗ *a waver of liability*.

A voluntary surrender of a right, a claim, or a requirement — and the paper that records it.

1. The camp will not take a child without a signed *waiver of* liability.
2. The state asked for a *waiver from* the federal work requirement.
3. Nothing in this section is a *waiver of* any other right under the contract.
4. She got a tuition *waiver for* her first two semesters.
5. Accepting one late payment is not a *waiver of* the right to insist on time.
6. He cleared *waivers* on Tuesday and signed with Detroit on Thursday.

**See also:** [exemption from](#exemption-from), [immunity from](#immunity-from), [breach of](#breach-of), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [notice of/to](#notice-ofto), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### warranty as to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈwɔrənti æz tu/ &middot; *WAWR-un-tee az too* &middot; weak /ˈwɔrənti əz tə/
**Русский:** юр. заверение относительно чего-л. — договорная гарантия по конкретному предмету
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** **legal drafting** — the representations-and-warranties section of a purchase agreement, and the all-caps disclaimer on a bill of sale. An American meets it in the sentence beginning *SELLER MAKES NO WARRANTY AS TO...* on nearly every used-equipment or as-is sale.
**Complement:** **as to**: a noun phrase or a ***wh*-clause** naming the subject warranted (*no warranty as to the accuracy of the figures*, *no warranty as to whether the software will run*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a warranty as to be accurate*). A gerund is grammatically possible (*no warranty as to meeting the specification*) but is almost never drafted; the noun phrase is the idiom. The same noun also takes **of** (*a warranty of merchantability*), **that** + clause (*a warranty that the goods conform*), and, in consumer usage, **on** and **for** (*the warranty on the transmission*, *a warranty for three years*).
**Pattern:** *no warranty as to* [subject] &middot; *representations and warranties as to* [title / condition / authority] &middot; *a warranty of* [merchantability / fitness for a particular purpose / title] &middot; *a warranty that* [clause] &middot; *the warranty on* [a product] &middot; *under warranty* &middot; *an extended warranty*
**Variants:** *warranty with respect to* and *warranty regarding* are drafted just as often and mean the same thing — see [with respect to](10-topic-reference-role.md#with-respect-to) &middot; British contracts prefer *warranty as to*; American ones split between *as to* and *with respect to*
**Contrast:** ***As to*** **fences off a topic,** ***of*** **names the promise, and** ***that*** **states it.** *No warranty **as to** the mileage* declines to say anything about mileage at all; *a warranty **of** title* is a specific promise; *a warranty **that** the roof is sound* spells the promise out in words. Everyday consumer speech uses a third frame entirely — *the warranty **on** the dishwasher*, *a warranty **for** three years* — and ✗ *the warranty as to the dishwasher* would be strange anywhere but a contract. Outside legal and academic writing *as to* reads as stiff and *about* or *on* is the natural choice: see [as to](10-topic-reference-role.md#as-to). Note also that *guarantee* is the everyday synonym of the noun but not a term of art — see [guarantee of](#guarantee-of).

A contractual promise about a fact, framed by the topic it covers.

1. The seller makes no *warranty as to* the accuracy of the square footage.
2. The bill of sale disclaims any *warranty as to* the condition of the equipment.
3. Section 4 contains the buyer's representations and *warranties as to* its authority to sign.
4. There is an implied *warranty of* merchantability unless the contract disclaims it in writing.
5. The dealer gave us a written *warranty* on the transmission and nothing else.
6. She would not sign anything that carried no *warranty as to* title.

**See also:** [guarantee of](#guarantee-of), [breach of](#breach-of), [remedy for/against](#remedy-foragainst), [liability for/to](#liability-forto), [exhibit to/schedule to/annex to](#exhibit-toschedule-toannex-to), [as to](10-topic-reference-role.md#as-to)

### way of/to

**Pronunciation:** /weɪ ʌv/, /weɪ tu/ &middot; *way uv, way too* &middot; weak /weɪ əv/ → /weɪ ə/; /weɪ tə/
**Русский:** способ сделать что-л. (way to + инф.); манера, привычка (way of + -ing)
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **of** + **gerund**: a manner or habit (*a way of speaking*) — **never a bare infinitive after *of*** (✗ *a way of speak*). **to** + **bare infinitive**: a means to an end (*a way to fix it*) — **never an *-ing* form after *to*** (✗ *a way to fixing it*). **to** + noun phrase: a route (*the way to the station*). ✗ *a way for doing it*.
**Pattern:** *a way of* [verb-ing] &middot; *a way to* [verb] &middot; *the way to* [place] &middot; *in a way* &middot; *by way of* [route / means] &middot; *no way* &middot; *the way* [clause] &middot; *get one's way*
**Variants:** *a way of doing it* and *a way to do it* — both standard; the first names a manner, the second a means. Where the two overlap (*a way of making bread*, *a way to make bread*) either is fine
**Contrast:** **The clearest minimal pair in the file.** *A way **to** fix this* answers "how do I get the result I want?" and takes the bare infinitive. *A way **of** turning every conversation into an argument* answers "what is this person's manner?" and takes *-ing*. The overlap is real but the edges are sharp: ✗ *a way to turning it into an argument* is impossible, and *her way to say thank you* is wrong because a manner is meant — write *her way **of** saying thank you*. ✗ *a way for doing it* and ✗ *a way of do it* are both errors. Note the separate route sense (*the way **to** the station*), which takes a noun, and the bare *the way* + clause (*the way he says it*), which needs no preposition at all.

A method, a manner, or a route.

1. There has to be a *way to* fix this without repouring the slab.
2. He has a *way of* making everyone in the room feel slightly accused.
3. It was her *way of* saying thank you without having to say it.
4. Can you tell me the *way to* the county courthouse?
5. That's not the *way to* talk to somebody who's doing you a favor.
6. In a *way*, the delay saved us.
7. There's no *way of* knowing how long the pump has been running dry.

**See also:** [reason for](#reason-for), [key to](#key-to), [solution to](#solution-to), [approach to](#approach-to), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### willingness to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈwɪlɪŋnəs tu/ &middot; *WIL-ing-nus too* &middot; weak /ˈwɪlɪŋnəs tə/
**Русский:** готовность сделать что-л. — согласие пойти навстречу
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** **to** + **bare infinitive** only (*a willingness to compromise*) — here *to* is the **infinitive marker, not a preposition**, so ✗ *a willingness to compromising* is an error and so is ✗ *a willingness to the compromise*. There is no *of* frame and no clause frame.
**Pattern:** *a willingness to* [verb] &middot; *show / demonstrate a willingness to* [verb] &middot; *an unwillingness to* [verb] &middot; *a willingness on the part of* [person] *to* [verb]
**Contrast:** **The test settles it in one move: no noun fits the slot.** ✗ *a willingness to a compromise* is impossible, which proves *to* is the infinitive marker and the bare verb is required. That makes *willingness* the exact opposite of [objection to](#objection-to), [opposition to](#opposition-to), and [alternative to](#alternative-to), where a noun does fit and *-ing* is required — and a learner who has drilled those three will overcorrect into ✗ *a willingness to compromising*. Note the parallel nouns that behave the same way: *ability to*, *readiness to*, *reluctance to*, *refusal to*, *decision to*, *right to*, all infinitive, none prepositional.

Readiness to do something.

1. What the job needs most is a *willingness to* be corrected in public.
2. Her *willingness to* work nights got her the shift she wanted.
3. The county showed no *willingness to* revisit the assessment.
4. There's a general un*willingness to* put anything in writing.
5. His *willingness to* drive four hours for a two-hour meeting is not admirable.
6. The settlement depended on a *willingness to* drop the counterclaim.

**See also:** [tendency to/toward](#tendency-totoward), [desire for](#desire-for), [need for](#need-for), [objection to](#objection-to), [reluctant to](15-dependent-adjectives.md#reluctant-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### wish for

**Pronunciation:** /wɪʃ fɔr/ &middot; *wish for* &middot; weak /wɪʃ fɚ/
**Русский:** желание чего-л., стремление к — рус. родительный
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **for**: a noun phrase naming what is wanted (*a wish for privacy*). The same noun takes a **to-infinitive with no preposition** (*a wish to be left alone*) and a **that-clause** (*a wish that things had gone differently*). ✗ *a wish of privacy* and ✗ *a wish for to be alone* are errors.
**Pattern:** *a wish for* [noun phrase] &middot; *a wish to* [verb] &middot; *a wish that* [clause] &middot; *best wishes for* [occasion] &middot; *against someone's wishes* &middot; *make a wish*
**Contrast:** ***For*** **takes a thing;** ***to*** **takes an act, exactly as with [desire for](#desire-for)**. ✗ *my wish of a quiet retirement* is an error; write *my wish **for** a quiet retirement*. The verb has three frames and only one takes *for*: *wish **for** a raise* (long for), *wish **to** speak* (formal "want"), and *wish someone a happy birthday* (two objects, no preposition) — so ✗ *wish for you a happy birthday* is wrong. Note also the counterfactual *I wish I had* + past perfect, which takes no preposition at all.

A desire for something, or a hope expressed.

1. Her only *wish for* the last year was to stay in her own house.
2. He expressed a *wish to* be cremated and nobody wrote it down.
3. Best *wishes for* a quick recovery.
4. The sale went ahead against the *wishes* of half the family.
5. There's a widespread *wish for* something simpler than the current form.
6. She made a *wish* and told everybody what it was, which ruins it.

**See also:** [desire for](#desire-for), [hope for/of](#hope-forof), [need for](#need-for), [preference for](#preference-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### worry about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈwɜri əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *WUR-ee uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ˈwɜri ˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** беспокойство о чём-л., тревога из-за чего-л.
**Type:** noun + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** **about / over**: noun phrase, gerund (*worries about losing the lease*), or *wh*-clause (*worry about what the tests will show*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *a worry to lose the lease*). The noun also takes a bare **that-clause**: *a worry that the roof would not last*.
**Pattern:** *a worry about / over* [noun phrase / verb-ing] &middot; *a worry that* [clause] &middot; *worries for* [someone's safety] &middot; *no worries* &middot; *a source of worry* &middot; *the least of my worries*
**Variants:** *worry about* — the default &middot; *worry over* — slightly more anxious and more literary &middot; *worry for* — used of a person's safety (*worried for her sister*), where *about* would be neutral
**Contrast:** ***About*** **for the noun,** ***by*** **for the passive adjective.** *Worried **about** the audit* is your own anxiety; *worried **by** the audit* is the audit acting on you — the second names an external cause, like any passive agent, so ✗ *worried about by the audit* is a blend. ✗ *worry of* is an error. The adjective *worried* takes *about*, *by*, and a *that*-clause but **never an infinitive of the thing feared**: ✗ *worried to lose the lease* should be *worried about losing the lease*. Compare [concern about/for](#concern-aboutfor), which splits *about* and *for* along the same anxiety-versus-welfare line.

Anxiety about something, and the thing causing it.

1. Her main *worry about* the move is finding a school midyear.
2. *Worries over* the water table have been raised at every hearing since 2019.
3. There's a real *worry that* the roof won't make it through another winter.
4. The rent is the least of my *worries* right now.
5. He never expressed any *worry about* the inspection until the day before.
6. She's the family's main source of *worry* and always has been.

**See also:** [concern about/for](#concern-aboutfor), [doubt about](#doubt-about), [fear of](#fear-of), [risk of](#risk-of), [worried about](15-dependent-adjectives.md#worried-about), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)


## Zero-preposition traps: verbs that take no preposition at all

**The other half of the noun-versus-verb shift.** Every verb below takes a direct object with nothing in between, while the noun built on the same root usually demands a preposition — *discuss the budget* but *a discussion **about** the budget*, *marry Tom* but *a marriage **to** Tom*, *lack funding* but *a lack **of** funding*. Learners who have absorbed the noun patterns in the rest of this file reliably leak the preposition backward onto the verb, producing ✗ *discuss about it*, ✗ *married with her*, ✗ *he lacks of experience*. The entries here exist so that the bare frame gets memorized as deliberately as the prepositional ones.

Two cautions. First, several of these verbs **do** take a preposition in a *different* sense (*attend to a patient*, *enter into an agreement*, *answer to a supervisor*), and those senses are marked below; the error is using the prepositional form for the plain transitive meaning. Second, the weak forms in this section belong to the object pronoun, not to a preposition — there is no preposition to reduce, which is exactly the point.

### answer something

**Pronunciation:** /ˈænsɚ ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *AN-ser SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce** — the verb runs straight into its object: *answer it* /ˈænsɚ ɪt/, *answer them* /ˈænsɚ ðəm/
**Русский:** отвечать на что-л. — англ. глагол без предлога (✗ answer to the question)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase — a question, a letter, a phone, a door, a person. It does **not** take *to* in this sense (✗ *answer to the question*). A separate intransitive sense takes *to* (report to) and *for* (be accountable for).
**Pattern:** *answer* [question / letter / phone / door] &middot; *answer* [person] &middot; *answer to* [supervisor] &middot; *answer for* [misconduct] &middot; *answer back* (talk back)
**Variants:** *respond to* and *reply to* — the prepositional synonyms; use them when you want the preposition
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Answer to the question*** **is the error and it is produced by the noun**, which does require *to*: *the answer **to** the question*. Keep the pair straight — verb bare, noun *to*. The prepositional verb exists but means something else entirely: *she answers **to** the regional director* (is supervised by), *he'll answer **for** the missing funds* (be held accountable). See [answer to](#answer-to) for the noun, and compare [reply to](#reply-to), where the preposition survives in both classes.

To give a response to a question, letter, phone, door, or person.

1. Just *answer* the question you were asked.
2. Nobody *answered* the phone at the district office all afternoon.
3. She never *answered* my second letter.
4. *Answer* the door, would you?
5. He *answers to* the regional director, not to me.
6. Somebody is going to have to *answer for* how this was approved.

**See also:** [answer to](#answer-to), [reply to](#reply-to), [response to](#response-to), [reaction to](#reaction-to)

### approach something

**Pronunciation:** /əˈproʊtʃ ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *uh-PROHCH SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *approach it* /əˈproʊtʃ ɪt/
**Русский:** приближаться к чему-л.; браться за что-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ approach to)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase — a place, a person, a problem, or a number (*approaching sixty*). It does **not** take *to* (✗ *approach to the desk*). The noun built on it does: *an approach **to** the problem*.
**Pattern:** *approach* [place / person] &middot; *approach* [person] *about* [matter] &middot; *approach* [problem] &middot; *approach* [number / age]
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Approach to the runway*** **as a verb is the error**, and it comes straight from the noun, which requires *to*: *the approach **to** the runway*. Say *the plane approached the runway*. When you name the subject you raised with someone, use *about*: *she approached me **about** the vacancy*, ✗ *approached me for the vacancy* in that sense. See [approach to](#approach-to) for the noun.

To come near to something, or to begin dealing with it.

1. The plane *approached* the runway too fast and went around.
2. She *approached* me after class about switching sections.
3. You have to *approach* the whole problem differently.
4. Don't *approach* the animal, no matter how tame it looks.
5. He was *approaching* sixty and had never taken a vacation.
6. Two men *approached* the counter and asked for the manager.

**See also:** [approach to](#approach-to), [attitude to/toward](#attitude-totoward), [way of/to](#way-ofto), [contact someone](#contact-someone)

### attend something

**Pronunciation:** /əˈtɛnd ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *uh-TEND SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *attend it* /əˈtɛnd ɪt/
**Русский:** присутствовать на чём-л., посещать — без предлога (attend to — совсем другое)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase — a meeting, a school, a funeral, a conference. It does **not** take *to* in this sense (✗ *attend to the meeting*). The separate phrasal verb *attend **to*** means to deal with or care for, and it is a different verb.
**Pattern:** *attend* [meeting / school / event] &middot; *attend to* [patient / matter] &middot; *attendance at* [event] &middot; *attended by* [number of people]
**Variants:** *go to* — the everyday alternative (*go to the meeting*), and much commoner in speech than *attend*
**Contrast:** **The preposition is not just unnecessary, it changes the verb.** *She attended the patient* means she was present at his bedside as a doctor; *she attended **to** the patient* means she treated him; *she attended **to** the meeting* is simply wrong. The noun requires a preposition — *attendance **at** the meeting*, ✗ *attendance of the meeting* — so the pair is another verb-bare / noun-prepositional split. Note that *attend* is formal in American English; in speech people *go to* things.

To be present at an event or enrolled at an institution.

1. Only nine people *attended* the budget hearing.
2. She *attended* the state university on a music scholarship.
3. I can't *attend* Thursday's session; I'll send the notes.
4. The funeral was *attended* by most of the department.
5. A nurse *attended to* the cut while we waited for the ambulance.
6. He has *attended* every meeting since 1994.

**See also:** [attention to](#attention-to), [visit to](#visit-to), [interview with](#interview-with), [access to](#access-to)

### contact someone

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑntækt ˈsʌmwʌn/ &middot; *KON-takt SUM-wun* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *contact them* /ˈkɑntækt ðəm/
**Русский:** связаться с кем-л. — англ. глагол без предлога (✗ contact with him)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase — a person, an office, or an agency. It does **not** take *with* as a verb (✗ *contact with the office*). The noun does: *contact **with** the office*.
**Pattern:** *contact* [person / office] &middot; *contact* [person] *about* [matter] &middot; *be in contact with* [person] &middot; *make contact with* [person] &middot; *a contact at* [organization]
**Variants:** *get in touch with* — the idiomatic everyday equivalent, and it does take *with*
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Please contact with our office*** **is the error**, and the noun is what causes it: *in contact **with** our office*, *make contact **with** them*. The verb takes the person straight: *contact the office*. Note the American stress difference between the noun and the verb is disappearing — both are now usually KON-takt — and that *a contact* meaning a useful person takes *at*: *a contact **at** the permitting office*.

To get in touch with a person or organization.

1. Please *contact* the registrar's office before the fifteenth.
2. I've been trying to *contact* him about the easement for two weeks.
3. *Contact* your carrier if the message doesn't clear in an hour.
4. She's been in *contact with* the family since Sunday.
5. We finally made *contact with* someone who knew where the records were.
6. He has a *contact at* the county assessor's office.

**See also:** [connection with/to/between](#connection-withtobetween), [encounter with](#encounter-with), [interview with](#interview-with), [phone someone](#phone-someone)

### describe something

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈskraɪb ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *dih-SKRYBE SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *describe it* /dəˈskraɪb ɪt/
**Русский:** описывать что-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ describe about it)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase, a *wh*-clause (*describe what you saw*), or *how* + clause. It does **not** take *about* (✗ *describe about the house*). **It cannot take an indirect object without *to***: ✗ *describe me the house* — write *describe the house to me*.
**Pattern:** *describe* [noun phrase] &middot; *describe* [noun phrase] *to* [person] &middot; *describe* [wh-clause] &middot; *describe* [someone] *as* [adjective]
**Contrast:** **Two errors, one verb.** ✗ *Describe about your trip* adds a preposition the verb never takes. ✗ *Describe me your trip* moves the recipient into a slot this verb does not have — unlike *tell* and *give*, *describe* is one of the Latin-derived verbs that refuses the double-object frame, so the person must come after *to*: *describe your trip **to** me*. The same restriction hits *explain*, *suggest*, *announce*, *mention*, and *report*: see [explain something to someone](#explain-something-to-someone).

To give an account of what something is like.

1. *Describe* the vehicle as precisely as you can.
2. She *described* the house to me over the phone and I bought it.
3. Can you *describe* what you saw before the lights went out?
4. The listing *describes* the roof as "newer," which means nothing.
5. He *described* himself as semi-retired, which was generous.
6. *Describe* how the machine sounded when it stopped.

**See also:** [explain something to someone](#explain-something-to-someone), [mention something](#mention-something), [report on/of](#report-onof), [comment on](#comment-on)

### discuss something

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈskʌs ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *dih-SKUSS SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *discuss it* /dəˈskʌs ɪt/
**Русский:** обсуждать что-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ discuss about the budget)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase, a gerund (*discuss selling the building*), or a *wh*-clause (*discuss what to do next*). It does **not** take *about* (✗ *discuss about the budget*) and **never a that-clause** (✗ *discuss that we should sell*).
**Pattern:** *discuss* [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] &middot; *discuss* [topic] *with* [person] &middot; *under discussion* &middot; *discuss* [noun phrase] *at length*
**Variants:** *talk about* — the everyday equivalent, and it does take *about*; *talk to* and *talk with* are both American, *with* implying more of an exchange
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Discuss about*** **is the single most persistent preposition error in advanced English**, and it comes from the noun, which requires one: *a discussion **about** the budget*. The verb is bare — *we discussed the budget* — and the person you discussed it with comes after *with*: *I discussed it **with** the treasurer* — and the object should not be left stranded after the *with* phrase, as in the graceless *I discussed with the treasurer the budget*. See [discussion about/of](#discussion-aboutof) for the noun, and note that the near-synonyms split: *talk **about***, *speak **about***, but *discuss* Ø and *debate* Ø.

To talk something through.

1. We *discussed* the budget for two hours and settled nothing.
2. I'd like to *discuss* the easement with you before you sign.
3. The board *discussed* selling the annex and voted it down.
4. Let's *discuss* what happens if the grant doesn't come through.
5. That was *discussed* at length in the March meeting.
6. She refuses to *discuss* it with anyone but her attorney.

**See also:** [discussion about/of](#discussion-aboutof), [argument with/about](#argument-withabout), [comment on](#comment-on), [mention something](#mention-something)

### emphasize something

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛmfəsaɪz ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *EM-fuh-syze SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *emphasize it* /ˈɛmfəsaɪz ɪt/
**Русский:** подчёркивать что-л., акцентировать — без предлога (✗ emphasize on)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase or a **that-clause** (*emphasized that the deadline was firm*). It does **not** take *on* (✗ *emphasize on the deadline*). The noun requires *on*: *emphasis **on** the deadline*.
**Pattern:** *emphasize* [noun phrase] &middot; *emphasize that* [clause] &middot; *place emphasis on* [noun phrase] &middot; *stress* [noun phrase] &middot; *stress that* [clause]
**Variants:** *stress* — behaves identically: *stress the risk*, ✗ *stress on the risk*, but *stress **on** the second syllable* as a noun
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Emphasize on*** **and** ✗ ***stress on*** **are errors produced entirely by the nouns**, both of which take *on*: *emphasis **on** safety*, *stress **on** the second syllable*. The verbs are bare: *emphasize safety*, *stress the risk*. This is one of the most visible non-native markers in academic English, because the construction appears in almost every abstract. See [emphasis on/stress on](#emphasis-onstress-on). Note the American spelling *-ize*; British writing allows *-ise*.

To give special importance to something.

1. The instructor *emphasized* the deadline three times.
2. I want to *emphasize* that nothing has been decided.
3. The report *emphasizes* rural access over headline speed.
4. She *stressed* the risk of signing before the survey came back.
5. The training *emphasizes* de-escalation rather than restraint.
6. He *emphasized* the word "voluntary" in a way that made it sound otherwise.

**See also:** [emphasis on/stress on](#emphasis-onstress-on), [attention to](#attention-to), [focus on](#focus-on), [importance of/to](#importance-ofto)

### enter something

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛntɚ ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *EN-ter SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *enter it* /ˈɛntɚ ɪt/
**Русский:** входить куда-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ enter into the room в прямом смысле)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase — a room, a building, a country, a contest, or data. In the literal sense it does **not** take *into* (✗ *enter into the room*). *Enter **into*** is a separate idiom used with agreements, negotiations, and abstractions.
**Pattern:** *enter* [place / contest] &middot; *enter* [data] &middot; *enter into* [an agreement / negotiations] &middot; *entry into* [place / market] &middot; *entrance to* [building]
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Enter into the room*** **is the error;** *enter the room* is the English. *Enter **into*** is reserved for things you take part in rather than places you walk into: *enter into a contract*, *enter into negotiations*, *enter into an agreement*, and the fixed *that doesn't enter into it* (is not relevant). The nouns take yet other prepositions — *entry **into** the market*, *entrance **to** the building* — so all three forms differ.

To go into a place, or to take part in something.

1. Two officers *entered* the building through the loading dock.
2. You have to *enter* the country on the same passport you booked with.
3. She *entered* the contest on a dare and won.
4. *Enter* your policy number in the second field.
5. The city *entered into* a twenty-year agreement with the utility.
6. How much it costs doesn't *enter into* it.

**See also:** [access to](#access-to), [introduction to](#introduction-to), [agreement with/on](#agreement-withon), [approach something](#approach-something)

### explain something to someone

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspleɪn ˈsʌmθɪŋ tu ˈsʌmwʌn/ &middot; *ik-SPLAYN SUM-thing too SUM-wun* &middot; *to* weakens to /tə/ and links: *explain it to me* /əkˈspleɪn ɪt tə mi/
**Русский:** объяснять что-л. кому-л. — порядок жёсткий (✗ explain me the rule)
**Type:** verb + direct object (+ *to* + person) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase, a **that-clause** (*explain that the form was late*), or a *wh*-clause (*explain why it failed*). The recipient must be introduced by *to*: ✗ *explain me the rule*. It does **not** take *about* (✗ *explain about the rule*, though *explain about it* is heard in casual speech).
**Pattern:** *explain* [noun phrase] *to* [person] &middot; *explain to* [person] *that* [clause] &middot; *explain* [wh-clause] &middot; *explain* [noun phrase] &middot; *an explanation of* [thing] *to* [person]
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Explain me the rule*** **is the error, and it is nearly universal** among speakers of Spanish, French, Italian, Russian, and German, all of which allow the double-object frame. English does not for this verb: the person takes *to*, and the thing explained comes first — *explain the rule **to** me*. When the explanation is a clause, *to me* moves ahead of it: *explain **to me** why the form was rejected*, where *explain why the form was rejected to me* is grammatical but clumsy. The same rule governs *describe*, *suggest*, *announce*, *mention*, *recommend*, and *report*. Contrast *tell*, *give*, *show*, and *send*, which do take the double object: *tell me the rule*, where *tell the rule to me* is stiff but not wrong.

To make something clear to someone.

1. Can you *explain* the appeal process *to* me in plain English?
2. She *explained to* the board that the money had already been spent.
3. Nobody has *explained* why the second inspection was canceled.
4. *Explain* it to your brother the way you just explained it to me.
5. He tried to *explain* the rules of cricket on a four-hour drive.
6. The letter never *explains* what triggered the review.

**See also:** [describe something](#describe-something), [mention something](#mention-something), [information about/on](#information-abouton), [reason for](#reason-for)

### join something

**Pronunciation:** /dʒɔɪn ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *joyn SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *join it* /dʒɔɪn ɪt/
**Русский:** вступать в что-л., присоединяться к — англ. без предлога (✗ join to the club)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase — an organization, a line, a group, or a person. It does **not** take *to* or *in* when it means becoming a member (✗ *join to the club*). *Join **in*** means take part in an ongoing activity; *join X **to** Y* means physically connect two things.
**Pattern:** *join* [organization / line / person] &middot; *join in* [activity] &middot; *join* X *to* Y &middot; *join up* (enlist) &middot; *join* [someone] *for* [meal / event]
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Join to the union*** **and** ✗ ***join with the club*** **are errors**; the verb is bare — *join the union*. Three related frames are not: *join **in** the singing* (participate), *join the two pipes **together*** (connect), and *join us **for** dinner* (accompany). The noun is a different word class entirely (*a joint*, *a junction*), so there is no noun-preposition pair here — the trap is purely the pull of *to* from equivalents in other languages.

To become a member of something, or to come together with someone.

1. She *joined* the volunteer fire department at nineteen.
2. *Join* the line behind the woman with the folding chair.
3. He *joined* the Navy two weeks after graduating.
4. Won't you *join* us *for* dinner on Saturday?
5. The kids eventually *joined in* the singing.
6. The plumber *joined* the new line *to* the existing stack.

**See also:** [contact someone](#contact-someone), [attend something](#attend-something), [agreement with/on](#agreement-withon), [contribution to](#contribution-to)

### lack something

**Pronunciation:** /læk ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *lak SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *lack it* /læk ɪt/
**Русский:** не иметь чего-л., испытывать нехватку — глагол без of (✗ he lacks of experience)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase, usually uncountable or plural (*lacks funding*, *lacks options*). It does **not** take *of* (✗ *lacks of funding*). The noun requires *of*: *a lack **of** funding*. The adjective requires *in*: *lacking **in** detail*.
**Pattern:** *lack* [noun phrase] &middot; *a lack of* [noun phrase] &middot; *be lacking in* [quality] &middot; *lack for* [nothing] &middot; *for lack of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** **Three words in one family, three constructions, and no overlap.** Verb: *the report lacks detail* — bare. Noun: *a lack **of** detail*. Adjective: *the report is lacking **in** detail*. ✗ *The report lacks of detail* is the error, and it is the standard transfer from French *manquer de*, Spanish *carecer de*, and Italian *mancare di*. The one place *for* belongs is the fixed litotes *he lacked for nothing* (he had everything he needed). See [lack of](#lack-of).

To be without something needed.

1. The proposal *lacks* any detail about who maintains the trail.
2. He doesn't *lack* confidence; he lacks a permit.
3. The building *lacks* a second exit, which is why it can't be used as a classroom.
4. What the argument *lacks* is a single verifiable number.
5. The estate was large and the children *lacked for* nothing.
6. The whole plan is *lacking in* anything resembling a budget.

**See also:** [lack of](#lack-of), [need for](#need-for), [supply of](#supply-of), [difficulty with/in](#difficulty-within)

### marry someone

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmɛri ˈsʌmwʌn/ &middot; *MAIR-ee SUM-wun* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *marry him* /ˈmɛri ɪm/ in fast speech
**Русский:** жениться на ком-л., выходить замуж за — без предлога (✗ marry with her)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase naming the spouse. It does **not** take *with* or *to* (✗ *marry with Tom*, ✗ *marry to Tom*). The **participial adjective does take *to***: *married **to** Tom*. The noun takes *to* as well: *her marriage **to** Tom*.
**Pattern:** *marry* [person] &middot; *get married to* [person] &middot; *be married to* [person] &middot; *marry into* [a family] &middot; *marry off* [a child] &middot; *the officiant married them*
**Variants:** *get married to* — the everyday American form; *marry* alone is slightly more formal, and *wed* is headline English
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Married with her*** **is the most recognizable preposition error in English**, carried in from Spanish *casarse con*, Italian *sposarsi con*, and Portuguese *casar com*. The English sequence is: **marry Ø** (*she married Tom*), **married to** (*she is married to Tom*), **marriage to** (*her marriage to Tom*). *Married **with** children* is correct only because *with* there means "having." Note that *marry* is also transitive in the officiant's sense (*the judge married them*) and takes *into* for joining a family (*he married into money*). See [marriage to](#marriage-to).

To take someone as a spouse, or to perform the ceremony.

1. She *married* Tom in her mother's backyard in 1998.
2. They got *married to* each other twice, ten years apart.
3. He's been *married to* the same woman since he was twenty-two.
4. A justice of the peace *married* them on a Tuesday morning.
5. She *married into* a family that has run the hardware store for a century.
6. *Married with* two kids and a thirty-year mortgage, he wasn't going anywhere.

**See also:** [marriage to](#marriage-to), [relationship with/between](#relationship-withbetween), [love for/of](#love-forof), [connection with/to/between](#connection-withtobetween)

### mention something

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmɛnʃən ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *MEN-shun SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *mention it* /ˈmɛnʃən ɪt/
**Русский:** упоминать о чём-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ mention about it)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase, a gerund (*mentioned seeing him*), a **that-clause** (*mentioned that the road was closed*), or a *wh*-clause. It does **not** take *about* (✗ *mention about the meeting*). The recipient takes *to*: ✗ *mention me the meeting*.
**Pattern:** *mention* [noun phrase / verb-ing / that-clause] &middot; *mention* [noun phrase] *to* [person] &middot; *not to mention* [noun phrase] &middot; *make mention of* [noun phrase] &middot; *don't mention it*
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Mention about it*** **and** ✗ ***mention me it*** **are the two errors.** The verb is bare and the recipient takes *to*: *mention it **to** her*. The **noun**, by contrast, requires *of*: *he made no mention **of** the fee*, *there is a mention **of** it in the minutes* — one more verb-bare / noun-prepositional pair. *Not to mention* is a fixed connector meaning "and also," and *don't mention it* is a response to thanks.

To refer to something briefly.

1. He never *mentioned* the second appraisal.
2. Did she *mention* the road closure *to* you?
3. The realtor *mentioned* that the furnace was original to the house.
4. Somebody *mentioned* seeing him at the hardware store on Sunday.
5. The lease says nothing about pets, not to *mention* parking.
6. There's no *mention of* the easement anywhere in the file.

**See also:** [comment on](#comment-on), [reference to](#reference-to), [discuss something](#discuss-something), [describe something](#describe-something)

### phone someone

**Pronunciation:** /foʊn ˈsʌmwʌn/ &middot; *fohn SUM-wun* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *call her* /kɔl ɚ/ in fast speech
**Русский:** звонить кому-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ phone to me)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase naming the person or office called. It does **not** take *to* (✗ *phone to your mother*, ✗ *call to the office*). *Call **on*** and *call **for*** exist as separate idioms with different meanings.
**Pattern:** *phone / call* [person] &middot; *call* [person] *about* [matter] &middot; *give* [someone] *a call* &middot; *call on* [someone] (visit or ask to speak) &middot; *call for* [something] (demand) &middot; *a call to* [number / person]
**Variants:** *call* — the American default; *phone* and *ring* are more British, and *ring someone up* is not American English
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Call to your mother*** **is the error**, produced by the noun, which does take *to*: *a call **to** her mother*. The verb is bare: *call your mother*. Two lookalikes mean other things: *call **on** the mayor* is to pay a visit or to invite her to speak, and *call **for** an investigation* is to demand one. Note that American English says *call*, uses *phone* mainly as a noun, and does not use *ring* in this sense at all.

To telephone a person or place.

1. *Call* your mother before she calls the state police.
2. I *phoned* the clinic three times and got a recording.
3. *Call* the office about the missing invoice, not me.
4. She gave him a *call* on the way home.
5. The board *called on* the auditor to explain the discrepancy.
6. There were four *calls to* the same number that night.

**See also:** [contact someone](#contact-someone), [letter to/from](#letter-tofrom), [request for](#request-for), [reply to](#reply-to)

### protest something

**Pronunciation:** /prəˈtɛst ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *pruh-TEST SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *protest it* /prəˈtɛst ɪt/ — and note the stress shift, verb pro-TEST against noun PRO-test /ˈproʊtɛst/
**Русский:** протестовать против чего-л. — амер. без предлога; брит. protest against
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** in American English &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase in American English (*protest the ruling*, *protest the tuition increase*), and a **that-clause** in the separate "insist" sense (*he protested that he had never seen the file*). British English keeps a preposition for the demonstration sense — *protest **against** the ruling*, *protest **at** the delay* — so *protest against* is not an error, simply British. The **noun** takes a preposition in both dialects: *a protest **against** the ruling*, *a protest **over** pay*.
**Pattern:** *protest* [noun phrase] &middot; *protest that* [clause] &middot; *protest against / at* [noun phrase] — British &middot; *a protest against / over* [noun phrase] &middot; *in protest of* [noun phrase] &middot; *under protest*
**Variants:** *protest* Ø — American &middot; *protest against* — British and Commonwealth for the demonstration sense &middot; *protest at* — British, chiefly with a decision (*protested at the delay*)
**Contrast:** **A genuine transatlantic split, not an error on either side.** Americans *protest the war*; the British *protest **against** the war* — an American editor cuts the *against*, a British one puts it back. The noun is prepositional everywhere (*a protest **against** the war*), which is exactly where the *against* keeps creeping back into the American verb. Two American frames keep a preposition and are fixed: *in protest of the ruling* and *under protest* (unwillingly). Keep the "insist" sense separate: it takes a clause and no preposition in both dialects — *she protested that the fee had already been paid*, ✗ *she protested about that the fee had been paid*. Compare [oppose](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#oppose), which is bare in both dialects while its noun takes *to*.

To object publicly to something, or to declare something in the face of doubt.

1. Students *protested* the tuition hike for three straight weeks.
2. Two hundred people *protested* outside the courthouse on Friday morning.
3. He *protested* that he had never seen the memo before that morning.
4. She signed the settlement under *protest* and filed an appeal the same day.
5. The London papers reported that the crowd had *protested against* the closures.
6. The union filed a formal *protest against* the new scheduling rule.

**See also:** [objection to](#objection-to), [opposition to](#opposition-to), [resistance to](#resistance-to), [complaint about](#complaint-about), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### reach something

**Pronunciation:** /ritʃ ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *reech SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *reach it* /ritʃ ɪt/
**Русский:** достигать чего-л., добираться до; дозвониться до кого-л. — без предлога (✗ reach to)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase — a place, a person, a total, or an agreement. It does **not** take *to* (✗ *reach to the summit*, ✗ *reach to a decision*). *Reach **for*** means to stretch toward something, and *reach **out to*** is a separate idiom meaning to make contact.
**Pattern:** *reach* [place / person / total] &middot; *reach* [an agreement / a decision / a verdict] &middot; *reach for* [object] &middot; *reach out to* [person] &middot; *out of reach* &middot; *within reach of* [noun phrase]
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Reach to the summit*** **is the error;** the verb is bare — *they reached the summit at noon*. *Reach **for*** is a different action (*he reached for the salt* — he stretched toward it, whether or not he got it), and *reach **out to*** is American business idiom for making contact, now so common that it has crowded out plain *contact*. The nouns take *of*: *out of reach*, *within reach **of** the freeway*. Note the fixed collocations *reach an agreement*, *reach a decision*, *reach a verdict*, all bare.

To arrive at something, or to get in touch with someone.

1. We *reached* the summit a little after noon.
2. I couldn't *reach* her all weekend; her phone was off.
3. The fund *reached* its target three days before the deadline.
4. The two sides finally *reached* an agreement on the driveway.
5. He *reached for* the flashlight and knocked over the lamp.
6. The top shelf is out of *reach* for anybody under six feet.

**See also:** [access to](#access-to), [contact someone](#contact-someone), [agreement with/on](#agreement-withon), [limit to/on](#limit-toon)

### request something

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkwɛst ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ &middot; *rih-KWEST SUM-thing* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *request it* /rəˈkwɛst ɪt/
**Русский:** просить, запрашивать что-л. — глагол без предлога (✗ request for a copy)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** formal
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase, a **to-infinitive** (*requested to see the file*), or a **that-clause with the subjunctive** (*requested that the fee be waived*). It does **not** take *for* (✗ *request for a copy*). The noun requires *for*: *a request **for** a copy*.
**Pattern:** *request* [noun phrase] &middot; *request to* [verb] &middot; *request that* [clause with subjunctive] &middot; *request* [noun phrase] *from* [person] &middot; *a request for* [noun phrase] &middot; *as requested*
**Variants:** *ask for* — the everyday equivalent, and it **does** take *for*; the pair *ask for* / *request* Ø is the thing to memorize
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Request for a copy*** **as a verb is the error**, and it is reinforced from both sides: the noun takes *for* (*a request **for** a copy*) and the plain synonym takes *for* (*ask **for** a copy*). Only the verb *request* is bare. Note the American subjunctive: *requested that he **be** present*, not ✗ *that he is present*. See [request for](#request-for).

To ask for something formally.

1. We *requested* the inspection records in writing.
2. She *requested* to see the original signature page.
3. The court *requested* that all exhibits be filed by Friday.
4. He *requested* a transfer to the night shift and got it.
5. Copies may be *requested* from the clerk's office.
6. The documents were produced as *requested*.

**See also:** [request for](#request-for), [demand for](#demand-for), [order for](#order-for), [permission for](#permission-for)

### resemble someone

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈzɛmbəl ˈsʌmwʌn/ &middot; *rih-ZEM-bul SUM-wun* &middot; **no preposition to reduce**: *resemble him* /rəˈzɛmbəl ɪm/
**Русский:** быть похожим на кого-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ resembles to his father)
**Type:** verb + direct object — **zero preposition** &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a direct noun phrase. It does **not** take *to* or *with* (✗ *resembles to his father*). The noun requires *to*: *a resemblance **to** his father*. The verb is **not used in the progressive**: ✗ *he is resembling his father*.
**Pattern:** *resemble* [person / thing] &middot; *bear a resemblance to* [person] &middot; *a family resemblance* &middot; *look like* [person]
**Variants:** *look like* — the everyday equivalent, also bare (*he looks like his father*), and much commoner in speech
**Contrast:** ✗ ***Resembles to his father*** **is the error**, imported from the noun (*a resemblance **to** his father*) and from Romance equivalents that are prepositional. The verb is bare: *he resembles his father*. Two further restrictions: it is a stative verb, so ✗ *he is resembling his father* is wrong, and it is somewhat formal — American speech prefers *looks like*, *takes after*, or *has his father's eyes*.

To look or be like someone or something.

1. He *resembles* his father more every year.
2. The finished porch *resembled* the drawing only in outline.
3. Nothing in the final bill *resembles* what was introduced in January.
4. She bears a striking *resemblance to* her grandmother at the same age.
5. The two accounts *resemble* each other closely enough to be suspicious.
6. There's an unmistakable family *resemblance* around the eyes.

**See also:** [similarity to/between](#similarity-tobetween), [comparison with/to](#comparison-withto), [difference between/in](#difference-betweenin), [link between/to](#link-betweento)
